It was not long ago that the north of England began to see a steady stream of young curates—men fresh from Oxford and Cambridge, eager for duty, full of zeal, and scattered thickly across the hills like seeds blown by the wind. Every parish seemed to have one or more, and their presence promised hope for renewal in quiet country towns. But we are not speaking of today. We go back—far back—to the early years of the nineteenth century, when the world was still shaped by simpler rules, and the air hung heavy with the scent of damp wool, woodsmoke, and old stone.

This is no tale of romance, reader. If you expect passion, drama, or moonlit confessions beneath willow trees, you may as well close this book now. This story is not spun from dreams, but from the solid, unyielding threads of everyday life. It is as plain and unadorned as a Yorkshire morning—cold, clear, and without pretense. The first course on our table will not be roses or wine, but lentils and vinegar; unleavened bread and bitter herbs. It may not please the fancy, but it will feed the soul.

In those days—1811–12—the shower of curates had not yet fallen. They were rare, almost precious. There was no Pastoral Aid Society, no fund to send young men into the parishes. No one thought of sending a man of twenty-two to serve beside an aging rector unless he could afford him. And so, the churches stood with few hands to tend them, and fewer hearts to warm them.

Yet even then, there were curates—three in particular, scattered across twenty miles of West Riding countryside, like stars in a sparse sky. These were not men of great learning or deep piety, nor were they saints. They were young, restless, and full of talk. Their names: Mr. Donne, curate of Whinbury; Mr. Malone, of Briarfield; and Mr. Sweeting, of Nunnely. And though each lived in his own modest lodging, they saw little reason to stay apart.

On this day, they gathered at Mr. Donne’s house—a neat garden cottage just outside Whinbury, rented from John Gale, a small clothier. Mrs. Gale, who ran the household with a sharp eye and sharper tongue, served dinner with a look that said she would rather have been alone. But hospitality was part of the bargain, and so she allowed them in.

The three sat at the table, eating roast beef and Yorkshire pudding, drinking flat beer, and arguing over trifles. They were all young—no older than twenty-five—and filled with energy that had nowhere else to go. Their vicars wished they would visit the sick, teach the children, or preach with sincerity. But these men preferred something far less noble: the endless round of dinners, teas, and suppers among themselves.

They moved between lodgings like clockwork—Malone to Donne, Donne to Sweeting, Sweeting to Malone—regardless of weather or time. Snow, rain, mud, wind—it mattered not. They came and went, staying longer than welcome, leaving behind disorder and grumbling landladies. Mrs. Gale, Mrs. Hogg, and Mrs. Whipp all complained alike: “It’s just for naught but to give folk trouble.” By “folk,” they meant themselves.

And indeed, they did. Mrs. Gale especially resented the way they treated her. She worked hard, cooked for them, cleaned their rooms, and yet they spoke of her as if she were beneath them. One of them, Mr. Malone, particularly offended her. He was tall, broad-shouldered, and carried himself with a kind of pride that felt foreign in such a place. His speech was thick with Irish brogue, and his manner rough. When he demanded, “Cut it, woman,” she nearly struck him—not out of anger, but because the tone grated against every fiber of her Yorkshire upbringing.

Still, they ate heartily. The beef was tough, the beer flat, but they consumed it all. A dish of vegetables vanished quickly, followed by cheese and a spice cake—so swiftly that Abraham, Mrs. Gale’s six-year-old son, burst into tears when he saw the empty plate. “I wanted a piece!” he cried. But no one heard him.

After dinner, they drank wine—plain, ordinary wine—and turned to argument. Not politics, not literature, not even theology in any serious sense. No, their debate was about the smallest points of church ritual—whether a surplice should be worn indoors, how many candles ought to burn during communion, whether the clerk should bow before the bishop. To outsiders, it seemed trivial. To them, it was everything.

Mr. Malone, having drunk two glasses while the others had only one, grew bold. He laughed loudly, mocked his companions, and teased them relentlessly. He joked about Mr. Donne’s thin frame and odd clothes—especially the chocolate-colored coat he wore in rain, which looked threadbare and absurd. He ridiculed Mr. Sweeting’s height, his flute-playing, and the way he always spoke of his sisters as if they were angels.

Sweeting took it lightly, smiling through it all. Donne, however, bristled. He responded with stiff dignity, trying to hold onto what little self-respect he had left. But soon, the teasing became too much. They turned on Malone, asking how many boys had shouted “Irish Peter!” after him that day (his name was Peter Augustus Malone), mocking his pronunciation—“vele” instead of “veil,” “firrum” for “firm”—and questioning whether Irish clergymen really carried pistols and shillelahs on pastoral visits.

Malone flared up. He rose, stamped his foot, and declared he would not stand for such insults. He called them “Saxons,” “snobs,” and worse. They retorted that he came from a conquered land, that Ireland was a nation of beggars and rebels. The room erupted. Glasses rattled. Voices rose. It sounded like a quarrel that might end in violence.

But it didn’t. Because this was not the first time such a scene had occurred. Mrs. Gale and her husband sat quietly in the kitchen, listening to the noise, knowing full well that no real harm would come of it. These young men fought like brothers, not enemies. They would argue fiercely tonight, but tomorrow they’d be laughing together again.

Then came a knock at the door.

A man stepped inside—Mr. Helstone, middle-aged, dressed in black, with a stern face and a hat perched high on his head. He walked straight through the kitchen, opened the parlour door, and paused—listening.

“What!” he said, once inside. “Has the miracle of Pentecost returned? Is this the sound of seventeen tongues? Parthians, Medes, Elamites—I hear them all! The whole house is alive with noise!”

He surveyed the three curates, arms folded, eyes sharp. He was short but upright, with a hawk-like gaze and a voice that dropped low, deep, and hollow, as if carved from stone.

“You call yourselves apostles?” he asked. “No. You are Babylonian masons—nothing more. This is not the gift of tongues, but the confusion of them.”

Mr. Donne tried to apologize. “We were only talking, sir—settling the Dissenters.”

“Oh, settling the Dissenters?” Mr. Helstone replied, coldly. “It sounded more like settling each other. You make more noise than Moses Barraclough and his Methodists down the lane. And I know whose fault it is—yours, Malone.”

“Mine, sir?”

“Yes, yours. Before you arrived, the others were quiet. Now, chaos. You bring your Dublin ways here—your loudness, your arrogance. Such conduct brings shame not only upon yourselves, but upon the Church itself.”

There was a silence. Then Mr. Helstone continued, his tone firm but measured.

“I met Supplehough earlier—going to preach at Milldean. He baptized sixteen adults in one day. Barraclough draws crowds with his fiery sermons. And here you sit, drinking wine and quarreling like women. No wonder the people turn away from us.”

He paused, then said, “I came to see you, Malone. I have a task for you.”

“What is it?” Malone asked, annoyed.

“There is no funeral. But there is danger. Moore is alone at Hollow’s Mill. He sent his workers to Stilbro’, and only two women remain. The path is clear. Someone might take advantage.”

“I don’t care for Moore,” Malone said.

“You do not fear him either,” Mr. Helstone replied. “But there is a chance of trouble. Moore expects new machinery tonight. Scott and a few men are going to fetch it. But someone must be there—someone to witness if anything happens.”

“I won起 to go,” Malone said.

“Then you are afraid.”

“No, sir. But Moore is strange. I wouldn’t go for his company.”

“And yet you will go—for duty. For the sake of peace.”

Malone hesitated. Then nodded.

“Very well. I’ll go.”

“Good. And take your weapons. The pistols I gave you. Your blackthorn.”

Malone reached for them.

As he did, Mr. Sweeting spoke up. “Sir, I heard something the other day—about Mike Hartley.”

“The Antinomian weaver?”

“Yes. He’s drunk often, and when he is, he goes to Nunnely vicarage to tell Mr. Hall how terrible his sermons are. But he’s also a Jacobin—violent, angry. He talks of regicide. And lately, he’s said he wants Moore to be made an example.”

“A sacrifice?” Mr. Helstone asked.

“Yes. He called Moore the smartest mill-owner in Yorkshire—and said that makes him the perfect offering.”

Mr. Helstone frowned. “Mike Hartley is not right in the head. Or perhaps he is. Either way, he is dangerous.”

“But he sees visions,” Sweeting added.

“He does. Last Friday, he came to my door, breathless, describing one. In Nunnely Park—he saw a figure walking among the trees, cloaked in red, holding a sword. And he said the figure pointed toward Hollow’s Mill.”

A silence fell.

Mr. Helstone looked at Malone. “You go. Not for Moore’s sake—but for the sake of order. For the sake of peace.”

Malone nodded again.

And so, with pistols in hand and a mind full of doubt, he left the house—into the dark, wet night, toward the mill where shadows waited.

The evening was black as ink—no moon, no stars, only a heavy sky choked with rain-laden clouds that looked like smudged charcoal. Malone wasn’t the sort to notice such things. Nature’s moods passed him by without stirring a thought. He could walk for miles through April showers and never see the sun catch the hills in golden light or feel the hush of a soft rain washing over them. He didn’t pause to wonder where the stars had gone or mourn the absence of the clear, deep blue night he might have known on a colder, clearer evening. He simply trudged forward, head down, hat pulled back on his head in the way Irishmen often wore it, stepping steadily along the causeway—"tramp, tramp"—then splashing through muddy ruts where the paving stones gave way to wet earth—"splash, splash." His eyes were fixed only on landmarks: first, the spire of Briarfield Church; then, farther ahead, the flickering lights of Redhouse Inn.

He came close to it, and the sight of firelight glowing through a half-drawn curtain, glasses on a round table, and voices laughing from an oak settle nearly made him turn aside. A tumbler of whisky-and-water sounded tempting. But this wasn’t just any inn—it was Mr. Helstone’s parishioners gathered there. They knew him. He sighed, shook his head, and kept walking.

Now the highroad ended. To reach Hollow’s Mill, a shortcut across open fields would save time. These fields were flat and bare, offering no shelter or beauty. Malone cut straight through them, leaping over hedges and stone walls. He passed one building—a large, odd-shaped house, like a hall built without plan. There was a high gable, then a long front, then another low one, topped by a thick chimney stack. Trees stood behind it, but no light shone from any window. It was utterly still—the only sounds were the rain trickling from the eaves and the quiet whistle of wind around the chimneys and through the branches.

Beyond that, the land sloped sharply downward. Below lay a valley, where water murmured through the dark. One small light glimmered far off—his destination.

He reached a little white cottage, visible even in the dense gloom, and knocked. A young servant opened the door, holding a candle. The narrow passage led to a narrow staircase. Crimson baize doors and a strip of crimson carpet contrasted with pale walls and a white floor, making the place look clean and warm.

"Mr. Moore is at home, I suppose?"

"Yes, sir, but he’s not in."

"Not in? Where is he?"

"At the mill—in the counting-house."

Then one of the red doors opened.

"Are the wagons come, Sarah?" asked a woman’s voice—and a woman’s face appeared at the same moment. She wasn’t exactly beautiful—curl-paper wound around her temples spoiled any claim to goddess-like grace—but she wasn’t frightening either. Still, Malone flinched as if he’d seen something dreadful. Though big and sturdy, he stepped back into the rain, muttering, "I’ll go to him," and hurried down a short lane toward the looming shadow of the mill.

Work hours were over. The workers had gone. The machinery was silent. The mill was shut. Malone walked around its soot-blackened side until he found a crack of light. He knocked with the thick end of his shillelah, beating out a sharp rhythm.

A key turned. The door opened.

"Is it Joe Scott? What news of the wagons, Joe?"

"No," said Malone. "It's me. Mr. Helstone sent me."

"Oh! Mr. Malone." The voice held a faint note of disappointment. After a pause, it continued politely, though stiffly:
"I beg you will come in. I regret deeply that Mr. Helstone should have thought it necessary to send you so far. There was no need—I told him so—and on such a night. But please step inside."

Malone followed through a dim, indistinct room into a brighter one within. It seemed dazzling after the darkness outside—brightened by a good fire and a lamp with a vivid glow on the table. Yet the room itself was plain: wooden floor, no carpet; a few stiff green chairs that once belonged to a farm kitchen; a solid desk, a simple table, and framed plans on the walls—buildings, gardens, machines.

Plain, yes—but it suited Malone. Once he hung up his wet coat and hat, he dragged a creaky chair to the hearth and planted his knees almost inside the bars of the red-hot grate.

"Comfortable quarters you’ve got here, Mr. Moore. All snug to yourself."

"Yes, but my sister would be glad to see you, if you’d prefer coming into the house."

"Oh no!" Malone laughed. "Ladies are best left alone. I’m no lady’s man. You don’t mistake me for Sweeting, do you?"

"Sweeting? Which one? The gentleman in the chocolate coat—or the little one?"

"The little one—Nunnely’s boy. The cavalier of the Misses Sykes, who’s in love with all six of them, ha! ha!"

"Better to love them all than just one," Moore replied quietly.

"But he’s specially in love with one. When I and Donne pressed him to choose, he named—guess who?"

Moore smiled faintly. "Dora, of course. Or Harriet."

"Ha! Ha! You guessed well. But how did you know?"

"Because they’re the tallest, the handsomest—and Dora, at least, is the sturdiest. And since your friend Mr. Sweeting is slight and small, I assumed he'd prefer a contrast."

"You're right—Dora it is. But he has no chance, does he?"

"What does Mr. Sweeting have besides his curacy?"

That question made Malone burst out laughing—for three full minutes before he could speak.

"What does Sweeting have? Why, David has his harp—or flute, which is much the same. A pinchbeck watch, ditto ring, ditto eyeglass. That’s his fortune."

"How would he support Miss Sykes in gowns?"

"Ha! Ha! Excellent! I’ll ask him next time. I’ll roast him for his presumption. But surely he thinks old Christopher Sykes will help out. He’s rich, isn’t he? They live in a big house."

"Sykes runs a large business."

"So he must be wealthy?"

"He must have plenty to do with it. In these times, he’d be about as likely to pull money from his trade to give dowries as I’d be to tear down that cottage and build a mansion as big as Fieldhead."

"Do you know what I heard, Moore, the other day?"

"No. Perhaps that I *was* planning such a thing. Your Briarfield neighbors can say anything—sillier things, even."

"That you were going to lease Fieldhead—and marry a Miss Sykes there. Dora, I’m sure. You said she was the handsomest."

Moore sighed. "How many times have I been betrothed since I came to Briarfield? Every eligible woman in the district has been assigned to me in turn. First the two Misses Wynns—one dark, one fair; then Miss Armitage, red-haired; then Ann Pearson. Now it’s the whole clan of the Misses Sykes. On what grounds? Heaven knows. I visit no one. I avoid female society as thoroughly as you do, Mr. Malone. If I ever go to Whinbury, it’s only to call on Sykes or Pearson in their counting-houses—where we talk business, not marriage. We discuss cloth we can’t sell, hands we can’t employ, mills we can’t run. The world turns against us. Our minds are filled with troubles—not with courtships or dowries."

"I agree completely, Moore. If there’s one idea I hate more than any, it’s marriage—as people usually mean it. Two poor fools tying themselves together in some sentimental knot, pretending it’s love. Humbug! But a marriage based on dignity, mutual respect, lasting interests—that’s different."

"No," Moore answered, distracted. He seemed lost in thought. After a while, he stared into the fire, then suddenly turned.

"Hark! Did you hear wheels?"

He rose, went to the window, opened it, listened. Then closed it again.

"Just the wind rising. The stream’s swollen, rushing down the hollow. I expected the wagons at six. It’s nearly nine now."

"Seriously," Malone asked, "do you think installing this new machinery puts you in danger? Helstone seems to think so."

"I only wish the frames were safely inside. Once they’re up, I defy anyone. Let the frame-breakers come. My mill is my castle."

"One despises such scoundrels," Malone said solemnly. "I almost wish a gang would show up tonight. But the road seemed quiet when I came. Saw nothing moving."

"You came by Redhouse?"

"Yes."

"There’d be nothing there. The risk lies toward Stilbro’."

"And you think there’s real danger?"

"They’ve done it to others. They may do it to me. The difference is—most manufacturers crumble when attacked. Take Sykes: when his dressing-shop burned, when cloth was torn from his tenters and left in ruins, he did nothing. Gave up like a rabbit under a ferret. But I—well, if I know myself—I’ll stand by my mill, my trade, my machinery."

"Helstone says these three are your gods. That 'Orders in Council' are your seven deadly sins. Castlereagh your Antichrist, and the war-party his legions."

"Yes. I hate them because they ruin me. They block my path. I can’t move forward. I can’t carry out my plans. At every turn, I’m thwarted by their effects."

"But you’re rich and thriving, Moore?"

"Rich in cloth I can’t sell. Come see my warehouse—stacked to the roof. Roakes and Pearson are the same. America used to take their goods. But the Orders in Council cut that off."

Malone yawned, knocking his boots together.

"And yet," Moore went on, unaware of his guest’s boredom, "they keep pestering me about marriage! As if life were only about paying attention to some young lady, then marrying her, taking a honeymoon tour, visiting relatives, and starting a family. Oh, que le diable emporte!" He broke off abruptly, then added calmer, "I believe women only talk about such things. So naturally, they assume men do too."

"Of course," Malone agreed. "But never mind them." He whistled, glanced around impatiently, clearly wanting something.

This time Moore noticed.

"Mr. Malone," he said, "you must be hungry after that walk. I’ve forgotten hospitality."

"Not at all," Malone replied—but his eyes lit up. Moore rose, opened a cupboard.

"I like having everything ready," he said. "No need to depend on the women in the cottage for food or drink. I often spend evenings here alone, sleep with Joe Scott in the mill. Sometimes I’m my own watchman. I don’t sleep much. On fine nights, I wander with my musket around the hollow. Mr. Malone—can you cook a mutton chop?"

"Try me. I’ve done it hundreds of times at college."

"There’s a dishful. And the gridiron. Turn them quickly. Know how to keep the juices in?"

"Never fear. Hand me a knife and fork."

Malone rolled up his sleeves and set to work with energy. Moore placed plates, bread, a black bottle, and two tumblers on the table. Then he took out a small copper kettle, filled it from a stone jar, set it beside the sizzling gridiron, fetched lemons, sugar, and a china punch bowl. While brewing, a knock came.

"Is it you, Sarah?"

"Yes, sir. Will you come to supper?"

"No. I won’t be in tonight. I’ll sleep in the mill. Lock the doors. Tell your mistress to go to bed."

He returned.

"You’ve your household in order," Malone said, cheeks flushed from the heat, turning the chops with care. "You’re not under petticoat rule, like poor Sweeting—whew! How the fat spits! Burnt my hand—there’s a fine brown one for you, full of gravy. You and I, Moore—we’ll have no gray mares in our stables when we marry."

"I don’t know," Moore said. "If the gray mare is handsome and gentle, why not?"

"The chops are done. Is the punch ready?"

"There’s a glass. Taste it. If Joe and his crew return with the frames intact, they’ll share this."

Malone grew loud and cheerful over the meal. He laughed at trifles, made jokes and laughed at them himself, growing boisterous. Moore remained quiet, absorbed.

Reader, it’s time you saw the man who sat opposite him.

At first glance, Moore looked unusual—thin, dark, sallow, with foreign features and hair falling carelessly across his forehead. He paid little attention to grooming. His face, though finely shaped with southern symmetry, was marred by a look of worry and weariness. His eyes were large, gray, grave—thoughtful rather than warm, searching rather than kind. When he smiled, it softened his expression slightly—not cheerfully, but with a quiet charm that hinted at patience, kindness, perhaps loyalty. He was young—no more than thirty—tall and slender. His speech grated, with a foreign accent that clung despite his careful diction. It unsettled Yorkshire ears.

For Moore was not fully English. His mother was foreign-born. He was born abroad, raised partly overseas. A hybrid, perhaps, in nature—he felt alien to parties, sects, customs. He likely preferred isolation, focused solely on Robert Gérard Moore’s success, not on broader causes. Trade ran in his blood—the Gérards of Antwerp had been merchants for centuries. Once wealthy, they fell through bad luck and war. The French Revolution shattered them. With them collapsed the English branch of Moore, tied to the Antwerp firm. Robert Moore, son of the Antwerp partner, married Hortense Gérard, hoping to inherit her father’s share. He inherited only debts. Though creditors settled, some believed Robert accepted those debts as legacy, vowing one day to repay them and rebuild the house of Gérard and Moore to its former glory. Others said he carried childhood grief and adult hardship deep in his soul.

Yet he lacked the means to act grandly. When he came to Yorkshire, his ancestors’ grand estates were gone. He rented a dilapidated cloth-mill in a remote corner, a cottage nearby, and a few rough acres for pasture and cloth-tenters—all from the trustees of Fieldhead estate, then owned by a minor.

In two years, he’d shown energy. The dingy cottage became neat and tasteful. He turned part of the rugged land into a garden, cultivated with Flemish precision. The mill, old and outdated, he despised from the start. He reformed it slowly, limited by funds. Poverty frustrated him. “Forward” was his creed—but he was held back.

So he didn’t stop to consider how his progress hurt others. Not being native or long-settled, he didn’t care when new machines displaced workers. He never asked where the unemployed would eat. And in that, he was no different from thousands who ignored the starving poor of Yorkshire.

The time was bleak in British history—war at its peak, Europe engulfed. England, though not broken, was worn. Many begged for peace, no matter the cost. National honor meant little when hunger gnawed. For a bite of meat, they’d sell their birthright.

Happiness, it seems, depends less on what lies outside us than on what stirs within. This may sound like a worn-out truth, but I mention it because I know full well that Mr. Helstone and Mr. Moore stepped out from the mill-yard gates—leading their small band of men—with spirits as high as the stars above. When the lantern light caught Mr. Moore’s face, his eyes sparkled with an unusual animation, and a new energy lit up his dark features. And when the rector’s visage was revealed by the same flickering glow, his usually stern lines softened into a broad grin, alive with glee. Yet here they were, braving a cold, drizzling night and a treacherous journey—circumstances one might think would dampen even the most hopeful soul. If any of the workers stranded on Stilbro’ Moor had glimpsed this party, they might have been tempted to take aim from behind a stone wall. And indeed, both leaders knew that danger lurked in the shadows. But rather than fear, they felt a kind of fierce exhilaration—a thrill born of knowing how close they were to conflict, and how ready they were for it.

I know, dear reader, that you may be quick to point out how unseemly it is for a clergyman to revel in such boldness. You may remind me that a parson should be peaceable, gentle, forgiving. I do not dispute that a minister’s calling is one of mercy and moral guidance. I understand well whose servant he is, whose message he carries, whose example he ought to follow. But let me say plainly: if you despise clergymen, if you see them only as hypocrites or tools of oppression, then you need not expect me to join your chorus of condemnation. I will not rise with you in scornful lamentations, nor echo your bitter denunciations against “the cloth.” I will not raise my hands in horror at the rector of Briarfield as though he were some devil incarnate.

He was no devil. The fault, if there was one, lay not in his nature but in his fate—he had been born into the wrong vocation. He should have been a soldier. Circumstance had made him a priest. That was all. For the rest, he was a man of principle, of grit, of courage. Hard-hearted, perhaps; unyielding, yes; lacking in tenderness, certainly. But he was honest, loyal, and steadfast. A man who did not bend easily, yet never broke. He was not without flaws—prejudices ran deep, and sympathy came rarely—but he stood firm where others wavered. And so, while I may not condone every act of his life, I cannot condemn him either. Not when he walked with integrity, however rigid.

Nor shall I curse Helstone, though many in his parish did just that. Some adored him; others loathed him. Such is often the lot of those who love fiercely and hate just as fiercely, who cling to their beliefs with the same tenacity they wield against their enemies. Men like him are neither wholly good nor wholly bad—they simply choose sides, and once chosen, stand fast.

Helstone and Moore, riding side by side now, were both in high spirits, united in purpose. One might expect them to speak amiably. But no—these two men, both of strong tempers and sharp minds, found little comfort in each other’s company. Their conversations were more often clashes than exchanges. The war was their constant battleground.

Helstone was a staunch Tory, loyal to tradition and monarchy. Moore, though English-born, leaned toward Whig ideals—especially when it came to opposing the war. His politics were not born of ideology, but necessity. He believed Bonaparte invincible, England weak, Europe helpless. He mocked Wellington as a dull, mechanical general, led by a government too ignorant to know its own strength. He argued coolly that surrender to Napoleon was inevitable—and better sooner than later.

Helstone could not abide such talk. It offended him deeply. Yet he refrained from violence—not out of kindness, but because Moore was, in his way, an outsider. Half-Flemish blood ran in his veins, and Helstone respected that. He also admired Moore’s stubborn consistency—the dogged way he held fast to his opinions, even when they angered everyone around him. So he endured the insults, though he burned inside.

As they turned onto the road to Stilbro’, the wind met them head-on, driving rain into their faces. Moore, stirred by the chill air and the sting of the downpour, began again.

“Still pleased with your Peninsular news?” he asked.

“What do you mean?” Helstone replied, stiff and curt.

“I mean—do you still trust that wooden idol of yours, Lord Wellington?”

“And what do you mean now?” the rector snapped.

“Do you still believe this hollow-faced, heartless figure can summon fire from heaven to burn the French army you’re so eager to sacrifice?”

“I believe Wellington will drive Bonaparte’s marshals into the sea the moment he chooses.”

“But you can’t truly believe that,” Moore said, amused. “Bonaparte’s generals are brilliant men, guided by a genius of unmatched power. Your Wellington? A plodding martinet, hampered by a clueless government.”

“Wellington is the soul of England,” Helstone insisted. “He is the rightful champion of a nation that is strong, determined, sensible, and honest.”

“And what is your ‘good cause,’ pray?” Moore countered. “The return of Ferdinand—weak, corrupt, unworthy—to a throne he disgraced? And your hero? A dull farmer’s agent, acting for an even duller master? Against them stands genius, victory, and supremacy.”

“Against legitimacy stands usurpation,” Helstone retorted. “Against quiet, righteous resistance stands arrogant ambition. God defends the right!”

“God often defends the powerful,” Moore replied.

“Oh?” Helstone challenged. “Then tell me—was the handful of Israelites standing dry-shod on the eastern shore of the Red Sea more powerful than Pharaoh’s vast army? Were they stronger? Better armed? More numerous? No. They were slaves, worn down by centuries of bondage. Women and children thinned their ranks. They marched barefoot, armed with crooks and tools. Their leader carried only a rod. And yet—what triumphed? The right! The Lord delivered Israel from the Egyptians. The waters closed over the enemy. ‘The depths covered them, they sank to the bottom as a stone.’ The right hand of the Lord became glorious in power!”

“You forget the true parallel,” Moore said quietly. “France is Israel. Napoleon is Moses. Europe, with its decaying empires and rotten dynasties, is Egypt. France, bold and free, is the Twelve Tribes. And her great Usurper—the Shepherd of Horeb.”

“I scorn to answer you,” Helstone said.

But Moore answered himself, lowering his voice. “Oh, in Italy he was magnificent—greater than any Moses. He was born for leadership, for revolution, for renewal. I still wonder how the conqueror of Lodi could stoop to become emperor—a fool’s title, a vulgar farce. And how a people who once called themselves republicans could descend so shamefully back into slavery. I despise France. If England had advanced as far as France did, she would never have retreated so disgracefully.”

“You don’t mean to say imperial France is worse than republican France?” Helstone demanded, furious.

“I mean nothing. But I can think what I please—about revolutions, regicides, restorations, divine right, non-resistance, war—”

His words were cut short by the sudden roll of a gig approaching rapidly. It stopped abruptly in the middle of the road. Both men had been so absorbed in their argument that they hadn’t noticed it coming.

“Nah, maister—did th’ wagons get home?” a voice called from the vehicle.

“Could that be Joe Scott?”

“Ay, ay!” another voice replied. The gig carried two men, visible by the dim glow of its lamp.

The lantern-bearers had fallen behind; the riders had taken the lead. Now, one of the men in the gig spoke.

“Ay, Mr. Moore—it’s Joe Scott. I’m bringing him back to you in a fine state. Found him on the moor, him and three others. What’ll you give me for returning him?”

“Thanks, I suppose,” Moore said. “Though I could’ve spared a better man. Is that you, Mr. Yorke, by your voice?”

“Ay, lad, it’s me. I was coming home from Stilbro’ market. Just as I reached the center of the moor, whipping along as fast as the wind (for these aren’t safe times, thanks to a bad government!), I heard a groan. I pulled up. Most would’ve driven on. But I’ve nothing to fear. I don’t believe there’s a lad in these parts would harm me—though I’d give them as good as they gave if they tried.”

“I said, ‘Is there anything wrong?’” he continued. “‘Deed is there,’ someone says, speaking out of the ground, like.”

“What’s going on? Be quick about it,” I ordered.

“Nobbut four of us lying in a ditch,” says Joe, as calm as could be.

I told them they had no excuse, and bid them get up—or I’d lend them a crack of the whip. I thought they were just tired.

“We’d ha’ done that an hour ago,” says Joe, “but we’re tied with a bit o’ band.”

So I climbed down and freed them with my penknife. Scott wanted to ride with me to explain what happened. The others are following as fast as their legs can carry them.”

“Well, I’m grateful to you, Mr. Yorke,” Moore said.

“Are you, my lad?” Yorke replied. “You know you’re not. But here come the others. And look—by the Lord, another group with lights, like Gideon’s army! And since we’ve got the parson with us—good evening, Mr. Helstone—we’ll manage.”

Mr. Helstone returned the greeting with icy formality.

“We’re eleven strong,” Yorke declared. “And we’ve horses and chariots among us. If we could only fall upon some of those frame-breakers, we could win a grand victory. We could each be a Wellington—that would please you, Mr. Helstone—and write such thrilling articles for the papers! Briarfield would be famous. But we’ll get a column and a half in the *Stilbro’ Courier* for this job, I daresay.”

“And I’ll write it myself,” the rector promised.

“To be sure!” Yorke laughed. “And mind you recommend that the ones who broke the frames and tied Joe Scott’s legs should be hanged without benefit of clergy. It’s a hanging matter, or should be.”

“If I judged them,” Moore said sharply, “I’d give them short shrift!”

“But I’ll leave them alone this time,” Moore added. “Let them have rope enough—I’m certain they’ll hang themselves.”

“Leave them alone?” Yorke asked. “Do you promise?”

“Promise? No. All I mean is, I won’t go out of my way to catch them. But if one crosses my path—”

“You’ll snap him up, of course,” Yorke finished. “Only you’d prefer they did something worse than merely stopping a wagon before you deal with them. Well, we’ll say no more on that now. Here we are at my door, gentlemen. Come in—I hope you’ll step inside. None of you will be the worse for a little refreshment.”

Moore and Helstone refused at first, but the invitation was so warm, and the night so harsh, and the windows ahead so warmly lit, that they finally agreed.

Yorke dismounted, left the gig in charge of a man who emerged from a nearby outbuilding, and led the way inside.

It might have been noted that Mr. Yorke shifted between dialects—sometimes speaking broad Yorkshire, sometimes pure English. His manner, too, changed with the moment—polite one instant, blunt the next. His station was hard to place by speech or bearing alone. Perhaps his house would clarify it.

He directed the men to the kitchen, saying he’d “see them served wi’ summat to taste presently.” The gentlemen entered through the front door.

They found themselves in a hall lined with pictures almost to the ceiling, carpeted in thick matting. From there, they were led into a large parlour, glowing with warmth from a grand fireplace. It was not opulent, but refined—evidence of a cultivated mind. Italian landscapes adorned the walls—true works of art, carefully selected, genuine and valuable. Even in candlelight, the scenes shimmered: clear skies, soft distances, blue air trembling between hill and eye, fresh tints, balanced light and shadow. All pastoral, all sunlit.

On a sofa lay a guitar and sheets of music. Cameos, delicate miniatures, Greek-style vases graced the mantel. Books stood neatly arranged in elegant bookcases.

Yorke bade them sit. Then he rang for wine, instructing the servant to see the men were well fed in the kitchen.

Helstone remained standing, uneasy. He refused the wine offered.

“E’en as you will,” Yorke said lightly. “I reckon you’re thinking of Eastern customs, Mr. Helstone—fearing we might be forced to be friends. But I’m not so superstitious. You might drink that whole decanter, and I might send you a bottle from your own cellar, and I’d still oppose you at every turn—in vestry meetings, justice meetings, wherever we meet.”

“That’s exactly what I’d expect of you, Mr. Yorke,” Helstone replied.

“Does it agree with you now, Mr. Helstone, to ride out after rioters on a wet night at your age?”

“It always agrees with me to do my duty,” Helstone said. “And in this case, duty is pleasure. To hunt down vermin is noble work—fit for an archbishop.”

“Fit for you, at least,” Yorke smiled. “But where’s the curate? He’s either visiting a sick person or hunting vermin elsewhere.”

“He’s doing garrison-duty at Hollow’s Mill.”

“You left him a glass of wine, I hope, Bob?” Yorke asked, turning to Moore.

“No need to wait,” he said quickly, addressing Moore, who had settled into an old-fashioned chair near the fire. “Move it, Robert! Get up, my lad! That seat is mine. Take the sofa or another chair, but not this one. It belongs to me—and no one else.”

“Why are you so particular about that chair, Mr. Yorke?” Moore asked, rising obediently.

“My father sat in it before me,” Yorke said. “That’s all the reason I’ll give you. And it’s as good a reason as Mr. Helstone has for his beliefs.”

“Moore,” Helstone said, “are you ready to go?”

“Not yet,” Yorke replied. “Robert’s not ready. Or rather, I’m not ready to part with him. He’s a poor lad, and needs correcting.”

“What have I done?” Moore asked.

“Made yourself enemies on every side.”

“So?” Moore shrugged. “What does it matter whether your Yorkshire louts hate me or love me?”

“There it is,” Yorke said. “The lad is an alien among us. His father would never have spoken so. Go back to Antwerp, where you were born and bred, mauvaise tête!”

“Mauvaise tête vous-même,” Moore shot back. “Je ne fais que mon devoir. Quant à vos lourdauds de paysans, je m’en moque!”

“En ravanche, mon garçon,” Yorke replied in nearly perfect French, “nos lourdauds de paysans se moqueront de toi. Sois en certain.”

“C’est bon! C’est bon! Et puisque cela m’est égal, que mes amis ne s’en inquiètent pas.”

“Tes amis! Où sont-ils, tes amis?”

“Je fais écho, où sont-ils? Et je suis fort aise que l’écho seul y répond. Au diable les amis! Je me souviens encore du moment où mon père et mes oncles Gérard appellèrent autour d’eux leurs amis… et Dieu sait si les amis se sont empressés d’accourir à leur secours! Tenez, M. Yorke, ce mot, ami, m’irrite trop; ne m’en parlez plus.”

“Comme tu voudras,” Yorke said, and fell silent.

And as he sits back in his carved oak chair, I shall take this moment to sketch the portrait of this French-speaking Yorkshire gentleman.

MR. YORKE (_continued_).

Hiram Yorke was a man of Yorkshire through and through—every inch of him. At fifty-five, he looked older, his hair a full silver white, like frost on the moors at dawn. His forehead was broad but not lofty; his face weathered yet fresh, with the sharp angles and sturdy lines that spoke of northern blood. His voice carried the rough cadence of the dales, as if shaped by wind and stone. There was nothing refined about his features—no trace of Norman grace or aristocratic elegance. To some, it might have seemed coarse; to others, unmistakably honest. But those who knew how to read faces would have seen in every crease and curve a mind of keen intelligence, stubborn independence, and a dry, unyielding wit. It was a face that refused to be led, let alone driven—a man who stood apart, not by choice, but by nature.

Tall and wiry, he moved with a quiet dignity, his posture straight and assured. There was no hint of clumsiness or awkwardness in his bearing—no suggestion of the clown. He carried himself like a man who had earned his place in the world, not inherited it.

But describing his appearance was easy compared to capturing his soul. If you expected a gentle, wise old gentleman—or even one touched by kindness—you’d be mistaken. Yes, he’d spoken with reason and feeling to Mr. Moore, but that did not mean he was always just or kind. In truth, Mr. Yorke was far more complex than appearances suggested.

He lacked the organ of veneration—a flaw that shaped his entire worldview. Without reverence, he could not bow to kings, bishops, or institutions. Thrones, parliaments, churches—all were to him little more than hollow pretensions, built on sand. He saw no purpose in them, no virtue in their traditions. He believed the world would be better off without them, their foundations razed and their occupants buried beneath the ruins. And this lack of awe robbed him of something deeper: the joy of admiration. He could not feel the electric thrill of seeing greatness, nor appreciate beauty for its own sake. He believed in God and heaven, yes—but his vision of both was stripped of wonder, of tenderness. His faith was bare bones, cold and unyielding.

Then there was his weakness in comparison—the ability to see things from another’s point of view. He could preach tolerance and fairness in general terms, yet held deep-seated prejudices against certain classes. He spoke of “parsons” and “lords” with bitter scorn, often with an insolence that went beyond criticism. He couldn’t imagine what it was like to walk in their shoes, to wrestle with their burdens. He judged them harshly, sometimes wishing for cruel measures to correct what he saw as tyranny—ironically, the same kind of tyranny he railed against. He claimed to champion equality, but at heart, he was proud. He treated his workers well—fatherly, even—but only so long as they accepted their place. If anyone dared to stand above him in rank, he resented them fiercely. Rebellion ran in his blood, passed down from father to son. He could not endure control, nor would he ever submit to it.

His lack of benevolence made him impatient with weakness. He had no mercy for fools or failures, and his sarcasm cut deep—without regret, without pause. He didn’t care how much he wounded, so long as he made his point. And when it came to imagination—he had little use for it. He dismissed poetic fancy as useless, perhaps dangerous. Imagination, he thought, was a form of madness, a distraction from real life. He admired music and art—when they produced tangible results—but the quiet poet? The dreamer? That man would have been invisible to Hiram Yorke, ignored, perhaps despised.

Yet, as many such men exist in the world, so too do poets who carry their fire within. They may seem still on the outside, but inside burns a spirit fierce and sharp. They know how to measure those who look down on them, and they understand the worth of the paths others scorn. They find joy in nature, in silence, in their own company—unbothered by the world’s indifference. And when the world calls them useless, they laugh quietly to themselves, knowing that their value lies beyond the reach of utilitarian minds.

But these are not Mr. Yorke’s thoughts. We must return to him.

I’ve told you of his faults. Now, his virtues. He was one of the most respected men in Yorkshire—even those who disliked him admitted his integrity. The poor loved him, for he was kind and generous. His workers trusted him. When he dismissed them, he tried to help them find new work or move to places where jobs might be found. And if any worker showed signs of defiance, Yorke—like many who hate being ruled—knew how to rule with firmness. He crushed rebellion before it could grow, like a weed pulled at the root. With his affairs so orderly, he felt free to criticize others, blaming their misfortunes on their own failings, siding with laborers against masters, and speaking boldly for reform.

His family was the oldest and most distinguished in the region. Though not the richest, he was one of the most influential. He’d had a good education and, in his youth, traveled across Europe before the French Revolution. He spoke French and Italian fluently, and during two years in Italy, he collected fine paintings and rare curiosities that now adorned his home. When he chose, his manners were those of a gentleman of the old school—polished, precise, dignified. His conversation, when he cared to engage, was lively and original. And though he usually spoke in the Yorkshire dialect, he did so by choice. “A Yorkshire burr,” he once said, “is as much better than a cockney’s lisp as a bull’s bellow is to a raton’s squeak.”

He knew everyone for miles around, yet few were his true friends. He preferred strong, rough characters—whether high or low. A clever cottager or a shrewd workman could hold his attention for hours. But a refined, dull gentleman or a fashionable lady would bore him. He valued originality above all, and found comfort only among those who didn’t analyze him, didn’t expect refinement, and didn’t mind his bluntness. Among such people, he was king—respected, obeyed, never questioned. They accepted him without reflection, and that ease pleased him deeply.

Still, he wasn’t entirely cold toward Mr. Moore. Several reasons drew him slightly toward the younger man. First, Moore spoke English with a foreign accent and French with perfect purity. His thin, dark face had an air of foreignness—anti-British, anti-Yorkshire. These details stirred memories in Yorke—of youthful travels, of Parisian cafés, of Italian streets. Seeing Moore reminded him of his own past, and for a moment, he felt young again.

Second, he had known Moore’s father—though their business dealings had ended in loss. Third, Robert Moore proved himself a capable businessman. Yorke sensed he’d succeed, and respected his resolve and sharp mind. And fourth, Yorke was one of the guardians of the minor whose estate included Hollow’s Mill—so Moore often consulted him on improvements.

As for the other guest, Mr. Helstone, the relationship between him and Yorke was strained—by nature and by history. The freethinker despised the disciplinarian. The lover of liberty loathed the upholder of order. And there was talk that once, they had both courted the same woman.

In his youth, Yorke had favored spirited, lively women—those with charm and wit. But then, suddenly, he fell deeply in love with Mary Cave—everything he’d once avoided. She was still, silent, serene—like a statue carved from living marble. She answered only in monosyllables, never smiled at his jokes, never returned his glances. Yet he loved her completely. For him, she was perfect—not because she matched his taste, but because she was unlike anything else. He adored her stillness, her calm, her quiet presence.

Mr. Helstone, the curate of Briarfield, also loved her—or thought he did. Many admired her beauty, but the clergyman was chosen for his position. Perhaps the office gave him a kind of dignity that others lacked. He didn’t share Yorke’s passion. He saw Mary clearly, not as a fantasy. He married her, and she accepted him without hesitation.

But nature had not designed Helstone to be a great husband—especially to a quiet wife. He assumed silence meant contentment. If she didn’t complain, she was happy. If she didn’t speak, she had no opinions. He didn:’t try to understand her, nor see her as a person. After a few years, she grew distant—quietly fading away. When she died, he mourned, but not deeply. He was not a man given to tears. His dry-eyed grief shocked the housekeeper and nurse, who had seen her illness closely. They whispered over her body, recounting stories of her suffering, of loneliness, of broken heart. They blamed Helstone for neglect, for cruelty—though none of it was true.

Rumors spread quickly. Soon, people whispered that Mary had died of a broken heart, mistreated by her husband. Yorke heard these tales—and believed some of them. Already, he resented Helstone for winning the woman he had loved. Though married now, to a woman very different from Mary, he could not forget the pain of losing her. To hear that she had been mistreated by another—by the man who had taken her from him—fueled a deep, bitter hatred. A hatred Helstone never fully understood.

He knew only that they disagreed politically and religiously. He did not know of the past, of the love, of the rumors. Had he known, he might never have entered Yorke’s house again.

* * * * *

Yorke did not continue his lecture to Moore. The conversation resumed, broader now, though still charged with tension. The unrest in the country—the recent attacks on mills—offered plenty of fuel for argument. Each man held different views.

Mr. Helstone believed the mill owners were wronged, the workers unreasonable. He condemned the growing rebellion against authority, the refusal to accept hardship as inevitable. His solution? Strong government, strict magistrates, and military force when needed.

Yorke scoffed. “Will your interference feed the hungry? Will it give work to those no one will hire?” He rejected the idea of inevitable suffering. Public patience, he said, was like a camel already carrying every ounce it could bear. Resistance was now a duty. The spirit of rebellion, he insisted, was the most hopeful sign of the age. Yes, the masters were aggrieved—but their troubles came from a “corrupt, base, and bloody” government. Pitt, Castlereagh, Perceval—madmen, demons, fools—were the true tyrants, destroying trade and national pride. Their endless war, their crushing taxes, their infamous Orders in Council—these were the chains around England’s neck.

“But what’s the use of talking?” he demanded. “What chance does reason have in a land ruled by kings, priests, and peers? Where a mad monarch sits on the throne, a debauched ruler holds power, and hereditary legislators mock common sense? Where bishops and a pampered church are revered as frauds? Where a standing army and idle clergymen live off the people’s sweat?”

Helstone rose, put on his shovel-hat, and replied firmly: “I’ve seen men like you—brave enough while strong and prosperous. But there comes a time when fear grips the heart, when the strong tremble. That moment reveals the true nature of rebellion. I’ve stood by the bedside of a dying man who once railed against God and king—now begging for mercy, finding none. I warn you, Mr. Yorke: blasphemy is deadly sin. There is judgment to come.”

Yorke nodded. “I believe in judgment. If not, how explain the success of scoundrels? Men who break hearts, abuse privilege, steal from the poor, and bow to the rich—how are they punished? But when I grow weary, I open my Bible—any page—and find a verse blazing with divine fire. I know where some folk are headed, as well as if an angel had told me.”

“Sir,” said Helstone, gathering his dignity, “the greatest wisdom is to know oneself, and the path one walks.”

“Ay, ay,” replied Yorke. “And remember—Ignorance was carried from the gates of heaven, borne through the air, and thrust into hell.”

“And I recall,” said Helstone, “that Vain-Confidence, blind to danger, fell into a pit made by the prince of the grounds to catch fools, and was shattered.”

Moore, who had watched silently, now broke in. “You’ve both blackballed each other thoroughly. You’ve shown how much you detest one another. But for me—my anger is still focused on the rogues who broke my frames. I’ve no room left for personal quarrels, let alone politics. Gentlemen, you both seem worse than I imagined. I dare not stay the night with a rebel like you, Yorke—and I scarcely dare ride home with a tyrant like Mr. Helstone.”

Mr. Moore awoke the next morning in high spirits, as he had done the night before. He and Joe Scott had both spent the evening at the mill, making use of makeshift sleeping quarters tucked away in the front and back counting-houses. The master, always an early riser, was up even earlier than usual. As he dressed, he sang a French song to wake his man.

"Ye're not custen dahn, then, maister?" Joe called out.

"Not a stiver, mon garçon—meaning, my lad. Get up, and let’s take a turn through the mill before the hands arrive. I’ll tell you about my plans. We’ll have the machinery yet, Joseph. You’ve never heard of Bruce, perhaps?"

"And th’ arrand (spider)? Yes, but I hev. I’ve read th’ history o’ Scotland, and happen knaw as mich on’t as ye; and I understand ye to mean to say ye’ll persevere."

"I do."

"Is there mony o' your mak’ i’ your country?" Joe asked, folding up his bedroll and putting it away.

"In my country? Which is my country?"

"Why, France—isn’t it?"

"Not it, indeed! Just because the French seized Antwerp, where I was born, doesn’t make me a Frenchman."

"Holland, then?"

"I’m not Dutch. Now you’re confusing Antwerp with Amsterdam."

"Flanders?"

"I scorn the insinuation, Joe! I a Flamand? Have I a Flemish face—the clumsy nose standing out, the mean forehead falling back, the pale blue eyes ‘à fleur de tête’? Am I all body and no legs, like a Flamand? But you don’t know what they’re like, those Netherlanders. Joe, I’m an Anversois. My mother was an Anversoise, though she came from French stock, which is why I speak French."

"But your father war Yorkshire, which maks ye a bit Yorkshire too; and onybody may see ye’re akin to us, ye’re so keen o’ making brass, and getting forrards."

"Joe, you’re an impudent dog; but I’ve always been used to this kind of boorish insolence since youth. The *classe ouvrière*—the working people in Belgium—treat their employers brutally. And by *brutally*, Joe, I mean *brutalement*—which, when properly translated, might be *roughly*."

"We allus speak our minds i’ this country; and them young parsons and grand folk fro’ London is shocked at wer 'incivility;' and we like weel enough to gi’e 'em summat to be shocked at, 'cause it's sport to us to watch 'em turn up the whites o' their een, and spreed out their bits o' hands, like as they're flayed wi' bogards, and then to hear 'em say, nipping off their words short like, 'Dear! dear! Whet seveges! How very corse!'"

"You _are_ savages, Joe. You don't suppose you're civilized, do you?"

"Middling, midding, maister. I reckon that us manufacturing lads i’ th’ north is a deal more intelligent, and knaws a deal more nor th’ farming folk i’ th’ south. Trade sharpens wer wits; and them that's mechanics like me is forced to think. Ye know, what wi' looking after machinery and sich like, I've getten into that way that when I see an effect, I look straight out for a cause, and I oft lig hold on't to purpose; and then I like reading, and I'm curious to knaw what them that reckons to govern us aims to do for us and wi' us. And there's many 'cuter nor me; there's many a one amang them greasy chaps 'at smells o' oil, and amang them dyers wi' blue and black skins, that has a long head, and that can tell what a fooil of a law is, as well as ye or old Yorke, and a deal better nor soft uns like Christopher Sykes o' Whinbury, and greet hectoring nowts like yond' Irish Peter, Helstone's curate."

"You think yourself a clever fellow, I know, Scott."

"Ay! I'm fairish. I can tell cheese fro' chalk, and I'm varry weel aware that I've improved sich opportunities as I have had, a deal better nor some 'at reckons to be aboon me; but there's thousands i’ Yorkshire that's as good as me, and a two-three that's better."

"You're a great man—you're a sublime fellow; but you're a prig, a conceited noodle with it all, Joe! You need not think that because you've picked up a little knowledge of practical mathematics, and because you've found some scantling of chemistry at the bottom of a dyeing vat, that therefore you're a neglected man of science. And you need not suppose that because trade doesn't always run smooth, and you and such as you are sometimes short of work and bread, that therefore your class are martyrs, and that the whole form of government under which you live is wrong. Moreover, you need not insinuate that virtue has fled to cottages and abandoned slated houses. Let me tell you, I particularly abominate that sort of talk, because I know human nature is human nature everywhere—whether under tile or thatch—and that in every breath of life, vice and virtue are blended, in greater or lesser proportions, and that proportion isn't decided by station. I've seen villains who were rich, and villains who were poor, and villains who were neither, but lived in fair and modest comfort. The clock is going to strike six. Away with you, Joe, and ring the mill bell."

It was mid-February, and by six o'clock dawn was just beginning to steal into the night, piercing its brown shadows with a pale ray and giving a faint translucence to the dark. That morning, the light was weak—no colour tinged the east, no warmth flushed it. To see how slowly day lifted its heavy lid, how wan its glance swept the hills, one might have thought the sun’s fire had been quenched in the night’s rain. The air was cold as its appearance; a raw wind stirred the mass of night-cloud, revealing, as it rose, not blue sky but a layer of paler vapour around the horizon. The rain had stopped, but the earth was soaked, and the pools and streams overflowed.

The mill windows were lit, the bell still rang loudly, and now the children began to stream in—too eager, perhaps, to feel the chill. By contrast, the morning seemed almost pleasant to them, for they had often come to work through snowstorms, heavy rain, and hard frost.

Mr. Moore stood at the entrance, watching them pass. He counted them as they went by. To those who arrived late, he gave a gentle word of warning, which Joe Scott repeated more sharply when they reached the workrooms. Neither master nor overseer spoke harshly. They were not cruel men, though both were firm. One boy who came significantly late was fined—a penny down before entering, with a warning that another offence would cost him twopence.

Rules, of course, were necessary. Coarse and cruel masters made coarse and cruel rules, and enforced them tyrannically in those times. But while I describe imperfect characters—every character in this book being more or less flawed—I have chosen not to portray utterly degraded ones. Child-torturers, slave-drivers—I leave them to the jailer. The novelist may be forgiven for avoiding such horrors.

Instead of describing lashes and beatings, I am pleased to say that neither Mr. Moore nor Joe Scott ever struck a child in the mill. Joe had once severely flogged his own son for lying and refusing to admit it—but like his employer, he was too calm, too reasonable, to resort to physical punishment regularly. It remained an exception.

Mr. Moore wandered through the mill, the yard, the dye-house, and the warehouse until the sickly dawn strengthened into daylight. The sun rose—at least, a white disc, clear and tintless, almost icy in appearance, peered over the dark crest of a hill, turning the edge of the cloud silver, and casting a solemn gaze down the length of the narrow valley where we are now confined. It was eight o'clock. The mill lights were extinguished. The signal for breakfast sounded. The children, released for half an hour, hurried to their small tin cans of coffee and baskets of bread. Let us hope they had enough to eat. It would be a shame if they didn’t.

At last, Mr. Moore left the millyard and walked toward his cottage. It was only a short distance, but the hedge and high bank on either side of the lane gave it a sense of quiet seclusion. The house was small, whitewashed, with a green porch over the door. Brown stalks showed in the garden soil near the porch and beneath the windows—buds and flowers absent now, but promising creepers in summer. A grassy plot and flower borders lay in front. The borders held only dark earth, except where snowdrops or crocuses poked through, green as emerald. Spring was late. Winter had been severe and long. The last deep snow had vanished only yesterday; on the hills, patches still gleamed, dotting hollows and crowning peaks. The lawn was not green, but bleached, as was the grass on the bank and under the hedge. Three trees, gracefully grouped, rose beside the cottage. Not tall, but unchallenged in their spot, they looked noble and strong.

This was Mr. Moore’s home—a snug place for peace and reflection, though one where ambition could not rest for long.

Yet the cottage seemed to hold no special charm for its owner. Instead of entering, he fetched a spade from a shed and began digging in the garden. For nearly fifteen minutes, he worked without pause. Then a window opened, and a woman’s voice called:

"Eh, bien! Tu ne déjeûnes pas ce matin?"

He replied in French, and the conversation followed in the same tongue. Since this is an English tale, I will translate.

"Is breakfast ready, Hortense?"

"Certainly. It’s been ready for half an hour."

"Then I am ready too. I have a canine hunger."

He dropped his spade and stepped inside. The narrow passage led to a small parlour, where coffee, bread and butter, and stewed pears—unusual in England—were laid out. Overseeing the meal was the woman who had spoken from the window. Let me describe her.

She seemed slightly older than Mr. Moore—perhaps thirty-five—tall and stoutly built. Her hair was very black, now twisted up in curl-papers. She had a high colour in her cheeks, a small nose, and bright black eyes. The lower half of her face was larger than the upper; her forehead was small and slightly wrinkled. Her expression was fretful, though not unkind—something about her made one want to be both annoyed and amused. Most striking was her dress: a rough petticoat and striped cotton camisole. The petticoat was short, revealing feet and ankles that lacked symmetry.

You might think I’ve painted a slovenly woman, reader. Not at all. Hortense Moore—Mr. Moore’s sister—was orderly and economical. The petticoat, camisole, and curl-papers were her morning attire, worn in her native land. She refused to adopt English customs simply because she lived here. She clung to Belgian ways, believing them superior.

Mademoiselle had a high opinion of herself—not entirely unjustified, for she possessed solid virtues. But she exaggerated their worth and ignored her flaws. She could never be convinced she was prejudiced, narrow-minded, overly sensitive to her dignity, or quick to take offense. Yet these traits were true. Still, when no one challenged her, and no prejudice was wounded, she could be kind and warm-hearted. She loved her two brothers—Robert and Louis—deeply. As the last of their family line, they were sacred to her. But she knew little of Louis. He had been sent to England as a child, educated in an English school. Unsuited to trade, perhaps by nature, he had become a teacher—first an usher, now tutor in a private household. Hortense said he had "des moyens," but was too quiet and reserved. Her praise of Robert was different—full of pride. She saw him as the greatest man in Europe. Everything he said or did was remarkable in her eyes. She expected others to see him the same way. Opposition to Robert—or to herself—was unthinkable.

So, as soon as Robert sat at the table, and she had served him stewed pears and a generous slice of Belgian tartine, she burst into horror at the night’s events—the destruction of the frames.

"Quelle idée! To destroy them. Quelle action honteuse! One could see clearly that the workers here are both beasts and wicked. Exactly like English servants—especially the maids! Nothing so unbearable as Sara, for instance!"

"She looks clean and industrious," Mr. Moore remarked.

"Looks! I don’t care how she looks. I won’t say she’s dirty or idle, mais elle est d'une insolence! Yesterday she argued with me for half an hour about the beef—said I boiled it to rags, that English people couldn’t eat our bouilli, that the broth was nothing but greasy warm water, and the choucroute—she insists she can’t touch it! That barrel in the cellar—prepared by my own hands—she called a tub of hog-wash, meaning pig food. I’m tormented by the girl, yet I cannot dismiss her, lest I find someone worse. You’re in the same boat, pauvre cher frère!"

"I’m afraid you’re not very happy in England, Hortense."

"It is my duty to be happy where you are, brother. But otherwise, there are a thousand things that make me regret our native town. Everyone here seems ill-bred (mal-élevé). My habits are ridiculed. If a girl from your mill comes into the kitchen and finds me in my jupon and camisole cooking dinner—well, you know I can’t trust Sarah to cook anything—she sneers. If I accept a tea invitation, I notice I’m put aside; I don’t receive the attention I deserve. What a fine family the Gérards are—we know that! And the Moores too! We have a right to respect. When it’s withheld, it wounds. In Antwerp, I was always treated with distinction. Here, it seems everyone thinks I speak English with a ridiculous accent, though I assure you I pronounce it perfectly."

"Hortense, in Antwerp we were known to be rich. In England, we were known only as poor."

"Precisely. And how mercenary mankind is! Again, dear brother, last Sunday was wet. So I went to church in my neat black sabots—not something one wears in a fashionable city, but common in the countryside for walking in mud. As I walked up the aisle, composed and tranquil—as I always am—four ladies and four gentlemen laughed and hid behind their prayer-books."

"Well, well! Don’t wear the sabots again. I told you before I thought they weren’t quite suitable here."

"But, brother, they’re not ordinary sabots—like peasants wear. I tell you, they are sabots noirs, très propres, très convenables. At Mons and Leuze—cities near Brussels—they’re commonly worn by respectable people in winter. Try wading Flemish roads in Paris brodequins, and you’ll hear news!"

"Never mind Mons and Leuze. Do as the Romans do. And the camisole and jupon—I’m not sure about those either. I’ve never seen an English lady dressed like that. Ask Caroline Helstone."

"Caroline! _I_ ask Caroline? _I_ consult her about my dress? It is _she_ who should consult _me_. She is a child."

"She is eighteen, or at least seventeen—old enough to know about gowns, petticoats, and shoes."

"Do not spoil Caroline, I entreat you, brother. Do not make her more important than she ought to be. She is modest and unassuming—let us keep her so."

"With all my heart. Is she coming this morning?"

"She will come at ten, as usual, for her French lesson."

"You don’t find she sneers at you?"

"She does not. She appreciates me better than anyone here. She has intimate knowledge of me—she sees I have education, intelligence, manners, principles—all that belongs to a person well born and well bred."

"Are you fond of her?"

"For _fond_, I cannot say. I do not take sudden fancies. Thus, my friendship is reliable. I regard her as my relative. Her position inspires interest. And her conduct as my pupil has been such as to strengthen, rather than weaken, the attachment arising from other causes."

"She behaves well at lessons?"

"To _me_, she behaves very well. Though you know, brother, I have a manner that discourages familiarity, wins esteem, and commands respect. Yet, having insight, I see clearly that Caroline is not perfect—there is much to desire in her."

"Give me another cup of coffee, and while I drink it, entertain me with her faults."

"Dear brother, I’m glad to see you eating with appetite after your exhausting night. Caroline is defective—but with my guidance, almost maternal care, she may improve. There is something about her—an occasional reserve—that I dislike, because it lacks girlish submission. And I see glimpses of restless haste in her nature, which unsettles me. Yet she is usually calm—sometimes too dejected, too thoughtful. With time, I doubt not, I shall make her uniformly serene and proper, without being inexplicably pensive. I disapprove of what is unclear."

"I don’t understand your description at all. What do you mean by 'unsettled hurries,' for example?"

"An example may clarify. I sometimes make her read French poetry for pronunciation practice. She’s studied much of Corneille and Racine—steadily, soberly, as I approve. Occasionally, she showed languor—more apathy than sobriety—and apathy I cannot tolerate in those under my instruction. The other day, I gave her a volume of short poems. I sent her to the window to learn one by heart. When I looked up, I saw her flipping through the pages impatiently, curling her lip in scorn as she glanced at the verses. I rebuked her. 'Ma cousine,' she said, 'tout cela m'ennuie à la mort.' I told her that was improper language. 'Dieu!' she exclaimed, 'il n'y a donc pas deux lignes de poésie dans toute la littérature française?' I asked what she meant. She apologized with proper humility. Soon she was silent. I saw her smiling to herself over the book. She began to study diligently. Half an hour later, she stood before me, handed me the volume, folded her hands—just as I require—and recited Chénier’s 'La Jeune Captive.' If you had heard her delivery, and her incoherent remarks afterward, you’d understand what I meant by 'unsettled hurry.' One would think Chénier moved more deeply than all of Racine and Corneille combined. You, brother, with your wisdom, will see that this disproportionate passion reveals an unbalanced mind. But she is fortunate in her preceptress. I will give her a system, a method, a set of beliefs. I will guide her feelings completely."

"Be sure you do, Hortense. Here she comes. I believe I saw her shadow pass the window."

"Ah! Truly. She is too early—half an hour ahead of time. My child, what brings you here before I’ve finished breakfast?"

This question was addressed to a young girl who entered the room, wrapped in a winter cloak, its folds gathered with grace around a slender figure.

"I came quickly to see how you were, Hortense, and how Robert was too. I was sure you’d both be upset by what happened last night. I didn’t hear until this morning. My uncle told me at breakfast."

"Ah! It is unspeakable. You sympathize with us? Your uncle sympathizes?"

"My uncle is very angry—but he was with Robert, I believe. Did he go with you to Stilbro’ Moor?"

"Yes, we set out in martial style. But the prisoners we went to rescue met us halfway."

"Of course, nobody was hurt?"

"No—only Joe Scott’s wrists were a little bruised from being bound too tightly behind his back."

"You weren’t there? You weren’t with the wagons when they were attacked?"

"No. One seldom has the fortune to be present at events one wishes to witness."

"Where are you going this morning? I saw Murgatroyd saddling your horse in the yard."

"To Whinbury. It’s market day."

That morning, Mademoiselle Moore had a pupil who seemed lost in thought. Caroline repeatedly forgot the lessons being taught her, yet she bore each gentle reprimand with a quiet smile, as if no fault had been made. Seated near the window, bathed in sunlight, she appeared to draw warmth not just from the rays but from something deeper—something that softened her heart and lifted her spirit. In this mood, she was at her most radiant, and her beauty shone with a quiet grace.

She was blessed with natural charm. One need not know her well to feel drawn to her. Her presence was pleasing even at first glance. Her figure was light and graceful, perfectly suited to her youth—slender, poised, and balanced. Her face was delicate, expressive, and kind; her eyes held a warmth that could melt reserve, and at times sparkled with a softness that touched the soul. Her lips were gently shaped, her skin fair and smooth, and her chestnut hair—abundant and wavy—fell in lovely curls about her shoulders. She wore her clothes with taste: modest, unostentatious, yet harmonious in color and cut. Her winter dress was of soft merino, the same rich brown as her hair, trimmed with a pink ribbon at the collar and fastened with a small bow. No jewelry adorned her—only simplicity, elegance, and quiet dignity.

This was Caroline Helstone. As for her mind or character—if they existed—they would reveal themselves in time.

Her family history was brief. Her parents had separated soon after her birth, their union undone by irreconcilable differences. Her mother was the half-sister of Mr. Moore’s father, making Caroline, though without blood relation, a distant cousin to Robert, Louis, and Hortense. Her father, brother to the Reverend Mr. Helstone, was a man whose name was best left unsaid after death had settled all accounts. He had brought sorrow to his wife, and rumors of his misconduct had cast doubt upon the reputation of his more upright brother. Caroline never knew her mother—taken from her in infancy, she’d seen her only once, long ago. Her father died young, leaving her under the care of her uncle, the rector. But he was ill-suited to guide a child’s life. He took little interest in her education, and had it not been for Caroline’s own quiet determination—her occasional pleas for books, for instruction, for learning—she might have grown up entirely untaught. Still, she carried within her a quiet shame, feeling herself behind others of her age and station. So when Hortense offered to teach her French and embroidery shortly after arriving at Hollow’s Mill, Caroline accepted eagerly.

Hortense delighted in the role. It gave her authority. She saw Caroline not as an equal, but as a student—perhaps even a bit backward. When Caroline progressed quickly, Hortense attributed it not to talent or effort, but to her own superior method. Even when Caroline revealed knowledge on topics where Hortense was ignorant, she believed the girl had simply absorbed wisdom from conversation, unaware of its source. The idea was illogical—but Hortense clung to it with firm conviction.

Proud of her “esprit positif,” and devoted to practical studies, Hortense kept Caroline focused on grammar and logic. She assigned endless “analyses logiques”—exercises that required dissecting sentences into principal and subordinate clauses, identifying determinative and applicative incidents, determining whether a proposition was complete, elliptical, or implicit. To Caroline, these tasks felt like puzzles without purpose. She believed she could learn French without them—and resented the hours spent wrestling with abstract terms. When she grew confused, she sometimes slipped her book to Robert in the counting-house while Hortense was upstairs rummaging through drawers—an odd habit that consumed much of her day. Robert, calm and clear-minded, could unravel any difficulty in moments. With two words, he would unlock what had stumped her for hours. She often wished Hortense could teach like him. Grateful and admiring, she would return to the cottage, her spirits lifted, wishing—just once—that she were a boy so she might sit beside Robert, be his clerk, and learn not from rules, but from reason.

Occasionally, she visited Hollow’s Cottage in the evening. These visits were rare. Sometimes Robert was away at market or visiting Mr. Yorke. Other times, he was engaged with guests. But when he was home and free, the evening passed swiftly—too swiftly. There was no room in England more pleasant than that small parlour when the three cousins gathered. Hortense, when not teaching or scolding, was not cruel. By nightfall, she softened. If coaxed to play her guitar, she became almost charming—her voice clear, her fingers sure. Her music was soothing, though tinged with formality, as if every note were measured by duty rather than joy.

Robert, freed from business, listened with quiet pleasure. He was not lively, but he was attentive. He smiled at Caroline’s stories, answered her questions with patience, and sat beside her with a presence that felt comforting. At times, he seemed almost warm—gentle, even tender. But come morning, he was once again reserved, distant. Though he enjoyed the evenings, he rarely invited them. This puzzled Caroline. “If I had a source of happiness,” she thought, “I would use it often. I would keep it bright, not let it grow cold.”

Yet she never acted on this thought. Though she loved visiting the cottage, she never came uninvited. Often, when Hortense urged her, she refused—because Robert did not encourage it. That morning, however, he had spoken first, inviting her with such kindness that the simple words filled her with joy, lasting through the entire day.

The morning unfolded as usual. Hortense bustled between kitchen and parlour—scolding Sarah, checking Caroline’s work, listening to her recitation. No matter how well the lesson was done, she never praised. Praise, she believed, undermined a teacher’s dignity. Blame, in some form, was essential. If there was no flaw in the work, then it was the pupil’s posture, expression, or attire that needed correction.

Dinner arrived with the usual drama. Sarah placed the tray on the table with a look that said plainly, “I’ve never seen worse food in my life.” Yet the meal was far from terrible. A thick pea soup, prepared with great lamentation over the absence of haricot beans in England. Then a dish of meat—unidentifiable, chopped with bread crumbs, seasoned uniquely, and baked in a mold. Odd greens, roughly chopped, followed, and a fruit pâté made with a recipe from Madame Gérard Moore’s grandmother—so sweet it likely used molasses instead of sugar.

Caroline found nothing wrong with the meal. In fact, she liked it—especially as a change. Had she shown disapproval, it would have ruined her standing with Hortense. Dislike of foreign food was a greater sin than any other.

After dinner, Caroline managed to coax Hortense upstairs to dress. It required skill. To suggest that the jupon, camisole, or curl-papers were absurd would have been disastrous. Instead, she pretended to crave a change of scene. Once in the bedroom, she persuaded her cousin to stay and finish dressing there. While Hortense delivered a sermon on her own moral superiority—on ignoring fashion—Caroline quietly removed the camisole, dressed her in a decent gown, arranged her collar and hair, and made her presentable. But Hortense insisted on finishing touches: a thick handkerchief around her neck and a large black apron—both symbols of domestic virtue. She would never appear without them. The fichu was a matter of morality; the apron, a sign of thrift. She had made similar outfits for Caroline, and their one serious quarrel—still fresh in her mind—had begun when Caroline refused to wear them.

“I wear a high dress and collar,” Caroline said. “I’d suffocate with a handkerchief. My short aprons are enough. I’d rather make no change.”

But Hortense persisted—until Robert overheard the argument and declared Caroline’s aprons sufficient. Since she was still a child, he added, she could go without the fichu—especially since her curls nearly reached her shoulders.

There was no arguing with Robert. Hortense reluctantly yielded, though she disapproved deeply of Caroline’s neatness and grace. She preferred something simpler, more homely—“beaucoup plus convenable.”

The afternoon was spent sewing. Hortense, like many Belgian women, excelled at needlework. She saw no waste in spending hours on embroidery, lace, knitting, or especially mending stockings—each stitch meant to imitate the original fabric exactly. It was a sacred duty, she believed, passed down through generations. She had been given a torn stocking as a child, and by six, she’d already demonstrated her skill to guests. When she discovered Caroline knew nothing of darning, she wept inwardly at the neglect.

Without delay, she found a pair of hose with holes in both heels and set Caroline to mending them. Two years had passed, and the stockings still lay in her workbag.
