She stitched a few rows daily—as penance for her ignorance. They were a burden. She wanted to burn them. Once, Robert, seeing her sighing over them, suggested a secret incineration in the counting-house. But Caroline knew better—such defiance would only bring another, worse pair. So she endured.

All afternoon, the two sat sewing until fatigue crept into hands and eyes. The sky darkened after dinner. Rain fell hard, steady and heavy. Caroline began to fear Robert wouldn’t return—he might stay in Whinbury until the storm passed. Five o’clock struck. Clouds streamed. A wind sighed through the roof. Daylight faded. The fire in the parlour glowed like twilight.

“It won’t clear until moonrise,” Hortense declared. “So I’m certain Robert won’t return till then. Indeed, I’d be sorry if he did. We’ll have coffee. Waiting is useless.”

“I’m tired. May I stop now, cousin?”

“You may. It’s too dark to see. Fold your work, put it in your bag. Then go to the kitchen and ask Sarah to bring in the *goûter*.”

“But it’s not yet six. He might still come.”

“He will not. I know his ways. I understand my brother.”

Suspense is wearying. Disappointment, bitter. Everyone knows this. Obedient, Caroline entered the kitchen. Sarah was stitching a dress at the table.

“You’re to bring in coffee,” she said listlessly, leaning against the mantelpiece, staring into the fire.

“How low you seem, miss! But it’s because your cousin keeps you working so hard. It’s a shame!”

“Nothing of the kind, Sarah.”

“Oh, but I know it is. You’re fit to cry just now—nothing but sitting still all day. It would dull a kitten.”

“Sarah, does your master often come home early when it rains?”

“Never, hardly. But today, for some reason, he’s different.”

“What do you mean?”

“He’s here. I saw Murgatroyd lead his horse into the yard five minutes ago. He’s in the counting-house with Joe Scott.”

“You’re mistaken.”

“What should I be mistaken for? I know his horse.”

“But you didn’t see him?”

“I heard him speak. He was saying something about settling things, new frames coming in the mill, and four soldiers from Stilbro’ barracks guarding the wagon.”

“Sarah, are you making a gown?”

“Yes. Is it handsome?”

“Beautiful! Get the coffee ready. I’ll finish cutting out that sleeve and give you some trimming. I have narrow satin ribbon that matches perfectly.”

“You’re very kind, miss.”

“Be quick. And first—put your master’s shoes on the hearth. He’ll take off his boots when he comes in. I hear him. He’s coming.”

“Miss, you’re cutting the cloth wrong.”

“So I am. But it’s just a snip. No harm done.”

The kitchen door opened. Mr. Moore stepped in, wet and cold. Caroline turned slightly from her task, then returned to it—for a moment, as if to gain time. Bent over the dress, her face hidden, she tried to compose herself. When she finally looked up, her expression lit with joy.

“We stopped expecting you. They said you wouldn’t come.”

“But I promised to return soon. _You_ expected me?”

“No, Robert. I dared not when it rained so hard. And you’re wet and chilled. Change everything. If you caught cold, we’d blame ourselves.”

“I’m not soaked. My coat is waterproof. Just dry shoes. There—the fire feels good after miles of wind and rain.”

He stood on the hearth. Caroline stood beside him. Robert gazed at the brasses above the shelf, then glanced down—into a face flushed, smiling, full of warmth, framed by soft curls, glowing with life. Sarah had gone to the parlour with the tray, detained by her mistress.

Robert placed a hand on Caroline’s shoulder, leaned down, and kissed her forehead.

“Oh!” she breathed, as if the gesture unlocked her heart. “I was miserable when I thought you wouldn’t come. I’m almost too happy now. Are you happy, Robert? Do you like coming home?”

“I think I do—tonight, at least.”

“Are you certain you’re not fretting about your frames, your business, the war?”

“Not now.”

“Are you sure you don’t find Hollow’s Cottage too small, too narrow, too dull?”

“At this moment, no.”

“Can you truly say you’re not bitter that rich and powerful people forget you?”

“No more questions. You’re wrong if you think I seek favor from them. I only want a chance—a position—a path.”

“Which your talent and goodness will win you. You were made to be great. You _shall_ be great.”

“I wonder what recipe you’d give me for greatness—if you spoke honestly. But I know it. Would it work? Yes—poverty, misery, bankruptcy. Oh, life is not what you imagine, Lina.”

“But you are what I imagine.”

“I am not.”

“You are better, then?”

“Far worse.”

“No—far better. I know you are good.”

“How do you know?”

“You look so, and I feel you _are_ so.”

“Where do you feel it?”

“In my heart.”

“Ah! You judge me with your heart, Lina. You should judge me with your head.”

“I do—and then I’m proud of you. Robert, you cannot tell all my thoughts about you.”

Robert’s dark face warmed. His lips curved, yet stayed tight. His eyes sparkled, yet his brow remained knit.

“Think poorly of me, Lina,” he said. “Men are generally scum—very different from anything you can imagine. I claim no better.”

“If you did, I wouldn’t esteem you as much. It’s because you’re modest that I trust your worth.”

“Are you flattering me?” he asked sharply, turning to study her face.

“No,” she replied softly, laughing at his sudden intensity. She seemed to think no defense necessary.

“You don’t care if I think you flatter me?”

“No.”

“You’re so sure of your intentions?”

“I suppose so.”

“What are they, Caroline?”

“Only to ease my mind by speaking what I feel—and then to make you feel better about yourself.”

“By telling me my kinswoman is sincere?”

“Exactly. I am your sincere friend, Robert.”

“And I am—what chance and change shall make me, Lina.”

“Not my enemy, then?”

Before she could answer, Sarah and Hortense burst into the kitchen—both agitated. They had argued over the coffee—Sarah called it a waste, a “queer mess”; Hortense called it “a royal drink,” too fine for anyone who objected.

The others withdrew to the parlour. Before Hortense followed, Caroline whispered, “Not my enemy, Robert?”
And Robert, with quiet gravity, replied, “Could I be?”
Then he sat at the table, and pulled her beside him.

The Curates at Tea.

Caroline Helstone was eighteen—just eighteen—and at that age, life’s true story had scarcely begun. Before then, we live in a world of dreams, listening to tales that are wondrous and sometimes heartbreaking, but always tinged with fantasy. Our world is filled with heroes and half-gods, shadowy forests and glowing skies, rivers that roar like thunder and flowers that bloom with impossible sweetness. The moon we gaze upon glows with a brilliance no earthly light can match; the sun burns not just as a star, but as a divine fire, the home of gods.

At eighteen, we stand on the edge of illusion, looking toward reality. Behind us lies the enchanted land of childhood—the realm of elves and magic. Ahead, the shores of truth rise slowly into view, distant and hazy, yet promising peace and clarity. We see green meadows beneath the blue horizon, silver streams shimmering in the sunlight, and believe that if only we could reach them, we would never hunger or thirst again. But between us and that promised land lie deserts, floods, and rivers darker than death. True joy must be earned—not given. And those who have fought for great things know how deeply it costs: the heart’s blood stains the brow of the warrior before victory’s crown rests upon it.

At eighteen, we don’t know this. Hope smiles upon us, and we believe her promises without question. Love arrives like a lost angel at our door, and we welcome him instantly, embracing him without fear. We do not see his quiver, nor feel the sting of his arrows—only the warmth of a new awakening. There is no thought of poison, no dread of wounds that cannot heal. Passion, though it may one day become agony for some, feels now like pure delight. At eighteen, we step into the school of experience—but we do not yet know its lessons: humbling, crushing, grinding, yet purifying and strengthening.

Alas, Experience! No other teacher bears such a cold face, wears such a black robe, or wields such a heavy rod. None so sternly drags the novice into duty, none compels learning with such unrelenting force. Yet without her, how many stumble through life? How many trespass on forbidden ground, fall down deadly slopes?

Caroline, returned home by Robert Moore, had no wish to spend the evening with her uncle. His room was sacred territory—she rarely entered it. Tonight, she stayed away until the bell rang for prayers. In Mr. Helstone’s household, part of the evening service was read aloud from the Bible. He recited it in his usual nasal tone—clear, loud, and monotonous. When it ended, Caroline approached him as she always did.

"Good-night, uncle."

"Hey! You've been out all day—visiting, dining, whatnot!"

"Just at the cottage."

"And your lessons?"

"Yes."

"And the shirt?"

"Only half done."

"Well, that’ll do. Stick to your needle. Learn to make shirts, gowns, piecrusts—and you’ll be a clever woman someday. Now go to bed. I’m busy with a pamphlet."

Soon, Caroline was alone in her small bedroom. The door bolted, her white dressing gown draped over her shoulders, her long hair loose and flowing like waves to her waist. Pausing after combing it, she leaned her cheek on her hand, staring at the carpet. And there, rising around her, came the visions of eighteen.

Her thoughts spoke to her, gently, soothingly. She smiled as she listened. She looked lovely in quiet contemplation—but lovelier still was the spirit within her: youthful hope. According to this hopeful voice, disappointment was behind her. She had stepped into morning—a real spring dawn—and her sun would soon rise. She couldn’t imagine being deceived. Her expectations seemed grounded in truth.

"When people love," she mused, "they marry. I love Robert. I know he loves me. I’ve believed it before, but today I *felt* it. When I looked up after reading Chénier’s poem, his eyes—how handsome they are—spoke directly to my heart. Sometimes I fear speaking to him, afraid I might be too bold, too forward. I’ve regretted words said too freely, worried I’ve revealed more than he expected. But tonight… tonight I could have told him anything. He was so kind. So gentle walking up the lane. He doesn’t flatter or say silly things. His way of loving—friendship, really, though I don’t yet call him my lover—yet I hope he will be—is better than any I’ve read about. It’s original, quiet, strong, honest. I truly like him. I’d make a good wife. I’d tell him his faults—yes, he has a few—but I’d care for him, comfort him, try to make him happy. I’m sure he won’t be cold tomorrow. I think… I *know*… he’ll come here or ask me to go to him."

She resumed combing her hair—long, thick, like a mermaid’s. Turning her head, she caught her reflection in the mirror. For plain women, such sights sober the mind—seeing themselves without enchantment, knowing others see no charm. But for the fair, the image is captivating, and must be so. Caroline saw a graceful form, a face that, captured in that moment, would have been beautiful. She could not help drawing strength from the sight. With renewed joy, she went to bed.

And with the same joy, she rose the next morning. As she entered her uncle’s breakfast room, greeting him softly, even the stern old man paused, thinking for a moment that his niece was growing “a fine girl.” Usually quiet and shy with him, she was unusually talkative today. Only harmless topics were allowed between them—Mr. Helstone never discussed deeper matters with a woman. She told him about flowers blooming in the garden, asked when the gardener would trim the borders, mentioned that starlings were building nests in the church tower (Briarfield Church stood near the rectory), and wondered why the bells didn’t scare them.

“Like all fools who’ve just paired,” he replied, “they’re blind to inconvenience.”

Encouraged by her spirits, Caroline ventured a remark she’d never dared before.

“Uncle,” she said, “you always speak of marriage with scorn. Do you think people shouldn’t marry?”

“It’s wisest to stay single—especially women.”

“Are all marriages unhappy?”

“Millions are. If everyone told the truth, perhaps all are, more or less.”

“You’re always annoyed when asked to marry a couple. Why?”

“Because I don’t like being an accomplice to pure folly.”

He spoke easily, clearly pleased to share his views. Emboldened, Caroline pressed further.

“But why is it folly? If two people care for each other, why shouldn’t they choose to live together?”

“They grow tired of each other—within a month. A spouse isn’t a companion. They’re a fellow-sufferer.”

It wasn’t innocence that prompted her next words—it was discomfort, a sense of opposition to his beliefs.

“One would think you’d never been married, uncle. One would think you were an old bachelor.”

“Practically, I am.”

“But you *were* married. Why did you marry, then?”

“Every man is mad once or twice in his life.”

“So you grew tired of Aunt Helstone, and she of you? You were miserable?”

Mr. Helstone pursed his lips, wrinkled his brow, and grunted.

“Did she not suit you? Was she ill-tempered? Did you grow used to her? Were you sorry when she died?”

“Caroline,” he said, lowering his hand slowly toward the table, then slamming it down sharply, “understand this: it’s vulgar and childish to confuse general truths with individual cases. Every rule has exceptions. Your questions are stupid, infantile. Ring the bell. Breakfast is over.”

After the meal, the pair usually parted until dinner. But today, Caroline remained. She sat on the window seat, watching the garden. Mr. Helstone glanced at her uneasily—wishing she’d leave—but she paid no attention. He returned to his newspaper, which carried exciting news from the Peninsula, including long dispatches from General Lord Wellington.

Meanwhile, Caroline’s mind swirled with thoughts—thoughts stirred by their conversation, but not born of it. They buzzed like bees disturbed in a hive. These thoughts had first taken root years ago, but now they surged anew.

She reflected on her uncle’s character, his opinions—especially about marriage. Again and again, she had compared them to her own feelings, seeing the vast chasm between them. On the other side of that divide stood another figure—faint, shadowy, almost unreal—the half-remembered image of her father, James Helstone, brother to Matthewson Helstone.

Whispers had reached her about him. Old servants had hinted. She knew he was not a good man. She remembered little—only dark fragments. A time spent with him in a great city, where she had no maid, locked in a bare garret room with no carpet, a bed without curtains, scarcely any furniture. He left early every morning, often forgetting to return for her meals. When he came back at night, he was either furious, raving, or worse—stupid, helpless, senseless. She fell ill. One night, he burst into her room, shouting he’d kill her because she was a burden. Her screams brought help. From that moment, she never saw him alive again—only in his coffin.

That was her father. And she had a mother—though Mr. Helstone never spoke of her, though she could not remember ever seeing her. But she knew her mother lived. This woman was the drunkard’s wife. What had their marriage been?

Turning from the lattice, where she had watched the starlings (though unseen), Caroline broke the silence in a low, bitter voice:

“You call marriage miserable, I suppose, because of my parents. If my mother suffered as I did with Papa, she must have lived a terrible life.”

Mr. Helstone turned sharply in his chair, peering over his spectacles.

“Your father and mother? Who’s been talking to you about them?”

“No one. But I remember something of Papa. And I pity Mama. Where is she?”

The question had been on her lips for years—but never spoken.

“I hardly know,” he replied. “I barely knew her. I haven’t heard from her in years. Wherever she is, she thinks nothing of you. She never asks about you. I believe she doesn’t want to see you. Come, it’s time for school. You’re going to your cousin at ten, aren’t you? The clock has struck.”

Perhaps she would have said more—but Fanny entered, informing Mr. Helstone the churchwardens wished to speak with him in the vestry. He hurried off, and Caroline set out for the cottage.

The path from the rectory to Hollow’s Mill sloped downward. She ran most of the way. Exercise, fresh air, and the thought of seeing Robert lifted her spirits. Approaching the white house, hearing the mill’s thunder and rushing water, she saw Moore at his garden gate.

He stood there in his belted Holland blouse, a light cap on his head—a look that suited him well. He was looking down the lane, not toward her. She stopped, hiding behind a willow, studying him.

“He has no equal,” she thought. “He’s as handsome as he is wise. What a sharp eye! What clear, spirited features—thin and serious, yet graceful. I like his face. I like him—more than any of those clumsy curates—more than anyone. Bonny Robert!”

She hurried to meet him. But when she appeared, Robert seemed to shrink from her presence. He would have vanished like a ghost—if he could. But he was flesh and blood, so he endured the greeting. It was brief—cousinly, brotherly, friendly—but not romantic. The magic of last night was gone. The same man stood before her—but not the same heart beat in his chest.

Rude disappointment. Sharp pain. At first, she refused to believe it, though she felt it. It was hard to pull her hand away from his, wanting at least a warm squeeze. It was hard to turn her eyes from his, hoping for more than a cool nod.

A man in love might explain. A woman in love can say nothing. To speak would bring shame, guilt, self-betrayal. Nature would punish such weakness with the lightning strike of self-contempt. Accept what is. Ask no questions. Say nothing. That is wisdom. You expected bread—you got a stone. Break your teeth on it, don’t scream. Your mental stomach, if you have one, is stronger than an ostrich’s. The stone will digest. You held out your hand for an egg—fate gave you a scorpion. Close your fingers tightly. Let it sting. Don’t flinch. In time, your hand will swell, your arm will tremble—but the scorpion will die. And you will learn the great lesson: how to endure without a sob.

For the rest of your life—if you survive—your strength will grow, your wisdom deepen, your sensitivity fade. You may not know this now. But nature, your ally in sorrow, seals your lips, silences your voice, commands calm. At first, you wear a mask of ease. Later, it settles into sadness, paleness. Then it fades, leaving stoicism—bitter, yes, but powerful.

Bitter? Yes. Bitterness is strength. Sweetness after suffering is a myth. After torment, there may be numbness. Or energy—dangerous energy, deadly when faced with injustice.

Have you read the ballad of “Puir Mary Lee”? An old Scottish song, author unknown. Mary was wronged—perhaps made to believe a lie passed for truth. She does not complain. She sits alone in a snowstorm, and we hear her thoughts. Not heroic thoughts, but those of a peasant girl, deep in grief, fierce in resentment. Anguish has driven her from the hearth to the icy hills. Crouched under the cold drift, she recalls horrors: “the yellow-wymed ask,” “the hairy adder,” “the auld moon-bowing tyke,” “the ghaist at e’en,” “the sour bullister,” “the milk on the taed’s back.” She hates them—but worse, she hates Robin-a-Ree.

“Oh, ance I lived happily by yon bonny burn—
The warld was in love wi’ me;
But now I maun sit 'neath the cauld drift and mourn,
And curse black Robin-a-Ree!

“Then whudder awa, thou bitter biting blast,
And sough through the scrunty tree,
And smoor me up in the snaw fu' fast,
And n'er let the sun me see!

“Oh, never melt awa, thou wreath o' snaw,
That's sae kind in graving me;
But hide me frae the scorn and guffaw
O' villains like Robin-a-Ree!”

But these reflections do not belong to Caroline Helstone—or to the situation between her and Robert. Robert had done her no harm. He had told her no lie. If blame existed, it was hers. Any bitterness she felt should be poured upon herself. She had loved without being asked—a natural, sometimes inevitable risk—but one fraught with pain.

Robert had shown affection at times. Why? Because she had made herself pleasing to him. Despite his efforts, he could not suppress his feelings—even though his judgment disapproved, his will resisted. He was preparing to withdraw from close contact with her. He did not want his emotions entangled, nor to be drawn into a marriage he considered imprudent.

So what was she to do? Give in to her heart, or master it? Pursue him, or turn inward? If weak, she would chase him—losing his respect, gaining his dislike. If wise, she would govern herself, resolve to tame her emotions, to face life honestly, to learn its harsh truths, to study its difficult puzzles with seriousness and conscience.

She showed signs of wisdom. She left Robert quietly—without complaint, without tears. She returned to her studies with Hortense as usual. At dinner, she went home without lingering.

After eating, finding herself alone in the rectory drawing-room while her uncle lingered over port, she faced a new problem: “How shall I get through this day?”

Last night, she had hoped it would be like yesterday—filled with happiness, with Robert. Today, she knew her mistake. Yet she could not accept that no chance would bring her back to Hollow’s Cottage, or bring Robert into her life again.

He had walked up after tea more than once to visit her uncle. The bell would ring, his voice echo in the hall at twilight—when she least expected joy. This had happened twice after he had treated her with unusual reserve. Though he rarely spoke to her in front of her uncle, he had looked at her with softness when seated across from her. The few words he offered were comforting. His farewell was warm.

Now, perhaps, he would come tonight, whispered False Hope. She knew it was False Hope—but she listened.

She tried to read—her thoughts wandered. She tried to sew—each stitch was boredom. She opened her desk, attempted a French composition—only errors emerged.

Suddenly, the doorbell rang sharply. Her heart leapt. She rushed to the door, opened it slightly, peered out. Fanny was admitting a visitor—a gentleman—tall, exactly Robert’s height. For one second, she thought it was him. She exulted. Then the voice asked for Mr. Helstone. Irish. Not Robert. Malone, the curate.

He was led into the dining room, where, no doubt, he helped the rector empty the decanters.

The morning dawned clear and mild, a rare comfort for February. Moore had risen before the sun, already mounted and riding to Whinbury and back before his sister had even begun her breakfast preparations. He returned with no word of what he’d done there—no explanation offered, none expected. Hortense never questioned him; it was not in her nature to pry, nor in his to account for himself. The affairs of trade were sealed within him like secrets buried deep, emerging only rarely to startle Joe Scott or send shivers through foreign correspondents. It seemed that reserve was in his blood—a quiet, unshakable habit born of commerce itself.

After breakfast, Moore retired to his counting-house. Henry, Joe Scott’s son, delivered the letters and newspapers. Moore sat at his desk, broke the seals, and scanned the documents with a quick eye. They were brief, but far from sweet—more sour than honey. As he laid down the last, a sharp sniff escaped his nostrils—defiant, almost scornful—and though he said nothing, the fire in his gaze seemed to summon the devil himself, demanding he sweep the whole business into hell. Yet, without a word, he picked up a pen, snapped off the feathered tip in a sudden burst of restless energy (his face still calm), and wrote out replies swiftly. He sealed them and stepped outside, walking through the mill before returning to sit and read the paper.

It held little interest. He set it aside more than once, arms folded, eyes fixed on the flames dancing in the hearth. He glanced toward the window, checked his watch—his mind clearly elsewhere. Perhaps he longed to be out in the open air, breathing the crisp morning, feeling the warmth of the sun on his skin. The door stood wide, letting in wind and light, but no scent of spring came with it—only the occasional sulfurous puff from the mill’s towering chimney, belching smoke into the sky.

Then, a figure in dark blue—Joe Scott, fresh from the dye vats—appeared at the doorway. “He’s come, sir,” he announced, then vanished.

Moore didn’t look up. A large man entered—broad-shouldered, thick-limbed, dressed in coarse fustian and gray woolen stockings. He was greeted with a nod and invited to sit. Removing his battered hat, he placed it under the chair, wiped his brow with a spotted handkerchief, and declared, “Raight dahn warm for Febewerry.”

Moore grunted something that might have been agreement. The visitor carefully set down a staff beside him—official-looking, formal—and whistled softly, as if settling in.

“You’ve got what you need?” Moore asked.

“Ay, ay! All’s right.”

He resumed whistling. Moore returned to reading. But soon, he reached over, opened a cupboard within arm’s reach, pulled out a black bottle—the same one he’d shown Malone—along with a tumbler and a jug. He placed them on the table.

“Help yourself. There’s water in the jar by the corner.”

“I dunnut knaw that there’s mich need,” said the visitor, rising. “But a body is dry in the morning.” He poured himself a drink, tested it with a deep swallow, then leaned back, satisfied.

“Will you tak naught yourseln, Mr. Moore?” he asked.

Moore shook his head slightly, murmuring a refusal.

“Yah’d as good,” the man continued. “It ‘uld set ye up wald a sup o’ this stuff. Uncommon good hollands. Ye get it fro’ furrin parts, I’se think?”

“Ay!”

“Tak my advice and try a glass on’t. Them lads ‘at’s coming ‘ll keep ye talking, nob’dy knows how long. Ye’ll need propping.”

“Have you seen Mr. Sykes this morning?” Moore asked.

“I seed him a hauf an hour—nay, happen a quarter sin’, just afore I set off. He said he aimed to come here, and I sudn’t wonder but ye’ll have old Helstone too. I seed ‘em saddling his little nag as I passed at back o’ t’ rectory.”

He spoke true. Five minutes later, the clip-clop of hooves echoed in the yard. A familiar nasal voice called out: “Boy!”—likely addressing Harry Scott, who usually loitered near the mill from nine to five.

Helstone strode in, brisk and alert, looking sharper than usual, his cheeks flushed by the cold air.

“Beautiful morning, Moore. How do, my boy? Ha! Whom have we here?” He turned to the man with the staff. “Sugden! What! You’re going to work directly? On my word, you lose no time. But I come to ask explanations. Your message was delivered. Are you sure you’re on the right scent? How do you mean to proceed? Have you a warrant?”

“Sugden has.”

“Then you’re going to seek him now? I’ll accompany you.”

“You will be spared that trouble, sir,” Moore replied. “He’s coming to seek me. I’m just now sitting in state, waiting for his arrival.”

“And who is it? One of my parishioners?”

Joe Scott had slipped in unnoticed. Now he stood leaning on the desk, half his body stained indigo, a silent specter. Moore smiled at the rector’s question. Joe took the cue, adopting a calm yet sly expression.

“It’s a friend of yours, Mr. Helstone—a gentleman you often speak of.”

“Indeed! His name, Joe? You look well this morning.”

“Only the Rev. Moses Barraclough; t’ tub orator you call him sometimes, I think.”

“Ah!” Helstone drew out his snuffbox and took a generous pinch. “Ah! Couldn’t have supposed it. Why, the pious man never was a workman of yours, Moore. He’s a tailor by trade.”

“And so much the worse grudge I owe him,” Moore said, “for interfering and turning my dismissed men against me.”

“And Moses was actually present at the battle of Stilbro’ Moor? He went there, wooden leg and all?”

“Ay, sir,” Joe answered. “He went there on horseback, so his leg wouldn’t be noticed. He was captain, wore a mask. The rest just blackened their faces.”

“And how was he found out?”

“I’ll tell you, sir,” Joe said. “T’ maister’s not fond of talk. I’ve no objections. He courted Sarah, Mr. Moore’s servant lass, and she wouldn’t have nothing to do with him—either she didn’t like his wooden leg or she thought him a hypocrite. Happen (for women are queer hands—we may say that amang werseln when there’s none nigh) she’d have encouraged him anyway, just to pass time. I’ve known some do as mich—even the bonniest, trimmest ones—ay, clean young things that looked as pure as daisies, and yet turned out stinging nettles.”

“Joe’s a sensible fellow,” Helstone interjected.

“Howsiver,” Joe continued, “Sarah had another string to her bow. Fred Murgatroyd, one of our lads, is for her. And as women judge men by their faces—Fred has a middling face, while Moses is none so handsome—we all knaw—and so the lass took on wi’ Fred. A two-three months sin’, Murgatroyd and Moses chanced to meet one Sunday night—both lurking about these premises, hoping to talk Sarah into a walk. They fell out, had a tussle, and Fred was worsted—he’s young and small, while Barraclough, for all he’s one-legged, is almost as strong as Sugden there—indeed, anyone who hears him roaring at a revival or love-feast knows he’s no weakling.”

“Joe, you’re insupportable,” Moore interrupted. “You spin out your tale like Moses spins out his sermons. The short of it is—Murgatroyd was jealous of Barraclough. Last night, as he and a friend sheltered in a barn from a shower, they heard and saw Moses conferring with others inside. From their talk, it was clear he led not only at Stilbro’ Moor but in the attack on Sykes’s property. They planned a deputation to visit me this morning—Barraclough to lead it—in the most religious and peaceful spirit, to beg me to remove the accursed machine from my mill. I rode to Whinbury this morning, secured a constable and a warrant, and now wait to give my friend the welcome he deserves. Here comes Sykes. Mr. Helstone, you must cheer him up. He’s timid about prosecuting.”

A gig rolled into the yard. Mr. Sykes entered—a tall, stout man of fifty, pleasant-faced but weak in expression. He looked anxious.

“Have they been? Are they gone? Have you got him? Is it over?”

“No,” Moore replied, calm and steady. “We’re waiting.”

“They’ll not come—it’s nearly noon. Better give it up. It will stir up bad feeling—cause trouble—perhaps fatal consequences.”

“You need not appear,” Moore said. “I shall meet them in the yard. You can stay here.”

“But my name must be in the proceedings. A wife and family, Mr. Moore—a wife and family make a man cautious.”

Moore looked disgusted. “Give way, please. Leave me to myself. I have no objection to acting alone. Only know this—you won’t find safety in submission. Pearson gave in, conceded, forbore. Yet they tried to shoot him in his own house.”

“My dear sir,” Helstone said, “take a little wine and water.”

Mr. Sykes mixed a brimming tumbler—hollands and water—and drank it. In moments, color returned to his face. He felt emboldened.

“I hope I’m above being trampled on by the common people,” he declared. “I’ve made up my mind. If money and spirit can put down these rioters, they shall be put down. Mr. Moore may do as he likes—but _I_, Christie Sykes, will spend my last penny in law before I yield. I’ll settle them—or see them settled.”

“Take another glass,” Moore urged.

Sykes didn’t mind. “It’s a cold morning,” he said. “Necessary to take care. This season, it’s proper to keep the damp out. I’ve a cough already”—he coughed to prove it—“something like this”—lifting the bottle—“is excellent taken medicinally.” He poured more into his glass. “I don’t usually drink spirits in the morning, but occasionally it’s prudent to take precautions.”

“Quite prudent,” Moore agreed. “Take them by all means.”

Sykes turned to Helstone, who stood by the hearth, watching with keen eyes.

“You, sir,” Sykes said, “as a clergyman, may find it disagreeable to witness scenes of hurry and peril. I dare say your nerves won’t stand it. You’re a man of peace. But we manufacturers, living in the world, always in turmoil, grow quite belligerent. There’s an ardor stirred by danger that makes my heart pant. When Mrs. Sykes fears the house will be attacked—every night—I grow excited. I couldn’t describe my feelings. Honestly, if thieves came—I believe I’d enjoy it, such is my spirit.”

A low, hard laugh escaped the rector—not cruel, but knowing. Moore would have offered a third tumbler, but Helstone stopped him.

“Enough is as good as a feast, is it not, Mr. Sykes?” he said.

Sykes nodded, then watched Joe remove the bottle at a signal from Helstone—smiling smugly, eyes gleaming with regret. Moore looked as though he could have teased him mercilessly. What would Caroline, his cousin, have thought if she’d seen this Robert—her Coriolanus—now? Could she recognize the mischievous, sarcastic man who had spent the evening so gently with her, reading Shakespeare, listening to Chénier?

Yes, it was the same man—just a different side, one Caroline had not yet truly seen, though she may have suspected it. She was human, after all. She, too, had flaws. Had she seen Moore at his worst, she might have forgiven him—love can excuse almost anything except meanness. But meanness kills love, cripples affection. Without esteem, love cannot survive. Moore, despite his faults, was worthy of respect. He had no moral corruption—no rot in his soul, no deadly stain like falsehood. He was not enslaved by desire. His life of action left no room for the hollow pursuits of pleasure-seekers. He was undegraded—a disciple of reason, not sense. The same could be said of old Helstone. Neither lied. Neither had any use for the black bottle now hidden away. Both could claim the proud title of “lord of creation”—for no animal vice ruled them. They were superior beings compared to poor Sykes.

A sound of gathering footsteps came from the yard—then silence. Moore walked to the window. Helstone followed. They stood side by side, the taller man behind the shorter, peering out cautiously, unseen from outside. Their only comment was a shared, cynical smile.

A deep, theatrical cough echoed, followed by a sharp “Whisht!” meant to silence voices. Moore opened the casement slightly.

“Joseph Scott,” began a wheezing voice—Scott standing guard at the door—“might we inquire if your master is within, and may be spoken to?”

“He’s within, ay,” Joe replied nonchalantly.

“Would you then, if _you_ please” (emphasis on “you”), “have the goodness to tell _him_ that twelve gentlemen wish to see him.”

“He’d happen ax what for,” Joe suggested. “I mught as weel tell him that at t’ same time.”

“For a purpose,” came the reply. Joe stepped inside.

“Please, sir, there’s twelve gentlemen wants to see ye, ‘for a purpose.’”

“Good, Joe; I’m their man.—Sugden, come when I whistle.”

Moore stepped out, chuckling quietly. He walked into the yard, one hand in his pocket, the other in his waistcoat, cap brim shading his eyes, masking the glittering scorn beneath.

Twelve men waited—some shirt-sleeved, some in blue aprons. Two stood forward. One, a small, strutting man with a turned-up nose. The other, broad-shouldered, with a wooden leg and crutch—his face solemn, eyes distrustful, lips curled in a sneer. He seemed to mock something unseen, his whole bearing false, untrustworthy.

“Good-morning, Mr. Barraclough,” Moore said, smooth and cool.

“Peace be unto you!” Barraclough replied, closing his half-shut eyes completely.

“I’m obliged. Peace is excellent. Nothing I desire more. But that’s not all you have to say, I suppose? Peace is not your purpose?”

“As to our purpose,” Barraclough began, “it may sound strange or foolish to ears like yours. For the children of this world are wiser in their generation than the children of light.”

“To the point, please. Let me hear it.”

“Ye’se hear, sir. If I cannot get it off, there’s eleven behind to help me. It’s a grand purpose—and” (his voice shifting from sneer to whine) “it’s the Looard’s own purpose, and that’s better.”

“Do you want a subscription for a new Ranter’s chapel, Mr. Barraclough? Unless your errand is something like that, I fail to see its relevance.”

“I hadn’t that duty in mind, sir. But since Providence has led you to mention it, I’ll make it i’ my way to tak ony trifle ye may spare. The smallest contribution will be acceptable.”

With that, he doffed his hat and held it out—like a begging bowl—while a bold grin spread across his face.

“If I gave you sixpence, you’d drink it.”

Barraclough raised his palms and whites of his eyes—an absurd parody of piety.

“You seem a fine fellow,” Moore said, perfectly calm. “You don’t care to show me you’re a double-dyed hypocrite, that your trade is fraud. You expect me to laugh at your cleverness—while thinking you’re fooling the men behind you.”

Barraclough’s face darkened. He realized he’d gone too far. Before he could speak, the second leader stepped forward—impatient, self-assured, though not betraying the mark of a traitor.

“Mr. Moore,” he began, speaking slowly, each word enunciated as if for effect, “it might justly be said that reason, rather than peace, is our purpose. We come first to request you to hear reason. Should you refuse, it is my duty to warn you—in very decided terms—that measures will be resorted to which may likely terminate in—bringing you to a sense of the unwisdom, the foolishness, guiding your actions as a tradesman in this part of the country. Hem! Sir, I would beg to allude that, as a foreigner, arriving from distant shores, another hemisphere of this globe, cast, as I may say, a perfect outcast upon these cliffs of Albion—you lack understanding of our ways, which might benefit the working classes. If, to come to particulars, you’d consider giving up this mill and returning home without further delay, it would perhaps be best. I see no objection to such a plan.—What hev ye to say tull’t, lads?” he turned to the group.

“Hear, hear!” they cried.

“Brayvo, Noah o’ Tim’s!” muttered Joe Scott, standing behind Moore. “Moses’ll niver beat that. Cliffs o’ Albion, and t’ other hemisphere! My certy! Did ye come fro’ th’ Antarctic Zone, maister? Moses is dished.”

But Moses refused to be dished. Glancing sharply at “Noah o’ Tim’s,” he tried again—this time with seriousness, abandoning sarcasm.

“Or iver you set up the pole o’ your tent amang us, Mr. Moore, we lived i’ peace and quietness—yea, I may say, in loving-kindness. I’m not yet aged, but I remember twenty years back when hand-labour was respected, and no mischief-maker dared bring in these machines so pernicious. I’m not a cloth-dresser myself, but a tailor by trade. However, my heart is softish. I’m a feeling man. When I see my brethren oppressed, like my great namesake of old, I stand up for them. That’s why I speak to you today—to advise you to abandon your infernal machinery and employ more hands.”

“What if I refuse, Mr. Barraclough?”

“The Looard pardon you! The Looard soften your heart, sir!”

“Are you connected with the Wesleyans now, Mr. Barraclough?”

“Praise God! Bless His name! I’m a joined Methody!”

“Which doesn’t stop you from being a drunkard and a swindler. I saw you last week, dead-drunk by the roadside, returning from Stilbro’ market. While you preach peace, you spend your life stirring up dissent. You care nothing for the poor in distress—no more than you care for me. You incite them to outrage for your own ends—as does the man called Noah of Tim’s. You two are restless, meddling, impudent scoundrels, driven by selfish ambition—dangerous and childish. Some behind you are honest, misguided men. But you two—altogether bad.”

Barraclough opened his mouth.

“Silence! You’ve had your turn. Now I will speak. To be dictated to by you, or any Jack, Jem, or Jonathan—never. You demand I leave the country. You ask I abandon my machinery. If I refuse, you threaten. I _refuse_—point-blank! I stay. I stand by this mill. I’ll bring in the finest machinery inventors can provide. What will you do? The most you _can_ do—and this you’ll never _dare_—is burn my mill, destroy it, and shoot me. Then what? Suppose the building is ruins and I’m dead—what then, you lads behind these two scamps? Would that stop invention? Exhaust science? Not for a moment! Another, better mill would rise. A more enterprising owner would take my place. Hear me! I’ll make my cloth as I please, using whatever means I choose. Whoever interferes with me after this—let them take the consequences. An example shall prove I’m in earnest.”

He whistled—sharp and loud.

Sugden appeared, staff in hand, warrant in pocket.

Moore turned sharply to Barraclough. “You were at Stilbro’,” he said. “I have proof. You were on the moor. You wore a mask. You knocked down one of my men with your own hand—you! A preacher of the gospel! Sugden—arrest him!”

Moses was seized. A cry rose, a rush to rescue. But Moore’s hand, hidden all along, emerged—holding a pistol.

“Both barrels are loaded,” he said. “I’m quite determined. Keep off!”

And the yard fell still.

Mr. Moore returned to the others after sending off the delegation, and Mr. Helstone and Mr. Sykes greeted him with cheerful congratulations on his firmness and resolve. But Moore remained quiet, his face as still and dark as a cloudless night—no warmth in it, no motion. The rector glanced at him sharply, then tucked away his praise as if it were too much for the moment, and turned to Sykes, who seemed unaware that his presence was unwelcome. “Come along,” he said. “Our paths lie together. Let’s walk home together. We’ll bid Moore good morning and leave him to whatever thoughts he chooses to dwell upon.”

“And where is Sugden?” Moore asked, looking up.

“Ah!” Helstone exclaimed. “I’ve been busy while you were talking to that solemn-looking man—Farren, I believe. While you were negotiating, I opened the back window, called to Murgatroyd in the stable to bring round Mr. Sykes’s gig, and slipped Sugden and brother Moses—wooden leg and all—through the opening. I saw them climb into the carriage, of course, with Mr. Sykes’s full approval. Sugden took the reins—he drives like a storm—and within twenty minutes Barraclough will be safely locked up in Stilbro’ jail.”

“Very well,” Moore replied. “Thank you. Good morning, gentlemen.” He courteously escorted them to the door and watched them depart before closing it behind them.

For the rest of the day, Moore was silent and serious. He didn’t even exchange a witty remark with Joe Scott, who kept his words brief and businesslike but often glanced at his master from the corner of his eye. Joe came by to stoke the fire more than once, and when Moore was locking up for the evening—trade had slowed, so the mill was running short hours—he remarked, “It’s a fine night, sir. You might take a walk up th’ Hollow. It’d do you good.”

At this, Moore broke into a short laugh. “What’s all this concern about?” he asked. “Do you think me a child or a woman?” He snatched the keys from Joe’s hand and gently pushed him out of the room. But just as Joe reached the yard gate, Moore called him back.

“Joe,” he said, “do you know the Farrens? They’re not doing well, are they?”

“They can’t be,” Joe answered. “They haven’t worked in three months. You’d see it yourself—William’s changed, looks worn down. They’ve sold most of what they had in the house.”

“He wasn’t a bad worker?”

“Never better since you started trade, sir.”

“And decent people—the whole family?”

“Niver dacenter. The wife’s clean as a whistle—ye could eat your porridge off the floor. They’re hard hit. I wish William could get a job as gardener or something—he knows the craft well. Once lived with a Scotchman who taught him all the secrets.”

“Well, you may go now, Joe. No need to stand there staring.”

“You’ve no orders, sir?”

“None, except to take yourself off.”

And Joe did.

* * * * *

Spring evenings can be sharp and biting, even after a warm day. Though the sun had shone brightly, the air grew cold at sunset, the ground crusted over, and by dusk a thin frost crept across the grass and budding plants. It whitened the pavement in front of Briarmains—Mr. Yorke’s home—and silently damaged the tender greenery in his garden and on the mossy lawn. The great tree near the road, strong-trunked and wide-armed, stood defiant against the frost, as did the bare walnut trees rising behind the house.

In the dim, starlit night, lights glowed warmly from the windows. This was no lonely or silent scene. Briarmains stood close to the highway, an old house built before the road was cut, when a winding lane through fields was the only way to reach it. Briarfield was barely a mile away—its hum could be heard, its glow seen clearly. Nearby rose Briar Chapel, a large, new Wesleyan church, just a hundred yards distant. Inside, a prayer meeting was underway. Its windows lit the road, and a hymn—unusual in rhythm and fervor—echoed through the countryside. A Quaker might have danced to such a tune. The lyrics drifted through the night in fragments:

“Oh! who can explain
This struggle for life,
This travail and pain,
This trembling and strife?
Plague, earthquake, and famine,
And tumult and war,
The wonderful coming
Of Jesus declare!

“For every fight
Is dreadful and loud:
The warrior's delight
Is slaughter and blood,
His foes overturning,
Till all shall expire:
And this is with burning,
And fuel, and fire!”

Then came a burst of passionate prayer, followed by groans. A shout rang out: “I’ve found liberty!” “Doad o’ Bill’s has fun’ liberty!” And the congregation erupted again.

“What a mercy is this! What a heaven of bliss!
How unspeakably happy am I!
Gathered into the fold,
With Thy people enrolled,
With Thy people to live and to die!

“Oh, the goodness of God
In employing a clod
His tribute of glory to raise;
His standard to bear,
And with triumph declare
His unspeakable riches of grace!

“Oh, the fathomless love
That has deigned to approve
And prosper the work of my hands.
With my pastoral crook
I went over the brook,
And behold I am spread into bands!

“Who, I ask in amaze,
Hath begotten me these?
And inquire from what quarter they came.
My full heart it replies,
They are born from the skies,
And gives glory to God and the Lamb!”

After another long pause filled with shouts, cries, and groans, the final verse rose in a thunderous crescendo:

“Sleeping on the brink of sin,
Tophet gaped to take us in;
Mercy to our rescue flew,
Broke the snare, and brought us through.

“Here, as in a lion’s den,
Undevoured we still remain,
Pass secure the watery flood,
Hanging on the arm of God.

“Here——”

(The last line burst forth in a strained, ear-splitting shout.)

“Here we raise our voices higher,
Shout in the refiner’s fire,
Clap our hands amidst the flame,
Glory give to Jesus’ name!”

The chapel roof did not collapse—a testament to its sturdy slate.

Yet if the chapel pulsed with energy, so too did Briarmains, though in quieter fashion. Lights glowed in several windows. Lower casements opened onto the lawn, curtains partly veiling the candlelight inside, yet not muffling the sound of laughter and conversation. We step inside—into the heart of the home.

No guests fill the house. Only the Yorke family gathers in the farthest room to the right—the back parlour. This is their evening sanctuary.

By daylight, the windows would reveal stained glass—purple and amber hues surrounding medallions of Shakespeare and Milton. On the walls hang Canadian scenes—green forests, blue rivers—and in the center, a painting of Vesuvius erupting at night, glowing fiercely against cool waterfalls and dark woods.

The fire blazing here is one southern readers rarely see in private homes. High, clear coal flames fill the wide chimney. Mr. Yorke insists on such fires—even in summer. He sits beside it, book in hand, a small candlestand at his elbow. But he isn’t reading. He watches his children.

Opposite him sits his wife—a woman whose features I could describe in detail, but I hesitate. Yet I see her plainly: tall, grave, burdened with care—not crushing, but constant, like a shadow she chose to carry. She believed sorrow was noble, joy profane. She disapproved of cheerfulness, especially in women. To her, mirth was frivolity, laughter was irreverence. She never made distinctions. Still, she was a devoted wife, attentive mother, loyal to her husband. Her flaw? She wanted him to have no friend but herself. His relatives she distrusted, kept at bay.

Yorke and she got along well enough, though he was naturally sociable, fond of family ties, and once preferred lively, spirited women. Why he married her, how they suited each other—puzzling, but perhaps explained by his own dual nature. His darker side found comfort in her unrelenting seriousness. She was strong-minded, never weak or trite, skeptical of society, cynical of humanity, convinced of her own perfection and the world’s failure. Her greatest fault? An endless, unshakable suspicion of men, ideas, beliefs—everything. It clouded her vision, misled her steps.

Their children, shaped by such parents, were no ordinary souls. Six of them.

The youngest, a baby, rests on her mother’s lap. Still entirely hers, untouched by doubt. She feeds from her, clings to her, loves her above all. The mother feels certain of this bond—because the child lives by her, it must love her. So she loves in return.

Next are two girls—Rose and Jessy—now seated at their father’s knee. They rarely approach their mother unless forced. Rose, twelve, resembles her father—but softer, like marble carved from stone. Her face is gentle, childlike, cheeks rosy. Her gray eyes hold something deeper—a serious soul already stirring. Young, yes, but destined to grow stronger, purer, more noble than either parent. She is quiet, sometimes stubborn. Her mother wants to mold her into a dutiful, somber woman, but Rose carries seeds of thought her mother never knew. It hurts her to have those thoughts crushed. She hasn’t rebelled yet—but if pressed too far, she will. And once, it will be forever.

She loves her father. He doesn’t rule her harshly. He is kind. And he fears she won’t live—so bright are the sparks of intelligence in her gaze. That fear makes him tender, almost fearful.

Jessy, younger and livelier, is his favorite. Gay, talkative, mischievous, affectionate when praised. Passionate when provoked, fearless—especially of her mother, whom she often defied. With her bright face and charming ways, she’s meant to be loved. And she is.

Now imagine, reader, if a mirror showed you both daughters twenty years hence. First, Jessy.

You recognize this place—though never seen. Cypress, willow, yew. Stone crosses, eternal flowers. Green sod, gray marble. Here lies Jessy. She lived one April day—loved deeply, loving much. She wept, she suffered, but smiled often, bringing light wherever she went. She died peacefully in Rose’s arms, in a foreign land. The soil there holds her now.

Now Rose, two years later. The crosses seem strange, but the landscape—hills, woods—feels wilder still. This is far from England. A virgin land. Birds unknown flutter near the forest. A river unlike any European one winds beneath her. The quiet Yorkshire girl is alone, an emigrant in the southern hemisphere. Will she ever return?

Three boys—Matthew, Mark, Martin—sit together in the corner, playing. Their heads look alike at first glance, but closer inspection reveals differences. All have dark hair, dark eyes, red cheeks, English features, a blend of both parents. Yet each bears a distinct face, a unique spirit.

Matthew, the eldest, draws attention. Jet-black hair, white brow, high-cheeked, quick eyes. Handsome, yes—but his face echoes something else: the eruption of Vesuvius. Flame and shadow—no sunlight, no peace. He seems English in form, but not in mind. Like an Italian blade wrapped in British leather. He scowls—crossed in the game. Mr. Yorke sees it and whispers, “Mark, Martin—don’t provoke your brother.” This is their constant tone. They claim no favoritism, yet Matthew is never challenged. They avoid angering him as if he were a powder keg. “Concede, conciliate”—their motto. The republicans unknowingly crown a tyrant. The younger boys sense this injustice, feel it deeply. Rebellion simmers beneath the surface. Seeds of discord are sown among Yorke’s olive branches.

Mark, calm, steady. Regular features, shrewd smile. Can say the sharpest things in the quietest voice. His brow hints at temper—smooth waters can hide danger. Too still, too unmoved. He will never truly enjoy life. By twenty-five, he’ll wonder why people laugh. Poetry? Rant. Enthusiasm? Ridiculous. He’ll be middle-aged in spirit by fourteen. His body young, his soul aged.

Martin, the youngest, is different. Life may be short—or long—but it will be vivid. He’ll pass through illusions, half-believe, wholly enjoy, then outlive them. Not handsome now—plain, even rough. A shell around him. But it will shed. Near twenty, he’ll become striking. His manners clumsy, clothes plain—yet the chrysalis holds the power to transform. Then he’ll be vain, eager, thirsty for pleasure and knowledge. He’ll drink deep from every source. After that? Unknown. He might become remarkable. Or not. No vision reveals the path.

Together, these six children hold more mental power, originality, and vigor than half a dozen ordinary families combined. Mr. Yorke knows this. He takes pride in them. Yorkshire breeds such families—racy, strong-blooded, proud, untamed. Lacking polish, yes, but true-hearted, bold, like eagles on cliffs or horses on steppes.

A soft tap sounds at the door. The boys have been noisy, little Jessy singing a sweet Scotch song—her father adores such music, and has taught her many. The outer ring went unnoticed.

“Come in,” Mrs. Yorke says, her voice solemn, always modulated to a funereal tone—no matter the subject. “Come in.”

Robert Moore enters.

Moore’s quiet demeanor and abstemious habits have earned him some favor with Mrs. Yorke. She hasn’t yet accused him of secret affairs or hidden motives—unlike most of her husband’s friends, whom she dismissed early in marriage. She’s not yet discovered his secret entanglement, nor that he may wear a sheep’s skin over a wolf’s heart.

“Well, is it you?” she asks, as he approaches and offers his hand. “Roving about at this hour? You should be at home.”

“Can a single man truly call a place home?” he replies.

“Pooh!” she answers, scornful of pretense. “Don’t play games with me. A man can have a home if he wants. Does your sister not make one for you?”

“No,” Mr. Yorke adds. “Hortense is honest, but when I was Robert’s age, I had five or six sisters—just as proper. Yet I still sought a wife.”

“And sorely regretted it,” Mrs. Yorke says, cracking a dry joke. “Repented in sackcloth and ashes. Look at him now—look at the punishment” (she gestures to the children). “Who would choose such a brood? Not just bringing them into the world—feeding, clothing, raising, settling them. Young sir, when you think of marrying, look at our four sons and two daughters. Think twice before you leap.”

“I’m not tempted now,” Moore says. “These are not times for marriage.”

Mrs. Yorke nods gravely. Such sentiments pleased her. But then she adds, “I don’t trust the wisdom of a Solomon your age. One fancy and it’s all undone. Sit down, sir. You can talk as well seated as standing.”

That was her way of inviting him to sit. As soon as he does, little Jessy leaps from her father’s knee and runs into Moore’s arms—held ready.

“You talk of marrying him,” she says to her mother, indignant, as she’s lifted onto his knee. “But he’s already married—or as good. He promised last summer, the first time he saw me in my new white frock and blue sash. Didn’t he, father?” (These children never said “papa” or “mamma”—their mother forbade such “namby-pamby” terms.)

Time passed, and spring deepened into full bloom. England’s surface softened with the season—fields turned emerald, hills wore a fresh green mantle, gardens burst into flower—but beneath it all, the country remained troubled. The poor still suffered, the wealthy were strained by economic strain, and commerce trembled under the shadow of war. Though some victories came from the Peninsula, they arrived slowly, separated by long silences broken only by Napoleon’s boastful proclamations of his own triumphs. To those who bore the burden of conflict, the war felt endless, unjust, and hopeless. They begged for peace—any peace. Men like Moore and Yorke, men whose livelihoods hung by a thread, fought for it with desperate energy.

They gathered in meetings, delivered impassioned speeches, sent petitions to Parliament—whatever it took to force an end to hostilities. The terms didn’t matter; survival did.

Men, when viewed individually, are seldom free from self-interest; in groups, they become utterly so. British merchants were no exception. Their minds were fixed on profit, their hearts closed to broader national ideals. Chivalry, honor, patriotism—these had long since faded from their souls. If England were ruled solely by such men, she would surrender not out of Christian humility, but out of Mammon’s greed. They might endure insults, give up their cloaks, even offer their coats—only to cling fiercely to their last garment, the one that held their purse. And if that were touched? Then, suddenly, they would transform into fierce bulldogs, snapping at the invader’s throat until every coin was reclaimed.

When these merchants spoke against war, they claimed to hate its violence, as if they were uniquely civilized, gentle souls. But many were cold, narrow-minded, indifferent to anyone outside their trade. They saw others as burdens—useless, parasitic, undeserving of air or bread. They dismissed the arts, education, and service as idle pursuits, accusing all who practiced them of living off the labor of others. It grieved Caroline to think that one day England might truly become a nation of shopkeepers.

Moore, though not a patriot in the noble sense, was deeply affected by the war’s toll. When he felt himself slipping again toward ruin, he resisted fiercely. He stirred unrest in the north, urging others with greater influence to join him. Yet sometimes, even he glimpsed the absurdity of his demands. When news reached him of Bonaparte threatening Europe, of Russia rising in defiance—of a frozen land defending its serfs and despotism from foreign conquest—he knew England could not now make concessions. A free nation could not submit while others stood firm. And then there were the reports from the Peninsula—the steady, relentless advance of Wellington’s forces. Reading the general’s dispatches—modest, truthful, unassuming—Moore felt a quiet conviction grow within him: there was a strength in those troops, a quiet resolve, that would eventually prevail.

But that victory seemed distant. For Moore, the future looked bleak. His own life, his ambitions, his hopes—they mattered more than any grand ideal. He would act to preserve himself, and he did.

His actions soon brought him into open conflict with Mr. Helstone, his old Tory friend and mentor. They clashed at a public meeting, then exchanged sharp letters in the newspapers. Mr. Helstone denounced Moore as a Jacobin, refused to speak to him, and forbade Caroline from visiting Hollow’s Cottage. She must stop her French lessons. The language, he declared, was frivolous, dangerous—especially for women. “Who first made it fashionable to teach ladies French?” he mused aloud. “It’s like feeding a sick child on chalk and water.” Caroline must abandon both the lessons and her cousins. They were dangerous, subversive people.

Mr. Helstone expected resistance. He anticipated tears. He rarely paid attention to Caroline’s comings and goings, but lately he’d noticed how often she went to Hollow’s Cottage—and how she seemed drawn to Robert Moore’s presence at the rectory.

He had observed something peculiar: when Malone visited, he would amuse himself by pinching the ears of the black cat curled beside Caroline’s feet, or by firing a fowling piece at the toolshed door, shouting loudly about his aim. At such times, Caroline would quietly slip away upstairs, disappearing until supper. But when Robert Moore came, there was no noise, no commotion. He sat calmly, petting the cat, letting it climb onto his shoulder. And Caroline stayed. She knitted missionary socks, stitched jeweled pin-cushions—quiet, content.

She said little, and Robert rarely addressed her directly. But Mr. Helstone, ever watchful, had seen them exchange a glance once—just once—as they parted for the night. It wasn’t a secret look, nor one of love. There was no hidden meaning. But in that moment, Moore’s eyes had found hers clear and gentle; hers, meeting his, had acknowledged his strength and depth. Each had sensed something admirable in the other. Moore smiled faintly. Caroline blushed slightly.

And Mr. Helstone, seeing this, felt irritation flare. Why? He couldn’t say. If asked what Moore deserved, he would have said a horsewhip. If asked about Caroline, a slap across the face. If pressed further, he would rage about flirtation and nonsense—no such behavior under his roof.

So he decided to separate them. One evening, as Caroline sat near the drawing-room window, he told her plainly: no more visits to Hollow’s Cottage. No letters to Hortense. No contact at all. “Their principles,” he declared, “are Jacobinical.”

Caroline nodded. “Yes, uncle.”

“Of course you’ll obey?”

“Yes, certainly.”

“And no correspondence with your cousin. I disapprove of their family.”

“Very well,” she replied quietly.

There was no protest, no tears. Her face remained calm, thoughtful. She obeyed—not because she feared him, but because she already knew the truth: going to Hollow’s Cottage no longer brought her joy. Robert seemed absent—distant, preoccupied. When she asked after him, the answer was always the same: he was away, or buried in work. Hortense worried he was overworking himself. He barely ate at home, lived in the counting-house.

At church, she saw him only occasionally. She rarely looked at him. To do so was too painful, too sweet—too much emotion. And she had learned, painfully, that it was wasted.

Once, on a dreary Sunday, when few attended church and certain observant ladies were absent, she allowed herself to glance toward his pew. He was alone—Hortense had stayed home due to the rain and a new hat. During the sermon, he sat with arms folded, head lowered, looking sorrowful, detached. In low moods, his skin seemed darker, more olive, more solemn. As she studied his face, she sensed his thoughts were far from her—far from anything she could understand. He was lost in responsibilities, decisions, pressures she could not share.

That night, Caroline retreated to her room after tea. Fanny had lit a small fire, the weather being cold and windy. Alone, silent, she walked back and forth, hands clasped, head bowed. Sitting was unbearable; her mind raced.

The house was quiet. The study door muffled the voices of the gentlemen. The servants read books Caroline had lent them—books she called “fit for Sunday reading.” She had one open on her table, but she could not read it. Its theology was alien, and her thoughts were too restless.

Her imagination filled with images: Robert walking beside her through Nunnely Wood in winter, the warmth of summer afternoons spent in the forest, the soft songs of birds in spring, the autumn harvest of nuts and blackberries she once gathered for him—each berry placed gently in his hand, each nut offered like food to a fledgling bird.

She heard his voice clearly in her mind, felt his rare touches. But soon, the visions dimmed. The warmth faded. The kiss she imagined on her forehead felt like a cold raindrop. She returned from fantasy to reality: her small chamber, the rain on the windowpane, the wind moaning, and only her own shadow on the wall.

Turning from that pale reflection, she sat down. Inaction suited her mood. She whispered to herself: “I may live seventy years. I’m healthy. Half a century lies ahead. What will I do with it?”

She thought.

“I won not marry,” she continued. “Robert doesn’t care for me. I’ll never have a husband, children. Until recently, I assumed I’d be a wife, a mother—those roles would fill my life. But now I see I may be wrong. I may become an old maid. I’ll live to see Robert marry someone else—someone rich, perhaps. I’ll never marry. What was I made for? Where is my place?”

She paused.

“Oh,” she murmured, “that’s the question every old maid asks. Others tell us: ‘Your purpose is to help others, to serve.’ That sounds noble. But I wonder—is giving oneself entirely to others enough? Is it real life? Is there not emptiness, longing, in a life given away because one has nothing else to give? I suspect there is. Is virtue really found in self-denial? I don’t believe so. Humility can breed tyranny. Weakness breeds selfishness. The Roman Church teaches renunciation, submission—yet its priests are often the most grasping tyrants. Every person has rights. Perhaps happiness comes when each holds fast to their own, as a martyr clings to faith.”

Strange thoughts. Were they right? She wasn’t sure.

“Well,” she said, “life is short. Seventy years pass like a dream. All paths lead to the grave—the tiny crack in the earth where the reaper casts the seed. It falls, decays, and rises again when the world turns. That’s the body. The soul, meanwhile, soars upward, halts at the edge of fire and glass, and sees reflected the vision of the Trinity—the Father, the Son, the Holy Spirit. Such words are meant to describe the unsayable. But what becomes of the soul after death? Who can know?”

Her fire died to ashes. Malone had left. The study bell rang for prayers.

The next day, her uncle dined with Dr. Boultby, leaving Caroline alone. She spent the hours thinking—looking ahead, wondering what she would do with her life. Fanny, passing by, noticed she sat motionless, bent over her sewing, not speaking.

“You’re very diligent today, Miss Caroline,” she said.

“I’m tired of it, Fanny.”

“Then why keep at it? Put it down. Read something. Do something to cheer yourself.”

“It’s lonely here, Fanny. Don’t you feel it?”

“No, miss. Me and Eliza keep each other company. But you’re too still. You should visit more. Go see Miss Mann or Miss Ainley. I’m sure either would be glad to see you.”

“But their houses are gloomy. They’re both old maids. I’ve heard old maids are terribly unhappy.”

“They’re not, miss. They can’t be. They take care of themselves. They’re selfish.”

“Miss Ainley isn’t selfish. She’s kind. She cared for her stepmother till the end. Now, alone in the world, she helps the poor as much as she can. But no one notices her. Gentlemen mock her.”

“They shouldn’t, miss. I think she’s good. But gentlemen only care about looks.”

“I’ll go see her,” Caroline said, rising. “And if she invites me to tea, I’ll stay. It’s wrong to ignore people just because they’re not young or beautiful. I’ll call on Miss Mann too. She may not be pleasant—but what made her that way? What has life done to her?”

Fanny helped her pack away her work and dress.

“You won’t be an old maid, Miss Caroline,” she said, smoothing her curls and tying her sash. “Not a sign of it.”

Caroline looked in the mirror. She thought she saw signs—her complexion paler, shadows around her eyes, a quiet sadness in her expression. She was not as bright as before.

Fanny offered no reply, only a reassurance: “People change. At your age, a little fading means nothing. You’ll be plumper, rosier soon.”

With that, she wrapped Caroline warmly in shawls and handkerchiefs until she nearly choked.

And so, wrapped and ready, Caroline stepped out into the world—not to seek romance, but to understand her place in it.

Caroline could not surrender without a fight. There was a quiet strength within her—a fire that burned low but refused to go out. And she used it. People never struggle so fiercely as when they are alone, with no one to witness their pain, no confidant to comfort them, no voice to guide or console. She stood in that silence, her suffering the only fuel for her will. It was sharp, real, and unrelenting—yet it stoked her resolve. Determined to conquer this ache that gnawed at her soul, she threw herself into every task, every movement, every breath. Never had she been so busy, so absorbed, so restless. She walked long distances in all weather, through empty lanes and over open moors, returning each evening pale and drained, yet never truly tired. As soon as she removed her bonnet and shawl, she would begin pacing her room—not to rest, but to exhaust herself. Sometimes she wouldn’t sit until she felt faint. She claimed she did it to sleep better. But sleep eluded her. While others rested, she tossed beneath her sheets, or sat upright in the dark, forgotten by the night, lost in thoughts too heavy for tears.

And yet—when the weight became unbearable—she wept. Oh, how she wept! Not just with sorrow, but with a grief so deep it broke her spirit, leaving her trembling and helpless, like a child who has lost everything. In those moments, whispers crept into her mind: *Write to Robert. Tell him you’re hurting. Say you miss seeing him, Hortense—say you fear he’ll forget you entirely. Ask him to remember you. To write back.* She wrote such letters—more than once—but never sent them. Shame held her hand; reason pulled her back.

At last, she reached the edge. The life she lived was no longer bearable. She needed change—or else her heart and mind would break under the strain. She longed to leave Briarfield, to vanish somewhere far away. But deeper still, beneath that longing, stirred another—quiet, persistent, almost shameful: the desire to know her mother. Each day, it grew stronger. Yet with it came dread. What if she found her—and didn’t love her? Her mother had never been spoken of with warmth. Her uncle spoke of her sister-in-law with a cold reserve. An old servant, who had served Mrs. James Helstone briefly after her marriage, called her “queer,” said she “didn’t understand her.” Those words were like ice in Caroline’s veins. If she knew her mother, would she despise her? Was it better never to know?

But one plan remained—one that seemed possible, even hopeful: becoming a governess. It was the only path left open to her. A small event brought her to the brink of speaking her decision aloud. Her walks—long, solitary, often late—always led her near the Hollow. She rarely descended into the valley, but she visited its edge at twilight, as regularly as the stars rose above the hills. Her favorite spot was a stile beneath an ancient thorn tree, where she could see the cottage, the mill, the dewy garden, and the still, deep dam. From there, she watched the counting-house window. At a certain hour, the lamp inside would flare to life—bright, sudden, familiar. That light was her anchor. When it appeared, she felt a spark of hope. If a shadow passed across it, her heart leapt—it was Robert. She’d return home comforted, carrying in her mind a clearer image of his face, his voice, his smile. Sometimes, she believed—if only she could be near him—he might still welcome her. He might reach out his hand, draw her close, shelter her as he once had. That night, though she might cry, the tears would feel less hot. The pillow softer. The ache in her temples, lighter.

The shortest route from the Hollow to the rectory passed near Fieldhead—the old, abandoned mansion where Malone had once wandered on a moonlit night. Though vacant for ten years, it was not ruined. Mr. Yorke had ensured it stayed well kept—an old gardener and his wife lived there, tending the grounds, maintaining the house. It wasn’t grand, but it was striking. Time had painted its stone gray and mossy, its windows latticed, its porch carved, its roof and chimneys rich in shadow and texture. Behind it rose bold trees; before it, a majestic cedar. Granite urns lined the wall; a fretted arch framed the gate. For an artist, it was a feast for the eye.

One soft May evening, near moonrise, Caroline lingered near the gate. Though weary, she couldn’t bring herself to go home—where only sorrow waited. She sat on the mossy ground, gazing toward the cedar and the house. The night was calm, dewy, cloudless. The west-facing gables glowed amber, reflecting the horizon. The oaks behind were black; the cedar, darker still. Beneath its thick boughs, a patch of sky shimmered blue. The moon hung high, solemn and gentle, watching over her.

She found the scene mournfully beautiful. She wished she could be happy. She wished peace would come. Why did Providence seem so indifferent? Why offer no comfort? Memories of lovers’ trysts from old ballads flooded her mind—how sweet they must have been in such a place. Where was Robert now? She hadn’t seen his lamp. Had he gone away? Would she ever speak to him again?

Then—suddenly—the door of the porch opened. Two men stepped out. One older, white-haired; the other young, tall, dark-haired. They crossed the lawn, passed through the garden gate, and walked down the road. Caroline saw them pass the stile, descend the field, disappear. Robert Moore had walked past her—with Mr. Yorke. Neither had seen her.

The moment was fleeting—gone before she could grasp it. But it lit her blood. It left her shaken, feverish, furious. It found her despairing; it left her desperate.

“Oh, had he but been alone! Had he but seen me!” she cried inwardly. “He would have said something. He would have taken my hand. He _must_ care for me a little. I would have read comfort in his eyes, in his lips. But it’s gone. He passed like wind, like shadow—silent, empty. I’ve been mocked. And Heaven is cruel.”

With that, she turned and walked home, her heart shattered.

The next morning, at breakfast, she looked pale, hollow-eyed, as if she had seen a ghost. She asked her uncle, “Uncle, do you object if I look for a position in a family?”

Mr. Helstone, unaware of her inner torment, stared in disbelief.

“What nonsense is this?” he said. “Are you bewitched?”

“I’m not well,” she replied. “I need a change.”

He studied her. Without knowing it, he saw what she had become: the rose had faded to a snowdrop, bloom vanished, flesh thinned. She sat before him drooping, colorless, fragile. Only her eyes—soft and brown—and her flowing hair saved her from being plain.

“What on earth is wrong with you?” he demanded. “What ails you?”

No answer. Just a tremble in her lips, a swelling in her eyes.

“Look for a situation, indeed!” he scoffed. “What kind? What have you done with yourself? You’re not well.”

“I would be well if I left home.”

“These women!” he muttered. “They startle you with surprises. One day they’re bright and rosy, the next, shriveled and broken. What causes it? Who knows? She eats, she’s free, she has a good house and clothes. Once it was enough. Now she’s a poor, pale, whimpering thing. Provoking! What shall I do? I suppose I must call a doctor.”

“No, uncle,” she said quietly. “A doctor can’t help. I just need a change of air and scene.”

“Well, if it’s a whim, it shall be granted. You shall go to Cliff Bridge. Fanny will go with you.”

“But uncle,” she said, “one day I must work. I have no fortune. I should begin now.”

“You shall not become a governess, Caroline,” he declared. “I won’t have it said my niece is a governess.”

“But the later one begins such a change,” she said gently, “the harder it is. I’d rather grow used to it before habits of ease take root.”

“Don’t harass me,” he said sharply. “I will provide for you. I’ll buy an annuity. I’m fifty-five—healthy, strong. Plenty of time. Don’t worry about the future.”

“It’s not the future,” she whispered. “It’s the need for change.”

He laughed. “There speaks the woman!” he said. “Always fantastical! Always wanting a change. Well, it’s in her nature.”

“But it’s not fantasy,” she insisted.

“What then?”

“Necessity,” she said. “I feel weaker. I believe I should have more to do.”

“Admirable!” he said. “She feels weak—so she should be set to hard labor. ‘Clair comme le jour,’ as Moore—confound Moore! You shall go to Cliff Bridge. Two guineas for a new dress. Come, Cary, don’t fear. We’ll find balm in Gilead.”

“Uncle,” she began, “I wish you were less generous and more—”

“More what?”

“Sympathizing,” she meant to say—but stopped. She knew he’d laugh. So she said nothing.

He took her silence as agreement. “The truth is,” he said, “you don’t know what you want.”

“I do,” she said. “I want to be a governess.”

“Pooh! Nonsense! I won’t hear of it. Don’t mention it again. It’s too feminine. I’ve finished breakfast. Ring the bell. Put your foolish ideas aside and amuse yourself.”

“What with?” she thought, as she left the room. “My doll?”

A week passed. Her health neither improved nor worsened. She was exactly at the point where, if she carried seeds of illness—consumption, slow fever—those would now rage unchecked. People don’t die of grief alone, but grief can awaken hidden diseases and send them spiraling. Strong people endure agony—bodies break, beauty fades—but life persists. They grow pale, thin, frail. Others think they’ll soon fall ill, die quietly. But they live on. Like a blossom nipped by March winds but not swept away—it may hang on, a withered fruit, into autumn. Having braved spring frost, it may also brave winter’s first chill.

Everyone noticed the change in Miss Helstone. Most said she was dying. She didn’t believe it. She felt no sickness, no pain. Her appetite dwindled—because she wept so much at night. Her strength waned—because sleep fled, dreams haunted her. In the distance, she still imagined a time when this pain would end, when she might be calm again—though perhaps never happy.

Her uncle urged her to visit, to accept invitations. She avoided them. She couldn’t pretend cheerfulness. She felt watched—curiosity, not kindness. Old ladies offered advice, remedies. Young ones looked at her with pity, with understanding. Their eyes said: *She’s been disappointed.* By whom? They weren’t sure.

Ordinary young women can be as harsh as ordinary young men—as worldly, as selfish. Sufferers should avoid them. They scorn grief, see it as divine punishment for weakness. To them, “love” is a scheme for a good match. To be “disappointed” is to have that scheme fail. They assume everyone thinks like them, and judge accordingly.

Caroline knew this—by instinct, by observation. She withdrew. She kept her pale face and thin figure hidden. Living in seclusion, she lost touch with the world.

One morning, her uncle entered the parlour where she sat painting wildflowers gathered at the top of the Hollow fields.

“Come, child,” he said abruptly. “You’re always bent over palette or book. Leave that. By-the-bye—do you put your pencil to your lips when you paint?”

“Sometimes, uncle,” she said, “when I forget.”

“Then that’s poisoning you,” he declared. “Those paints are deadly—white lead, red lead, verdigris, gamboge—poisons all. Lock them up! Get your bonnet on. I want you to make a call with me.”

"With *me*?" she asked, surprised. She never went anywhere with him.

“Quick! I have no time to lose.”

She gathered her things, asking where they were going.

“To Fieldhead.”

“Fieldhead? To see James Booth, the gardener?”

“No. To see Miss Shirley Keeldar.”

“Miss Keeldar? Is she here? At Fieldhead?”
