“She is. She’s been there a week. I met her last night—at that party you refused to attend. I liked her. I want you to meet her. It will do you good.”

“She’s come of age?”

“She is. She’ll stay for a while. I lectured her on duty. She’s not stubborn. Rather a lively girl. Nothing dull about her.”

“I don’t think she’ll care to see me. What can I offer her?”

“Pshaw! Put your bonnet on.”

“Is she proud, uncle?”

“Don’t know. Do you think she’d show pride to *me*? A girl like that wouldn’t presume to give airs to the rector of her parish.”

“No. But how did she behave?”

“Didn’t notice. She holds her head high. Probably saucy when she dares. But she’s a woman—what else?”

“Very well. But I’m not eager.”

“Enough! Away!”

Though naturally shy, weakened and discouraged, Caroline felt her spirits sink as they walked up the broad, paved path to Fieldhead’s porch. She followed her uncle reluctantly into the dim, vast hall beyond.

It was sombre—long, dark, shadowed. A single latticed window cast feeble light. The great fireplace, filled with willow branches, held no fire. The gallery above was barely visible. Carved deer heads with real antlers stared down grotesquely. The house was old, rambling, impractical. Yet it held a thousand pounds a year—a fortune passed to a woman, since no sons remained. Merchant families nearby earned twice as much, but the Keeldars, by birth and title, claimed precedence.

They were shown into a parlour. Of course, in such a Gothic dwelling, it was lined with oak—dark, polished, imposing. Beautiful, yes—but oppressive. Anyone who’s seen servants scrubbing these panels in summer heat knows the horror. Yet someone had painted the drawing-room pink-white—a rebellious act, perhaps, but one that brought light and relief.

This parlour was furnished in old style—real antique furniture. On either side of the mantel stood two solid oak chairs. In one sat a woman—matronly, with auburn hair, young features, but no youth in her bearing. Her gown was fine but outdated, arranged in stiff folds. She looked eccentric.

She greeted them with English diffidence—polite, uncertain, anxious to please. Miss Helstone felt it, and sympathized. She sat quietly beside her, offering gentle conversation. The woman had a clear, soft voice—surprisingly musical for her age. Caroline liked it. They would have gotten along well—had not Mr. Helstone stood rigid on the rug, his eyes sharp, impatient, disapproving. His presence made the woman flustered. She tried to talk of the weather, the country—but he pretended not to hear. She repeated herself. Then, just as she rose nervously to search for Miss Keeldar, the younger woman appeared.

She came through a glass door from the garden—slight, erect, holding a silk apron full of flowers. She gave Mr. Helstone her hand and said pleasantly, “I knew you’d come to see me, though you *do* think Mr. Yorke has made me a Jacobin. Good morning.”

“But we won’t have you a Jacobin,” he said. “No, Miss Shirley. I’ll teach you sound doctrine—both political and religious. Now that you’re among us, you’ll be my pupil.”

She smiled. “I’ll try not to disappoint you.”

Shirley proved her sincerity in saying she would welcome Caroline’s company by seeking it often. Indeed, without such effort, she might never have had it—Miss Helstone was slow to open herself to new acquaintances. She always hesitated, convinced that others could not possibly want her, that she could not entertain them. A bright, cheerful young woman like the heiress of Fieldhead seemed to her too self-sufficient, too radiant in her own world, to ever truly value someone so quiet and unassuming as herself.

Yet Shirley might shine, and perhaps be happy, but no one is truly independent of warmth and companionship. Though she had made the acquaintance of nearly every family in the surrounding parishes within a month, and was on easy, familiar terms with the Misses Sykes, the Misses Pearson, and even the two celebrated Misses Wynne of Walden Hall, none of them seemed to stir her spirit deeply. She did not form close bonds with any of them—not really. If she had been born Shirley Keeldar, Esq., lord of Briarfield Manor, there wasn’t a single young lady in these parts whom she’d consider asking to become Mrs. Keeldar, mistress of the estate. She confessed this to Mrs. Pryor, who took it calmly, as she did most of Shirley’s bold remarks. “My dear,” she replied gently, “do not let this habit of speaking of yourself as a gentleman grow stronger. It’s unusual. Those who don’t know you might think you’re trying to imitate masculine ways.”

Shirley never mocked her former governess—even the smallest formalities or quirks of Mrs. Pryor were respected in her eyes. To laugh at her would have shown weakness; only the weak turn dignity into ridicule. So Shirley accepted the gentle reproof in silence. She stood quietly near the window, watching the great cedar tree on her lawn, where a bird perched on one of its lower branches. Then, softly, she began to chirrup. The sound grew clearer, then turned into a whistle. Soon, she was whistling a tune—sweetly, skillfully.

“My dear!” Mrs. Pryor exclaimed.

“Was I whistling?” Shirley asked, startled. “I didn’t realize. I’m sorry, ma'am. I promised myself I wouldn’t do it in front of you.”

“But Miss Keeldar, where did you learn to whistle? You must have picked it up since coming to Yorkshire. I never knew you to do it before.”

“Oh, I learned long ago.”

“Who taught you?”

“No one. I just listened and copied. I stopped for years. But yesterday evening, walking up our lane, I heard a gentleman whistling that very tune from the field beyond the hedge. That brought it back.”

“What gentleman was it?”

“We have only one gentleman hereabouts, ma'am—Mr. Moore—at least, he’s the only one who isn’t gray-haired. My two beloved elders, Mr. Helstone and Mr. Yorke, are fine old gentlemen, infinitely better than any of the dull young ones.”

Mrs. Pryor said nothing.

“You don’t care much for Mr. Helstone, do you?”

“My dear, his position protects him from criticism.”

“You usually leave the room when he arrives.”

“Are you going out today, my dear?”

“Yes. I’ll visit the rectory and find Caroline Helstone. I’ll coax her into a walk. She shall have a brisk stroll across Nunnely Common.”

“If you go that way, please remind Miss Helstone to wrap up well. There’s a fresh wind, and she seems to need care.”

“I’ll obey you exactly, Mrs. Pryor. And will you come with us?”

“No, my love. I’d only hold you back. I’m not as fleet-footed as you’d like.”

Shirley easily persuaded Caroline to join her. Once they were on the peaceful road crossing the wide, empty stretch of Nunnely Common, she drew her into conversation with ease. After the first shyness passed, Caroline found herself eager to talk with Miss Keeldar. Just a few brief exchanges told each of them what kind of person the other was.

Shirley admired the green sweep of the common and especially the heather along its ridges—it reminded her of moors. She remembered traveling near the Scottish border, journeying all afternoon through an endless sea of deep heath under a sultry, clouded sky. From noon until sunset, they saw nothing but wild sheep, heard only the cries of birds.

“I can picture how the heath would look on such a day,” Caroline said. “Purple-black—darker than the sky itself, which would be sickly pale.”

“Yes,” Shirley agreed. “Quite livid, with brass-edged clouds, and here and there a flash of white, more chilling than the red glow—so sharp you’d expect it to burst into lightning.”

“Did it thunder?”

“It rumbled far off, but the storm broke only after we reached our inn—an isolated house at the foot of mountains.”

“Did you watch the clouds roll over the hills?”

“I did. I stood at the window for an hour. The mountains looked wrapped in sullen mist. When the rain came down in sheets, they vanished entirely—washed away from sight.”

“I’ve seen storms like that in Yorkshire’s hills. At their peak, when the sky was all waterfalls and the earth flooded, I’ve thought of the Deluge.”

“It’s oddly refreshing afterward—when calm returns and sunlight breaks through the clouds, soft and comforting, reminding you the sun hasn’t died.”

“Miss Keeldar, stop now and look down at Nunnely Dale and the wood.”

They paused on the green slope. Below lay the deep valley dressed in May’s greenery—meadows dotted with daisies and golden king-cups. The light danced across the land in emerald and amber hues. Nunnwood—the last remnant of ancient British forest in a region once covered in woodland—rested beneath a shadow. Distant hills shimmered in silvery blues, soft purples, fleeting greens and rose tints, melting into fleecy white clouds as pure as snow. The air was crisp, sweet, and invigorating.

“Our England is a lovely island,” Shirley said. “And Yorkshire is one of its loveliest corners.”

“You’re a Yorkshire girl too?”

“I am—by blood and birth. Five generations of my family rest beneath the arches of Briarfield Church. I drew my first breath in the old black hall behind us.”

At that, Caroline offered her hand. Shirley took it, and they shook. “We are countrywomen,” she said.

“Yes,” Shirley nodded solemnly.

“And that,” Miss Keeldar asked, pointing to the forest, “is Nunnwood?”

“It is.”

“Have you ever been there?”

“Many times.”

“To the heart of it?”

“Yes.”

“What’s it like?”

“It feels like an encampment of giant sons of Anak. The trees are immense and ancient. Standing at their roots, their crowns seem to belong to another world. The trunks stand firm like pillars, while the branches sway with every breeze. Even in stillness, the leaves whisper; in high wind, it sounds like a river rushing, a sea roaring above you.”

“Was it one of Robin Hood’s hiding places?”

“Yes. And traces remain. To enter Nunnwood is to step back into the dim past. Can you see a break in the trees, near the center?”

“Yes, clearly.”

“That’s a hollow—a deep cup lined with grass as short and green as this common. The oldest oaks, twisted and mighty, crowd the edge. In the bottom lie the ruins of a nunnery.”

“We’ll go—you and I alone, Caroline—to that wood one bright summer morning. We’ll spend the whole day there. We can bring pencils, sketchbooks, books we enjoy, and food. I have two small baskets—Mrs. Gill could pack our provisions—and we can carry our own. Won’t it tire you?”

“Oh no. Especially if we rest all day. I know every lovely spot. I know where to find nuts in autumn, where wild strawberries grow. I know hidden glades, untouched by feet, carpeted with strange mosses—some golden like gilding, some gray, some gem-green. I know clusters of trees that delight the eye: rough oaks, delicate birches, glossy beeches, all contrasted; ash trees tall as Saul, standing alone; ancient giants draped in ivy. I could show you.”

“You wouldn’t find me dull?”

“I wouldn’t. I think we’d suit. And who else, of our age, could join us without spoiling the joy?”

“Indeed, I know no lady nearby. As for gentlemen…”

“An outing changes completely when gentlemen are involved,” Caroline interrupted.

“I agree. It becomes something different altogether.”

“We meant simply to see the old trees, the old ruins—live a day in the past, surrounded by silence and peace.”

“You’re right. Gentlemen ruin that charm. If they’re the wrong sort—like your Malones, your young Sykes, your Wynnes—irritation replaces serenity. Even if they’re good men, there’s still a shift—hard to name, easier to feel.”

“We forget Nature, _imprimis_.”

“And Nature forgets us. She veils her calm face, hides her beauty, and withdraws the quiet joy she’d give us—if we’d only worship her alone.”

“What does she give instead?”

“More excitement, more anxiety. Time flies, and trouble stirs the hours.”

“Our ability to be happy depends largely on ourselves,” Caroline said wisely. “I’ve gone to Nunnwood with large groups—curates, gentry, ladies—and found it tedious. But I’ve gone alone, or with Fanny, who stayed in the woodman’s hut sewing or chatting with the wife, while I wandered, sketched, read. I’ve known real peace. But that was two years ago.”

“Have you ever gone with your cousin Robert Moore?”

“Yes. Once.”

“What’s he like as a companion?”

“A cousin is different from a stranger.”

“I know. But cousins, if dull, are worse than strangers—because you can’t easily avoid them. But your cousin isn’t dull?”

“No. But…”

“Well?”

“If foolish company irritates, wise company brings its own ache. When a friend’s goodness or talent is beyond question, you begin to doubt whether you deserve to be near him.”

“Oh! I can’t follow that. Such thoughts don’t appeal to me. I believe I’m worthy to be the companion of the finest men—gentlemen, I mean—though that’s saying much. Where they’re good, they’re very good. Your uncle, by the way, is a decent example of an older gentleman. I always enjoy seeing his keen, sensible face—whether in my home or elsewhere. Do you love him? Is he kind to you? Now, speak honestly.”

“He raised me from childhood, no doubt, as he would have done his own daughter. That’s kindness. But I don’t love him. I’d rather be away from him than near.”

“Strange, when he seems so pleasant.”

“Yes, in public. But at home, he’s stern and silent. He puts away his cane and hat in the hall—as if he locks away his cheerfulness in his study. The brow knits, words are brief by the fire; smiles, jests, wit—reserved for company.”

“Is he tyrannical?”

“Not at all. He’s neither tyrant nor hypocrite. He’s simply a man more liberal than kind, more brilliant than warm, more scrupulously fair than truly just—if you can grasp such subtle differences.”

“Oh yes. Good-nature implies indulgence—he lacks that. Geniality means warmth—he doesn’t possess it. True justice comes from sympathy, which I imagine your bronzed old friend lacks entirely.”

“I often wonder, Shirley, if most men are like my uncle at home. Do they need strangeness to seem likable? Is it impossible for them to keep affection alive for those they see daily?”

“I don’t know. I ponder the same questions. But let me tell you a secret: if I believed men were inherently fickle—soon cold, unfeeling—I’d never marry. I couldn’t bear to discover that what I loved didn’t love me, that it was tired of me, that no effort would matter, because change is in their nature. Once I knew that, what would I desire? To leave, to escape from a presence where my company brought no joy.”

“But you couldn’t leave if married.”

“No. That’s the horror. I’d lose my freedom forever. A terrible thought—it chokes me! Nothing burdens me more than the idea of being a burden, a bore—inevitable, endless. Now, when I feel unwanted, I can wrap myself in independence like a cloak, lower my pride like a veil, and retreat to solitude. Married, I couldn’t.”

“I wonder we don’t all decide to stay single,” Caroline said. “We would, if we listened to experience. My uncle always calls marriage a burden. He sees every man who marries as a fool—or at least doing a foolish thing.”

“But not all men are like your uncle. Surely not.”

She paused, thoughtful.

“I suppose we each find an exception—in the one we love—until we’re married,” Caroline suggested.

“I suppose so. And we believe that exception is made of true worth. We imagine harmony. We hear his voice and trust it speaks a heart that won’t harden. We see in his eyes a faithful love. I don’t trust passion, Caroline. I think it’s just dry tinder—blazing fast, dying quick. But we watch him—kind to animals, children, the poor. Kind to us. Patient with women, comfortable in their presence. He likes them not for vanity, but because he likes them—just as we like him. We see he’s honest, just, conscientious. We feel joy when he enters a room, sadness when he leaves. We know he was a kind son, a kind brother. Would anyone dare say he won’t be a kind husband?”

“My uncle would say yes—‘He’ll be sick of you in a month.’”

“Mrs. Pryor would hint the same.”

“Mrs. Yorke and Miss Mann would suggest it darkly.”

“If they’re true prophets, it’s best never to fall in love.”

“Very true—if you can avoid it.”

“I choose to doubt them.”

“I fear that proves you’re already caught.”

“Not I. But if I were, whose advice would I seek?”

“Tell me.”

“Neither man nor woman, young nor old: the barefoot Irish beggar who knocks at my door; the mouse that slips from the wall; the bird that pecks at my window in winter; the dog who licks my hand and rests beside my knee.”

“Have you ever seen anyone kind to such creatures?”

“Have you ever seen creatures drawn to someone instinctively—following, trusting, relying?”

“We have a black cat and an old dog at the rectory. I know someone the cat loves to climb onto, who purred against his shoulder. The dog wags and whines whenever he passes.”

“And what does he do?”

“He strokes the cat gently, lets her sit as long as possible. When he must rise, he sets her down softly, never throws her off. He whistles to the dog, gives him a pat.”

“He does? It’s not Robert?”

“It is Robert.”

“Handsome fellow!” Shirley said, her eyes shining.

“Isn’t he? Fine eyes, strong features, noble forehead?”

“He has all that. Bless him—he’s both graceful and good.”

“I knew you’d see it. When I first saw your face, I knew you would.”

“I liked him before I met him. Liked him when I saw him. Admire him now. Beauty has charm in itself, Caroline—but when joined with goodness, it’s powerful.”

“When mind is added, Shirley?”

“Who can resist it?”

“Remember my uncle, Mrs. Pryor, Mrs. Yorke, Miss Mann.”

“Remember the frogs of Egypt. He’s noble. When men are good, they are lords of creation—sons of God. Made in His image, even the smallest spark of His spirit lifts them almost above death. Undeniably, a great, good, handsome man is the finest thing created.”

“Above us?”

“I’d scorn to claim equality with him. Should my left hand challenge my right? My heart quarrel with my pulse? My veins resent the blood within?”

“Men and women, husbands and wives, quarrel terribly, Shirley.”

“Poor things. Poor, fallen, broken things. God made them for higher purpose, deeper feelings.”

“But are we equal to men—or not?”

“Nothing charms me more than meeting my superior—one who makes me feel he truly is.”

“Have you ever met him?”

“I’d welcome him any day. The higher, the better. To stoop is degrading; to look up is glorious. What troubles me is that when I try to admire, I’m thwarted. When I feel spiritual, I find only false gods to worship. I refuse to be a pagan.”

“Miss Keeldar, shall we go in? We’re at the rectory gates.”

“Not today. But tomorrow, I’ll come for you. Caroline Helstone, if you’re truly as you seem, we’ll suit. I’ve never talked to a young woman as freely as I have with you today. Kiss me—and goodbye.”

* * * * *

Mrs. Pryor, too, seemed inclined to foster a friendship with Caroline. She, who rarely visited anywhere, called early one afternoon at the rectory. The rector was out. It was a hot day, and the warmth had flushed her cheeks. She appeared unsettled, perhaps unnerved by entering a new house—her habits were quiet and secluded. When Caroline found her in the dining room, she sat trembling on the sofa, fanning herself with her handkerchief, struggling with a nervous tremor that threatened to become full-blown distress.

Caroline was surprised—this was unlike a woman of her age and strength. Mrs. Pryor quickly blamed the heat, the walk, the exertion. Yet, as she repeated these excuses in haste and confusion, Caroline gently helped her—unfolding her shawl, removing her bonnet. Such attentions, Mrs. Pryor would not accept from everyone. Usually, she recoiled from touch, from closeness, with a mix of embarrassment and coldness that discouraged kindness. But with Miss Helstone’s light hand, she yielded peacefully, and seemed calmed by the contact. Within minutes, the trembling ceased, and she regained composure.

Once settled, she resumed conversation. In mixed company, Mrs. Pryor seldom spoke. If she did, it was constrained, and thus not effective. But in dialogue, she was articulate and thoughtful. Her language was precise, her ideas just, her knowledge broad and accurate. Caroline found it pleasing to listen—more so than she expected.

On the wall opposite the sofa hung three portraits: a woman in the center, above the mantel, flanked by two men.

“That’s a beautiful face,” Mrs. Pryor said, breaking a brief silence after half an hour of lively talk. “The features are perfect—no sculptor could improve them. It’s a portrait from life, I assume?”

“It’s Mrs. Helstone.”

“Mrs. Matthewson Helstone? Your uncle’s wife?”

“Yes. It’s said to be a good likeness. Before her marriage, she was considered the district’s greatest beauty.”

“I’d say she earned the title. Every line is exact. Still… it’s a passive face. The original couldn’t have been what people call ‘a woman of spirit.’”

“She was remarkably quiet and reserved.”

“One would scarcely expect, my dear, that your uncle would choose such a partner. Doesn’t he enjoy lively conversation?”

“In company, yes. But he says he could never manage a talkative wife. He needs peace at home. You go out to chat, he says; you come home to read and reflect.”

“Mrs. Matthewson lived only a few years after her marriage, I believe?”

“About five.”

“Well, my dear,” Mrs. Pryor said, rising to leave, “I hope it’s understood that you’ll visit Fieldhead often. I hope you will. You must feel lonely here, with no female relative nearby. You must spend much time alone.”

“I’m used to it. I’ve grown up by myself. May I help you with your shawl?”

Mrs. Pryor allowed it.

“If you need help with your studies,” she said, “you may command me.”

There were moments when Shirley allowed herself to drift into a gentle stillness, as though the world had paused just for her. She found joy in doing nothing—no task to complete, no thought to chase. In those quiet hours, simply being alive was enough: the sky above, the earth beneath, the rustle of leaves, the hum of bees—all woven together into a tapestry of peace. Often, after a busy morning, she would lie beneath a broad tree, letting the sun warm her face and the breeze stir her hair. She needed no one near but Caroline; even that was not necessary—only knowing she could call if she wished. The sky, vast and blue, held clouds like drifting dreams; the silence was rich with life. Her only books were memory and hope—two volumes she read with eyes brightened by wonder, her lips curling now and then in soft smiles, as if catching glimpses of stories yet to unfold. There was no sorrow in her heart, no shadow on her mind. Fate had smiled upon her, and she believed it would continue to do so. Her past was sweet, her future glowing with promise.

Yet one afternoon, when Caroline approached to rouse her from her repose, she found Shirley’s cheek glistening with tears—fresh as dew. Her eyes shimmered, brimming with unshed emotion.

"Shirley," Caroline whispered, the word “you” emphasized with sudden concern, "why are you crying?"

Miss Keeldar lifted her head, her expression calm, almost amused. "Because I find it deeply satisfying," she replied. "My heart is both heavy and light at once. But why don’t you join me? I shed only joyful tears—easy to wipe away. You might weep bitterness, if you chose."

"Why should I weep bitterness?"

"Mateless, solitary bird," Shirley murmured, half to herself.

"And aren’t you too, Shirley?"

"At heart—no."

"Oh! Then who dwells there?"

Shirley laughed—a clear, sparkling sound—and sprang up. "I’ve been dreaming," she said. "Just a daydream—bright, perhaps, but likely baseless."

* * * * *

Miss Helstone, meanwhile, had long since abandoned illusions. She faced the future with sober clarity, sensing the path her own life and others’ were taking. Yet old habits clung to her—especially the evening ritual of walking to the field-style and the ancient thorn tree overlooking the Hollow. That night, the night after the mysterious note, she stood as usual, waiting for the beacon she once trusted. But no lamp lit the hillside. She waited until stars rose high, signaling the hour had passed. On her way back, moonlight caught her attention—Fieldhead glowed under the silver orb. Trees and halls stood serene, bathed in pearly light; shadows deepened around the oak-wreathed roof, and the stone pavement gleamed like polished marble. Two dark figures cast sharp silhouettes on the ground—still at first, then moving slowly, speaking in low tones.

Caroline watched them emerge from behind the cedar. "Is that Mrs. Pryor and Shirley?"

It was Shirley—there was no mistaking her slender, proud figure, or the grace with which she walked. Her face was visible—thoughtful, playful, tender, all at once. Her curls tumbled freely, framing her neck and shoulders. A gold chain sparkled through the folds of her scarf, and a large gem glittered on her white hand. Yes—definitely Shirley.

And beside her—was it Mrs. Pryor?

No. The figure was tall, young, stately—a man. Robert Moore, her tenant.

They spoke softly, their words lost in the night. To linger was not spying, not when the moon revealed every detail. And Caroline could not help staying.

She remembered how, in summer nights past, Moore had walked with her—holding her hand, sharing quiet talks beneath the stars. He had never touched Shirley like that. Not yet. And yet Shirley was not haughty when she loved. There was dignity in her posture, yes—but no arrogance. Robert must see her beauty clearly, with a man’s gaze. She had fire in her eyes—soft, generous, magnetic. She smiled—what stirred that smile? Caroline saw Robert respond, drawn in by it. They looked like spirits bound together, walking on a shore beyond death’s flood. And she—she stood in shadow, hidden, her mind darker than her hiding place. What was she? A mortal, burdened, asking why she lived, what purpose she served. Her thoughts circled endlessly: how would she face death? Who would stand beside her?

"This is the hardest part I’ve reached," she thought. "But I knew it would come. I gave Robert up the moment I saw Shirley—rich, young, radiant. She has him now. He loves her. She will be his darling, more so when they marry. The more he knows her, the deeper he’ll love her. They will be happy. I don’t begrudge them happiness—but I suffer. Some pain is unbearable. Truly, I wish I had never been born. They should have silenced me at birth."

As Shirley stepped aside to pick a dew-damp flower, the pair turned toward a path near the gate. A few words drifted to Caroline. She didn’t stay to listen. She slipped away quietly, and the moon kissed the wall where her shadow had lingered. But the reader may remain—and hear what passed between them.

"I can’t understand why nature didn’t give you a bulldog’s head," Shirley said. "You have all its tenacity."

"Not a kind comparison. Am I so low?"

"And something of its silent ways. You move without warning, strike without noise, hold fast."

"You’re guessing. I’ve done no such thing before you."

"Your silence speaks for you. How little you say—yet how much you plan. You’re far-seeing, calculating."

"I know these people. I’ve gathered their intentions. My note last night told you Barraclough was convicted and sentenced. His allies will seek revenge. I’ll prepare for them. That’s all. Now—do you approve?"

"I’ll stand with you—as long as you’re defending, not attacking. Yes."

"Good. Even without your support, I’d act this way—but differently. Now I feel satisfied. I enjoy this role."

"I suppose you do. It shows. You relish this work more than making army cloth."

"It feels right."

"So would old Helstone. True, your motives differ—perhaps greatly. Should I speak to Mr. Helstone? If you wish."

"Do as you please. Your judgment is sound. I’d trust it in worse crises. Though I warn you—he’s biased against me."

"I know. But it will fade. He can’t resist an alliance like this."

"I’d welcome him. He’s made of true stuff."

"I think so too."

"An old blade—rusty, but still sharp."

"Well, you shall have him, Mr. Moore—if I can win him."

"Who can’t you win?"

"Perhaps not the rector. But I’ll try."

"Effort? He’ll yield to a word—a smile."

"No. It will take tea, toast, cake, and endless persuasion. It’s growing cold."

"I see you shiver. Am I keeping you too long?"

"It’s calm—almost warm. And your company is rare pleasure. If you wore a thicker shawl—"

"I might stay longer. But it’s late. Mrs. Pryor would chide. We keep early hours here. I’m sure your sister does too."

"Yes. Hortense and I agree—we each do as we please."

"How do you please?"

"Three nights a week I sleep at the mill. I need little rest. When it’s moonlit, I often walk the Hollow till dawn."

"When I was a child, my nurse told me fairies appeared in that Hollow. Before the mill was built, it was wild and lonely. You’ll fall under enchantment."

"I fear it’s already done," Moore said softly.

"But there are worse things than fairies to beware of," Shirley added.

"More dangerous ones," he replied.

"Far more so. For instance—what if you met Michael Hartley, that mad Calvinist weaver? They say he poaches, walks the woods at night with a gun."

"I’ve already met him. We argued one night. Strange little incident—it intrigued me."

"Intrigued you? I admire your taste! Michael isn’t sane."

"His madness holds a wild charm. He’d be half-poet if not wholly mad—perhaps prophet, if not profligate. He told me hell was foreordained for me. He saw the mark of the beast on my brow. Said I’d been an outcast from the start. God’s vengeance was preparing—through a vision, he saw my doom. I asked more—but he left with, 'The end is not yet.'"

"Have you seen him since?"

"About a month later, returning from market, I found him and Barraclough, both drunk, praying wildly by the roadside. They called me Satan, demanded deliverance. Recently, Michael came to the counting-house—hatless, shirt-sleeved—his coat pledged. He told me to set my house in order. My soul, he said, would soon be required."

"Do you take it lightly?"

"He’d been drinking for weeks—bordering on delirium tremens."

"Then he might act on his prophecies."

"We mustn’t let such things unsettle us."

"Mr. Moore, go home!"

"So soon?"

"Go straight down the fields—not by the lade."

"It’s early."

"It’s late. I’m going in. Promise not to wander in the Hollow tonight?"

"If you wish."

"I do. May I ask—do you think life is worthless?"

"Not at all. On the contrary—I value it more than ever."

"Recently?"

"Existence once felt aimless, hopeless. Three months ago, I was drowning—wishing it over. Then a hand reached out—so delicate I barely dared trust it. But its strength saved me."

"Are you truly saved?"

"For now. Your help gave me another chance."

"Then live well. Don’t offer yourself to Michael Hartley. Goodnight."

* * * * *

The next day, Miss Helstone had promised to spend the evening at Fieldhead. She kept her word, though the hours leading up to it had been heavy with sorrow. Most of the time, she stayed locked in her room, leaving only for meals. Fanny, curious, asked about her plans. Caroline lied: "I’m busy altering a dress. Prefer sewing upstairs to avoid interruptions."

She did sew. Needle flew, thread pulled, fabric stitched—her hands moved without pause. But her mind raced faster. Again, she craved purpose—any purpose, however harsh. She would talk to Mrs. Pryor, then her uncle. Her brain labored as hard as her fingers, weaving plans while stitching muslin. Tears sometimes welled—quickly wiped away. The pain passed. She re-threaded her needle, adjusted the trim, and worked on.

Late in the afternoon, she dressed and arrived at Fieldhead just as tea was served. Shirley noticed her tardiness.

"Why so late?" she asked.

"Making my dress," Caroline replied. "These sunny days made me ashamed of my winter wool. So I refreshed a lighter one."

"You look lovely," Shirley said. "You’re a lady-like little person, Caroline. Isn’t she, Mrs. Pryor?"

Mrs. Pryor rarely praised appearance. This time, she brushed Caroline’s curls aside, touched her cheek gently, and said, "You’re getting thin, my dear. Pale. Do you sleep well? Your eyes look weary."

"I dream troubling dreams," Caroline confessed. "When I wake in the night, I think of the rectory—how dreary it is. It’s near the churchyard. The back is ancient—some say the kitchens were once inside the graveyard. Graves lie beneath. I want to leave."

"My dear, you’re not superstitious?"

"No. But I feel… nervous. Things seem darker. I fear omens, disasters. A weight presses on me—unbearable. I’d give anything to shake it off. I can’t."

"Strange," Shirley said. "I never feel that way." Mrs. Pryor remained silent.

"Sunshine, pleasant days—they bring me no joy," Caroline continued. "Calm evenings feel restless. Moonlight, once mild, now looks mournful. Is this weakness, Mrs. Pryor? I struggle. I reason. But nothing helps."

"Exercise," Mrs. Pryor suggested.

"Exercise? I exercise until I collapse."

"My dear, you should leave home."

"I would, Mrs. Pryor—but not aimlessly. I want to be a governess, like you. Would you speak to my uncle about it?"

"Nonsense!" Shirley burst in. "Be a governess? Better be a slave. Why dream of such pain?"

"My dear," Mrs. Pryor said gently, "you’re too young, not strong enough. Governesses endure hard duties."

"And I believe I need hard duties—to occupy me."

"Occupy you?" Shirley exclaimed. "When are you idle? I’ve never seen a girl so industrious. Come—sit by me. Have some tea. Eat something. Laugh. Stay home."

Caroline shook her head, sighed. She knew how hard it would be to convince anyone to help her change her life. If only she could follow her own judgment—she believed she could find a cure, harsh but effective. But this judgment, rooted in secrets she couldn’t explain—even to Shirley—seemed absurd to others. They saw no need. Her uncle might provide for her. Their logic was sound, but they didn’t understand her torment—her sleepless nights, her bleak days. Explanation was impossible. Endurance was her only choice. Many who lacked food and clothes lived brighter lives than she.

"Now, is your mind calmer?" Shirley asked. "Will you stay?"

"I won’t leave against my friends’ wishes," Caroline replied. "But I believe they’ll come to see my view."

Mrs. Pryor looked uneasy. Her usual reserve kept her from speaking plainly. Alone, she might have said more. But Shirley’s presence silenced her. She showed concern indirectly—asking if the fire was too warm, placing a screen, closing a window, watching Caroline closely.

Shirley resumed: "Having destroyed your plan—thankfully—I’ll create a new one. Every summer, I take a trip. This year, I propose two months at the Scottish lochs or English lakes—provided you come with me. If not, I won’t go."

"You’re very kind, Shirley."

"Drink tea. Eat. Laugh. Stay home."

"Of course, he’ll marry Shirley," Caroline murmured to herself as she rose that morning. "And he should—she can help him in ways I never could." But the thought that followed was sharp and cold: "Yet when they are married… I will be forgotten. Truly forgotten. And what then? What will become of me when Robert is no longer mine? Where shall I turn? _My_ Robert—I wish I could say those words without guilt. But I am nothing but poverty and weakness; she has wealth, power, beauty, and love. I cannot deny it. This isn’t a petty rivalry. She loves him—not with lesser feelings, but with a depth that makes him proud to be loved. There’s no fault to find. Let them wed. But after that… I shall mean nothing to him. To pretend to be his sister? That would be a lie I could not bear. I want either everything or nothing from a man like Robert. No half-measures, no false kindness. Once they are united, I will leave them. I cannot stay, playing at friendship while my heart bleeds. I couldn’t be their friend any more than their enemy. I couldn’t stand between them—or crush them beneath me. Robert is a great man, in my eyes. I have loved him, I do love him, I must love him. If I could be his wife, I would. Since I cannot, I must go where I will never see him again. There is only one choice—to cling to him as if I were part of him, or to be torn from him as far as the poles are apart. So tear us apart, Providence. Do it quickly."

These thoughts swirled through her mind late in the afternoon when she saw a familiar figure pass by the parlour window. Miss Keeldar strolled past, her walk slow, her expression a blend of quiet longing and careless ease—the look she wore when still, but which, when stirred, gave way to warmth and charm, so that her laughter and smile carried a tender sentiment that made mirth feel like something pure and alive, not hollow or mocking.

"What do you mean by not coming to see me this afternoon, as you promised?" Shirley asked briskly upon entering.

"I wasn’t in the mood," Caroline replied honestly.

Shirley had already fixed her with an unnerving gaze.

"No," she said, "I can see you’re not in the mood for liking me. You’re in one of your shadowed, stormy moods—when even another person’s presence feels like an intrusion. You have such moods. Do you know it?"

"Do you intend to stay long, Shirley?"

"Yes. I’ve come for tea—and I’ll have it before I leave. So I’m going to take off my bonnet without asking."

She did so, standing on the rug with her hands behind her back.

"You’ve a peculiar expression on your face," Shirley continued, watching her closely, though not harshly—more with pity than judgment. "You look wonderfully self-contained, like a deer wounded in solitude, afraid I’ll discover you’re hurt and bleeding."

"I’ve never feared you."

"But sometimes you dislike me. Often you avoid me. Shirley can feel when she’s being slighted. If you hadn’t walked home with him last night, you’d be a different woman today. What time did you arrive at the rectory?"

"By ten."

"Humph. It took you three-quarters of an hour to walk a mile. Was it you, or Moore, who lingered?"

"Shirley, you’re talking nonsense."

"_He_ talked nonsense—though I doubt it. Or he looked it, which is worse. I can see his reflection in your eyes right now. I’m tempted to challenge him—if only I could find a trustworthy second. I’m furious. I was last night, and I’ve been all day."

"You don’t ask why," Shirley went on after a pause, "you little silent, overly modest creature. And you don’t deserve that I pour out my secrets without invitation. Honestly, I could have followed Moore yesterday evening with deadly intent. I have pistols—and I know how to use them."

"Nonsense, Shirley! Who would you shoot—me or Robert?"

"Neither, perhaps. Maybe myself. More likely a bat or a branch. Your cousin is a puppy—a quiet, serious, sensible, ambitious puppy. I see him now, standing before me, speaking his half-stern, half-gentle words, pressing his purpose down on me, as I know he does. And yet—I have no patience with him!"

Miss Keeldar began pacing the room, repeating with fervor that she had no patience with men—especially her tenant.

"You’re wrong," Caroline insisted, anxious. "Robert isn’t a puppy or a flirt. I can vouch for that."

"_You_ vouch for it? Do you think I’ll accept your word on this? There’s no testimony I’d trust less than yours. For Moore’s sake, you’d cut off your own hand."

"But not lie. And if I speak truth, I must say he was merely civil to me last night—that was all."

"I never asked what he was. I can guess. I saw him from the window take your hand in his long fingers just as he left my gate."

"That means nothing. I’m not a stranger. I’m an old acquaintance—his cousin."

"I’m indignant," Shirley declared. "That’s all there is to it. My peace is shattered by his meddling. He keeps inserting himself between you and me. Without him, we’d be friends. But this six-foot puppy casts a constant eclipse over our friendship. Again and again, he crosses my path, blinding my light. He turns me into a bore, a nuisance in your eyes."

"No, Shirley, no."

"He does. You didn’t want me this afternoon, and I feel it deeply. You’re naturally reserved, but I’m social—unable to live alone. If we were left undisturbed, I’d cherish you enough to sit beside you forever, never wishing to be rid of you. But you can’t say the same about me."

"Shirley, I can say anything you wish. I like you."

"You’ll miss me tomorrow, Lina."

"I won’t. I grow more fond of you every day. I’m too English to rush into deep friendships overnight—but you’re so much better than most young women. You’re unlike anyone else. I value you, I respect you—you’re never a burden. Do you believe me?"

"Partly," Shirley replied, smiling slightly, unconvinced. "But you’re unusual. Quiet as you seem, there’s strength and depth inside you—hard to reach, hard to understand. Still, you’re clearly unhappy."

"And unhappy people are rarely good," Caroline ventured.

"Not at all. I mean that unhappy people are often distracted, not in the mood for conversation with someone like me. Worse, some unhappiness doesn’t just weigh down—it eats away. And I fear that’s yours. Will pity help, Lina? If it will, take some from me. I offer plenty—and it’s genuine."

"Shirley, I never had a sister—you never had one. But it strikes me now how sisters feel—affection woven into life, unshaken by shocks, crushed only briefly by quarrels, springing back stronger when the pressure lifts. Affection that no passion can truly surpass—even love itself can only compete. Love hurts, Shirley. It tears us apart, burns our strength. But affection gives comfort, not fire. When you’re near—only you—my soul is calm, soothed. Do you believe me now?"

"I always believe when the idea pleases me. Then we really are friends, Lina, despite the black eclipse?"

"We really are," Caroline answered, drawing Shirley toward her and making her sit. "No matter what happens."

"Then let’s talk about something else than the Troubler." But at that moment the rector entered, and the subject was dropped until Shirley’s departure. As she lingered in the hall, she turned to Caroline.

"Caroline, I need to tell you—I carry a heavy weight. My conscience troubles me, as if I’ve committed or am about to commit a crime. Not my private conscience—but my duty as landowner, as lord of the manor. I’ve fallen under the grip of an eagle with iron claws. A stern influence I don’t fully approve, but cannot resist. Something will happen soon—something I deeply regret. To ease my mind and prevent harm, I mean to begin a series of good works. Don’t be surprised if I suddenly become wildly charitable. I don’t know how to start—but you must advise me. We’ll talk more tomorrow. And please invite Miss Ainley up to Fieldhead. I have an idea of placing myself under her guidance. Won’t she have a precious pupil? Drop her a hint, Lina, that though well-meaning, I’m rather neglected—so she won’t be shocked by my ignorance of clothing societies and such things."

The next day, Caroline found Shirley seated solemnly at her desk, surrounded by an account book, banknotes, and a full purse. She looked grave, yet puzzled.

"I’ve been reviewing our weekly household expenses," Shirley explained. "Trying to find where I might cut back. I also just spoke with Mrs. Gill, the cook. I sent her away convinced I’m losing my mind. I lectured her on thrift in a way entirely new to her. I was so eloquent on economy that I surprised myself—for this is a fresh idea. I’ve never thought or spoken on it before. But it’s all theory. In practice, I couldn’t reduce a single pound of butter, nor investigate the fate of dripping, lard, bread, cold meat, or any kitchen item. We don’t hold illuminations at Fieldhead, but I couldn’t ask about unexplained pounds of candles. We don’t wash for the parish, yet I silently noted entries of soap and bleaching powder that would satisfy any curious investigator. None of us are carnivores—Mrs. Pryor, Mrs. Gill, or I—but I only blinked and widened my eyes when I saw butcher bills whose numbers seemed to prove otherwise—falsehood, I mean. Caroline, you may laugh, but you can’t change me. I’m weak in certain areas. I feel it. There’s a base alloy of moral cowardice in me. I blushed and lowered my head before Mrs. Gill—when she should have been confessing to me. I couldn’t summon the courage even to suggest, let alone prove, that she was cheating. I lack calm dignity, true courage."

"Shirley, what is this sudden self-criticism? My uncle, who rarely speaks kindly of women, says there aren’t ten thousand men in England as genuinely fearless as you."

"I’m fearless physically. I’m never nervous about danger. I wasn’t shaken when Mr. Wynne’s great red bull charged at me, bellowing, head lowered, across the cowslip field. But I was afraid of seeing Mrs. Gill humiliated. You, whom no argument can persuade to pass a bull, however quiet, would have firmly shown my housekeeper her mistake, gently corrected her, and forgiven her if she repented. I couldn’t do that. Yet, despite my flaws, I still live within our means. I have money—and I must use it wisely. The poor of Briarfield are suffering. They must be helped. What should I do, Lina? Should I distribute the cash at once?"

"No, Shirley. You’ll mismanage it. I’ve noticed your charity is usually random—giving shillings and half-crowns carelessly, which invites abuse. You need a guide. You mentioned Miss Ainley yourself. I’ll speak to her. Until then, promise not to spend a penny. How much you have, Shirley! You must feel very rich."

"Yes. I feel important. It’s not a fortune, but I feel responsible for it—and that responsibility weighs more than I expected. They say families in Briarfield are nearly starving. Some of my own tenants are in terrible straits. I must help them."

"Some say we shouldn’t give alms to the poor, Shirley."

"They’re fools. For those who aren’t hungry, it’s easy to talk about the shame of charity. But they forget how short and bitter life is. None of us lives long. Let’s help each other through hardship, without caring for the empty philosophy of others."

"But you already help, Shirley. You give generously."

"Not enough. I must give more—or one day, my brother’s blood will cry out against me. After all, if political agitators come here and attack my property, I’ll defend it like a tigress. I know I will. Let me listen to Mercy while she’s near. Her voice drowned by shouts of defiance, and I’ll be filled with rage to resist. If the poor rise in mob form, I’ll turn against them as an aristocrat. If they bully me, I’ll defy them. If they attack, I’ll fight—and win."

"You speak like Robert."

"I feel like Robert—only fiercer. Let them touch Robert, his mill, his interests, and I’ll hate them. Right now, I’m not a noblewoman, nor do I see the poor as inferiors. But if they violently wrong me or mine—and then demand to dictate to us—I’ll forget pity and respect, lost in scorn for their ignorance and fury at their insolence."

"Shirley, how your eyes blaze!"

"Because my soul burns. Would you, any more than I, let Robert be overwhelmed by numbers?"

"If I had your power to aid Robert, I would use it as you plan. If I could be such a friend to him as you can, I’d stand by him—until death."

"And now, Lina, though your eyes don’t flash, they glow. You lower your lashes, but I saw a spark. Still, it’s not time for conflict. What I want is to _prevent_ it. I cannot forget—day or night—that these bitter feelings stem from suffering. The poor wouldn’t hate or envy us if they didn’t believe we’re happier. To ease their pain, and thus lessen their hatred, I’ll give freely from my abundance. And to make it count, I’ll give wisely. We must bring clear, calm sense to our efforts. Go fetch Miss Ainley."

Without a word, Caroline put on her bonnet and left. It may seem odd that neither she nor Shirley consulted Mrs. Pryor—yet they knew instinctively it would only cause her distress. Mrs. Pryor was wiser, better read, deeper-thinking than Miss Ainley. But she lacked administrative drive. She’d gladly give her small share in secret—but not lead public initiatives. She couldn’t originate plans. Shirley knew this. So she spared her the discomfort of useless discussions that would only highlight her limitations.

It was a bright day for Miss Ainley when summoned to Fieldhead to shape a plan so close to her heart. Seated with honor at a table, pen, paper, ink, and—most thrilling—actual funds before her, she was asked to design a practical scheme to relieve Briarfield’s destitute. She knew them all, studied their needs, and had long known how best to help—had the means existed. Now, she felt capable, and a quiet joy warmed her kind heart as she answered the girls’ questions with clarity and confidence, showing how deeply she understood her neighbors.

Shirley placed £300 at her disposal. At sight of the money, Miss Ainley’s eyes filled with tears—she already saw the hungry fed, the naked clothed, the sick comforted. She swiftly drafted a simple, thoughtful plan. She assured them brighter days would follow, for she believed the lady of Fieldhead’s example would inspire others. She’d seek additional donations and build a fund—but first, she must consult the clergy. On this point, she was firm: Mr. Helstone, Dr. Boultby, Mr. Hall—must be consulted. Not only Briarfield, but Whinbury and Nunnely must be included. It would be presumption, she said, to act without their approval.

To Miss Ainley, the clergy were sacred. No matter how insignificant the individual, his office made him holy. Even the curates—whom she privately considered too trivial to tie her patten-strings—she saw as budding saints. No matter how obvious their faults, she remained blind to them. The white surplice covered a multitude of sins.

Shirley, aware of this innocent devotion, insisted that the curates have no say in the funds. Their interference must be prevented. The rectors, of course, were paramount—they had experience, wisdom, and Mr. Hall, at least, had compassion. But the younger men? They must be kept in place, taught silence and subordination.

Miss Ainley heard this with horror. But Caroline stepped in, gently praising Mr. Sweeting, calming her. Sweeting was indeed her favorite. She tried to respect Messrs. Malone and Donne, but the sponge cake and cowslip wine she offered Sweeting during visits came with maternal affection. She once served the same to Malone—he scorned it, and she never repeated it. To Donne, she always offered it—and was delighted when he ate two pieces and tucked a third into his pocket.

Eager to act, Miss Ainley wanted to walk ten miles to visit the rectors immediately. But Shirley stopped her, suggesting instead a gathering that evening at Fieldhead. The clergy would meet, discuss the plan in private council.

Shirley managed to assemble the senior priests, and before Miss Ainley arrived, she had charmed them into excellent spirits. She handled Dr. Boultby—a stubborn Welshman, hot-tempered but effective—and Mr. Helstone with kindness. She took them through the garden, gathered flowers, treated them like beloved elders. Mr. Hall, she left to Caroline—though in truth, he had chosen Caroline himself.

And so, under Shirley’s careful guidance, the wheels of mercy began to turn.

The next morning, Shirley shared with Caroline her delight at how smoothly the evening’s gathering had gone.

“I rather enjoy hosting a group of gentlemen,” she said. “It’s amusing to watch how they light up over a well-prepared meal. For us, these fine wines and elaborate dishes mean little—yet gentlemen seem to carry a childlike wonder about food. It’s pleasant to please them—especially when they show the kind of decorum our admirable rectors do. I sometimes study Moore, trying to figure out what might charm him—but he lacks that simple openness. Have you ever discovered his soft spot, Caroline? You’ve spent more time with him than I have.”

“It’s not like my uncle or Dr. Boultby’s,” Caroline replied, smiling. She always felt a quiet pleasure in following Miss Keeldar’s lead when discussing Robert’s character. Left alone, she would never have brought it up herself. But when prompted, the chance to talk about the man who occupied her thoughts was too tempting to resist. “Still,” she added, “I honestly don’t know. Every time I try to observe Robert, I end up realizing he’s already watching me.”

“There it is!” Shirley exclaimed. “You can’t fix your gaze on him without his eyes flashing back. He’s never off guard. He won’t let you gain an advantage. Even when he’s not looking, it feels as if his thoughts are tracing yours—reading your motives with calm precision. Oh! I know that sort of nature—or something very like it. It intrigues me deeply. How does it affect you?”

This question was typical of Shirley’s sharp, sudden shifts. At first, Caroline had been flustered by them, but now she’d grown accustomed to deflecting them with the quiet grace of a little Quakeress.

“Intrigues me? In what way?” she asked.

“Here comes someone!” Shirley suddenly announced, rising and rushing to the window. “A distraction! I never told you about a splendid conquest I recently made—achieved at those parties I can never persuade you to attend. And it happened without effort or intention on my part—I swear. Listen—the bell! And two of them! Do they only hunt in pairs? You may have one, Lina, and take your pick. I hope I’m generous enough. Listen to Tartar!”

The black-muzzled, tawny dog—first glimpsed when the reader met his mistress—now barked fiercely in the hall, his deep voice echoing through the hollow space. Then came a growl louder than the bark—menacing as thunder muttered beneath storm clouds.

“Listen!” Shirley laughed again. “You’d think it was the beginning of a bloody attack. They’ll be terrified. They don’t know old Tartar like I do. They don’t realize his noise is all sound and fury—meaning nothing!”

Soon, there was commotion. “Down, sir, down!” rang out a sharp, commanding voice, followed by the crack of a cane or whip. Then—a yell, a scurry, a run, a full-blown uproar.

“Oh, Malone, Malone!”

“Down! Down! Down!” the voice insisted.

“He’s really bothering them!” Shirley called out. “They’ve struck him. A blow is something he’s unaccustomed to—and he won’t accept it.”

She dashed outside. One gentleman was fleeing up the oak staircase, racing for safety in the gallery or chambers; another was backing away from the foot of the stairs, wildly waving a knotted stick while shouting, “Down! down! down!” The tawny dog bayed, roared, howled at him, while servants burst from the kitchen. The dog lunged—then the second man turned tail and bolted after his companion. The first had already locked himself in a bedroom, holding the door against his friend—nothing so cruel as fear. But the other struggled hard; the door trembled under his strength.

“Gentlemen,” Miss Keeldar called in her silvery yet firm voice, “spare my locks, please. Calm yourselves! Come down! Look at Tartar—he won’t harm a cat.”

She was stroking the dog. He lay crouched at her feet, front paws stretched out, tail still twitching with warning, nostrils flaring, bulldog eyes glowing with dull fire. He was honest, slow, stubborn—though mostly indifferent to the world beyond his mistress and John, the man who fed him. Peaceful enough, unless threatened or struck, which instantly stirred the devil within him.

“Mr. Malone, how are you?” Shirley continued, lifting her bright face toward the gallery. “That’s not the way to the oak parlour—that’s Mrs. Pryor’s room. Ask your friend Mr. Donne to leave. I’d be delighted to receive him in a lower room.”

“Ha! ha!” Malone laughed hollowly, stepping away from the door and leaning over the heavy balustrade. “That animal truly frightened Donne. He’s a bit timid,” he added stiffly, walking neatly to the stairhead. “I thought it better to follow, to reassure him.”

“Well, you succeeded. Now come down, please.” She turned to her manservant. “John, go upstairs and release Mr. Donne. Be careful, Mr. Malone—the stairs are slippery.”

Indeed, they were—polished oak. The warning came too late. Malone had already slipped during his dignified descent, saved only by grabbing the banister, which made the entire structure groan.

Tartar seemed to believe the visitor’s exit had been overly dramatic, and growled once more. But Malone wasn’t afraid. The dog’s spring had startled him, but now he passed by in suppressed rage rather than fear. If looks could kill, Tartar wouldn’t have lived. Overcome by sullen anger, Malone pushed past Miss Keeldar into the parlour before her. He glanced at Miss Helstone—could barely bring himself to bow. His glare swept both ladies. He looked as though, had either been his wife, he’d have made a fearsome husband. In each hand, he seemed eager to grab one and squeeze her to death.

But Shirley softened. Her laughter ceased. Caroline, true to her nature, didn’t smile even at someone clearly embarrassed. Tartar was dismissed. Peter Augustus calmed—Shirley had a way with animals, even bulls. He sensed that since he couldn’t challenge the dog’s owner, he’d better behave. And behave he did. His efforts were welcomed, and soon he became quite civil again. He’d come, after all, determined to charm and captivate. Rough beginnings greeted him at Fieldhead, but having survived them, he resolved to win favor. Like March, he entered like a lion—but intended to leave like a lamb.

For air, or perhaps to escape any new crisis, he chose a seat—not on the sofa where Miss Keeldar offered him a throne, nor near the fireplace where Caroline gently invited him, but on a chair close to the door. No longer angry, he grew awkward, constrained. He spoke in bursts, choosing only the most mundane topics. He sighed deeply at the end of every sentence, paused, sighed again, then sighed before speaking. Finally, seeking ease among his other charms, he pulled out a large silk handkerchief. This was to be his graceful prop. He folded the red-and-yellow square diagonally, whipped it open with a flourish, refolded it tightly, shaped it into a neat band. What would he do with it? Wrap it around his neck? Head? Comforter? Turban? Neither. Peter Augustus had an inventive mind. He was about to demonstrate something novel. Sitting cross-legged, he wrapped the bandana around his ankles and tied them together firmly. Clearly proud of this feat, he repeated it several times. The second time sent Shirley to the window, laughing silently and uncontrollably. It made Caroline turn her head, her curls shielding the smile spreading across her face. Miss Helstone found more than just amusement in Peter’s behavior. She was impressed by how swiftly his affections shifted—from her to the heiress. The £5,000 she might one day inherit meant nothing compared to Miss Keeldar’s estate and grand house. He made no secret of his calculations. He didn’t pretend a gradual change—he turned sharply, openly abandoning pursuit of the smaller fortune for the greater. On what grounds he expected success, only he knew—certainly not through skill.

From the long silence, it seemed John had trouble convincing Mr. Donne to descend. Eventually, however, the gentleman appeared. And as he stepped into the oak parlour, he showed no shame, no confusion—not a trace. Donne was coldly phlegmatic, immovably self-satisfied, utterly blind to humiliation. He had never blushed in his life. No disgrace could shake him. His nerves lacked sensitivity, so no color rose to his cheeks. He had no fire in his blood, no modesty in his soul—just arrogance and emptiness. And this man fancied courting Miss Keeldar! Yet he had no idea how to begin. He had no concept of pleasing a taste, reaching a heart. His plan: visit her formally a few times, then write a marriage proposal. He assumed she’d accept for love of his position. Then they’d marry, and he’d rule Fieldhead—live comfortably, command servants, eat the best food, become a great man. You’d never guess his intentions when he addressed her in an impertinent tone: “A very dangerous dog, Miss Keeldar. I wonder you keep such an animal.”

“Do I, Mr. Donne? Perhaps you’ll be more surprised when I tell you I adore him.”

“You must be joking. Can’t imagine a lady fond of that brute—it’s so ugly—just a cart horse’s dog. Please hang it.”

“Hang what I love?”

“And replace it with a sweet little pug or poodle—something suitable for ladies. Women usually prefer lapdogs.”

“Perhaps I’m an exception.”

“Oh, you can’t be. All women are alike in such matters. That’s universally accepted.”

“Tartar frightened you terribly, Mr. Donne. I hope you’re not hurt.”

“I shall be, undoubtedly. He gave me a shock I won of forget. When I _sor_ him” (such was Mr. Donne’s pronunciation) “about to leap, I thought I’d faint.”

“Perhaps you did faint in the bedroom—you were gone a long time.”

“No. I held the door fast. I refused to let anyone in. I kept a barrier between myself and the enemy.”

“But what if Mr. Malone had been attacked?”

“Malone must look after himself. Your servant finally convinced me to come out by saying the dog was chained in his kennel. Had I not been assured of that, I’d have stayed all day. But what’s that? I declare the man lied! The dog is here!”

And indeed, Tartar walked past the glass door leading to the garden—stiff, tawny, black-muzzled as ever. Still grumpy, he growled again and whistled half-strangled, a trait inherited from his bulldog ancestors.

“There are more visitors coming,” Shirley remarked, with the cool confidence of someone who owns a fierce-looking dog. Tartar bounded down the path toward the gate, bellowing with explosive force. His mistress calmly opened the glass door and stepped out, calling to him softly. His roar faded, and he lifted his massive, blunt, stupid head to greet the newcomers, ready to be petted.

“What! Tartar, Tartar!” a cheerful, slightly boyish voice called. “Don’t you recognize us? Good morning, old boy!”

And little Mr. Sweeting—whose natural kindness made him fearless of anyone or anything—came through the gate, petting the guardian. His vicar, Mr. Hall, followed. He had no fear of Tartar either, and Tartar had none for him. He sniffed both men thoroughly, then, satisfied they were harmless, withdrew to the sunlit front of the hall, leaving the archway clear. Mr. Sweeting tried to play with him, but Tartar ignored the attention. Only his mistress’s touch brought him joy. To everyone else, he remained stubbornly indifferent.

Shirley greeted Messrs. Hall and Sweeting warmly, shaking their hands. They’d come to share news of their morning’s success in collecting subscriptions for the fund. Mr. Hall’s eyes shone kindly behind his spectacles, his plain face glowing with goodness—so much so that when Caroline saw him, she ran out to meet him, placing both hands in his. He looked down at her with gentle, serene affection, resembling a smiling Melanchthon.

Instead of returning inside, they wandered through the garden, the ladies walking beside Mr. Hall. It was a breezy, sunny day—the air freshened their cheeks and tousled their curls. Both looked lovely—one radiant, the other serene. Mr. Hall spoke most often to the lively one, gazed most often at the quiet one. Miss Keeldar gathered handfuls of the blooming flowers whose scent filled the air. She handed some to Caroline, asking her to choose a bouquet for Mr. Hall. With her lap full of delicate, vibrant blossoms, Caroline sat on the steps of a summerhouse. The vicar stood nearby, leaning on his cane.

Shirley, unwilling to neglect the others, now called out to the pair left in the oak parlour. She escorted Donne past his dreaded foe—Tartar, now snoring peacefully under the midday sun. Donne wasn’t grateful—he never was—but he was relieved. Miss Keeldar, wanting fairness, offered flowers to the curates. They accepted with awkwardness. Malone struggled especially, holding a bouquet in one hand and his shillelah in the other. Donne’s “Thank you!” was rich with pride—implying the gift was a tribute to his worth, a bid for the heiress’s favor. Only Sweeting received the posy like a smart, sensible young man, placing it gallantly in his buttonhole.

As a reward, Miss Keeldar beckoned him aside and gave him a task. His eyes sparkled with delight. Off he went, circling the courtyard to the kitchen. No need for directions—he was at home everywhere. Soon he returned, carrying a round table, which he placed under the cedar tree. Then he collected six garden chairs from various corners of the grounds and arranged them in a circle. The parlour maid—Miss Keeldar kept no footman—brought a tray covered with a napkin. Sweeting’s quick hands helped set out glasses, plates, knives, and forks. He also assisted her in arranging a neat luncheon: cold chicken, ham, and tarts.

Such spontaneous feasts delighted Shirley. Nothing pleased her more than having a nimble, helpful friend like Sweeting to run errands, cheerfully receive and execute her hospitable ideas. David and she were on excellent terms. His devotion to the heiress was entirely disinterested—since it didn’t interfere with his loyal service to the magnificent Dora Sykes.

The meal proved joyful. Donne and Malone contributed little to the merriment—mostly managing cutlery and wineglasses. But when four spirits like Mr. Hall, David Sweeting, Shirley, and Caroline gathered in good health and harmony, under a sunny sky, amid a sea of flowers, dullness was impossible.

During conversation, Mr. Hall reminded the ladies that Whitsuntide was near—the grand Sunday-school tea and procession of Briarfield, Whinbury, and Nunnely parishes. Caroline, he knew, would be teaching, and he hoped Miss Keeldar would join. He hoped she’d make her first public appearance then. Shirley was not one to miss such an event. She loved festive excitement, gatherings of joy, combinations of happy details, crowds of smiling faces, hearts uplifted. She assured Mr. Hall she’d be there—no matter what role she played.

“And,” said Caroline, “you’ll promise to sit at my table, near me, Mr. Hall?”

“I shall not fail, _Deo volente_,” he replied. “I’ve occupied the seat beside her for six years now,” he added, turning to Miss Keeldar. “They made her a teacher when she was twelve. She’s not naturally confident—as you’ve seen. The first time she had to ‘take a tray’ and serve tea publicly, she trembled, flushed, and spilled the pot. I stepped in, took a seat near her, handled the urn and slop-basin, and made the tea for her—like any seasoned old woman.”

“I was very thankful,” Caroline interjected.

“You were. You told me so with such sincerity it repaid me well—because most twelve-year-old girls wouldn’t notice kindness, even if given it constantly. She stayed close to me the rest of the evening, walking with me among the children, following me into the vestry when summoned to church. I believe she’d have climbed the pulpit with me, had I not guided her to the rectory pew.”

“And he’s been my friend ever since,” Caroline said.

“And always sat beside her, near her tray, handing cups—that’s all I’ve done. Next time I’ll help her marry a curate or mill-owner. But mind, Caroline, I’ll inquire about the bridegroom’s character. If he’s not a gentleman worthy of making the little girl who walked with me hand-in-hand over Nunnely Common happy, I won’t perform the ceremony. So be warned.”

“The warning’s unnecessary. I’m not marrying. I’ll live single, like your sister Margaret, Mr. Hall.”

“Very well. You might do worse. Margaret isn’t unhappy. She has books for joy, her brother for care, and is content. If ever you need a home—if Briarfield rectory ceases to be yours—come to Nunnely vicarage. If the old maid and bachelor are still alive, they’ll welcome you warmly.”

“There are your flowers. Now,” said Caroline, who had held onto the bouquet she’d chosen until now, “you don’t care for a bouquet, but give this to Margaret. Only—let me be sentimental once—keep that little forget-me-not, a wild flower I picked from the grass. And—more sentimental still—let me take a few blue blossoms and place them in my souvenir.”

She drew out a small book with an enamelled cover and silver clasp. Opening it, she inserted the flowers and wrote in pencil: “To be kept for the sake of the Rev. Cyril Hall, my friend. May --, 18--.”

The Rev. Cyril Hall, in turn, tucked a sprig between the pages of his pocket Testament. He simply wrote in the margin: “Caroline.”

“Now,” he smiled, “I trust we’re romantic enough. Miss Keeldar,” he continued—while the curates, by the way, were too busy with their own jokes to notice the exchange at the far end of the table—“I hope you’re laughing at this moment of ‘exaltation’ in the old gray-headed vicar. But the truth is, I’ve grown so used to obeying this young friend of yours, I don’t know how to refuse her when she asks me to do something. You’d say it’s not much trouble to trade flowers and forget-me-nots—but when asked to be sentimental, I comply.”

“He’s naturally sentimental,” Caroline remarked. “Margaret told me so, and I know what pleases him.”

“That you should be good and happy? Yes—that’s one of my greatest joys. May God preserve you long in peace and innocence! By which I mean _comparative_ innocence—for in His sight, I know, _none_ are pure. What seems flawless to us, like angels, is to Him frailty, needing the blood of His Son to cleanse and the strength of His Spirit to sustain. Let us all cherish humility—I, as you, my young friends. We can well do so when we look into our own hearts and see temptations, inconsistencies, tendencies we blush to acknowledge. And it’s not youth, beauty, grace, or charm that makes either beauty or goodness in God’s eyes. Young ladies, when mirrors or men praise you, remember that in the sight of her Maker, Mary Ann Ainley—a woman neither mirror nor lips have ever praised—is fairer and better than either of you. She is indeed,” he added after a pause, “she is indeed. You young ones, wrapped in yourselves and earthly hopes, scarcely live as Christ lived. Perhaps you cannot yet—while life is so sweet and earth so beautiful. It would be too much to expect. She, with a humble heart and reverence, walks closely in her Redeemer’s footsteps.”

The relief fund had taken root and grown strong. Inspired by Miss Keeldar’s generosity, the tireless efforts of the three rectors, and the quiet yet steadfast support of their unmarried assistants—Mary Ann Ainley and Margaret Hall—a generous sum had been collected. Prudently managed, it offered real comfort to the unemployed poor in the region. For over two weeks now, no cloth had been burned, no mill or manor attacked across the three parishes. The air seemed lighter, the tension easing. Shirley felt a hopeful certainty that the storm she’d feared was passing. With summer approaching, trade would revive—it always did—and with it, peace would return. When peace came, what a surge of energy would ripple through commerce!

These were her usual words whenever she met Gérard Moore, her tenant, in private conversation. He listened quietly—too quietly for her liking. She would meet his silence with an impatient glance, silently demanding more: a deeper thought, a fuller explanation. Then, with a smile that softened his lips but left his brow solemn, he would reply, “I too place my hope in the finite nature of this war. That is the anchor of my faith. My plans depend on it.”

“And you know,” he added, “that I run Hollow’s Mill entirely on speculation. I sell nothing. There’s no market for my goods. I produce only for a future day—preparing to seize the first chance that opens. Three months ago, I couldn’t have done this. I’d exhausted every credit and every penny. You know who saved me—whose hand lent me the loan that kept me from ruin. It is because of that loan that I can now play this bold game I once feared I’d never dare again. Total collapse will follow loss, and profit remains uncertain. Yet I am at peace. As long as I can act, as long as I can strive—as long as my hands are free—I cannot be crushed. One year—perhaps just six months—of peace, and I shall be secure. As you say, peace will stir commerce. You’re right there. But about the calm we see now—the supposed good effect of your fund? I remain doubtful. Charity has never truly calmed the working class. It has never made them grateful. It cannot. Human nature won’t allow it. We ought not to need such humiliating aid if things were rightly ordered—and they feel that. We would feel it too, were we in their shoes. And whom should they thank? You, perhaps, or the clergy—but certainly not us, the mill-owners. They hate us more than ever. Besides, the discontented here are linked with those elsewhere. Nottingham, Manchester, Birmingham—all are hubs of unrest. Orders flow from leaders to followers; discipline is strict. No action is taken without careful planning. Like the sky in sultry weather, threatening thunder daily, yet clearing night after night, the danger isn’t gone—only delayed. The storm will break eventually. There’s a truth in both moral and physical skies.”

“Well, Mr. Moore,” she would say, each time bringing the talk to a close, “take care of yourself. If you believe I’ve ever helped you, repay me by promising to stay safe.”

“I do,” he said. “I’ll watch myself closely. I want to live, not die. The future stretches before me like Eden. And yet, when I gaze into the shadows of my paradise, I see a vision I prize more than any seraph or cherub—faintly glimpsed in distant glades.”

“What vision?” she asked.

“I see—”

The maid burst in with the tea tray.

Early May had begun with fine weather, then turned wet. But by its final week, with the moon’s change, the skies cleared. A fresh wind swept away the deep, rain-laden clouds, carrying them eastward until they vanished beyond the horizon, leaving behind a vault of pure blue, ready for the summer sun. On Whitsuntide morning, the sun rose broad and bright. The gathering of the schools was marked by perfect weather.

Whit-Tuesday was the grand day. In preparation, the two large schoolrooms at Briarfield—built by the current rector, mostly at his own cost—had been cleaned, whitewashed, repainted, and adorned with flowers and evergreens. Some blooms came from the rectory garden, two cartloads from Fieldhead, and a wheelbarrowful from De Walden, the home of Mr. Wynne. Twenty tables, each meant for twenty guests, were laid out with white cloths and benches. Above them hung at least twenty cages, each holding a canary. This was a local custom, cherished especially by Mr. Helstone’s clerk, who loved the birds’ sharp song and believed they sang loudest amid noise and confusion. These tables were not for the twelve hundred children gathered from the three parishes, but for the patrons and teachers. The children’s feast would be held outdoors. At one o’clock, the children would arrive; at two, they would be marshalled; until four, they would parade through the parish; then came the meal, followed by music and speeches in church.

Why Briarfield was chosen as the meeting point should be explained. It wasn’t the largest parish—Whinbury far surpassed it in population—or the oldest—Nunnely’s ancient church and moss-covered parsonage, nestled among age-old oaks, predated it. It was simply because Mr. Helstone wished it so. His will outweighed that of Boultby or Hall. The former could not challenge him, the latter would not. So they let him lead.

This annual event had always been difficult for Caroline Helstone. It forced her into public view, placing her before all the wealthy, respectable, influential people in the district. Without the kind presence of Mr. Hall, she would have felt utterly exposed. She had to stand out—walk at the head of the procession as the rector’s niece and head teacher of the first class—serve tea to a mixed crowd of ladies and gentlemen—all while lacking a mother, aunt, or chaperone. And she was naturally shy, deeply afraid of attention. No wonder she trembled at the thought of Whitsuntide.

But this year, Shirley would be with her. That changed everything. It transformed the ordeal into something almost joyful. Miss Keeldar was worth more than a hundred ordinary friends. Confident, spirited, easy in manner, aware of her standing yet never arrogant—just seeing her gave courage. The only worry was whether Shirley would arrive on time. She often dawdled, and Caroline knew her uncle would wait for no one. At exactly two o’clock, the church bells would ring, and the march would begin. Caroline must make sure Shirley didn’t miss it.

On Whit-Tuesday, Caroline rose with the sun. She and Fanny, Eliza spent the morning preparing the rectory parlours, setting out refreshments—wine, fruit, cakes—on the dining-room sideboard. Then came dressing. Her finest white muslin gown, fitting the occasion perfectly, was put on. Her new sash—a birthday gift from Margaret Hall, which she suspected Cyril himself had chosen—was tied by Fanny’s skilled hands. Her bonnet matched the sash; her simple white crape scarf completed the look. When finished, she looked not dazzling, but gently pleasing—soft in tone, pure in air, graceful in bearing. Her brown eyes and clear forehead revealed a mind modest, gentle, and harmonious, though thoughtful. She seemed harmless—so much so that lambs and doves might trust her, finding in her expression a kinship with their own quiet natures.

Yet she was flawed, imperfect. Fair in form and dress, yes—but not, as Cyril Hall once remarked, as good or great as the withered Miss Ainley, who now dressed herself in her best black gown and Quaker drab shawl in her small cottage room.

Caroline set off across secluded fields and hidden lanes toward Fieldhead. She moved swiftly beneath green hedges and over grassy meadows. No dust, no damp marred her clean hem or delicate sandals. After recent rains, everything was washed clean; under the sun, everything was dry. She walked boldly, through daisies and thickets, reached Fieldhead, and entered Miss Keeldar’s dressing room.

She was fortunate to have come—Shirley was still lying on a couch, lost in a book. Mrs. Pryor stood nearby, urging her to rise. Caroline wasted no words. She took the book, and began dressing Shirley herself. Indolent from the heat, cheerful in spirit, Shirley wanted to talk, laugh, linger. But Caroline, focused on punctuality, worked quickly—fastening strings, inserting pins. Only when the last hook and eye was secured did she pause to scold. “You’re very naughty,” she said, “to be so late. You look like the very picture of careless charm.” And indeed, Shirley did—though a charming picture of it.

She was quite the opposite of Caroline. Every fold of her dress, every line of her figure, spoke of style. Rich silk suited her better than simplicity. Her deep embroidered scarf fell gracefully, worn negligently but with flair. Her bonnet wreath sat well. Her taste in fashion, her skill in adornment, were natural to her. It all fit her—like the light in her eyes, the playful curve of her lips, her upright posture and light step. Caroline took her hand, hurried her downstairs, out into the open air. Together, they raced through the fields, laughing, looking like a snow-white dove and a jewel-hued bird of paradise flying side by side.

Thanks to Caroline’s promptness, they arrived in time. Even before the trees hid the church, they heard the bell tolling—measured, urgent—calling all to gather. The sound of many feet, many voices, drifted through the air. From a hilltop, they saw the Whinbury school approaching—five hundred children. Ahead marched the rector and curate, Boultby and Donne. Boultby loomed large in full canonicals, walking with priestly dignity beneath a shovel-hat, supported by a gold-headed cane. He wore the broadest black coat, the most ample vestments.
