Now and then, he flicked his cane and nodded with a dogmatic tilt of his hat toward Donne, his aide-de-camp.

Donne, thin and slight beside Boultby’s bulk, still managed to look every inch a curate. Everything about him was precise, self-satisfied—from his upturned nose and raised chin to his clerical gaiters, short trousers, and square-toed shoes.

Walk on, Mr. Donne! You’ve been judged. You think you look well. Whether the figures watching you from the hill agree is another matter.

Those figures—Caroline and Shirley—raced down as the procession passed. The churchyard was filled with children and teachers, all in their best holiday clothes. Despite hardship, they looked neat, even handsome. British pride in decency worked miracles. Poverty might strip an Irish girl bare, but it couldn’t rob an English girl of her sense of dignity. And Shirley, watching them, felt proud. Her timely generosity had eased many families’ worries and provided new frocks and bonnets for children. She knew it—and it lifted her heart. She couldn’t give charity like Miss Ainley—her nature wouldn’t allow it. But she found joy in knowing there was another way, one that suited her, that truly helped.

Caroline too felt glad. She’d given up dresses, ribbons, collars she couldn’t spare to outfit her pupils. Unable to donate money, she followed Miss Ainley’s example—giving time, labor, sewing for the children.

Not only the churchyard was crowded—the rectory garden teemed with people. Couples and groups strolled among lilacs and laburnums. Inside, at open windows, lively clusters of patrons and teachers stood. Behind the rectory, in the parson’s croft, the three parish bands waited with instruments. Fanny and Eliza, in crisp caps and gowns, white aprons, circulated among them, serving strong ale brewed weeks earlier under the rector’s supervision. Nothing he touched was allowed to be shabby. From building churches to cooking meals, he demanded grandeur, generosity, efficiency. Shirley shared this standard, and they admired each other’s ways.

Soon, Caroline and Shirley were among the company. Caroline moved easily—no longer retreating to a corner or hiding in her room. She wandered through the parlours, smiled, spoke freely, even before being addressed. She seemed reborn. It was Shirley’s presence that changed her. Seeing Miss Keeldar’s ease, her confidence, inspired her. Shirley had no fear of people. Unless someone was vulgar or arrogantly coarse, she welcomed them. Most men, unless proven truly bad, she assumed could be good—worth knowing, worth treating kindly. This openness made her beloved. Her wit was warm, not cutting; her laughter infectious. And her true friendships—especially with Caroline—were deeper, rooted in something different. Miss Helstone was her choice, her soulmate. Others, like the Misses Pearson, Sykes, Wynne, benefited from her charm, but weren’t central.

Donne entered the drawing-room just as Shirley sat on the sofa, the center of a circle. She had already forgotten her anger toward him. She bowed, smiled warmly. His reaction revealed his nature. He couldn’t decline gracefully—he lacked pride. Nor could he accept kindly. His punishment hadn’t humbled him; he felt no shame now. He wasn’t malicious enough to be actively cruel—only awkward, scowling, sheepish. He’d never forgive her, but neither could he rage. His sluggish nature prevented passion.

“He wasn’t worth a scene,” Shirley whispered to Caroline. “What a fool I was! To punish Donne for his petty grudge against Yorkshire is like crushing a gnat that bit a rhinoceros. Had I been a gentleman, I might have thrown him out physically. I’m glad I used only words. But he mustn’t come near me again. I don’t like him. He irritates me. There’s no fun in him. Malone is better sport.”

And indeed, Malone seemed determined to prove it. As soon as the words left her mouth, Peter Augustus approached—dressed in full splendor, gloves on, hair oiled and polished, holding a bouquet of cabbage roses, five or six fully bloomed. He presented them to Shirley with a flourish that even the finest artist couldn’t capture. Who now could claim Peter wasn’t a lady’s man? He’d gathered and given flowers—a poetic tribute to Love or Mammon. Hercules with a distaff was a pale image of Peter with roses. He must have known it, for he stepped back, chuckling softly to himself—then paused, turned, and stared to confirm he’d actually given the bouquet. Yes—there they were, six red blooms on purple satin, held by a pale hand with gold rings, ringlets framing a laughing face. Only half-hidden. Peter saw the laughter. He was mocked. His gallantry ridiculed. By two petticoats—Miss Helstone too was smiling. He felt seen through. His face darkened like a storm cloud. When Shirley looked up, his eyes locked onto hers—cold, furious. Malone had fire in his hatred. She saw it.

“Peter *is* worth a scene,” she whispered. “And he’ll get one, if he wants.”

Then, solemn in color but pleasant in expression, the three rectors appeared at the dining-room door. They’d been in church and now came to refresh themselves before the march. A large morocco chair awaited Dr. Boultby. He took it. Caroline, urged by Shirley to act as hostess, rushed forward with wine and macaroons. Boultby accepted, but noticed her not at all. He saw only the sweets. He took a handful. Mrs. Boultby insisted on mixing the wine with hot water, sugar, and nutmeg.

Mr. Hall stood near an open window, breathing in the scent of flowers, talking warmly to Miss Ainley. Caroline turned to him with pleasure. “He mustn’t serve himself—he must be waited on.” She fetched a salver, offering him variety. Margaret Hall joined them, then Shirley. The four women stood around their beloved pastor. They saw in him an earthly angel. Cyril Hall was their pope—infallible to them as Boultby was to his flock. A crowd gathered around old Helstone—twenty or more pressed close. No parson had ever commanded such loyalty. The curates clustered together like minor stars. Young ladies watched from afar, but dared not approach.

Mr. Helstone pulled out his watch. “Ten minutes to two,” he announced. “Time to form ranks. Come.” He grabbed his shovel-hat and marched off. All rose and followed in one sweeping movement.

The twelve hundred children formed three groups of four hundred. Bands stood at the rear of each. Teachers were posted between every twenty children. Helstone called out:

“Grace Boultby and Mary Sykes lead Whinbury.

“Margaret Hall and Mary Ann Ainley guide Nunnely.

“Caroline Helstone and Shirley Keeldar head Briarfield.”

Then he ordered:

“Now—march!”

They were not marching to war, nor hunting down foes—yet the procession, led by a priest and guided by women, carried music that echoed with battle rhythms. And though no enemy loomed ahead, some among them—Miss Keeldar, for one—felt their spirits stirred, not by violence, but by longing. Old Helstone, turning by chance, caught her gaze and laughed; she returned his smile.

“There’s no battle in sight,” he said, “and our country doesn’t need us to fight. No tyrant threatens, no foe questions our freedom. There’s nothing to do. We’re merely taking a walk. Keep your hand on the reins, captain—and calm that fire within. It’s not needed. A pity, really.”

“Take your own advice, doctor,” Shirley replied lightly. Then, whispering to Caroline, she added, “I’ll borrow from imagination what reality won’t give me. We’re not soldiers—bloodshed isn’t my desire. But if we are, then we’re soldiers of faith. Time has rolled back centuries, and we’re pilgrims bound for Palestine. Yet even that feels too dreamlike. I need something stronger. Let us be Lowland Scots, following a Covenanting leader into the hills, hiding from persecutors. We know prayer may lead to battle—and if battle comes, we believe heaven awaits us. So we’d gladly spill our blood on the moss. That music… it wakes every fiber in me. My heart pounds—not with its usual quiet beat, but with a fierce, new strength. I almost crave danger—something worth defending: a cause, a land… or at least a lover.”

“Look, Shirley!” Caroline interrupted. “What’s that red speck above Stilbro’ Brow? You have sharper eyes than I. Turn your eagle gaze toward it.”

Shirley looked. “I see it,” she said, then paused. “There’s a line of red now. They’re soldiers—cavalry. Moving fast. Six of them. They’ll pass us—no, they’ve turned off to the right. They saw us and veered away. Where are they going?”

“Maybe just riding drills,” Caroline suggested.

“Perhaps,” Shirley murmured. “But we don’t see them anymore.”

Mr. Helstone stepped forward.

“We’ll take Royd Lane,” he announced. “It’s a shortcut to Nunnely Common.”

And so they entered the narrow lane. So tight was the path that only two could walk side by side without stepping into the ditches flanking each side. Halfway through, tension rose among the clerical leaders. Boultby adjusted his spectacles; Helstone gripped his Rehoboam tightly. The curates nudged each other. Mr. Hall turned to the ladies and smiled.

“What’s happening?” someone asked.

He pointed ahead with his staff. There, emerging from the opposite end, came another procession—also led by men in black, also followed by music.

“Is this our reflection?” Shirley asked. “Our ghostly double? Here’s a card turned up.”

“If you wanted a battle,” Caroline whispered, laughing, “you’re likely to get one—at least of glances.”

“They shall not pass!” the curates declared in unison. “We won’t yield!”

“Yield?” Helstone snapped, turning sharply. “Who speaks of yielding? You boys—watch yourselves. The ladies, I trust, will stand firm. I know these women will defend the honor of the Church. What does Miss Keeldar say?”

“She asks what’s going on.”

“The Dissenters and Methodists—the Baptists, Independents, Wesleyans—all joined in unholy alliance, deliberately choosing this lane to block us and turn us back.”

“Rude,” Shirley declared. “And I hate rudeness. They’ll learn better.”

“A lesson in manners,” Mr. Hall suggested gently. “Not an example of insolence.”

Helstone marched on, quickening his pace. He advanced ahead of the group. Nearly upon the opposing leaders, a large, greasy man with flat-combed black hair called a halt. The procession stopped. He pulled out a hymn book, sang a verse, set a tune—and they began to chant a mournful psalm.

Helstone signaled to his musicians. Brass instruments blared forth, thunderous and bright. He ordered them to play *Rule, Britannia!* and instructed the children to sing along—so they did, with fervor and pride. The opposing voices faltered. Their song was drowned. In sound alone, they were defeated.

“Now, follow me!” Helstone commanded. “Not running—but steady, brisk, unwavering. Stay together. Hold onto each other’s skirts if need be.”

He strode forward with purpose, and his followers obeyed exactly—neither rushing nor stumbling, but moving with cool, united force. The curates, caught between Helstone and Miss Keeldar, had no choice but to keep pace. Both watched closely, ready to correct any misstep—one with cane, the other with parasol. The Dissenters, first stunned, then alarmed, were pushed back, overwhelmed, and finally forced to retreat, leaving the lane clear. Boultby struggled during the clash, but Helstone and Malone supported him, bringing him through whole, though breathless.

The fat man who had led the hymn was left sitting in the ditch. A spirit merchant by trade and leader of the Nonconformists, he reportedly drank more water that afternoon than he had in a year. Mr. Hall had protected Caroline, and she had returned the favor. Later, he and Miss Ainley exchanged quiet thoughts about the encounter. When the entire party passed safely through, Miss Keeldar and Mr. Helstone shook hands warmly. The curates began to cheer—but Helstone silenced them quickly. “You never know when to speak,” he said. “Best to stay silent. This wasn’t your doing.”

By half-past three, they turned back. At four, they returned to the starting point. Long benches lined the freshly mowed fields around the school. Children sat in rows. Great baskets covered in white cloths and smoking tin vessels were brought out. Before food was served, Mr. Hall offered a brief grace, which the children sang with sweet, clear voices—melodious and touching in the open air. Large currant buns and hot, sweet tea were distributed generously. No child was to receive less than twice what they could eat—so there would be leftovers to take home for those too old, sick, or unable to attend. Buns and beer flowed freely among the musicians and singers. Afterward, benches were removed, and games began.

A bell summoned teachers, patrons, and guests to the schoolroom. Miss Keeldar, Miss Helstone, and others were already inside, arranging trays and tables. Most of the neighborhood’s female servants, along with wives of clerks, singers, and musicians, had been enlisted as waitresses. Each vied in neatness and elegance, many young ones appearing strikingly beautiful. A dozen cut bread and butter; another dozen fetched hot water from the rector’s kitchen. Flowers and evergreens adorned the white walls. Silver teapots and fine porcelain sparkled on tables. People moved about—active figures, cheerful faces, bright dresses—creating a lively, joyful scene. Everyone chatted softly but happily, while canary birds sang shrilly from high cages.

Caroline, as the rector’s niece, took her place at one of the three main tables. Mrs. Boultby and Margaret Hall presided over the others. These tables hosted the elite—equality not being fashionable in Briarfield. Miss Helstone removed her bonnet and scarf, feeling the heat. Her long curls fell across her neck like a veil. Her muslin dress, modest as a nun’s robe, allowed her to go without a shawl.

The room filled. Mr. Hall stood beside Caroline, who now whispered observations about the day’s events. He seemed grave about the incident in Royd Lane, and she tried to lighten his mood. Miss Keeldar sat nearby—unusually quiet, still, watching everything. She appeared anxious that no one sit beside her. She spread her satin dress wide, placed her gloves and embroidered handkerchief on the bench, as if claiming territory. Caroline noticed this at last and asked whom she expected.

Shirley leaned close, her lips nearly brushing her ear, and whispered in a soft, musical tone—often used when her words touched a tender secret—“I expect Mr. Moore. I saw him last night. He promised to come with his sister and sit here. I’m certain he won’t fail me—but I fear he’ll arrive late, and we’ll be separated. Look—another group is coming. Every seat will be taken. Frustrating!”

Indeed, Mr. Wynne, the magistrate, arrived with his wife, son, and daughters—Briarfield gentry. Naturally, they claimed the first table. They filled all remaining seats. Mr. Sam Wynne, who had been keeping watch over the empty space Miss Keeldar had reserved, planted himself firmly on her gown, gloves, and handkerchief. He was one of her greatest dislikes—especially since he had shown serious interest in marrying her. The old gentleman had once publicly declared that Fieldhead and De Walden estates were delightfully “contagious”—a phrase that rumor had repeated often enough to reach Shirley.

Caroline’s ears still rang with that whisper: “I expect Mr. Moore.” Her heart still thumped, her cheeks still burned, when a note from the organ rang above the chatter. Dr. Boultby, Mr. Helstone, and Mr. Hall rose—everyone else followed. Grace was sung with music, and then tea began. Caroline was busy serving, but after the final cup was poured, she glanced around restlessly. Several people still stood without seats. Among them, she recognized Miss Mann—her spinster friend, drawn out of solitude by the weather or a persuasive companion. Miss Mann looked tired. A woman in a yellow bonnet brought her a chair. Caroline knew that hat, that black hair, that kind yet opinionated face beneath it, that black silk dress, even that gray linen shawl. She knew Hortense Moore. And suddenly, she wanted to leap up and run to her—to embrace her, not just for her sake, but for her brother’s too. She half rose, gasping softly—perhaps she would have dashed across the room—but a hand pulled her back. A voice behind her whispered, “Wait until after tea, Lina. Then I’ll bring her to you.”

When she could look again, Robert Moore stood directly behind her, smiling at her eagerness. He looked better than she had ever seen him—handsome, radiant, so vivid that she dared not look twice. His image flashed before her like lightning captured on film, burning itself into memory.

He moved on, speaking to Miss Keeldar. Shirley, annoyed by Sam Wynne’s unwanted attention—and by the fact that he still sat on her belongings—was clearly irritated. Moore’s lateness didn’t help. She shrugged, then muttered a sharp word about his “intolerable delay.” Moore neither apologized nor responded. He stood quietly nearby, waiting to see if she would recover. Within minutes, she did—offering him her hand. He took it, smiling—half apologetic, half grateful. A slight tilt of the head conveyed the former; perhaps a gentle squeeze, the latter.

“You may sit wherever you can now, Mr. Moore,” Shirley said, smiling. “There’s no room here—but plenty at Mrs. Boultby’s table, between Miss Armitage and Miss Birtwhistle. Go! John Sykes will be your neighbor, and you’ll sit with your back to us.”

Moore, however, lingered. He wandered down the length of the room, exchanging greetings with other standing gentlemen, but always returned to Shirley—bringing whispers each time.

Meanwhile, poor Sam Wynne looked increasingly uncomfortable. His neighbor seemed restless—fanning herself, complaining of heat and crowding. She declared that people should leave the tables after tea and warned she might faint if things continued. He offered to escort her outside—just the way to kill her with cold, she insisted. Eventually, he decided to retreat.

Moore was far away, deep in conversation with Christopher Sykes. Timothy Ramsden, Esq., a large corn-factor, stepped forward instead. Shirley acted swiftly. With a sweep of her scarf, she knocked over her teacup—spilling tea onto the bench and her satin dress. A waiter was summoned. Mr. Ramsden, stout and puffy, kept his distance. Shirley, usually careless about such accidents, made a fuss—overreacting like the most delicate woman. Ramsden opened his mouth, stepped back slowly, and as Miss Keeldar threatened to faint, he turned and fled.

At last, Moore returned. Surveying the chaos with calm amusement, he remarked that this end of the room was indeed the hottest—only suitable for cool tempers like his own. Then, dismissing the commotion, he settled into the seat destiny had marked. Shirley relaxed. Her features softened—the furrowed brow smoothed, the stubborn curve of her lips straightened. The wilfulness and mischief vanished, replaced by calm. All the restless gestures that had tormented Sam Wynne ceased—as if by magic. Still, she gave Moore no warm glance. Instead, she accused him of causing her great trouble and blamed him for losing Mr. Ramsden’s respect and Mr. Sam Wynne’s friendship.

“I wouldn’t have offended either man for the world,” she insisted. “I’ve always treated them with utmost respect. But thanks to you—look how they’ve been treated! I won’t be happy until I make amends. I must visit Royd corn-mill tomorrow to soothe the miller and praise the grain. Then I’ll call at De Walden—though I hate going there—and bring half an oatcake for Mr. Sam’s favorite dogs.”

“You know the surest way to win any man’s heart,” Moore said quietly. He seemed content at last to be where he was. He made no grand speech, offered no apology. His calm suited him perfectly—it made him look nobler, his presence more soothing. One wouldn’t guess, looking at him, that he was a struggling man seated beside a wealthy woman. Equality radiated from him—perhaps it lived within. Occasionally, when he looked down at Miss Keeldar, one might think his rank surpassed hers, just as his height did. Stern light sometimes flickered across his brow, gleamed in his eyes.

Their talk grew animated, though low. She pressed him with questions—he refused to satisfy her curiosity. Once, she sought his gaze. In her eyes, soft yet eager, she pleaded for truth. Moore smiled pleasantly—but said nothing. Then she turned away, annoyed. But within minutes, he drew her back. He seemed making promises—promises she accepted, though they weren’t answers.

The heat proved too much for Miss Helstone. She grew paler as tea dragged on. As soon as thanks were given, she left the table and hurried after her cousin Hortense, who had already gone outside with Miss Mann.

Robert Moore rose as well—perhaps to speak to her—but he had one last word with Miss Keeldar. By the time he finished, Caroline was gone.

Hortense greeted her former pupil with dignity, not warmth. She had been deeply offended by Mr. Helstone’s actions and blamed Caroline for obeying her uncle too strictly.

“You’re quite a stranger,” she said sternly, as Caroline held her hand.

Caroline knew her well enough not to protest. She let the formality pass, trusting that Hortense’s natural kindness—*bonté*, a word that means neither mere goodness nor cheerfulness, but something deeper—would prevail. It did. Upon seeing Caroline’s thin face, her expression softened. She kissed her on both cheeks and asked anxiously about her health. Caroline answered cheerfully. Had Miss Mann not interrupted, asking to be escorted home, Hortense might have launched into a lengthy interrogation and lecture. But the invalid was already weary—too tired to be polite. Caroline’s white dress and bright demeanor displeased her. Miss Mann preferred brown cloth and quiet melancholy. She barely recognized her young friend and left with a cool nod. Hortense, having promised to accompany her, departed with her.

Caroline now searched for Shirley. She found her—rainbow scarf and purple dress—surrounded by familiar ladies, though ones she usually avoided. Shyer than usual, she lacked courage to join them. Yet she couldn’t stand alone. So she approached a group of her older students—girls, really young women—who watched younger children playing blind-man’s buff.

Miss Helstone knew these girls liked her, yet she remained shy even with them. They respected her quietness. They gathered round her, not to dominate, but to protect. They admired her wisdom in class, her gentleness in life. Because she was wise and good when teaching, they overlooked her timidity when not. They didn’t exploit it. Though peasants, they possessed English sensibility. They stood politely, kindly receiving her fleeting smiles and rushed attempts at conversation—with grace born of goodwill—which soon eased her unease.

Mr. Sam Wynne rushed over, insisting the older girls join the game. Caroline was left alone again. She considered retreating to the house—when Shirley, spotting her isolation from afar, hurried to her side.

“Let’s go to the top of the fields,” she said. “I know you don’t like crowds, Caroline.”

“But it would mean taking you from all these fine people who adore your company—and who you charm so easily.”

Shirley smiled. “That’s why I’m choosing you.”

The evening was still and warm, thick with the quiet hum of summer. The sun dipped low, painting the sky in deep purples and golden hues—colors more fitting to an Indian twilight than an English one. These rich tones spilled across the hillsides, rooftops, tree trunks, winding lanes, and rolling pastures, turning everything into a dreamlike scene. Slowly, the two girls made their way down from the fields. By the time they reached the churchyard, the bells had fallen silent, and the congregation had gathered inside. The world outside seemed hushed, empty, and full of peace.

"How peaceful and calm it is!" Caroline said softly.

"And how stifling it will be in there," Shirley replied. "And what a long, tedious sermon Dr. Boultby will deliver! The curates will drone on with their rehearsed speeches. Honestly, I’d rather stay out here."

"But Uncle will be angry if we’re not present."

"I’ll take the blame," Shirley said, smiling. "He won’t devour me. I’ll miss his sharp remarks—always defending the church, always lashing out at dissenters. He won’t forget Royd Lane. And I’ll miss your chance to hear Mr. Hall’s honest, heartfelt sermon—full of that lovely Yorkshire charm. But I’m staying. Look—the gray church and the older graves glow under this crimson light. Nature is praying now. She kneels before those red hills, offering her evening worship. I see her prostrate on the steps of her great altar, begging for safe seas for sailors, for travelers in deserts, for lambs on moors, for young birds in trees. Caroline, I see her—and I’ll tell you what she looks like. She reminds me of Eve when Adam and she stood alone on earth."

"Not Milton’s Eve," Caroline said gently.

"Milton’s Eve? No, by the pure Mother of God, she isn’t! Cary, we’re alone—we can speak our minds. Milton was brilliant, but was he kind? His mind was clear—but what of his heart? He saw heaven, looked down upon hell. He saw Satan, Sin, and Death. Angels lined up before him, their shields flashing like stars. Devils marched past, dim and tarnished. Milton tried to picture the first woman—but he failed. He saw only a servant or a cook, perhaps Mrs. Gill making custards in summer heat, cooling the kitchen with roses and nasturtiums outside the window, preparing a cold feast for the rector—preserves and 'dulcet creams,' wondering which dish would best please, which order would delight without mixing flavors too harshly."

"That sounds quite charming," Caroline said.

"Let me remind him," Shirley continued, "that the first humans were Titans, and Eve was their mother. From her came Saturn, Hyperion, Oceanus. She bore Prometheus—"

"Pagan talk!" Caroline laughed. "What does any of that matter?"

"It matters because giants walked the earth then—giants who dared to reach for the heavens. The first woman’s heart, beating with life, held the courage to challenge God, the strength to endure endless bondage, the vitality to feed death through ages, the unbroken spirit and purity that, after centuries of suffering and sin, could bring forth a Savior. She was born of heaven. Her heart was the source of all nations’ blood. Her head wore the crown of creation—unshaken, noble, eternal."

"She ate an apple and was tricked by a serpent," Caroline said, shaking her head. "But you’ve mixed Scripture and myth so wildly, I can hardly follow. Still—you haven’t told me what you saw kneeling on those hills."

"I saw—still see—a woman-Titan. Her robe of blue air stretches to the edge of the heath where sheep graze. A veil as white as snow falls from her head to her feet, edged with lightning. Around her waist, a belt of purple like the horizon glows, and through it shines the evening star. Her eyes—I cannot paint them. They are clear, deep as lakes, raised in reverence, trembling with love and prayer. Her forehead is wide as a cloud, pale as the moon before nightfall. She rests her chest on Stilbro’ Moor, hands clasped beneath. In this posture, face to face with God, she speaks. That Eve is Jehovah’s daughter, just as Adam was His son."

"She’s vague and dreamy," Caroline said. "Come, Shirley—we should go in."

"No," Shirley insisted. "I’ll stay here with my mother Eve—Nature. I love her—this living, enduring force. Heaven may have faded from her brow when she fell, but all that’s beautiful on earth still shines within her. She draws me into her arms, shows me her soul. Hush, Caroline—if we’re both quiet, you’ll feel it too."

"I’ll humor you," she said. "But you’ll start talking again in ten minutes."

Miss Keeldar leaned against a tall headstone, captivated by the warmth of the evening. Her gaze fixed on the glowing west, and she drifted into a soft reverie. Caroline stepped aside, walking slowly beneath the rectory wall, lost in her own thoughts. Shirley had spoken of “mother”—a word that stirred something deep within Caroline. Not the vast, mystical figure of Shirley’s vision, but a gentle, human form—the image she carried of her own mother: unknown, unloved, yet never forgotten.

"Oh, that the day would come when she remembers me," she whispered. "That I might know her—and love her!"

Her childhood longing rose again. The wish that had kept her awake many nights, nearly extinguished by fear of illusion, flared bright once more. She dreamed of a happy day when her mother would send for her, look at her with tender eyes, and say in a sweet voice, “Caroline, my child, I have a home for you. You shall live with me. All the love you’ve missed since infancy—I’ve saved it for you. Come; let it comfort you now.”

A sound on the road broke her reverie. Shirley was pulled from her visions. They listened—horses clattered softly. Through the trees, they glimpsed flashes of scarlet, helmets gleaming, plumes fluttering. Six soldiers rode past in silence, orderly and deliberate.

"The same ones we saw earlier," Shirley whispered. "They’ve been waiting somewhere. They want to stay unnoticed, meeting at this quiet hour while people are in church. Didn’t I say we’d see strange things soon?"

Before the last hoofbeat faded, another sound pierced the stillness—a child’s loud cry. They turned. A man emerged from the church, carrying a small boy, about two years old, wailing fiercely. Two little girls, nine and ten, followed close behind. The fresh air and some flowers picked from a grave soon calmed the child. The man sat down, cradling the boy on his knee with gentle care. The girls took their places beside him.

"Good evening, William," Shirley said after a moment’s study. He had seen her before and waited to be noticed. Now he tipped his hat and grinned, pleased. He was rough-looking, weathered, not old but worn by hard work. His clothes were clean, his children’s attire unusually neat. It was William Farren, their familiar friend.

"You’re not going into church?" he asked, gazing at them with pride and a hint of shyness—not from fear of their status, but admiration for their grace and youth. With men like Moore or Helstone, William could be stubborn; with haughty women, defiant. But with kindness and fairness, he softened. His strong nature rejected rigidity in others. He had never liked Moore, though the gentleman respected him—unknowingly—even recommending him to Mr. Yorke. William carried a grudge over Moore’s sternness, unaware of the favor done. Recently, he often worked at Fieldhead. Miss Keeldar’s open-hearted ways delighted him. Caroline, whom he’d known since childhood, was his ideal of a lady. Her quiet manner, her step, her elegance moved something deep within him—like seeing rare flowers or a fine landscape. Both girls liked William; they loved lending him books, giving him plants, and found his company far richer than many higher-born but coarse people.

"Who was speaking when you came out?" Shirley asked.

"A gentleman ye hold in high regard, Miss Shirley—Mr. Donne."

"You seem to know me well, William. How did you guess my feelings for him?"

"Ay, Miss Shirley, sometimes there’s a bright spark in your eyes that gives you away. You look downright scornful when Mr. Donne is near."

"Do you like him yourself?"

"Me? I’m fed up with curates, and so’s my wife. They’ve no manners. They speak to poor folk as if they think us beneath them. Always boasting of their office. It’s a shame their office couldn’t make them better men—but it doesn’t. I hate pride."

"But you’re proud in your own way," Caroline said. "You’re what you call house-proud—everything must be tidy, handsome. Sometimes you look almost too proud to accept your wages. When you were out of work, you refused credit. For your children, I believe you’d rather starve than ask for help. When I offered you something, how hard it was to get you to take it!"

"Partly true, Miss Caroline. Any day I’d rather give than take—especially from someone like you. Look at us—ye’re a slight, young girl, and I’m a big, strong man—more than twice your age. It’s not right for me to take from you—to owe you. That day you came to our door and gave me five shillings—money I know you didn’t spare easily—well, I was furious. I thought it shameful that, willing and able to work, I should be in such need that a young girl—about the age of my eldest daughter—should feel the need to offer me her coin."

"You were angry with me?"

"Nearly. But I forgave you quickly. You meant well. Yes, I am proud—and so are you. But our pride is the right kind—what we call clean pride in Yorkshire. Mr. Malone and Mr. Donne know nothing of it. Their pride is dirty. I’ll teach my daughters to be as proud as Miss Shirley, and my sons as proud as myself. But I’d whip little Michael if I saw him showing signs of curate pride."

"What’s the difference, William?"

"You know the difference well enough—but you want me to explain. Mr. Malone and Mr. Donne are so proud they can’t do anything for themselves. We’re so proud we won even let anyone do anything for us. The curates can’t bear to speak kindly to those they think below them. We can’t bear to take an unkind word from those who think themselves above us."

"Now, William," Shirley said, "be humble enough to tell me honestly—how are you doing?"

"Miss Shirley, I’m very well. Since I began gardening with Mr. Yorке’s help, and since Mr. Hall helped my wife set up a little shop, I’ve nothing to complain of. My family has food and clothes. My pride makes me save a pound here and there for rainy days. I’d die before I’d go to the parish. Me and mine are content. But the neighbors—many are poor. I see much hardship."

"And so, there’s still unrest, I suppose?" Miss Keeldar asked.

"Of course—your word is right. Starving people cannot be satisfied. The country isn’t safe. I’ll say that much!"

"But what can be done? What more can I do?"

"Do? You’ve done what you can, poor young lady. You’ve given your money. If you could send Mr. Moore to Botany Bay, you might do better. People hate him."

"William, for shame!" Caroline exclaimed. "If people hate him, it’s their fault, not his. Mr. Moore hates no one. He only wants to do his duty and protect his rights. You’re wrong to speak so."

"I speak what I think. He has a cold, hard heart, that Moore."

"But," Shirley interjected, "if Moore were driven out and his mill destroyed, would people have more work?"

"Less," William said. "I know it, and they know it. Many honest boys are driven desperate, knowing no matter which way they turn, they can’t improve. And there are dishonest men ready to lead them to ruin—scoundrels who pretend to be the people’s friends, who know nothing of the people, and are as false as Lucifer. I’ve lived forty years, and I believe ‘the people’ will never have true friends except themselves—and a few good souls in different stations who care for all. Human nature, taken as a whole, is selfish. Only a few—exceptions like you two and me—can understand each other, be friends without servility or pride. Those who claim to be friends to lower classes for political gain are never to be trusted—they always try to use them. I won’t be patronized or misled. I’ve had offers lately—I saw them as treacherous, and I threw them back in their faces."

"You won’t tell us what offers?"

"I won’t. It wouldn’t help. It wouldn’t change anything. Let them deal with their own affairs."

"Ay, we’ll look after ourselves," said another voice. Joe Scott had stepped out of the church for fresh air.

"I’ll wager you will, Joe," William smiled.

"And I’ll wager my master," Joe replied. "Young ladies," he said, adopting a stiff tone, "you’d better go inside."

"Why?" Shirley asked, used to his rigid ways. Joe resented the fact that his master’s mill was, in effect, under female influence. He disliked the heiress’s visits to the counting-house.

"Because there’s nothing happening worth women’s concern."

"Indeed? There’s prayer and preaching in that church. Are we not concerned in that?"

"You weren’t present for either, ma'am, if I observed rightly. I meant politics. William was speaking of that."

"Well, then? Politics are our daily study, Joe. Do you know I read a newspaper every day—two on Sundays?"

"I imagine you read the marriages, murders, accidents—such things?"

"I read the leading articles, foreign news, market prices. In short, I read just what gentlemen read."

Joe looked as if she were chattering nonsense. He stayed silent.

"Joe," Miss Keeldar said, "I’ve never been sure whether you’re a Whig or a Tory. Which party holds your loyalty?"

"It’s difficult to explain where you’re sure not to be understood," he snapped. "As for being a Tory—I’d as soon be an old woman—or a young one, which is worse. Tories carry on wars and ruin trade. If I belong to any party—though parties are nonsense—I’m with the one most favorable to peace, and thus to commerce."

"So am I, Joe," Shirley said, enjoying teasing him. "Though I lean toward agriculture too—after all, I don’t want England under French rule, and while part of my income comes from Hollow’s Mill, more comes from the land around it. We shouldn’t harm farmers, Joe, do you agree?"

"The dew at this hour is unhealthy for ladies," Joe said.

"If you mean it for my sake, I assure you I’m immune to cold. I’d gladly watch the mill tonight with your musket, Joe."

Joe’s chin jutted forward farther than usual.

"But—back to my point," Shirley continued. "As a clothier, mill-owner, and farmer, I can’t shake off the idea that we manufacturers and businessmen sometimes grow too selfish, too blind to suffering, too eager for profit. Don’t you agree, Joe?"

"I cannot argue where I cannot be understood," he replied.

"Man of mystery! Your master argues with me sometimes. He’s not as stiff as you."

"Maybe not. We all have our ways."

"Joe, do you truly believe all wisdom lies in male heads?"

"I think women are tricky and stubborn. I respect the teachings in the second chapter of Paul’s letter to Timothy."

"What teachings?"

"'Let the woman learn in silence, with all subjection. I suffer not a woman to teach, nor to usurp authority over the man, but to be in silence. For Adam was first formed, then Eve.'"

"How does that relate?" Shirley asked. "It sounds like primogeniture. I’ll bring it up next time Mr. Yorke complains about it."

"And," Joe added, "Adam was not deceived, but the woman, being deceived, was in the transgression."

"More shame to Adam for sinning with his eyes open!" Caroline exclaimed. "To be honest, Joe, I’ve never felt comfortable with that chapter. It puzzles me."

"It’s very plain," Joe said. "He that runs may read."

"He may read it his way," Caroline said. "You allow private judgment, I suppose?"

"My certainty, yes! I allow it for every line of the holy Book."

"Women may exercise it too?"

"No. Women should take their husbands’ opinions—in politics and religion. It’s healthiest for them."

"Oh! oh!" both Shirley and Caroline exclaimed.

"Of course," Joe insisted. "No doubt about it."

"Consider yourself groaned down and shouted at for such foolishness," Miss Keeldar said. "You might as well say men should take their priests’ words without question. What value would such a religion have? It would be blind superstition."

"And what’s *your* interpretation, Miss Helstone, of these words?"

"Hem! I—well, I think Paul wrote it for a specific church, under special circumstances. And I suspect, if I could read Greek, I’d find many words mistranslated or misunderstood. With a little skill, one could twist it to say, 'Let the woman speak whenever she sees fit.' 'It is permitted to a woman to teach and lead, while the man should keep quiet.' And so on."

"That won’t wash, miss."

"I dare say it will. My ideas are dyed in stronger colors than yours, Joe. Mr. Scott, you’re dogmatic—always have been. I prefer William."

"Joe is fine in his own home," Shirley said. "I’ve seen him as quiet as a lamb. He’s one of the kindest husbands in Briarfield. He doesn’t preach to his wife."

"My wife is hard-working, plain. Time and trouble have taken all her vanity. But not you, young misses. You think you know so much—yet it’s only shallow vanities. I remember—perhaps a year ago—Miss Caroline coming into the counting-house while I was packing behind the desk. She didn’t see me, and brought a slate with a sum on it to the master. It was a simple problem—our Harry could’ve solved it in two minutes. She couldn’t. Mr. Moore had to show her. And even then, she didn’t understand."

"Nonsense, Joe!"

"No nonsense. And Miss Shirley—she pretends to listen intently when Mr. Moore talks business, as if she follows every word, as clear as a mirror. But all the while, she’s peeking out the window to see if the mare is calm, checking a stain on her riding skirt, glancing at the dust and cobwebs in our counting-house, thinking how dirty we are and how grand her ride over Nunnely Common will be. She hears nothing—no more than if he spoke Hebrew."

The evening had deepened into twilight, the sky clear and still, perfect for the stars to emerge one by one like silver sparks in the velvet dark.

"There will be just enough light to guide me home," said Miss Keeldar, standing at the edge of the rectory garden, preparing to leave Caroline behind.

"You must not go alone, Shirley. Fanny will come with you."

"That she shall not. What need have I to fear in my own parish? I’d walk from Fieldhead to the church any bright midsummer night, three hours later than this, just for the joy of watching the stars—and perhaps catching a glimpse of a fairy."

"But wait until the crowd has dispersed."

"Agreed. There go the five Misses Armitage, all in a row. Here comes Mrs. Sykes’s phaeton, Mr. Wynne’s close carriage, and Mrs. Birtwhistle’s little cart. I don’t care to say good-bye to them all, so let’s slip into the garden and hide among the laburnums for a moment."

From the church porch now emerged the rector, his curates, and the churchwardens, exchanging handshakes and cheerful remarks—praise for the sermon, warnings about the night air, and general gossip. Gradually the throng thinned, carriages rolled away. As Miss Keeldar stepped from her floral shelter, Mr. Helstone entered the garden and met her.

"Oh, I wanted you!" he said. "I feared you'd already gone. Caroline, come here."

Caroline approached, expecting a scolding for having been absent during the service. But the rector had other matters on his mind.

"I won’t be sleeping at home tonight," he continued. "I’ve just met an old friend and promised to join him. I’ll likely return by noon tomorrow. Thomas, the clerk, is engaged, and I can’t get him to stay in the house as I usually do when I’m away. Now—"

"Now," interrupted Shirley, "you want me to stand in as gentleman—the first gentleman of Briarfield, in short—to take your place, guard your niece and maids while you're gone?"

"Exactly, captain. I thought the role suited you. Will you do Caroline the favour of staying here for one night? Instead of returning to Fieldhead?"

"And what of Mrs. Pryor? She expects me home."

"I’ll send word. Come now, decide. It’s growing late; the dew is falling thick. You two will enjoy each other's company, I’m sure."

"I promise to stay with Caroline," replied Shirley. "As you say, we’ll enjoy each other’s company. We won’t be parted tonight. Now, go to your friend, and worry not for us."

"If anything should disturb the night, captain—if you hear a lock being picked, glass cut, or stealthy footsteps near the house—well, I needn’t hesitate to tell you, who wear such a bold heart beneath that girl’s ribbon sash—that such things are very possible these days—what would you do?"

"I don’t know. Perhaps faint—collapse and need lifting up. But Doctor, if you give me this post of honour, you must arm me. What weapons are in your fortress?"

"You couldn’t handle a sword?"

"No. A carving knife, though—I could manage that."

"You’ll find one in the dining-room sideboard—a lady’s knife, light and sharp as a poniard."

"It’ll suit Caroline. But I must have pistols. I know you have some."

"I have two pairs. One pair I can lend you. They hang over the mantelpiece in my study, in cloth cases."

"Loaded?"

"Yes—but not cocked. Cock them before bed. It’s a great compliment, captain, to lend you these. If you were part of the awkward squad, you wouldn’t get them."

"I’ll take care. You needn’t delay, Mr. Helstone. You may go now."

"He’s kind to lend me his pistols," she remarked as the rector passed through the gate. "But come, Lina—let’s go in and eat. I was too irritated at tea by Mr. Sam Wynne’s presence to eat, and now I’m actually hungry."

They entered the house and made their way to the dimly lit dining room. Through the open windows drifted the evening breeze, carrying the scent of flowers from the garden, distant echoes of retreating footsteps, and a soft, indistinct murmur—until Caroline leaned against the casement and whispered, “Shirley, I hear the beck in the Hollow.”

She rang the bell, asked for a candle and bread and milk—Miss Keeldar’s usual supper and her own. When Fanny brought the tray, she tried to shut the windows and shutters, but Caroline stopped her.

"Not yet," she said. "The twilight is too calm, the air too sweet to exclude."

They ate in silence. Once, Caroline rose to move a glass of flowers from the sideboard—their fragrance too strong for the warm room. On her way back, she half-opened a drawer and pulled out something that glittered sharply in her hand.

"You assigned this to me, then, Shirley?" she said. "It’s bright, keen-edged, finely tapered—dangerous-looking. I’ve never felt the urge to use it against another person. It’s hard to imagine circumstances that would make me raise this long knife."

"I’d hate to do it," Shirley replied, "but I think I could, if forced by certain needs I can picture." She sipped her milk quietly, her face thoughtful, slightly pale—as always, really. She was never flushed.

After finishing their meal, Fanny was called again. She and Eliza were told to retire—they were eager to, tired from the day’s work: slicing currant buns, filling urns and teapots, running errands. Soon, the sound of the maids’ chamber door closing echoed softly. Caroline took a candle and walked through the house, checking every window and door, even the haunted back kitchen and the vault-like cellars. Then she returned.

“There’s no spirit nor flesh in this house now that shouldn’t be,” she said. “It’s nearly eleven—time for bed. Yet I’d rather stay awake a little longer, if you don’t mind, Shirley.” She placed two pistols on the table. “I’ve brought them from my uncle’s study. Examine them if you wish.”

"Why do you want to stay up?" Miss Keeldar asked, picking up the guns, inspecting them, then setting them down.

"Because I feel strangely restless in my chest."

"So do I."

"Is this uneasiness caused by something in the air—electrical, perhaps?"

"No. The sky is clear, the stars countless. It’s a beautiful night."

"But very still. I can hear the water churning over its stones in Hollow’s Copse as clearly as if it ran below the churchyard wall."

"I’m glad it’s quiet. A howling wind or storm would drive me mad right now."

"Why, Shirley?"

"Because it would drown out the sounds I’m trying to hear."

"Do you listen toward the Hollow?"

"Yes. It’s the only direction from which we might hear anything now."

"The only one, Shirley."

They sat together near the window, arms resting on the sill, heads tilted toward the open lattice. By starlight and the lingering twilight of June, they saw each other’s young faces—soft, solemn, alert.

"Mr. Helstone thinks we don’t know where he’s gone, nor why, nor what he hopes for," murmured Shirley. "But I suspect much—do you not?"

"I suspect something."

"All those gentlemen—your cousin Moore included—think we’re already asleep, unaware."

"Caring nothing for them—hoping or fearing nothing," added Caroline.

They sat in silence for nearly half an hour. The night remained hushed—only the church clock marking time in quarters. A few words passed about the chill air. They drew their scarves tighter, put on their bonnets again, and waited.

Near midnight, the persistent, monotonous bark of the house dog broke the stillness. Caroline rose and slipped silently through the dark hall to the kitchen, intending to quiet him with a piece of bread. She succeeded. Returning to the dining room, she found it dark—Shirley had extinguished the candle. Her silhouette stood near the open window, leaning out.

Caroline said nothing. She crept beside her. The dog began barking wildly again. Suddenly, he stopped—listening. The two women listened too, no longer hearing only the mill stream. From the road below the churchyard came a new sound—near, muffled, rhythmic—a dull tramp of marching feet.

It grew louder. They realized it wasn’t just a few men—dozens, hundreds. They saw nothing—the high hedges formed a green wall between them and the road. But hearing wasn’t enough. As the column passed the rectory, they felt it more deeply. Then a voice—just one word—cut through the silence.

"Halt!"

The march ceased. A low conversation followed—too faint to catch.

"We *must* hear this," said Shirley.

She turned, took the pistols from the table, slipped out through the glass door of the dining room, and crept down the path to the garden wall. Caroline hesitated—she wouldn’t have left the house alone—but where Shirley went, she would follow. She glanced at the knife on the sideboard, but left it. Soon she stood beside her friend.

They dared not look over the wall—fear of being seen. They crouched behind it. These were the words they heard:

"It looks like a rambling old place. Who lives here besides the damned parson?"

"Only three women—his niece and two servants."

"Do you know where they sleep?"

"The girls at the back—the niece in a front room."

"And Helstone?"

"Yonder’s his chamber. He had a light, but none now."

"Where would you enter?"

"If I were ordered to do his job—and he deserves it—I’d try that long window—it opens to the dining room. I could feel my way upstairs. I know his room."

"What about the women?"

"Leave them be unless they scream. Then I’d soon silence them. I’d prefer to find the old man asleep. If he wakes, he’ll be dangerous."

"Does he have weapons?"

"Firearms—always—and always loaded."

"Then you’re a fool to stop us here. A shot would raise the alarm. Moore would be on us before we turned around. We’d miss our main goal."

"You could go on. I’d take Helstone alone."

A pause. One man dropped a weapon—it clanged on the stone path. At once, the rectory dog barked fiercely.

"That ruins everything!" the voice snapped. "He’ll wake. A noise like that could rouse the dead. You didn’t mention there was a dog. Damn you! Forward!"

Forward they went—tramp, tramp—with slow, steady, countless steps. They vanished.

Shirley stood upright, peered over the wall, along the road.

"Not a soul remains," she said.

She stood silent. Then: "Thank God."

Caroline repeated it—though less steadily. She trembled. Her heart pounded; her face was cold, her brow damp.

"Thank God for us!" she whispered. "But what happens elsewhere? They passed us so they could strike others."

"They did well," Shirley said calmly. "Others will defend themselves. They’re ready. We weren’t. My finger was on the trigger—ready to greet that man if he’d entered. But behind him came three hundred. I had neither hands nor weapons for them. I couldn’t protect you, myself, or the two women sleeping under this roof. So again, I thank God for danger avoided."

After a pause, she added: "What should I do next? Not sit idle, thankfully. But walk to the Hollow."

"To the Hollow, Shirley?"

"Yes. Will you come?"

"Where those men are going?"

"They took the highway—we won’t meet them. The field path is as safe and silent as a skywalk. Will you go?"

"Yes," she answered—mechanically. Not because she wished to, or was prepared, or even unafraid. But because she couldn’t abandon Shirley.

"Then we must secure the windows and leave everything as tight as we can. Do you know why we’re going, Cary?"

"Yes—no—because you want to."

"Is that all? Are you so obedient to my whim? What a docile wife you’d make for a stern husband! Your face is whiter than moonlight, and the aspen at the gate shakes more than your fingers. And yet—docile, terrified, devoted—you’d follow me into real danger! Let me give your loyalty a reason. We’re going for Moore’s sake—to see if we can help him, warn him of what’s coming."

"Of course! I’m blind and weak, and you’re wise and strong, Shirley. I’ll go with you—I’ll gladly go!"

"I don’t doubt it. You’d die blindly for me—but wisely, willingly, for Moore. But truly, there’s no death tonight. No risk at all."

Caroline quickly shut the shutters and lattices. "Don’t worry—I’ll run as fast as you, Shirley. Take my hand. Let’s cross the fields."

"But you can’t climb walls?"

"To-night I can."

"You’re afraid of hedges—and the beck we must cross?"

"I can cross it."

They started—ran. Walls slowed them, but didn’t stop them. Shirley moved like a deer—surefooted, agile. Caroline, timider, fell once or twice, bruising herself, but rose immediately, saying she was fine.

A quickset hedge blocked the last field. They searched for a gap—lost time. When found, it was narrow, but they squeezed through. Hair tore, skin scraped, silks snagged. But what mattered most was the delay. On the other side lay the beck—deep, rough-bedded. A narrow plank bridged it.

Shirley had crossed it many times—without fear. Caroline had never dared.

"I’ll carry you," said Miss Keeldar. "You’re light, and I’m strong. Let me try."

"If I fall in, you can fish me out," she replied, squeezing her hand gratefully.

Caroline stepped forward without hesitation, as if the plank were solid ground. Shirley followed—no more boldly. In their present state, fire or flood could not have stopped them. All Stilbro’ Moor ablaze would not have halted them. Yet one sound made them pause.

No sooner had they reached the bank than a shot cracked the air from the north. One second passed. Another blast echoed from the south. Within minutes, signals thundered from east and west.

"I thought we were dead at the first shot," Shirley breathed, drawing in a long breath. "I felt struck in the temples, thought your heart pierced. But the repeating shots explained it—signals. The attack is near. We should’ve flown. Our feet failed us."

Now came the copse. Emerging from it, they saw the mill below—its buildings, yard, road beyond. Their first glance confirmed Shirley’s guess. They were too late to warn.

The road, white by daylight, was now black with moving figures. Rioters gathered before the closed gates. A lone figure stood inside, addressing them. The mill itself was dark, motionless—no life, no light.

"Surely he’s prepared," Shirley whispered. "Surely that isn’t Moore facing them alone?"

"It is. We must go to him. I *will* go."

"_That_ you will not."

"Why did I come? Only for him. I’ll join him."

"Fortunately, you can’t. There’s no entrance."

"There *is* a small back entrance—secret. I know how to open it. I’ll try."

"Not with my permission."

Miss Keeldar wrapped her arms around Caroline’s waist and held her back. "Not one step," she insisted. "Moore would be shocked and embarrassed to see either of us. Men don’t want women near in real danger."

"I wouldn’t trouble—I’d help him."

"How? By inspiring courage? Pooh! These aren’t chivalric days. This isn’t a tournament—it’s about money, food, survival."

"It’s natural I should be at his side."

"As queen of his heart? His mill is his true love, Cary! Backed by factory and looms, he has all he needs. He fights not for love, but ledger and broadcloth. Don’t be sentimental. Robert isn’t like that."

"I *could* help him. I *will* reach him."

"Then go—I let you—seek Moore. You won’t find him."

She released her. Caroline darted off like an arrow from a bow. Behind her rang a teasing laugh: "Look well—don’t mistake!"

But there *was* a mistake. Caroline paused, gazed. The figure had retreated from the gate, rushing back to the mill.

"Hurry, Lina!" Shirley called. "Meet him before he enters."

Caroline returned slowly. "It’s not Robert," she said. "Not his height, form, bearing."

"I knew it wasn’t Robert when I let you go. How could you think it was? It’s a shabby private soldier—posted as sentinel. He’s safe inside now. I saw the door open for him. My mind eases. Robert is ready. Our warning would’ve been useless. I’m glad we came too late. It saved us a scene. Imagine bursting into the counting-house _toute éperdue_, only to find Messrs. Armitage and Ramsden smoking, Malone swaggering, your uncle sneering, Mr. Sykes sipping cordial, and Moore himself in full business mode! I’m thankful we missed it."

"Do you think many are in the mill, Shirley?"

"Plenty to defend it. The soldiers we saw earlier were headed there. The group around your cousin in the fields will be with him."

"What are they doing now? What’s that noise?"

"Hatchets and crowbars against the gates. They’re forcing them. Are you afraid?"

"No—but my heart races. I can barely stand. I’ll sit. Do you feel calm?"

"Hardly—but I’m glad I came. We’ll see what happens with our own eyes. We’re here—alone with the night, the stars, the whispering trees. No one knows. Instead of surprising the curate, the clothier, and the corn-dealer with a dramatic entrance, we stand unseen, witnesses to history."

"Shirley, Shirley—the gates are down! That crash sounded like felling oaks. Now they’re pouring through. They’ll break the mill doors as they broke the gate. What can Robert do against so many? Oh, if only I were closer—could hear him—speak to him! With my will, my desire to serve, I wouldn’t be a burden—I could help!"

"They come on!" cried Shirley. "How steadily they advance! Discipline in their ranks. I won’t say they’re brave—hundreds against tens isn’t proof—but" (she lowered her voice) "there’s suffering, desperation. These goads push them forward."

"Forward against Robert—and they hate him. Shirley, is there much chance they’ll win?"

"We’ll see. Moore and Helstone are of 'earth’s first blood'—not fools, not cowards."

A crash—smash—shiver—silenced their words. A volley of stones flew at the mill’s front, shattering every pane. A roar followed—a rioters’ yell—a Yorkshire, West Riding, cloth-district cry.

You may never have heard that sound, reader? Better so—for your ears, perhaps, and your heart. For if it tears the air in hatred of you or your ideals, wrath stirs. The lion rises to the hyena’s howl. Caste stands against caste. The middle class, indignant, descends upon the starving, furious mass. Tolerance fades. Justice wavers.

Caroline rose. Shirley put her arm around her. They stood together—still as trees.

That yell lasted long. When it ended, the night still hummed with a crowd.

"What next?" they whispered.

Nothing yet. The mill remained mute.

"He _can’t_ be alone!" Caroline breathed.

"I’d wager all I own he’s not alone—or alarmed," Shirley replied.

Shots fired from the mob. Had the defenders waited for this signal? It seemed so. The mill stirred—fire flashed from empty windows. A musket volley rang sharp through the Hollow.

"Moore speaks at last!" said Shirley. "And he seems gifted with tongues. That wasn’t one voice."

The two girls returned to the rectory in silence, passing through the quiet lanes without meeting a soul. They slipped into the house without a sound, climbed the stairs unseen, and found their way to their rooms under the soft glow of dawn. Shirley went straight to her bed. Though the room was unfamiliar—she had never slept there before—and though the events of the night had been more intense than anything she’d ever known, she fell asleep almost instantly. Her breath deepened, her face softened, and within moments she was wrapped in a restful slumber that banished every trace of fear and tension.

Shirley’s health was remarkable—her spirit strong, her nerves steady. She felt deeply, yet was not weakened by emotion. The storm had shaken her, yes, but it had not broken her. Each day brought new passion, each night restored her fully. Caroline watched her sleep, drawn to the peace reflected in Shirley’s features—the stillness of a mind at ease, even after such turmoil.

For Caroline, sleep was impossible. Her nature was different—more sensitive, more prone to reflection. Even the ordinary excitement of tea and school would have kept her awake; now, the horror of what had unfolded just hours before weighed heavily on her heart. She could not lie still. Sitting beside Shirley’s bed, she counted the minutes as the June sun climbed higher in the sky.

Hours like these—watches spent in silent suffering—are among life’s harshest trials. When the mind has no joy to feed on, no memories to comfort it, no hope to sustain it, it turns inward, trying to live on longing alone. But desire brings no nourishment. Hunger grows sharper, and the soul begins to starve. Then comes philosophy, then resolution, then resignation—each called upon in turn, each met with silence. There is no answer. No help. Only emptiness.

Caroline was a Christian. In her distress, she prayed—earnestly, repeatedly—asking for strength, patience, relief. Yet, despite her faith, she sometimes felt abandoned. Was God turned away from her? At times, she believed herself doomed, cast aside by divine will. She sank into despair, wondering if she were among those marked for rejection.

Many people know this feeling—the sense of being utterly forsaken. After long waiting, after endless hope deferred, the heart grows weary. It is a terrible moment, yet often the darkest hour before dawn. Just as winter's last wind carries both mourning and promise, so too does suffering hold within it the seed of deliverance. But the birds shivering in the cold cannot see spring coming. Neither can the grieving soul recognize salvation when it stands at its door.

Still, let no one who suffers lose faith. God does not deceive. He does not abandon. “Whom He loveth, He chasteneth.” These words are true. And they must be remembered.

By morning, the household stirred. Servants moved about, shutters opened, and daylight flooded the rooms. As Caroline rose from her restless vigil, she felt a renewal of spirit—the kind that comes with light and motion. She dressed carefully, smoothing her hair, adjusting her clothes, determined not to let her inner sorrow show. When she finished, she looked as fresh as Shirley—but where Shirley’s eyes sparkled, Caroline’s were dull with weariness.

“Today I shall have much to say to Moore,” Shirley announced, already standing before the mirror, twisting her curls with deft fingers. “He’ll have to endure a full examination. I suspect he thinks he’s outsmarted me. That’s how men treat women—hiding danger, pretending to protect them. They thought we knew nothing of where they’d gone last night. We _know_ they didn’t guess where we were. Men think women are children. That’s a mistake.”

She paused only briefly, then resumed as Caroline fastened her dress.

“If men saw us as we truly are, they’d be surprised. Even the sharpest minds among them are often mistaken. They don’t understand us—neither our goodness nor our darkness. Their ideal woman is half doll, half angel. Their wicked woman is always a monster. And then they go wild over fictional heroines—worshipping them in novels and plays! Yes, they may be beautiful, but often they’re artificial—false as the roses on my finest bonnet. If I spoke all I think about some so-called great female characters… well, I’d be buried under stones before the hour passed.”

“Shirley, you talk too much. I can’t finish dressing you. Be quiet. Besides, authors’ heroines are nearly as good as authoresses’ heroes.”

“Not at all. Women read men better than men read women. I’ll prove it in a magazine article someday—if I ever get the chance. But it’ll be rejected. ‘Declined with thanks,’ they’ll say, leaving it on the publisher’s desk.”

“To be sure. You couldn’t write well enough. You don’t know enough. You’re not learned, Shirley.”

“God knows I can’t argue with you, Cary. I’m as ignorant as a stone. But here’s one comfort—you’re not much better.”

They descended to breakfast.

“I wonder how Mrs. Pryor and Hortense Moore spent the night,” Caroline said, stirring the coffee. “I’ve been selfish—I didn’t think of either until now. They must have heard the noise. Fieldhead and the cottage are so close. Hortense is timid. So is Mrs. Pryor.”

“Don’t worry,” Shirley replied. “Moore likely sent his sister home with Miss Mann. She’ll be staying there tonight. As for Mrs. Pryor—I admit I’m concerned. But in half an hour we’ll be with her.”

By now, news of the events at the Hollow had spread across the countryside. Fanny, returning from Fieldhead with the milk, burst in breathless, reporting a battle at Mr. Moore’s mill—some said twenty men had died. Eliza, meanwhile, had learned from the butcher’s boy that the mill itself had burned to the ground. Both women rushed into the parlour, delivering their account with trembling certainty: “It’s clear master was involved. He and Thomas, the clerk—surely they joined Mr. Moore and the soldiers last night. Mr. Malone hasn’t been seen since yesterday. And Joe Scott’s family is distraught—no word of him.”

No sooner had they spoken than a knock came at the kitchen door. The Fieldhead errand-boy arrived, panting, bearing a note from Mrs. Pryor. It was hastily written, urging Miss Keeldar to return immediately—the neighborhood was in chaos, orders needed to be given, and only the mistress of the hall could manage it. In a postscript, she begged that Miss Helstone not be left alone at the rectory. It would be best if she came along.

“There’s no debate about that,” Shirley said, tying on her bonnet and hurrying to fetch Caroline’s. “But what about Fanny and Eliza? And if your uncle returns?”

“He won’t come back yet,” Shirley replied. “He has other matters to attend to. He’ll be riding between Briarfield and Stilbro’ all day, rallying magistrates and military. Fanny and Eliza can invite Joe Scott’s wife and the clerk’s wife to stay with them. And really, there’s no real danger now. Weeks will pass before the rioters gather again. I doubt Moore and your uncle won’t use last night’s outbreak to end the trouble once and for all. They’ll push the authorities into action. I only hope they won’t be too harsh—too cruel to the defeated.”

“Robert won’t be cruel,” Caroline said softly. “We saw that last night.”

“But he’ll be hard,” Shirley answered. “And so will your uncle.”

As they walked along the meadow path toward Fieldhead, they saw the highway already crowded with riders and foot travelers heading toward the Hollow. When they reached the hall, the backyard gates stood open, and the courtyard teemed with agitated milk-fetchers—men, women, children—all gathered in groups, chatting loudly. Mrs. Gill, the housekeeper, tried in vain to send them away.

Seeing the disorder, Shirley stepped forward. With calm authority, she greeted them all, her voice steady and open. She asked if everyone had received their milk. When they confirmed they had, she added, “Then I wonder what you’re waiting for?”

“We’re just talking about the battle at your mill, mistress,” one man replied.

“Talking!” Shirley laughed. “That’s all you ever do. You talk when someone dies. You talk when a fire breaks out. You talk when a mill-owner fails or is murdered. What good does it do?”

The lower classes love a firm rebuke—especially one delivered with honesty and humor. Flattery bores them. Plain speech delights them. Shirley’s sharp tone earned her instant attention.

“We’re not at war, nor above it, are we?” another man asked, smiling.

“Not a bit. You who should be models of industry are just as fond of gossip as the idle. Rich folks who do nothing may be forgiven for wasting time. But you—whose bread is earned by sweat—have no excuse.”

“That’s strange, mistress. Should we never have a holiday because we work hard?”

“Never,” Shirley said firmly. “Unless,” she added with a smile that softened her sternness, “you knew how to use it wisely—instead of gathering over rum and tea, or beer and pipes, to whisper lies about your neighbors. Now, friends,” she said, turning gentle, “please take your cans and go home. I expect visitors today. I need the paths clear.”

Yorkshire folk respond to reason more than force. Within minutes, the yard was empty.

“Thank you, and farewell,” Shirley said, closing the gate behind the departing crowd.

Let any refined Londoner dare criticize Yorkshire manners. Take them fairly, and most of the men and women of West Riding are gentlemen and ladies through and through. They only rebel against pretension and false pride.

Through the back entrance, the girls entered the kitchen—what locals call the *house*—and made their way to the hall. Mrs. Pryor rushed down the oak staircase to meet them. She was pale, trembling, her usual composure shattered. She didn’t cry out or rush into story—but her eyes darted nervously, her hands shook.

“You know,” she began, voice quivering, “that rioters attacked Mr. Moore’s mill last night. We heard the gunfire clearly. None of us slept. It was a terrible night. The house has been in chaos all morning. Servants have asked for orders—but I couldn’t give them. I didn’t feel I had the right. Mr. Moore sent for supplies for the soldiers and wounded. I couldn’t decide what to do. I feared delay might hurt someone. But this isn’t my house. You were gone, Miss Keeldar. What could I do?”

“Were no refreshments sent?” Shirley asked, her expression suddenly darkening.

“I think not.”

“And nothing for the wounded—no linen, wine, bedding?”

“I don’t know. I didn’t see what Mrs. Gill did. But I couldn’t risk sending provisions to soldiers. It seemed like allowing them to plunder. I meant to do right—but I wasn’t sure.”

“It’s simple,” Shirley said, turning sharply. “These soldiers risked their lives defending my property. They have a right to my gratitude. The wounded are human beings—we must help them. Mrs. Gill!”

She called with a voice that echoed through the thick walls—clear, commanding. Mrs. Gill, caught mid-knead in the kitchen, came running, flour still dusting her apron. She hadn’t dared stop to clean her hands. Miss Keeldar had only used that tone once before—when her dog Tartar was attacked by two carrier dogs. Then, she had stormed out bareheaded, wrestled with the animals, and fought off the carriers, refusing help. She’d ignored John for a week afterward.

Now, remembering that scene, Mrs. Gill trembled.

“Mrs. Gill,” Shirley said, “how long ago did the message arrive from the mill?”

“Not an hour, ma'am,” the housekeeper replied gently.

“Not an hour! You might as well have said not a day. They’ve probably sought help elsewhere by now. Send a man at once to tell them everything in this house is at Mr. Moore’s, Mr. Helstone’s, and the soldiers’ disposal. Do that first.”

As the order was carried out, Shirley stepped to the window, silent and distant. When Mrs. Gill returned, Shirley turned. A flush warmed her cheeks, a fire sparked in her eyes.

“Bring up the larder and wine cellar contents. Load them into the hay-carts and drive them to the Hollow. If there’s not enough bread or meat, go to the butcher and baker and ask for what they have. But I’ll check myself.”

She walked away.

“She’ll recover soon,” Caroline whispered to Mrs. Pryor. “She’ll blame herself more than you by evening. Go upstairs, dear madam. Try to be calm. She’ll need you.”

With gentle words, Caroline calmed Mrs. Pryor, escorted her to her room, and promised to return. Then she went to help where she could. The staff at Fieldhead was small, and the house was in disarray. Caroline’s quiet kindness, her quick hands, lifted spirits and eased tension. A glance from her—soft, understanding—brought a smile from Shirley, who was carrying a heavy basket from the cellar.

“This is shameful!” Shirley exclaimed, rushing to take it. “You’ll strain yourself.”

She carried it outside. When she returned, the storm in her face had passed. Her warmth returned, tempered now with quiet shame for her earlier anger.

She was still overseeing the loading when a gentleman entered the yard.

“I trust Miss Keeldar is well this morning?” he asked, studying her flushed face with quiet intensity.

She glanced at him, then turned back to her task—silent. A faint smile played on her lips, but she hid it. He leaned closer.

“Well enough, if she is good enough,” she replied. “And so is Mr. Moore, I suppose. To speak plainly, I’m not worried about him. A little misfortune would be just reward. His behavior has been—let’s say *strange*, for now. We’ll find a better word later. But what brings you here?”

“Mr. Helstone and I just received your message—that everything at Fieldhead is at our service. We assumed, from your generous wording, that you were overextending yourself. And indeed, we were correct. We’re not a regiment—only six soldiers and a few civilians. Allow me to reduce your supplies.”

Shirley blushed, then laughed—embarrassed by her own excess. Moore smiled quietly, then gave orders for baskets to be removed, vessels returned to the cellar.

“The rector must hear of this,” he said. “He’ll make a fine tale. What a brilliant army contractor Miss Keeldar would be!” He chuckled. “Exactly as I expected.”

“You should be grateful,” Shirley said, “not mocking. How could I know your numbers? For all I knew, fifty men needed feeding. You told me nothing. Provisioning soldiers suggests large scale.”

“It appears so,” Moore said, giving her another keen look. “Now,” he continued, addressing the carter, “take the rest to the Hollow. Your load will be lighter than intended.”

As the cart rolled away, Shirley regained her cheerfulness.

“What happened to the wounded?” she asked.

“None of ours were hurt,” Moore replied.

“You were injured—on the temples,” said a soft voice—Caroline’s. She had been hidden in the doorway, behind Mrs. Gill, unnoticed until now. Moore’s gaze searched the shadow.

“Are you badly hurt?” she asked.

“Like scratching your finger with a needle.”

“Lift your hair. Let us see.”

He removed his hat and obeyed—revealing only a narrow strip of plaster. Caroline nodded slightly, satisfied, and stepped back inside.

“How did she know?” Moore asked.

“By rumor, perhaps. But it’s noble of her to care. For my part, I was thinking of your opponents. What damage did they suffer?”

“One man died. Six were wounded.”

“What have you done with them?”

“They’re being cared for,” Moore said simply.

While Shirley was engaged in conversation with Moore, Caroline returned to Mrs. Pryor upstairs. She found her governess deeply troubled, though she would not admit that Miss Keeldar’s hasty words had wounded her. Yet the pain was evident—quiet, persistent, like a bruise beneath the skin. To anyone else, Mrs. Pryor might have seemed indifferent to the gentle, tender efforts Caroline made to comfort her; but Caroline understood that beneath her stillness lay deep feeling, and that every kind word was noticed, valued, and slowly mended her spirit.

“I lack self-confidence,” Mrs. Pryor said at last, her voice low and steady. “Always have. And yet I thought Miss Keeldar knew me well enough by now to understand that I never act without care—never without a desire to do what is right, even when it costs me.” She paused, then added, “The situation was so unusual—coming hard on the heels of last night’s alarms. I couldn’t make a decision for someone else’s sake. But I trust no real harm will come from my hesitation.”

A soft knock sounded at the door. It opened slightly.

“Caroline,” came a quiet voice. “Come here.”

Miss Helstone stepped out into the gallery. There stood Shirley, looking small and contrite, as if she were a child caught in a misdeed—ashamed, sorrowful, and full of regret.

“How is Mrs. Pryor?” she asked.

“Not very cheerful,” Caroline replied gently.

Shirley looked down. “I’ve behaved terribly—unfairly, ungratefully. How could I turn on her like that? It wasn’t her fault at all—only her kindness, her over-scrupulousness. I’m truly sorry. Please tell her that—and ask if she can forgive me.”

Caroline went back with a heart full of relief. Mrs. Pryor rose and stood at the doorway. She disliked scenes; they frightened her, as they do all who are timid. “Come in, my dear,” she said, her voice trembling slightly.

Shirley entered quickly, throwing her arms around her governess and kissing her warmly. “You must forgive me, Mrs. Pryor,” she whispered. “I couldn’t bear it if there were any distance between us. I need you too much.”

“There’s nothing to forgive,” Mrs. Pryor replied. “Let it go. The only thing this has shown me is how ill-equipped I am for moments like these.”

And that was the truth she could not shake off. No amount of Shirley’s or Caroline’s affection could erase the ache of her own perceived failure. She could forgive her pupil—but not herself.

Miss Keeldar, busy with callers throughout the morning, was soon summoned downstairs again. The rector arrived first, greeted with both warmth and playful reproach. He expected both, and in his good humour, accepted them with equal grace.

In the short time he stayed, he forgot entirely to ask after his niece. His mind was filled with the events of the night—the riot, the mob, the mill, the magistrates, the heiress. He spoke of his role in defending the Hollow.

“The wrath of the Pharisees will fall upon our heads for standing firm,” he declared. “But I defy every slanderer. I was there to uphold the law—to act as a man and a Briton. These roles, I believe, are fully compatible with those of priest and Levite, in their noblest sense.”

He praised Moore: “A cooler commander I’ve never seen, nor one more resolute. He showed sound judgment—first in preparing thoroughly for what happened, and later, when victory was secured, in knowing how to use power wisely. Some magistrates are now afraid, and fear makes men cruel. But Moore restrains them with admirable calm. Once despised, he may now find favor turning his way. People will realize they underestimated him, and rush to correct their mistake. And once he sees public opinion shifting, he’ll likely show more grace than he ever has before.”

Mr. Helstone was about to add a half-joking warning to Shirley about her rumored affection for Moore when a ring at the door interrupted him. A visitor arrived—a white-haired gentleman with a stern face and sharp eyes. It was Mr. Yorke, the rector’s old adversary.

At sight of him, the rector snatched up his hat, gave a brief farewell to Shirley, and nodded curtly at Yorke before leaving abruptly.

Mr. Yorke was in no mood for diplomacy. He launched into a fierce critique of the night’s events. Moore, the magistrates, the soldiers, the mob leaders—all were condemned in turn. But he reserved his harshest words—rich with Yorkshire dialect—for the clergy. The “sanguinary, demoniac” parsons, he called them. The church, he said, was now in a “bonny pickle.” It was time it fell when ministers began swaggering with soldiers, firing guns, taking lives.

“The church,” he declared, “is in a mess. It’s time it came down when parsons start acting like warriors, killing honest folk who’re better than themselves.”

“What would Moore have done if no one helped him?” Shirley asked.

“He’d have been drunk as he brewed, eaten as he baked.”
