“Which means you’d have left him alone to face the mob. Well, he has courage—but even the bravest heart couldn’t stand two hundred angry men.”

“He had soldiers—poor souls who sell their blood and spill others’ for pay.”

“You condemn soldiers almost as fiercely as you condemn clergymen,” Shirley retorted. “To you, red coats are national refuse, black gowns are national swindlers. You say Moore was wrong to call in troops—and worse, to accept help from anyone. So your logic says he should have abandoned his mill and his life to a mob of madmen, while Mr. Helstone and every other gentleman watched silently as the building burned and its owner died—without lifting a finger.”

“If Moore had treated his workers like a true master from the start,” Yorke insisted, “they wouldn’t feel as they do now.”

“Easy for you to say,” Shirley shot back, warming to her defense of Moore. “You’ve lived at Briarmains for six generations. The people know you. You understand their ways, their fears, their habits. But Mr. Moore came here as a stranger—poor, friendless, with only his talent and industry to rely on. Is it a crime that he couldn’t instantly win their love? That he didn’t become jocular and familiar with a peasantry he barely knew? That he introduced improvements without bending every change to suit their whims? Should he be punished by mob violence for such mistakes? Denied even the right to defend himself? And should those who stand beside him—like Mr. Helstone, however flawed—be mocked as criminals for doing what’s just?”

“Now, now,” Yorke said, smiling faintly at her intensity. “Be calm.”

“Calm? Must I stay calm while listening to nonsense—dangerous nonsense? No. I like you, Mr. Yorke, I really do. But I detest some of your principles. All that talk—excuse me, but I’ll say it again—*that cant* about soldiers and parsons—it grates on my ears. Ridiculous, irrational glorification of one class—whether aristocrat or democrat—and denigration of another—clerical or military—without regard for individuals, without justice. It’s sickening. All division, all party hatred, all tyranny masked as liberty—I reject it all. You think you’re a philanthropist, a champion of freedom. But let me tell you—Mr. Hall, the parson of Nunnely, is a far greater friend to humanity and liberty than Hiram Yorke, reformer of Briarfield.”

From most men, such words would have drawn anger. From many women, silence or defiance. But Yorke saw something else in Shirley—honesty and beauty, fire and charm. Her passion amused him. And secretly, he took pleasure in watching her defend Moore—because he cared deeply for Robert Moore himself. If he wished to wound her, he knew how: a single word could silence her, flush her cheeks, dim her eyes with shame.

“What more have you to say?” he asked, as she paused—not because she was spent, but to gather breath.

“Say? I have much to say—if only I could put it clearly. But I never can. I must say this: your views, and those of extreme politicians, are only possible for men who don’t have to govern. They’re opposition ideas—meant for speeches, not action. Make you Prime Minister tomorrow, and you’d abandon them. You condemn Moore for defending his mill. But if you were in his place, you’d have done exactly the same—honourably, sensibly. You attack Mr. Helstone for everything he does. He has flaws, yes—but more often he does right. If you were vicar here, you’d find it hard to carry on all the good works your predecessor started. Why can’t people judge each other fairly?

“When I hear Messrs. Malone and Donne speak of the church’s authority, the dignity of the priesthood, their outrage at Dissenters, their petty jealousies, their obsession with forms and traditions, their arrogance toward the poor, their servility to the rich—I see the Establishment in dire need of reform. And then I recall your sneers at ‘fat bishops,’ ‘pampered parsons,’ ‘old mother church.’ Your sweeping judgments against whole classes, without mercy for circumstances—then I wonder: are there any men left who are wise, fair, and just enough to lead reform? I don’t believe *you* are one.”

“You have a poor opinion of me, Miss Shirley,” Yorke said. “You’ve never told me this before.”

“I never had the chance. But I’ve sat on Jessy’s stool by your side in the back parlour, listening to your words—half admiring, half rebelling. You’re a fine old Yorkshireman. I’m proud to share your county, your parish. You’re truthful, upright, unshakable as a rock beneath the sea. But you’re also harsh, narrow, merciless.”

“Only to the proud and high-minded,” he replied.

“And what right have you to draw such lines? You’re prouder than anyone I know. You speak kindly to your inferiors—but you’re too proud, too ambitious, too jealous to treat those above you with respect. But you’re not alone. Mr. Helstone is proud and prejudiced. Moore, though fairer than either of you, is still stern, distant, selfish in public. It’s good there are men like Mr. Hall—men with wide hearts, who can love all mankind, who can forgive others for being richer, stronger, more powerful. Such men may lack your force, your originality—but they are better friends to humanity.”

“And when is it to be?” Yorke asked, rising.

“When is what?”

“The wedding.”

“Whose wedding?”

“Only that of Robert Gérard Moore, Esq., of Hollow’s Cottage, with Miss Keeldar, daughter and heiress of Charles Cave Keeldar of Fieldhead Hall.”

Shirley stared at him, her face flushing. But her eyes did not falter—they shone with a quiet fire, deep and bright.

“That’s your revenge,” she said slowly.

“Would it be an unworthy match?” she added. “Unworthy of the late Charles Cave Keeldar’s heir?”

“My lass,” Yorke answered, “Moore is a gentleman. His blood is as pure and ancient as mine or yours.”

“And we value ancient blood? We have family pride—even if one of us calls herself a republican?”

Yorke bowed. His lips remained silent, but his eyes confessed the truth. Yes—he had pride. It was written in every line of his bearing.

“Moore *is* a gentleman,” Shirley echoed, lifting her head with quiet joy. Then she stopped. Words surged in her mouth, but she held them back. Her look said everything. Yorke tried to read it—but failed. It was a poem in a language unknown—fervent, complex, not mere confession. It was deeper than love, more intricate than longing. He felt his revenge had missed. She was not humbled—she triumphed. He was baffled, uncertain. *She* owned the moment.

“And if Moore is a gentleman,” he said, “you can only be a lady. Therefore—”

“Therefore, no inequality in our union.”

“None.”

“Thank you for your approval. Will you give me away when I leave the name Keeldar for Moore?”

Instead of answering, Yorke stared at her, puzzled. Could she mean it? Was it jest or earnest?

“I don’t understand you,” he said, turning away.

She laughed softly. “Take courage, sir. You’re not alone in your confusion. But if Moore understands me—that will suffice, won’t it?”

“Moore may manage his own affairs from now on,” Yorke replied. “I’ll meddle no more.”

A sudden thought struck her. Her expression changed—eyes darkened, features set with gravity.

“Have you been asked to interfere?” she demanded. “Are you questioning me as someone else’s representative?”

“The Lord save us!” Yorke exclaimed. “Whoever weds you must watch his step! Keep your questions for Robert. I’ll answer none more. Good-day, lassie!”

* * * * *

The day was fair—soft clouds veiled the sun, and a blue haze rested gently on the hills. While Shirley was occupied with visitors, Caroline had persuaded Mrs. Pryor to put on her bonnet and summer shawl and take a walk up the narrow end of the Hollow.

Here, the sides of the glen drew close, thick with brushwood and stunted oaks, forming a wooded ravine. At its bottom, the mill-stream ran in broken course—tumbling over stones, chafing against banks, foaming through gnarled roots. Half a mile from the mill, the path led into deep solitude. Trees offered shelter, birds sang in abundance, and wildflowers bloomed untouched by foot. The air was fresh, the roses seemed to bloom unseen, like secrets in a hidden garden. Bluebells shimmered in sweet azure, and tiny white blossoms dotted the grass like stars fallen to earth.

Mrs. Pryor loved quiet walks. She avoided main roads, preferring winding lanes and secluded paths. Solitude frightened her—vague fears of encounters spoiled her peace. But with Caroline, she was safe. When they walked together, away from human habitation, a change came over her. Her face softened, her spirits lifted. With Caroline—only Caroline—her heart lightened, her thoughts loosened. She became cheerful, tender, willing to share knowledge, glimpses of her past, her understanding of nature and life.

Today, as they walked, she pointed out the birds singing in the trees—naming them, describing their habits. English natural history seemed part of her soul. Every wildflower along the path had a name, a story. Tiny plants growing near stones or in wall crevices—ones Caroline had never noticed—were identified and explained. She had studied botany closely, quietly, over years.

They reached the head of the ravine and sat on a moss-covered rock beneath a steep green hill. Mrs. Pryor looked around, speaking of the landscape as she remembered it long ago. She compared it with other parts of England, revealing a subtle eye for beauty, a quiet appreciation of nature’s contrasts—wild and cultivated, grand and plain. Her words painted scenes with simple grace.

Caroline listened with reverent attention—so quiet, so sincere, yet unmistakable. It stirred Mrs. Pryor’s spirit. Rarely had she known what it was to inspire such genuine affection in someone she could love. To see a young girl look up to her, lean on her, trust her—this was a rare delight. With a slight warmth in her voice, she gently brushed a curl from Caroline’s forehead.

“I hope this mountain air is good for you, my dear,” she said. “I wish I could see more colour in your cheeks. Perhaps you were never florid?”

“I had rosy cheeks once,” Caroline smiled. “Two years ago, I used to see a different face in the mirror—rounder, brighter. But when we’re young,” she added, “our minds are careless, our lives easy.”

“Do you worry about the future at your age?” Mrs. Pryor asked, forcing herself to speak boldly. “Believe me, it’s better not to. Let tomorrow take care of itself.”

“True, dear madam. I don’t pine for the future. But the present—sometimes it weighs too heavily. I long to escape it.”

“That is—the evil of the day—is it perhaps… your uncle? Do you find it hard to understand him? Does he not appreciate…”

She couldn’t finish. But Caroline understood.

“Oh, that’s nothing,” she replied. “My uncle and I get along well. We never quarrel. I don’t think he’s harsh. He never scolds me. Sometimes I wish someone loved me—but I don’t particularly wish for him to love me more than he does. As a child, I might have missed attention—but the servants were kind. And when people are indifferent to us for long, we grow indifferent to their indifference. It’s just his way—not to care for women and girls unless they’re ladies he meets in society. He couldn’t change, and I don’t want him to. I think it would frighten me if he tried to be affectionate now.”

“But you know, Mrs. Pryor,” she continued, “it’s scarcely *living* to measure time as I do at the rectory. The hours pass, and I endure them—but I don’t *live*. Since Miss Keeldар and you came, I’ve been… I was going to say happier—but that would be untrue.”

She paused.

“How untrue?” Mrs. Pryor asked gently.

“I *do* love Shirley,” Caroline said. “I admire her, I care for her. But I’m painfully trapped. For reasons I can’t explain, I want to leave this place—and forget it.”

Moore’s strength had shown itself fully when defending his mill—yet it was in the relentless pursuit of the riot’s leaders that he revealed his true nature, fierce and unyielding. He spared the common folk who had joined the mob, those driven by hunger and false promises. To him, they were not villains, but victims—men misled by others, their spirits broken by hardship. He believed vengeance on such souls was tyranny, not justice. Though he recognized many of them as the sun rose over the wreckage, he let them pass without a word, without a glance, as if they were shadows slipping through his life.

But the ringleaders? They were strangers—men from distant cities, emissaries of chaos. Most were not workers at all, but outcasts: men who had lost everything—credit, home, dignity—and now sought to burn down what remained. These were the ones Moore hunted with the ferocity of a bloodhound. And he relished it. There was something thrilling in the chase, something that stirred his spirit more than the drudgery of weaving cloth ever could. His horse must have known it too—for it was ridden hard and often, its breath ragged under the strain. Moore lived on the road now, breathing in the wind and cold air like a man born to freedom. The scent of damp earth, the cry of the curlew, the vastness of the moors—it all fed his soul. Even the magistrate’s fear pleased him. They were timid men, slow to act, quick to tremble. He liked to see them flinch, to feel the weight of danger press upon them—because he knew the truth: they feared assassination. That dread had long held back every manufacturer, every official in the region. Only Helstone had ever stood firm. The old warrior knew death might come swiftly, but he faced it without trembling. It would have been his choice, had he had one.

Moore, too, knew the risk. But he wore it like armor. The knowledge that he pursued men who meant to kill him only sharpened his resolve. Fear? He did not know it. Not because he lacked feeling, but because his nature was hard, proud, and quiet—like stone beneath a storm. He rode late into the night across the moors, moonlit or dark, and each journey left him more alive than any day spent behind the counting-house. He felt stronger, clearer, freer.

Four leaders were wanted. Two were cornered near Stilbro’ within two weeks. The other two vanished toward Birmingham, and Moore set off after them, determined to bring them down. Yet even as he chased justice, he did not forget his mill. Repairs were simple—just carpenters and glaziers needed. The machines, his “metal darlings,” had survived intact, untouched by the mob’s fury. Still, one wondered—amid this fever of duty and danger—did he ever pause? Did he ever allow himself a moment to remember something softer, something quieter? He rarely visited Fieldhead. When he did, it was brief. At the rectory, he came only for business, never lingering. His course was rigid, unbroken.

Meanwhile, the world outside grew no calmer. War still roared across Europe, its storms unrelenting. No peace broke through the clouds of smoke and battle-dust. No rain fell to bless the olive; only the red rain of war nourished the laurel. Beneath Moore’s feet, Ruin dug tunnels, sapping the foundations of his life. Whether he walked or rode, whether he crossed his hearth or galloped over Rushedge, he heard the hollow echo and felt the ground tremble beneath him.

While Moore lived thus, how passed the summer with Shirley and Caroline?

Let us first go to Shirley.

Does she sit pale and sorrowful, longing for a lover who ignores her? Does she spend her days sewing or reading, eyes fixed on the page, heart closed to the world? Not at all. Shirley is radiant. Her face still holds its wistful look—but also her careless smile. She fills the ancient manor house with light and sound. The gallery echoes with her laughter. The dim hall, once silent, now rustles with the sweep of silk as she moves from room to room—carrying flowers to the peach-bloom salon, opening casements to let in the scent of mignonette and sweetbriar, bringing plants from the staircase to bathe in sunlight at the porch.

She sews sometimes—but never for long. Her thimble is barely on, her needle threaded, when a sudden thought calls her away. Perhaps she runs upstairs to find an old ivory needle-book, though she doesn’t need it. Or maybe to fix her hair, or tidy a drawer she remembers was messy. Or just to stand at a certain window, watching Briarfield church and rectory nestled in green trees.

She returns—only to be summoned again. Tartar, her dog, scrapes and whines at the door. She must open it, see to his water, watch him drink. Then, through the kitchen, she sees the courtyard: turkeys with their poults, peahens with chicks, Guinea fowls spotted with pearl, pigeons white and blue and purple. She cannot resist. She fetches a roll from the pantry and scatters crumbs. The birds rush to her, eager, plump, joyful—her feathered vassals.

John is at the stables. She must talk to him, pet her mare. As the cows return to be milked, she stays to inspect them—perhaps there are newborn calves, rejected lambs. She feeds them herself, guided by John. Then comes a question about farming—the "crofts," the "ings," the "holms." She dons her gipsy straw hat and walks with him over stiles and along hedgerows, discussing the land as if it were her own.

The afternoon slips into evening. She returns for tea. Afterward, she reads—not sitting upright, but curled on the rug, her seat a footstool, or perhaps beside Mrs. Pryor’s feet—where she always sat as a child. Old habits hold strong.

Tartar lies beside her, massive and calm, his black muzzle resting on his paws, like a wolf’s. One hand rests gently on his head. If she removes it, he groans.

Her mind is lost in the book. She does not move, does not speak—unless Mrs. Pryor says, “My dear, you should not have that great dog so near. He’s crushing your dress.”

“Oh, it’s only muslin. I can wear another tomorrow.”

“My dear, you should sit at a table when you read.”

“I’ll try, ma'am—but it’s so comfortable to do as I’ve always done.”

“My dear, please put the book down. You’re straining your eyes in this dim light.”

“No, ma'am. My eyes never tire.”

At last, the moon rises. A pale glow falls on the page. She closes the book, stands, and walks through the room. The story has stirred her—refreshed her, warmed her. Her thoughts soar. The quiet parlour, the clean hearth, the twilight sky—these things make the world seem like Eden. A deep joy flows through her veins—pure, untouched, given freely by God and Nature. She feels almost divine, lifted high on green hills, standing near where angels once looked down. She sees life as she wishes it—no, not as she wishes, for she does not wish. The vision spreads faster than thought, brighter than desire.

She says nothing. But if Mrs. Pryor speaks, she quietly leaves, walking up the dim gallery.

If Shirley were less idle, less reckless, less ignorant, she might write down these moments. She might capture the beauty, fix the vision. But she does not. She does not know her dreams are rare, her feelings extraordinary. She does not realize that the spring within her heart keeps it forever green.

She takes life easily. Is it not written in her eyes? In her calm moments, they are soft and lazy; in anger, they blaze like fire. Her nature lives in her gaze. So long as she is at peace, indolence, humor, tenderness fill that large gray sphere. But provoke her, and a red spark ignites—flame in an instant.

By mid-July, Shirley and Caroline were to begin their northern tour. But fate intervened. An invasion came to Fieldhead—a genteel raid. An uncle, an aunt, and two cousins from the south—Mr., Mrs., and Misses Sympson of Sympson Grove—arrived in full state. Hospitality demanded surrender. Shirley gave in—easily, almost willingly. This surprised Caroline, who knew her to be decisive and clever when victory was hers. Even Miss Helstone asked why she submitted so readily.

“Old feelings still have power,” Shirley replied. “I spent two years of my youth at Sympson Grove.”

“And do you like them?”

“Not really. Nothing in common. Little Harry Sympson, the only son, was different—he was kind. But he’s not coming here.”

On Sunday, the Fieldhead pew in Briarfield Church held a prim gentleman shifting his spectacles every three minutes, a placid woman in brown satin, and two young ladies in perfect, patterned attire. Shirley stood among them like a black swan—out of place, lonely. We leave her now in this respectable company, and turn to Caroline.

Separated from Shirley, Caroline returned to the rectory. She walked alone in remote paths, sat in the quiet parlour, or in the garden alcove where the sun shone bright yet sad on ripening currants and climbing roses. Their shadows fell in chequered patterns across her white summer dress—still as a statue.

She read books from her uncle’s library. The Greek and Latin meant nothing to her. The rest—Lady’s Magazines stained with saltwater from a sea voyage, mad Methodist tracts full of visions and miracles, letters from the dead, old English classics—had once nourished her childhood. Now they tasted flat.

For change, and to do good, she sewed—clothes for the poor, according to Miss Ainley’s plans. Sometimes, as tears fell slowly onto the fabric, she wondered how Miss Ainley managed to stay so calm. Her cottage was dim, her life solitary, yet she never seemed despondent.

“She says she has trained her thoughts to rise to heaven,” Caroline mused. “She admits this world offers little joy. She looks forward to the next. Like nuns—locked in cells, lit by iron lamps, wrapped in robes like shrouds. She says she fears death not at all. But then, neither did St. Simeon on his column, nor the Hindu on his bed of spikes. They have broken nature—they grow morbid. I still fear death. But perhaps it’s because I am young.”

She continued: “Nobody is to blame for this state of things. I cannot say how to fix it. But I feel something is wrong. Women—especially single women—should have more to do. Better chances. More purpose. I don’t think I offend God by saying this. I’m not impious. I believe God hears the groans we ignore, the sorrows we scorn. Society forbids us to speak of such ills—because speaking of them reminds people of their own helplessness. They hate to be reminded. They’d rather laugh than act.

“Look at the girls here—the Armitages, the Birtwhistles, the Sykeses. Their brothers are in business, in professions. Their sisters? Sewing, cooking, visiting—nothing else. No hope. They grow weak, their minds shrink. Their only dream is marriage. Most will never marry. They will die as they live. They scheme, dress, flirt. Men mock them. ‘The market is overstocked,’ they say. Fathers scold them, send them home. What do they expect them to do? Sew and cook? Forever? As if they had no talents beyond that. As if men themselves could endure such lives!

“Lucretia spinning at midnight? Solomon’s virtuous woman? They are held up as ideals. But Lucretia kept her maidens awake late. I wouldn’t want to be one of them. And the virtuous woman—she wasn’t just spinning. She made linen, sold it, bought land, planted vineyards. She was a manager. A real woman. I like her better. Strength and honor were her clothing. Her husband trusted her. She spoke wisely, ruled kindly. Her children blessed her. That’s a model worth following.

“But are our daughters taught to be like her? Can they reach such heights? Can you give them a field to grow in? Men of England—look at your girls. Many fade into consumption, or become bitter, sour old maids, envious, hateful, because life is a desert. Or worse—driven to use tricks and artifice to gain respect through marriage. Fathers—can you not change this? Not all at once. But think on it. Do not dismiss it with a joke or insult. You want to be proud of your daughters. Then give them work, give them purpose. Let them grow. Keep their minds narrow, and they will be a burden. Cultivate them—and they will be your joy, your comfort, your strength.”

On a bright summer afternoon, Caroline sat alone in the garden alcove, her uncle away at Whinbury. The hours stretched long and silent—sunlit, breezeless, cloudless—as if time itself had paused in an endless, shadowless desert. Yet this was no barren waste, but the blooming garden of an English home, and still, to Caroline, it felt as desolate as any wasteland. Her fingers moved steadily over her needlework, stitching a child’s slip for the Jew’s basket, while her mind wandered restlessly, burdened by solitude and quiet grief.

Suddenly, Fanny appeared at the door, scanning the lawn and flowerbeds before calling out, “Miss Caroline!”

A soft voice replied, “Fanny!”—coming from within the alcove. Fanny hurried forward, holding a note, which she handed with careful hands. Caroline took it without question, without looking—letting it fall among the folds of her work.

“Harry Scott brought it,” Fanny said.

The name stirred something deep within her. She lifted her head, eyes alight with sudden life, and turned toward Fanny. “Harry Scott? Who sent him?”

“He came from the Hollow.”

Caroline snatched up the letter, tore open the seal, and read it in seconds. It was a warm message from Hortense, telling her that she had returned from Wormwood Wells, that Robert was away at market, and that she would be delighted to have Caroline join her for tea. With gentle insistence, she added that such a change would do Caroline good—especially now, when she had been deprived of both companionship and guidance since the rift between Robert and Mr. Helstone had severed her bond with her dear friend, Hortense Gérard Moore. A postscript urged her: *Put on your bonnet and come down at once.*

She needed no urging. Joy surged through her like a spring tide. She set aside her sewing, climbed the stairs, tied her straw bonnet over her curls, and wrapped the black silk scarf around her shoulders—its simple elegance framing her pale face and delicate dress. She longed to escape the silence, the sorrow, the weight of days that seemed to press upon her like a dream gone wrong. To walk down the green lane toward the Hollow, breathing in the sweet scent of wildflowers, was already a relief. True, Robert wasn’t there—but merely being near his cottage, stepping into the room where he had sat that morning, felt like a reunion. And in that moment, illusion whispered again, veiled in soft wings: *Robert may return while you’re here. You might see him. You might touch his hand. You might sit beside him, just for a moment.*

“Silence,” she murmured, though her heart beat faster.

From the window, Miss Moore likely saw the flash of white against the greenery and stepped out to greet her. As always, she moved with calm dignity—no haste, no flourish. But she smiled warmly, pleased to see Caroline’s joy, happy to receive her kiss and embrace. She led her inside, half deceived, wholly flattered. Half deceived? Perhaps. Had she truly known what stirred beneath Caroline’s happiness, she might have barred the gate and turned her away. Sisters rarely welcome young women falling in love with their brothers. It seems foolish, romantic, even presumptuous. They don’t love those men themselves—and so they resent others who do. The first reaction is often a mix of irritation and disdain. Only reason, if it prevails, can soften such feelings. But if not, the woman becomes an object of lifelong disfavor.

“You expected me alone,” Miss Moore said, guiding Caroline toward the parlour, “but I wrote this morning. Since dinner, we’ve had company.”

She opened the door to reveal a sea of crimson skirts spilling over an armchair by the fire, crowned by a cap more imposing than any crown. The cap had not arrived under a bonnet—it had been carried in a vast black silk bag, shaped like a balloon, stiffened with whalebone. Its frill stood wide around the wearer’s face, and ribbons—love-ribbon, thick and puffed—danced across her head. Mrs. Yorke wore it proudly. She wore the gown too—fitting her perfectly.

The great lady had come uninvited, to take tea with Miss Moore—an honor almost as rare as if the queen herself had dropped in on a humble household. Mrs. Yorke rarely visited; she scorned social calls, dismissed neighbors as “gossips.” Yet today, she had chosen to grace the cottage with her presence—a mark of favor few could claim.

Caroline approached with some hesitation. She knew little of Mrs. Yorke, and as a parson’s niece, she wondered how she’d be received. The reception was cool. She welcomed the chance to remove her bonnet and hide her discomfort. Then, a small figure in blue and sash leapt from behind the chair, arms flying toward her.

“My mother isn’t kind to you,” the girl declared, throwing her arms around Caroline’s neck and demanding a kiss.

“She doesn’t notice you either,” Caroline said gently, returning the embrace.

“Mother is frozen,” the girl said. “Rose never looks up. If a white angel with a star-crown walked in, mother would nod stiffly, and Rose wouldn’t lift her head. But I’ll be your friend—I’ve always liked you.”

“Jessie, restrain yourself!” Mrs. Yorke commanded.

“But mother, why are you so cold?” Jessie pleaded. “Miss Helstone hasn’t done anything wrong. Why can’t you be kind? You sit stiff, speak dry, look icy. That’s how you treat Miss Shirley Keeldar and every other guest. And Rose—she’s an automaton! A machine made in human shape. Between you two, you’ll drive everyone away. Martin says so.”

“I am an automaton?” Rose said, rising from the floor where she sat reading, a book open on her lap. “Then let me be left alone.” She glanced briefly at Caroline. “Miss Helstone, how do you do?”

Caroline studied her quietly—this young girl absorbed in her book, her lips moving slightly as she read, full of character. She had instinct, tact. She sensed Rose was different—unique. Approaching softly, she knelt beside her and peered over her shoulder. The book was *The Italian* by Mrs. Radcliffe.

They read together in silence. Soon, Rose asked, “Are you ready?” before turning the page.

Caroline nodded.

“Do you like it?” Rose asked later.

“I did when I was younger,” Caroline replied. “It promised such mystery—such a strange tale.”

“And when you read it, you feel far from England—really in Italy, under a southern sky, blue and burning.”

“You sense that, Rose?”

“It makes me want to travel,” Rose said. “When I’m a woman, I’ll go. I can’t stay in Briarfield forever. The world is small compared to creation. I must see beyond our round planet.”

“How much of it?”

“All of it. First this hemisphere, then the other. I mean to make my way. Not live like a toad buried in marble—nor like you, shut in that rectory, dying slowly.”

“Like me? What do you mean?”

“Wouldn’t it be better to wander than to stay locked in that house—like a windowed grave? I never see movement there. No sound. No smoke from the chimneys. What do you do?”

“I sew, read, learn.”

“Are you happy?”

“Wouldn’t I be happier wandering, as you wish?”

“Much happier—even if you did nothing but wander. But I’ll have a purpose. If you just drift like a fairy tale’s enchanted lady, you might still be happier than you are now. In one day’s walking, you’d pass hills, woods, streams—each changing with the sun, the weather, the light. Nothing changes in Briarfield rectory. The ceilings, walls, curtains, carpets—they’re all the same.”

“Is change necessary for happiness?”

“Yes.”

“Is it the same as happiness?”

“I don’t know. But monotony feels like death.”

Jessie interrupted. “Isn’t she mad?”

But Rose continued. “Better to try everything and find it empty than to try nothing and leave life blank. That’s the sin of the man who buried his talent in a napkin—a despicable sluggard!”

Mrs. Yorke spoke. “Solid satisfaction comes only from duty.”

“Right, mother,” Rose said. “If my Master gave me ten talents, my duty is to trade them and make ten more. Not bury them in a drawer. Not hide them in a broken teapot. Not smother them in woolen socks. Not lock them in a linen chest. Least of all”—she rose—“will I hide them in a tureen of cold potatoes, stacked with bread and butter on the larder shelf.”

She paused. “Mother, the Lord who gave us our talents will return one day and demand an account. The teapot, the old sock, the rag, the tureen—they’ll give up their barren coins in many homes. Let your daughters at least put their money to the exchangers, so they can repay Him with interest.”

“Did you bring your sampler?” Mrs. Yorke asked.

“Yes, mother.”

“Sit and mark a line.”

Rose obeyed. After ten minutes, her mother asked, “Do you feel oppressed now?”

“No, mother.”

“Yet you railed against domestic work.”

“I didn’t. I’d be sorry not to learn to sew. You’re right to teach me.”

“Even mending stockings and making sheets?”

“Yes.”

“So why rant about it?”

“Am I to do nothing else? I’ll do that—and more. Now, mother, I’ve spoken. I’m twelve. For four years, I’ll be your industrious apprentice. I won’t speak again on this until I’m sixteen.”

Mrs. Yorke turned to Caroline. “You see my daughters, Miss Helstone. Precociously wise in their own minds. Jessie sings ‘I’d rather this, I prefer that.’ Rose cries, ‘I *will*, and I *will not*!’”

“I give reasons, mother,” Rose said. “And my cry is bold, but only once a year. Each birthday, the spirit moves me to deliver one oracle. I say it and leave it. It’s for you to listen—or not.”

Mrs. Yorke addressed Caroline again. “I advise young ladies to study such children before marriage. Think how you’d handle the careless, the stubborn, the hard-to-train.”

“But with love, it needn’t be so hard,” Caroline said. “Mothers love their children deeply—almost more than themselves.”

“Sentimental talk,” Mrs. Yorke replied. “There’s the rough part of life yet to come.”

“But if I hold a baby—any poor woman’s infant—I feel I love it deeply, even if I’m not its mother. I’d do anything for it if it were mine.”

“You *feel*,” Mrs. Yorke said sharply. “Yes, yes. You’re ruled by emotion. You think yourself sensitive. But do you know you’ve trained your face into a lackadaisical expression—more suited to a novel heroine than a woman who must navigate the real world with common sense?”

“No,” Caroline said. “I don’t.”

“Look in the mirror. Compare your face with a milkmaid’s.”

“My face is pale, but not sentimental. Most milkmaids are less practical than I am. I think more clearly. Where they act weakly from lack of thought, I act wisely.”

“Oh, you’d be guided by impulse,” Mrs. Yorke said. “By feeling.”

“Of course I would. Feelings were given to me for a reason. If my feelings tell me to love someone, I *must* love them. I hope, if I ever have a husband or children, my feelings will compel me to love them. I hope my impulses will lead me to care.”

She said it with quiet strength. She enjoyed daring to speak so plainly before Mrs. Yorke. She didn’t care for sarcasm. She flushed—not with anger, but with passion.

Mrs. Yorke replied coolly, “Don’t waste your drama. That was fine. But it means nothing to two old women—an old wife and an old maid. There should have been a gentleman present. Is Mr. Robert hiding behind the curtains, Miss Moore?”

Hortense, who had been in the kitchen, looked puzzled. “Robert is at Whinbury.”

Mrs. Yorke laughed her short, sharp laugh. “Straightforward Miss Moore. You answer literally. Your mind knows nothing of intrigue. Strange things could happen around you, and you’d never notice. You’re not sharp-witted.”

Hortense stiffened. “I’ve always been noted for wisdom and discernment.”

“You never plotted to win a husband, I’ll wager. You haven’t the experience to spot when others do.”

Caroline felt the barb. She couldn’t defend herself—because to reply would be to admit the truth. Mrs. Yorke watched her—eyes downcast, cheeks burning, body trembling with shame. She relished the moment. She disliked sensitive people, nervous temperaments, delicate faces. Rarely did she encounter all three in one person—and even rarer did she find such a person within her grasp. Today, she was especially irritable—like a fierce cow ready to gore.

She lowered her head and struck again. “Your cousin Hortense is a good sister. But some ladies come here hoping to charm the mistress and take control. You enjoy her company, I suppose?”

“Which cousin?”

“The lady’s, of course.”

“Hortense has always been kind to me.”

“Every sister with a single brother is considered kind by spinster friends.”

“Mrs. Yorke,” Caroline said, lifting her eyes—clear, steady, calm—her blush fading into pale resolve—“may I ask what you mean?”

“To teach you about honesty. To disgust you with deceit and false sentiment.”

“Do I need such a lesson?”

“Most young ladies do. You’re modern—delicate, morbid, fond of solitude. That implies you find little worth in ordinary life. But ordinary people—honest folk—are better than you think. Better than any bookish, romantic girl who never ventures beyond her uncle’s garden wall.”

“Consequently, you know nothing of me. Excuse me—it doesn’t matter. You’ve attacked me without cause. I’ll defend myself without apology. You know nothing of my relationship with my cousins. In a fit of ill-humor, you’ve poisoned them with insinuations more cruel and false than anything you could accuse me of. My pallor, my shyness—none of it concerns you. My love of books, my dislike of gossip—that’s none of your business. Calling me a ‘romancing chit’ is mere guesswork. I’ve never romanced before you or anyone you know. Being the parson’s niece is not a crime, though you may think so. You dislike me. You have no reason. Keep your dislike to yourself. If you ever show it again, I’ll answer even less kindly.”

She stopped. She sat still, pale, composed. Her voice had been clear, calm, unshaken. Yet beneath it, her blood raced like a hidden stream.

Mrs. Yorke was not angry. She turned to Hortense, nodding. “She has spirit, after all. Always speak honestly, as you’ve done.”

“I reject such advice,” Caroline said, her voice steady. “Counsel tainted with insult I refuse. I have the right to speak as I choose. I won’t speak harshly unless provoked.”

“Mother, you’ve found your match,” Jessie said, impressed.

Rose remained silent. Then she said, “No. Miss Helstone isn’t your match. She lets herself be troubled. You’d wear her out in weeks. Shirley Keeldar handles you better. Mother, you’ve never hurt Miss Keeldar’s feelings. She wears armor under her silk dress—you can’t pierce it.”

The future sometimes whispers its warnings in hushed tones, like a storm gathering far off—its breath stirring the air, its clouds tearing across the sky, hinting at winds that may scatter ships or bring pestilence on silent fog. At other times, it bursts forth without warning, as if a mountain split open and revealed a grave from which a sleeper rises—unseen, unprepared, yet unmistakably present. One moment you are unaware, the next you stand face to face with a sorrow wrapped in silence—a new Lazarus returned.

Caroline Helstone returned home from Hollow's Cottage feeling well, or so she believed. The following morning, however, she woke with a strange heaviness in her limbs, a weariness unlike any she had known before. At breakfast, then at every meal through the day, appetite fled. Food that once pleased her now tasted of ash and dust.

"Am I ill?" she asked, gazing into the mirror. Her eyes sparkled, their pupils wide; her cheeks were flushed, fuller than usual. "I look well—then why can I not eat?"

She felt her temples throb with a quick pulse, her mind stirred by thoughts that danced too fast to follow—bright, scattered, brilliant images flickering behind her eyelids. A warmth seemed to glow within her, mirroring the flush on her skin.

Then came a night of feverish heat, dry throat, restless sleep. Toward dawn, a dream seized her—so vivid, so terrifying, it clung like a tiger’s grip. When she awoke, she knew: she was sick.

How she caught the illness—fever, they would soon call it—she could not say. Perhaps it was the breeze that carried her home, sweet with honeydew and something darker, invisible, poisonous. It slipped into her lungs, into her veins, finding already within her a fire of mental strain and long-held sorrow, and fanned the embers until they blazed.

Yet this fire burned gently at first. After two days of heat and troubled nights, no violent signs appeared. Her uncle, Fanny, the doctor, even Miss Keeldar when she called, saw nothing alarming. All agreed: a few days’ rest would restore her.

But the days passed, and still no recovery came. Though everyone still hoped, the improvement never arrived. Mrs. Pryor, who had visited daily, watched closely one morning—Caroline had been ill for nearly two weeks. She took her hand, felt her wrist, then quietly left the room. She went straight to Mr. Helstone’s study, where she remained for much of the morning. When she returned, she removed her bonnet and shawl, stood beside the bed, hands folded, rocking slightly—a motion familiar to her. Then she said softly, “I’ve sent Fanny to Fieldhead to fetch a few things I’ll need while staying here. I wish to remain with you until you’re better. Your uncle has kindly allowed me to stay. Will it please you, Caroline?”

“I’m sorry you should take such trouble,” Caroline murmured. “I don’t feel very ill—though I cannot refuse. It will be comfort just to know you’re near, to see you sometimes. But don’t confine yourself for my sake, dear Mrs. Pryor. Fanny cares for me well enough.”

Mrs. Pryor bent over her, smoothing her hair beneath the cap, lifting the pillow gently. Caroline smiled, raising her face to press a kiss upon her cheek.

“Are you free from pain? Are you at ease?” Mrs. Pryor asked, voice low and earnest, yielding to the touch.

“I think I am almost happy.”

“You wish to drink? Your lips are dry.”

She held a glass of cool liquid to her lips.

“Have you eaten today, Caroline?”

“I cannot eat.”

“But soon your appetite will return—it _must_ return… if God grants it.”

As she laid her back upon the couch, Mrs. Pryor drew her close, cradling her briefly against her chest, as though holding a fragile thing.

“I shall hardly wish to get well,” Caroline whispered, “if only to keep you always.”

Mrs. Pryor did not smile. A tremor passed over her face, and for moments she fought to still it.

“You’re more used to Fanny than to me,” she said after a pause. “My presence must seem strange, perhaps intrusive?”

“No,” Caroline replied. “It feels natural—so soothing. You must have cared for many sick people, ma'am. You move so quietly, speak so gently, touch me so lightly.”

“I am clumsy at most things, my dear,” Mrs. Pryor said. “You’ll find me awkward often—never careless.”

Careless, indeed, she was not. From that hour, Fanny and Eliza became shadows in the sickroom. Mrs. Pryor claimed it as her own. She lived there, day and night, performing every task with quiet grace. The patient protested—lightly at first, then not at all. Loneliness vanished. Instead, peace and care settled around her. They became inseparable—two souls bound in tenderness. Caroline, usually proud and self-reliant, accepted help without resistance. Mrs. Pryor, though not naturally skilled in small services, performed them effortlessly, as if born to the role. No trace of weariness crossed her face, no sign that the burden weighed upon her. There was little hardship in the work—but a hired nurse might have found it so.

Still, despite all care, Caroline did not improve. She faded like snow under spring sun, like a flower withered by drought. Miss Keeldar, who rarely entertained fears, began to notice the changes—the thinning frame, the pale eyes, the sinking breath. Her visits turned anxious. She spoke to Mr. Helstone with urgency, and he, though reluctant, finally admitted the truth: his niece was more than merely unwell. When Mrs. Pryor calmly requested a physician, he said she might send for two if she wished.

One doctor came. He spoke with solemn authority, delivered a dark pronouncement—words whose meaning would unfold only in time—wrote prescriptions, gave instructions, pocketed his fee, and departed. Likely he knew healing was beyond him, but he would not admit it.

No news of serious illness spread. At Hollow’s Cottage, it was assumed Caroline had only a bad cold—she had written Hortense as much—and mademoiselle responded with jam, a tisane recipe, and gentle advice.

Mrs. Yorke, hearing of the doctor’s visit, scoffed. “Rich idle people,” she said, “with nothing to occupy their minds, must summon doctors at the least ache. Hypochondria is their pastime.”

Meanwhile, Caroline grew weaker, her strength slipping away swiftly—except to one who understood how quickly a foundation undermined by sorrow could collapse.

Sick people often hold secrets even from those closest to them. Caroline had one such secret—one Mrs. Pryor could not at first decipher. Every Tuesday, at noon, she would insist on being dressed and seated by the window. Whether weak or stronger, she refused to rest until the church clock struck twelve. Then, as the final chime rang, she would grow calm, allow herself to be laid down, bury her face in the pillow, and draw the coverlets close—as if shutting out the world.

More than once, a faint tremor shook the bed, and a soft sob broke the silence. Mrs. Pryor noticed each time.

On a Tuesday morning, Caroline again asked to rise. She sat in her white dressing gown, leaning forward in the chair, staring out the lattice. Mrs. Pryor sat behind her, knitting, but watching. A light touched Caroline’s face—her eyes brightened, her expression softened. She half rose, looking intently outward. Mrs. Pryor crept closer, peering over her shoulder. Below, the churchyard stretched out, then the road. A rider approached—swift, clear. The figure was not yet distant enough to hide. Mrs. Pryor’s sight was sharp. She recognized Mr. Moore. Just as the hill concealed him, the clock struck twelve.

“May I lie down again?” Caroline asked.

Mrs. Pryor helped her to bed, drew the curtain, and stood nearby, listening. The bed trembled. A stifled sob stirred the air. A pang twisted Mrs. Pryor’s features. She wrung her hands. A groan escaped her lips. She remembered—Tuesday was market day. Mr. Moore passed the rectory every week on his way to Whinbury, just before noon.

Caroline wore a slender silk braid around her neck, attached to a small locket. Mrs. Pryor had seen the glint of gold but never glimpsed what lay inside. Her patient never parted with it. When awake, it rested hidden in her bosom; when asleep, it lay clasped in her hand.

That afternoon, a brief doze—more like lethargy—overtook her. The weather was hot. As she shifted in restless sleep, the coverlets slipped aside. Mrs. Pryor bent to adjust them. The small, frail hand lay limp on her chest, fingers curled around the treasure. Gently, she disengaged the chain, revealing a tiny locket—small, plain, suited to a modest purse. Beneath the glass, a curl of black hair—too short, too crisp, to have come from a woman’s head.

A sudden movement made the chain twitch. The sleeper stirred, woke. Her thoughts were scattered, her gaze wandering. Half-sitting, as if frightened, she gasped, “Don’t take it from me, Robert! Don’t! It is my last comfort—let me keep it. I never tell anyone whose hair it is. I never show it.”

Mrs. Pryor had already stepped behind the curtain. Seated in a deep armchair near the bed, she was out of sight. Caroline looked around, thinking herself alone. As her thoughts slowly returned—like weary birds landing on a desolate shore—she believed she was unattended. She was not fully herself; perhaps she would never be again. Perhaps the world of health and strength had already slipped from beneath her feet.

In health, she had never spoken aloud her thoughts. Now words slipped from her lips unbidden.

“Oh, I _should_ see him once more before all is over!” she cried. “Heaven might grant me this! God, give me a little comfort before I die!”

“But he won’t know I’m ill until I’m gone. He’ll come when I’m laid out—cold, stiff, senseless.”

“What can my soul feel then? Can spirits see or know what happens to the body? Can the dead return? Can they come through wind, water, fire? Will the elements carry me to Moore?”

“Why does the wind sometimes sound almost like speech—sing, as I’ve heard it sing lately at night—or pass the casement sobbing, as if mourning what’s to come? Does nothing haunt it? Nothing stir it?”

“It once brought me words—music I could have written down, if I’d dared rise in the dim watch-light to find pen and paper.”

“What is that electricity they speak of—whose shifts make us well or ill, whose absence or excess destroys, whose balance revives? What are all these unseen forces in the air—playing over our nerves like fingers on strings, calling forth joy or sorrow, swelling or weeping?”

“Where is the other world? What will life beyond consist of? Why do I ask? Do I not know the hour draws near—too near—when the veil must tear for me? Do I not know the Great Mystery may burst upon me too soon? O great Spirit, in whose mercy I trust, whom I have called Father since childhood, help this weak creature of Thy hands! Sustain me through this trial I dread! Give me strength! Give me patience! Give me—oh, _give me_ FAITH!”

She sank back onto the pillow. Mrs. Pryor slipped quietly from the room. She returned shortly, appearing calm, as if she had not heard the prayer.

The next day, visitors came. Word had spread that Miss Helstone was worse. Mr. Hall and his sister Margaret arrived. Both left the room in tears—shocked by how changed she had become. Hortense Moore came. Caroline seemed brighter in her presence. She smiled, assured her she was not seriously ill, spoke cheerfully in a low voice. During her visit, color returned to her cheeks. She looked better.

“How is Mr. Robert?” Mrs. Pryor asked as Hortense prepared to leave.

“He was well when he left.”

“Left? Is he gone from home?”

Hortense explained that police matters—about the rioters he was pursuing—had summoned him to Birmingham. He might not return for two weeks.

“He doesn’t know Miss Helstone is very ill?”

“No. Like me, he thought she only had a cold.”

Afterward, Mrs. Pryor avoided the bedside for an hour. She heard Caroline weep—and dared not watch.

As evening fell, she brought tea. Caroline opened her eyes from a brief sleep, glanced at her nurse with confusion.

“I smelt honeysuckles in the glen this summer morning,” she said, “standing at the counting-house window.”

Words like these, spoken from pale lips, pierce the heart deeper than steel. Romantic in books, they wound the soul in reality.

“My darling, do you know me?” Mrs. Pryor asked.

“I went in to call Robert to breakfast. I’ve been with him in the garden. He asked me to go. A heavy dew has refreshed the flowers. The peaches are ripening.”

“My darling! My darling!” the nurse repeated, over and over.

“I thought it was daylight—long after sunrise. It looks dark. Is the moon set?”

The moon, newly risen, gazed full and mild upon her. Floating in blue space, it watched her, unclouded.

“Then it’s not morning? I’m not at the cottage? Who is this? I see a shape at my bedside.”

“It is I—your friend—your nurse—your…” Mrs. Pryor paused, then whispered, “Lean your head on my shoulder. Collect yourself.” Lower, almost to herself: “O God, have mercy! Give _her_ life, and _me_ strength! Send me courage! Teach me words!”

Minutes passed in silence. The patient lay still, resting in the trembling arms, on the beating heart of the nurse.

“I am better now,” Caroline whispered. “Much better. I know where I am. This is Mrs. Pryor. I was dreaming. I talk when I wake from dreams—people do in sickness. How fast your heart beats, ma'am! Do not be afraid.”

“It is not fear, child—only anxiety. It will pass. I’ve brought you tea, Cary. Your uncle made it himself. He says he makes better tea than any housewife. Taste it. He worries about your eating so little. He’d be glad to see your appetite return.”

“I am thirsty. Let me drink.”

She drank eagerly.

“What time is it, ma'am?” she asked.

“Past nine.”

“Not later? Oh! I have a long night ahead. But the tea has strengthened me. I will sit up.”

Mrs. Pryor raised her, arranged the pillows.

“Thank Heaven,” Caroline said, “I am not always this miserable. The afternoon was hard after Hortense left. Perhaps the evening will be better. It’s a fine night, I think? The moon shines clearly.”

“Very fine—a perfect summer night. The old church-tower gleams white, almost silver.”

“And the churchyard—does it look peaceful?”

“Yes. And the garden. Dew sparkles on the leaves.”

“Can you see many weeds and nettles among the graves? Or do they look turfy and flowery?”

“I see closed daisy-heads gleaming like pearls on some mounds. Thomas has cut the dock-leaves and cleared the grass.”

“I always like that done. It soothes the mind to see order. And I suppose the moonlight shines softly inside the church now. It falls through the east window, full on the Helstone monument. When I close my eyes, I see poor papa’s epitaph—black letters on white marble. There’s room below for more names.”

“William Farren came this morning to tend your flowers. He feared, now you cannot care for them, they’d be neglected. He took two of your favorites home to nurse.”

“If I were to make a will,” Caroline said, “I’d leave William all my plants; Shirley my trinkets—except one, which must never leave my neck; and you, ma'am, my books.”

After a pause: “Mrs. Pryor, I have a longing—for something.”

“For what, Caroline?”

“You know I love to hear you sing. Sing me a hymn. That one—‘Our God, our help in ages past…’”

Mrs. Pryor sang.

No wonder Caroline loved her voice. Even in speech, it was sweet, clear as silver. In song, it was nearly divine—purer than flute or harp. But the tone was secondary to the emotion beneath—a tender quiver from a heart deeply moved.

Servants in the kitchen stole to the stairs to listen. Old Helstone, walking in the garden and pondering the fragility of women, stopped among the borders, drawn by the mournful melody. He could not say why it reminded him of his lost wife, nor why it stirred greater concern for Caroline’s fading youth. He was glad he had promised to visit Wynne, the magistrate, that evening. Gloomy thoughts he disliked. When they came, he forced them away. The hymn followed him faintly across the fields. He quickened his pace, trying to escape its echo.

“Thy word commands our flesh to dust,
‘Return, ye sons of men.’
All nations rose from earth at first,
And turn to earth again.

“A thousand ages in Thy sight
Are like an evening gone—
Short as the watch that ends the night
Before the rising sun.

“Time, like an ever-rolling stream,
Bears all its sons away;
They fly, forgotten, as a dream
Dies at the opening day.

“Like flowery fields, the nations stand,
Fresh in the morning light;
The flowers beneath the mower’s hand
Lie withering ere ‘tis night.

“Our God, our help in ages past,
Our hope for years to come,
Be Thou our guard while troubles last—
O Father, be our home!”

“Now sing a song—a Scottish song,” Caroline suggested. “‘Ye banks and braes o’ bonnie Doon.’”

Mrs. Pryor tried. At the end of the first verse, she stopped. She could not go on. Her heart overflowed.

“You are weeping,” Caroline said gently. “Come here—I’ll comfort you.”

Mrs. Pryor came. She sat on the edge of the bed, letting the thin arms wrap around her.

“You often soothe me,” the girl murmured, kissing her cheek. “Let me soothe you. I hope,” she added, “it is not for me you weep?”

No answer.

“Do you think I shall not recover? I don’t feel _very_ ill—only weak.”

“But your mind, Caroline—your mind is broken. Your heart is shattered. You’ve been so neglected, so cast aside, left so alone.”

“I believe grief has always been my worst illness. I sometimes think—if happiness came in abundance—I might revive yet.”

“Do you wish to live?”

“I have no purpose.”

“You love me, Caroline?”

“Very much—truly—inexpressibly. Just now, I feel as if I could grow into your heart.”

“I will return directly, dear,” Mrs. Pryor said, laying her down.

She slipped to the door, turned the key softly, locked it, and returned. She leaned over, drew back the curtain to let in the moonlight, and studied Caroline’s face.

“Then,” she said, voice changed, swift and fierce, “if you love me—if you feel you could ‘grow to my heart’—you will not be shocked to know that _this_ heart is the source of yours—that from _my_ veins flows the blood in _yours_; that you are _mine_—my daughter—my own child.”

“Mrs. Pryor…”

“My own child!”

“That means—you adopted me?”

“It means that, if I gave you nothing else, I gave you life. I bore you. I nursed you. I am your true mother. No other woman can claim the title. It is _mine_.”

“But Mrs. James Helstone—my father’s wife—whom I never saw—she is my mother?”

“She _is_ your mother. James Helstone was _my_ husband. I say you are _mine_. I have proved it. I thought perhaps you were entirely his—that would have been cruel. But it is not so. God allowed me to be the parent of your mind. It belongs to me. It is my right. These features—James’s. He had a fine face when young, unmarred by sin. Papa, my darling, gave you your blue eyes, your soft brown hair, the oval of your face, the symmetry of your features—the outside, he gave. But the heart and the mind are _mine_. The seeds are from _me_, and they have grown—developed—to excellence. I love my child, and I honor her.”

“Is this true? Is it no dream?”

Mrs. Pryor did not answer. She only held her, and the moon watched silently above.

Not all who wrestle with fate emerge victorious. Night after night, the sweat of anguish may glisten on a brow; the soul may cry out in silence—its voiceless plea rising to the unseen, pleading: "Spare my beloved. Heal what is most dear to me. Do not take from me what has become part of my very being. O God, bend, hear, have mercy!" And yet, when dawn breaks, it may bring no triumph—only the quiet horror of defeat. The morning that once greeted him with the sigh of zephyrs and the song of larks now breathes through lips grown pale and cold: “Oh, I’ve had such a terrible night. I’m worse this morning. I tried to rise… I cannot. Dreams I never knew before have haunted me.” Then the watcher draws near, sees the familiar face reshaped by suffering, feels the terrible truth—the moment of farewell is near. It is God’s will, and he bows his head, his heart crushed beneath the weight of inevitable sorrow.

But happy Mrs. Pryor! She was still lost in prayer, unaware that the summer sun had already climbed above the hills, when her daughter stirred gently in her arms. No piteous moan—no sound that drains strength even from the strongest resolve—preceded her waking. No long silence followed. Instead, the first words were clear, bright, full of life: “Mamma, I’ve slept so well. I only dreamed twice and woke up.”

Mrs. Pryor started upright, hiding her tears before her daughter could see them—the word “mamma,” spoken so naturally, the warmth of her return, filling her with joy she dared not fully claim. For days, she had allowed herself only fleeting hope, trembling with each flicker of improvement. A spark would flare—then fade. Excitement gave way swiftly to exhaustion. Caroline struggled to appear better, though her body often refused to obey her will. Her efforts to eat, to speak, to smile, faltered again and again. There were hours when Mrs. Pryor feared the thread of life might snap at any moment—even if its breaking could be delayed.

In those quiet days, mother and daughter seemed almost alone in Briarfield. It was late August. The weather had been harsh—dry, dusty, baked under an unrelenting east wind that had blown for weeks. The sky was cloudless, yet a faint, sickly haze hung over everything, dulling the blue of heaven, bleaching the green of earth, dimming the light of day. Nearly every household had fled to escape the heat and discomfort. Miss Keeldar and her friends were at the seaside, as was Mrs. Yorke’s family. Mr. Hall and Louis Moore, drawn together by shared views and temperaments, had journeyed northward on foot to the Lakes. Even Hortense, who longed to stay and help care for Caroline, had been persuaded by Miss Mann to return to Wormwood Wells, where her own health was failing under the oppressive air. Unable to refuse a request that touched both her kindness and her pride, she left reluctantly.

As for Robert, he had gone from Birmingham to London, where he remained.

While the desert wind parched Caroline’s skin and fevered her blood, her recovery lagged behind her returning peace of mind. But then came a change. The wind ceased its mournful groaning at the rectory’s eastern gable and the church’s oriel window. A small cloud, shaped like a hand, rose in the west. Gusts carried it forward, spreading it wide. Rain fell, fierce and brief, followed by thunder. When it passed, the sun emerged warmly, the sky regained its deep blue, and the land turned green again. The pallid, unhealthy tinge vanished from nature. The hills stood clear against the horizon, freed from the lingering haze.

Now, Caroline’s youth could aid her. So could her mother’s care—and God’s grace. Together, they brought the soft, cleansing west wind into her room, drifting through the open lattice like a whisper of healing. At last, Mrs. Pryor saw it—true convalescence had begun.

It wasn’t just that Caroline smiled more brightly or spoke with renewed cheer. Something deeper had shifted. A look—terrible, indescribable—had left her face. Those who have watched beside a dying bed will know the expression: hollow, distant, beyond pain, yet terrifying in its stillness. That look was gone. Before her cheeks filled or color returned, her whole presence softened. Where once there had been marble and glass, now there was life—fragile, yes, but unmistakably alive. She was no longer a statue, but a girl recovering.

She stopped asking constantly for water. The words “I am so thirsty” faded from her lips. Sometimes, after swallowing a morsel, she’d say, “That revived me.” Food no longer tasted uniformly bitter. She began to show preferences—tiny, precious signs of returning vitality. How carefully her mother prepared each dish! How closely she watched as Caroline ate. Nourishment brought strength. Soon, Caroline could sit up. Then she longed to breathe fresh air, to see her flowers, to watch how the fruit had ripened.

Her uncle, generous as ever, had bought a garden chair for her use. He carried her down himself, placed her gently in it, and William Farren stood ready to wheel her about the paths. He showed her what he had done among her plants, took her directions for new work. They talked easily—of bees burrowing under the cherry tree, of hedge-sparrows nesting in the hedges, of pearly eggs and young fledglings. If *Chambers's Journal* had existed then, it would have been their favorite reading. Both believed in the wisdom of animals, loved minute observations, and found wonder in the natural world. It explained why Caroline insisted on William guiding her chair—why his company made her outings meaningful.

Mrs. Pryor walked nearby, wondering how her daughter could feel so at ease with a man of the people. To her, William Farren was stiff, coarse, ill-matched. She couldn’t speak to him without formality, feeling a vast divide between their worlds. To cross it, she thought, would be to lower herself. One evening, she asked softly, “Are you not afraid, my dear, to talk so freely with him? He might grow bold, or too forward.”

“William presume?” Caroline replied, surprised. “You don’t know him. He never does. He’s too proud, too sensitive. William has very fine feelings.”

Mrs. Pryor smiled, skeptical—how could a rough-handed, fustian-clad man possess such delicacy?

Farren, for his part, met her with a sullen glance. He sensed her judgment, and when misunderstood, he became stubborn and hard to manage.

But evenings belonged entirely to mother and daughter. Then, alone together, no shadow of distance remained. Mrs. Pryor cherished these moments—when love flowed freely, unmediated by society or propriety.

During the day, however, she reverted to her usual reserve. With Mr. Helstone, their interaction was strictly formal—respectful, but distant. Familiarity would have bred contempt in either of them. Yet, through careful courtesy, they managed harmony. Toward the servants, her manner was cool, shy, even chilly. Perhaps it was not pride, but diffidence, that made her seem aloof—but Fanny and Eliza didn’t distinguish. They resented her, and she felt it. It weighed on her, making her dissatisfied with herself, melancholy, silent.

Only Caroline could melt this ice. Not with words of affection—those would have seemed insincere to Mrs. Pryor—but with quiet dependence, fearless trust. She leaned on her mother, confided in her, claimed her help with easy intimacy. “Mamma, do this.” “Please, mamma, fetch me that.” “Read to me, mamma.” “Sing a little, mamma.”

No one else had ever looked to her so naturally, so confidently. Others kept their distance, aware of her flaws. But Caroline saw none of that—not now, not ever. Yet she could still find fault. Blind to inherited weaknesses, she noticed habits that could change. On certain matters, she lectured her mother—not harshly, but with gentle firmness—and instead of being offended, Mrs. Pryor felt pleased. To be corrected by her daughter—by someone who felt so completely at home—was a comfort.

“Mamma,” Caroline said, “I won’t let you wear that old gown anymore. It’s not becoming. Too tight in the skirt. You must wear your black silk every afternoon. You look nice in it. And for Sundays—a real black satin dress. Not satinet, not a sham. And when you get it, you must wear it.”

“My dear,” Mrs. Pryor replied, “I thought the black silk would serve me for years. I meant to buy you things with the money.”

“Nonsense, mamma. Uncle gives me cash for what I want. He’s generous. And I want to see you in satin. Get it soon. Let me choose the pattern. You always try to hide yourself—like a grandmother. You make people think you’re old and plain. But you’re not! When you’re well dressed and cheerful, you’re very comely. Your smile is lovely, your teeth are white, your hair is still a beautiful light color. You speak like a young lady—with such a clear, sweet voice. And you sing better than anyone I’ve ever heard. Why do you wear such dresses and bonnets—ones no one else wears?”

“Does it bother you, Caroline?”

“Very much. It vexes me. People say you’re miserly. But you’re not—look at your gifts to the poor, to religious societies. Only you give quietly, so few know. But I’ll be your maid. When I’m stronger, I’ll help you. And you must do as I say.”

Caroline sat close, smoothing her mother’s handkerchief, brushing her hair back. “My own mamma,” she murmured, delighting in the thought. “Who belongs to me, and to whom I belong. I’m rich now. I have something I can love deeply—and not fear loving.”

“Who gave you this brooch, mamma?” she asked, gently unpinning it.

“My sister—my only sister, Cary. I wish Aunt Caroline had lived to see her niece.”

“Do you have anything of papa’s? A trinket? A gift?”

“I have one thing.”

“That you prize?”

“That I prize.”

“Valuable and pretty?”

“Invaluable and sweet to me.”

“Show it, mamma. Is it here or at Fieldhead?”

“It’s talking to me now. Leaning on me. Its arms are around me.”

“Oh, mamma—you mean your teasing daughter, who never lets you rest, who follows you like a dog.”

“Whose face still gives me a strange thrill sometimes. I half fear your beauty, child.”

“You don’t. You can’t. Mamma, I’m sorry papa wasn’t good. I wish he had been. Wickedness ruins everything. It kills love. If we thought each other wicked, we couldn’t love, could we?”

“And if we couldn’t trust each other, Cary?”

“We’d be miserable. Mother, before I knew you, I feared you—thought you weren’t good. That dread made me hesitant to meet you. But now—I’m joyful. I find you perfect—almost. Kind, clever, kind-hearted. Your only fault is being old-fashioned. And I’ll fix that.”

“Put down your work, mamma. Read to me. I like your southern accent—it’s so pure, so soft. No rough burr, no nasal twang like everyone else’s here. Uncle and Mr. Hall say you’re a wonderful reader. Mr. Hall said no lady read with such expression or purity of tone.”

“I wish I could say the same of you, Cary. But the first time I heard your dear friend read and preach, I couldn’t understand his broad northern speech.”

“Could you understand me, mamma? Did I sound rough?”

“No. I almost wished you had. I wished you looked unpolished. Your father, Caroline, spoke beautifully—correctly, gently, smoothly. You’ve inherited his gift.”

“Poor papa. When he was so agreeable, why wasn’t he good?”

“Why he was as he was—something you, child, cannot comprehend—I cannot say. It is a mystery. The key lies in His hands. There I leave it.”

“Mamma, you keep stitching. Put it down. I hate sewing. It takes up your lap—where I want my head. It holds your eyes—where I want them on a book. Here’s your favorite—Cowper.”

These little demands were her mother’s joy. If she hesitated, it was only to enjoy hearing them repeated—to relish her daughter’s playful urgency. And when she gave in, Caroline would say, mischievously, “You’ll spoil me, mamma. I always thought I’d like to be spoiled—and now I find it very sweet.”

And so did Mrs. Pryor.

By the time the Fieldhead party returned to Briarfield, Caroline was nearly recovered. Miss Keeldar, having received word by post of her friend’s improvement, wasted no time—she arrived at home and immediately made her way to the rectory.

A gentle but steady rain fell upon the late-blooming flowers and the russet-hued autumn shrubs when the garden gate creaked open, and Shirley’s familiar figure appeared in the window. As she stepped inside, her emotions showed themselves in her usual quiet way. When deeply stirred by sorrow or joy, she rarely spoke at length. Her feelings did not spill over into words, nor did her eyes betray them easily—only flickering glances, fleeting and guarded. She embraced Caroline, gave her a single look, a single kiss, and said simply, “You are better.”

Then, just a moment later: “I see you’re safe now—but take care. May God spare your health from any further shock.”

She began speaking animatedly about the journey, yet all the while her gaze kept returning to Caroline. In her eyes there was deep concern, a quiet unease, and something like wonder.

“She may be on the mend,” her expression seemed to say, “but how frail she still is! What a trial she has endured!”

Suddenly, her glance shifted to Mrs. Pryor—and pierced straight through her.

“When will my governess return to me?” she asked.

“May I tell her everything?” Caroline whispered to her mother. A nod granted permission, and soon Shirley learned what had transpired during her absence.

“Very good,” she replied coolly. “Very good. But it’s no surprise to me.”

“What? You knew?”

“I guessed long ago. I’ve heard bits of Mrs. Pryor’s story—not from her, but from others. I know every detail of Mr. James Helstone’s life and character. An afternoon with Miss Mann taught me much. And he’s one of Mrs. Yorke’s cautionary tales—one of the crimson warnings she displays to frighten young women away from marriage. I might have doubted the truth of such accounts—both these women delight in showing only the darkest side of life—but I asked Mr. Yorke himself. He said, ‘Shirley, my dear, if you want to know anything about that James Helstone, I can only say he was a man-tiger. Handsome, reckless, soft, treacherous, polite, cruel—’ Don’t cry, Cary. Let’s not speak of it more.”

“I’m not crying, Shirley—or if I am, it doesn’t matter. Go on. You’re not a true friend if you hide the truth from me. I hate this pretense of smoothing over, hiding, distorting reality.”

“Then I’ll say no more than I already have—except that your uncle himself confirmed Mr. Yorke’s words. He hates lies, and never uses those hollow excuses that are worse than falsehoods.”

“But Papa is dead. They should let him rest.”

“They should—and we _will_ let him rest. Cry, Cary. It will do you good. It’s wrong to suppress natural tears. Besides, I like to share the idea I see glowing in your mother’s eyes as she looks at you. Every tear washes away a sin. Weep! Your tears hold a power the rivers of Damascus never had. Like Jordan, they can cleanse even a leprous memory.”

Then, turning to Mrs. Pryor, she said, “Madam, did you think I could spend days watching you and your daughter together—seeing your remarkable likeness in so many ways, noticing (excuse me) your unguarded emotions when your child is near or gone—and not form my own guesses? I formed them, and they are exactly right. I begin to think myself rather clever.”

“And you said nothing?” Caroline asked, regaining her composure.

“Nothing. I had no right to speak. It wasn’t my place. I chose not to make it so.”

“You guessed such a deep secret—and didn’t hint at it?”

“Is that so hard?”

“It isn’t like you.”

“How do you know?”

“You’re not reserved. You’re open, honest.”

“I may be open—but I know where to draw the line. I may show my treasure, but I keep a few gems hidden—a rare, unclaimed stone, an amulet whose glow I rarely allow myself to glimpse. Goodbye.”

Caroline found herself seeing Shirley in a new light. Soon, that view would deepen.

No sooner had Caroline regained enough strength to enjoy a change of scenery, a little social warmth, than Miss Keeldar began asking daily for her presence at Fieldhead. Whether Shirley had grown weary of her relatives is unknown—she never said so. Yet she claimed Caroline with such eagerness that it was clear her company was not unwelcome.

The Sympsons were devout churchgoers. Naturally, the rector’s niece was welcomed with courtesy. Mr. Sympson was a man of impeccable respectability—strict in temper, pious in belief, worldly in outlook. His wife was kind, patient, well-bred. She had been raised on narrow views, fed on a few bitter prejudices, a handful of rigid preferences soaked till their flavor vanished, with no seasoning added. She had some noble principles, wrapped in a stiff crust of bigotry that made them hard to digest. Too obedient to question her upbringing, she accepted her diet without complaint.

Their daughters were models of propriety. Tall, each with a Roman nose, they had been educated perfectly. Everything they did was done with precision. History and solid books had shaped their minds. Their principles were fixed, beyond improvement. Their lives, feelings, manners, habits—all were strictly regulated. They knew by heart the rules of young-ladies’ conduct—on speech, bearing, behavior—and never deviated. They viewed any departure from these rules with silent horror. The Abomination of Desolation was no mystery to them; they had identified it in what others called Originality. Quick to spot its signs—whether in speech, gesture, or thought—they recoiled. Danger loomed above them, peril surrounded them. What was this strange thing? Since it was incomprehensible, it must be evil. It must be condemned and locked away.

Henry Sympson, the youngest and only son, was fifteen. He usually stayed with his tutor. When free, he sought out his cousin Shirley. Unlike his sisters, Henry was small, lame, pale. His large eyes shone dimly in a wan face—usually dull, but capable of sudden brilliance. Inner emotion could flush his cheeks and give purpose to his crippled movements. His mother loved him dearly; she saw his differences as marks of divine favor. He was not like other children, she admitted. She believed him reborn—a new Samuel—called by God from birth. He was meant to be a clergyman. Mr. and the Misses Sympson, unable to understand him, left him mostly alone. Shirley made him her favorite; he made her his companion.

In the midst of this orderly family, or rather apart from it, moved the tutor—the satellite.

Yes, Louis Moore was a satellite of the Sympson household—connected, yet distant; ever present, always removed. Each member treated him with proper dignity. The father was coldly civil, sometimes irritable; the mother, kind-hearted though formal; the daughters saw him as an abstraction, not a person. By their manner, it seemed their brother’s tutor did not truly live among them. They were learned—he was too—but not for them. They were accomplished—he had talents, invisible to their senses. A vivid sketch from his hand was blank to their eyes; a bold observation from his lips went unheard. Nothing surpassed their propriety.

Or so it seemed—until Caroline discovered something astonishing. Her cousin Shirley had no real friend at Fieldhead. To Miss Keeldar, Louis Moore was no more than a teacher—no gentleman, no man—just as he was to the respectable Misses Sympson.

What had happened to the compassionate Shirley? Why was she so indifferent to the loneliness of a fellow human being thus isolated under her roof? She wasn’t cruel, perhaps—but she never noticed him. He came and went, spoke or remained silent, and she rarely acknowledged his presence.

As for Louis Moore himself, he bore it with quiet resignation. He seemed used to such life, accepting it as temporary. His mind felt shut within him—silent, uncomplaining. He never laughed; rarely smiled. He fulfilled his duties with exactness. His pupil loved him. He asked nothing more from the world than basic civility—and seemed content to accept no more, especially in that house. When Caroline offered gentle friendship, he did not encourage it. He avoided her more than sought her. One living creature besides his frail student he cherished: the rough, sullen Tartar dog. Though surly to others, Tartar had a special bond with Moore. Once, when Moore entered the room uninvited and sat down quietly, Tartar rose from Shirley’s feet and settled beside him. Shirley noticed, extended her white hand, and softly tried to lure him back. Tartar looked, drooled, sighed—but refused, settling firmly at Moore’s side. The tutor gently pulled the dog’s black-muzzled head onto his knee, patted him, and smiled—just once, to himself.

Later that evening, an observer might have seen that after Tartar returned to Shirley’s side, Moore, with a single word and gesture, drew him back again. The dog pricked his ears, stood up, and came forward with affectionate submission. As Moore petted him, the same quiet smile crossed his face.

* * * * *

One day, as Caroline and Shirley sat alone in the summerhouse, Caroline said, “Shirley, did you know that my cousin Louis was tutor in your uncle’s family before the Sympsons came here?”

Shirley hesitated longer than usual before replying, “Yes—of course. I knew it well.”

“I thought you must have known.”

“Well, then?”

“It puzzles me why you never mentioned it to me.”

“Why should it puzzle you?”

“It seems odd. You talk so freely—how could this never come up?”

“Because it didn’t,” Shirley said, laughing.

“You’re a strange creature!” Caroline said. “I thought I knew you. Now I feel I don’t. You were silent about Mrs. Pryor—and now this. But why keep secrets? That’s the mystery.”

“I never made it a secret. I had no reason to. If you’d asked who Henry’s tutor was, I’d have told you. Besides, I thought you knew.”

“I’m puzzled by more than one thing. You don’t like poor Louis. Why? Are you annoyed by his position—what you see as servile? Do you wish Robert’s brother were higher in station?”

“Robert’s brother, indeed!” Shirley snapped, her voice sharp with scorn. With a proud gesture, she plucked a rose from a branch outside.
