“Yes,” Caroline repeated, gently firm. “Robert’s brother. He _is_ closely related to Gérard Moore of the Hollow—though nature gave him neither the handsome features nor noble bearing of his kinsman. But his blood is as noble, and he is as much a gentleman, if he were free.”

“Wise, humble, pious Caroline!” Shirley said ironically. “Hear her! Men and angels, listen! We shouldn’t despise plain faces or honest labor, should we? Look at the man you praise—he’s there in the garden,” she continued, pointing through the vines. And indeed, Louis Moore was walking slowly along the path.

“He’s not ugly, Shirley,” Caroline insisted. “Not ignoble. He’s quiet—his silence seals his thoughts. But I believe he’s intelligent. And remember, if he weren’t admirable in character, Mr. Hall wouldn’t seek his company.”

Shirley laughed—twice, each time with a faint edge of mockery. “Well, well,” she said. “On the grounds that he’s Cyril Hall’s friend and Robert Moore’s brother, we’ll just tolerate his existence, won’t we, Cary? You think he’s intelligent? Not quite an idiot, eh? Something commendable in his nature—meaning, not a brute. Very well. Your arguments weigh with me. So if he comes this way, I’ll speak to him.”

He approached the summerhouse. Unaware it was occupied, he sat on the step. Tartar, his constant companion, followed and lay across his feet.

“Old boy,” Louis said, pulling the dog’s tawny ear—the remnant of an organ torn in countless fights—“the autumn sun shines as warmly on us as on the richest. This garden isn’t ours, but we enjoy its greenness and scent, don’t we?”

He sat in silence, stroking Tartar, who licked him with great affection. A faint chirping began in the trees. Something fluttered down, light as leaves—small birds, landing shyly on the grass, hopping expectantly.

“The little brown elves actually remember I fed them yesterday,” Louis murmured. “They want more biscuit. Today I forgot to save a piece. Eager little spirits—I have no crumb for you.”

He reached into his pocket and pulled it out empty.

“A simple need,” whispered Miss Keeldar.

She took a sweet cake from her reticule—always stocked with something for birds or animals—and crumbled it. Bending over his shoulder, she placed the crumbs in his hand.

“There,” she said. “There’s a providence for the improvident.”

“This September afternoon is pleasant,” Louis said, calmly scattering the crumbs.

“Even for you?”

“As pleasant for me as for any king.”

“You take a kind of harsh, solitary pleasure in finding joy in nature and creatures below you.”

“Solitary, but not harsh. With animals, I feel I am Adam’s son—the heir of him who ruled over every living thing. Your dog follows me. When I enter the yard, the pigeons flutter at my feet. Your mare knows me as well as you, and obeys me better.”

“And your roses smell sweet to you, and your trees give you shade.”

“And,” he continued, “no whim can take these joys from me. They are _mine_.”

He walked off. Tartar followed, as if bound by duty and love. Shirley remained on the step, staring after him. Caroline saw her face—pale, as if her pride were bleeding inwardly.

“You see,” Caroline said gently, “he’s often hurt. That makes him moody.”

“You see,” Shirley replied sharply, “we’ll quarrel over him if we talk about him again. Drop it—forever.”

Caroline thought to herself: “He must have behaved this way before—that’s why she keeps distance. Yet why can’t she forgive his character and circumstances? Why don’t his modesty, honesty, sincerity move her? She’s rarely so unkind, so impatient.”

* * * * *

Two friends of Caroline’s confirmed her favorable view of Louis. William Farren, who had visited him with Mr. Hall, called him “a real gentleman”—there was none like him in Briarfield. “I’d do anything for that man. And to see how the children adore him, how the wife liked him from the first sight. He never steps into a house but the kids swarm around him. Those little ones seem to sense people’s true nature better than grown-ups.”

Mr. Hall, when asked by Miss Helstone what he thought of Louis Moore, replied quickly: “The best fellow I’ve met since Cambridge.”

“But he’s so serious,” Caroline objected.

“Serious? The finest company in the world! Full of quiet, unusual humor. Never enjoyed an outing more than the one I took with him to the Lakes. His mind and tastes are so refined—it does a man good to be near them. And his temper, his nature? I call them fine.”

“At Fieldhead he seems gloomy. I think he’s seen as misanthropic.”

“Oh, I suspect he’s just out of place—misplaced. The Sympsons are excellent people, but they don’t understand him. They care too much about form and ceremony—things entirely foreign to Louis.”

“I don’t think Miss Keeldar likes him.”

“She doesn’t know him—doesn’t know him. Otherwise, she has enough sense to recognize his worth.”

“Well, I suppose she doesn’t know him,” Caroline mused. She tried to excuse Shirley’s prejudice with that thought. But this explanation didn’t last long. She soon had to reject it.

One day, she was in the schoolroom with Henry Sympson, whose gentle, loving nature had quickly won her affection. The boy was working on a mechanical project—his lameness made him prefer sitting tasks. He searched his tutor’s desk for wax or twine. Moore was absent—Mr. Hall had taken him for a walk. Henry couldn’t find what he needed. He rummaged through compartment after compartment, and finally opened an inner drawer. There, instead of cord or beeswax, was a bundle of small, marble-colored notebooks tied with tape.

“What rubbish Mr. Moore keeps in his desk!” Henry said. “I hope he won’t keep my old exercises so carefully.”

“What is it?”

“Old copy-books.”

He tossed the bundle to Caroline. The packet looked neat—her curiosity sparked.

“If they’re just copy-books, I suppose I can open them?”

“Oh yes, freely. Mr. Moore lets me keep things in his desk. I give you permission.”

She examined them. They were French compositions, written in a compact, clean hand—exquisitely clear. The handwriting was unmistakable. She barely needed the names at the end of each page to confirm whose they were. Yet the name startled her: “Shirley Keeldar, Sympson Grove, ----shire” (a southern county), dated four years prior.

She tied the bundle back up, holding it tightly, lost in thought. She felt almost guilty—like she had intruded on a private memory.

“They’re Shirley’s,” Henry said casually.

“Did _you_ give them to Mr. Moore? She wrote them with Mrs. Pryor, I suppose?”

“She wrote them in my schoolroom at Sympson Grove, when she lived there. Mr. Moore taught her French—it’s his native language.”

“I know. Was she a good pupil, Henry?”

“She was wild and laughing, but delightful. She made lessons charming. She learned fast—you couldn’t tell when or how. French was nothing to her. She spoke it as quickly as Mr. Moore himself.”

“Was she obedient? Did she cause trouble?”

“She caused plenty—her own way. She was giddy, but I liked her. I’m desperately fond of Shirley.”

“Desperately fond—you silly boy! You don’t know what you’re saying.”

“I _am_ desperately fond of her. She’s the light of my eyes. I told Mr. Moore that last night.”

“He’d scold you for exaggerating.”

“He didn’t. He never lectures like governesses do. He was reading, and just smiled into his book. Said if Miss Keeldar was no more than that, she was less than he thought her. Because I’m a dim-eyed, short-sighted little chap. I’m afraid I’m a poor unfortunate, Miss Caroline Helstone. I’m a cripple, you know.”

“Never mind, Henry. You’re a lovely little fellow. And if God didn’t give you strength, He gave you a good heart and a keen mind.”

“I’ll be despised. I sometimes think both Shirley and you do.”

“Listen, Henry. I usually dislike schoolboys. I find them rude, cruel—killing birds, insects, kittens—taking joy in hurting the weak. But you’re different. I’m fond of you. You’re almost as wise as a man—far wiser, I think, than many men.”

“I _do_ like reading. I know I have sense. I know I have feeling.”

At that moment, Miss Keeldar entered.

“Henry,” she said, “I brought your lunch. I’ll prepare it myself.”

She placed a glass of fresh milk, a plate of something resembling leather, and a tool like a toasting-fork on the table.

“What are you two doing, ransacking Mr. Moore’s desk?” she asked.

“Looking at your old copy-books,” Caroline replied.

“My old copy-books?”

“French exercise-books. Look—these must be precious. They’re kept so carefully.”

She held up the bundle. Shirley snatched it. “I didn’t know one existed,” she said. “I thought they’d all burned in the kitchen fire or curled the maid’s hair at Sympson Grove. Why did you keep them, Henry?”

“It wasn’t me. I never thought they mattered. Mr. Moore put them in the inner drawer. Maybe he forgot.”

“C’est cela. He forgot, no doubt,” Shirley echoed. “They’re beautifully written,” she added, sounding pleased.

Miss Keeldar and her uncle were like two forces in nature that could never find balance—opposites, always at odds. He was quick to anger; she, spirited beyond restraint. He ruled with an iron hand; she craved freedom like air. He lived by the world’s rules; she, perhaps, by dreams of something higher.

He had not come to Yorkshire on a whim. His purpose was clear: to settle his niece’s future once and for all. He wanted her married—to someone suitable, respectable, well-placed. A husband who would take charge of her, secure her fortune, and let him forget her entirely.

But from the moment she could speak, Shirley had challenged his definitions. What he called “suitable,” she found dull. What he deemed “proper,” she saw as stifling. And now, as she stood at the threshold of marriage, it was uncertain whether she would yield even to him.

The test came swiftly.

Mr. Wynne proposed for his son, Samuel Fawthrop Wynne.

“Decidedly suitable! Most proper!” declared Mr. Sympson. “A fine estate, no debts, excellent connections. _It must be done._”

He summoned his niece to the oak parlour, shut the door behind them, delivered the offer, gave his opinion, and demanded her consent.

She refused.

“No,” she said firmly. “I will not marry Samuel Fawthrop Wynne.”

He stared. “Why? I must have a reason. In every way, he is more than worthy of you.”

She stood before the hearth, pale as the marble behind her, eyes wide and unblinking.

“And _I_ ask—what makes this man worthy of _me_?”

“He has twice your fortune, twice your sense, equal standing, equal respectability.”

“If he had my money multiplied fifty times, I would never vow to love him.”

“Then state your objections.”

“He has lived a life of petty, vulgar excess. That alone is reason enough to reject him.”

“Miss Keeldar, you shock me!”

“That conduct sinks him into a depth I cannot reach. His mind does not rise to anything I can honor. There is another barrier—he thinks narrowly, feels little, tastes poorly, behaves with vulgarity.”

“The man is wealthy and respected! To refuse him is arrogance!”

“I refuse, plainly. Do not bring this up again. I forbid it.”

“Do you intend to remain unmarried? Or do you choose celibacy?”

“I deny you the right to demand such a question.”

“Then do you expect some nobleman—a peer—to claim your hand?”

“I doubt if any peer exists whom I would give it to.”

“If madness runs in our blood, then you are mad. Your pride and whimsy border on frenzy.”

“Perhaps,” she said quietly, “by the time I finish speaking, you’ll see me leap over it.”

“You will!” he cried. “Foolish, impractical girl! Take warning! I challenge you to disgrace our name with a _mésalliance_!”

“Our name?” she echoed coldly. “Am _I_ called Sympson?”

“God be praised, you are not! But beware—I will not be mocked!”

“What power do you have over me? Why should I fear you?”

“Beware, madam!”

“I shall take scrupulous care, Mr. Sympson. Before I marry, I mean to esteem—to admire—to _love_.”

“Absurd! Indecent! Unfitting for a woman!”

“To love with all my heart. I know I speak a language you do not understand—but I care little if you do.”

“And if your love falls upon a beggar?”

“It will never fall upon one. Poverty is not noble.”

“Upon a clerk, an actor, a writer—perhaps—?”

“Have courage, Mr. Sympson. Or what?”

“A literary scribbler, a poor, whining artist?”

“I have no taste for the shabby or weak. But I value literature and art. And there, I wonder how your Mr. Wynne could ever suit me. He cannot write a letter without spelling errors; he reads only horse racing papers; he was the laughingstock of Stilbro’ grammar school!”

“Unladylike! Good God—where will she end?” He raised his hands in horror.

“Never at the altar with Sam Wynne.”

“Where will she end? Why aren’t the laws stricter so I might force her to listen?”

“Console yourself, uncle. Even if Britain were a serfdom and you the Czar, you could not _force_ me into this. I will write to Mr. Wynne myself. Leave the matter to me.”

* * * * *

Fortune, though often fickle, sometimes repeats its kindness. Miss Keeldar—her reputation, her spirit—had stirred the countryside. Wherever she went, whispers followed. After Mr. Wynne’s proposal, three others arrived, each with merit. Each was pressed upon her by her uncle—and each was rejected. Among them were men of character and wealth. Many wondered what she expected, what hidden prize she hoped to win.

At last, gossip claimed to have uncovered her secret—and her uncle believed it. The discovery changed everything. He saw his niece not as a reckless girl, but as a woman of rare judgment. His manner toward her softened, then deepened into reverence.

Fieldhead had grown too hot for both of them. Their aunt tried to mediate, but failed. The daughters avoided their arguments, whispering together in fear. Gertrude and Isabella dreaded being left alone with their bold cousin. But now, peace returned. Mr. Sympson was appeased. The household calmed.

Nunnely village had been mentioned—the old church, the forest, the ruins of a monastery. It also held the priory, a grander, older house than Briarfield or Whinbury. And it had a baronet—something neither of those places could boast. But for years, this title had been merely nominal. The young baronet, Sir Philip Nunnely, had lived far away, unknown in his own land.

During Miss Keeldar’s stay at Cliffbridge, she met Sir Philip. They crossed paths on the beach, along the cliffs, at dances. He seemed quiet, almost shy. His manner was simple—not arrogant, not proud, but gentle. He did not lower himself to their company; he seemed glad to be included.

Shirley, who loved honesty and sincerity, quickly warmed to him. She walked with him, sailed with him and his family, laughed with him. She liked him—for his kindness, his modesty, and the pleasure he took in her presence.

There was one flaw—though what friendship lacks this? Sir Philip wrote poetry. Sonnets, ballads, stanzas. Perhaps Shirley thought he clung too closely to his verses; perhaps she found the rhythm flat, the imagery stale. At any rate, when he spoke of them, she turned away. She tried, gently, to steer the conversation elsewhere.

He would coax her into moonlight walks across the bridge, just to recite his longest ballad. He’d lead her to secluded seats where the waves lapped softly, and when they were alone, with the sea before them and cliffs behind, he’d pull out his latest poems and read them with trembling voice. He didn’t seem to realize they were rhyme, not poetry. From Shirley’s downcast eyes and uneasy silence, it was clear she did.

She tried to guide him away from this obsession. It was his passion, but on other subjects he was sensible. She welcomed his questions about Nunnely. She delighted in describing the ancient priory, the wild park, the old church. She urged him to return, to gather his tenants beneath ancestral halls.

To her surprise, he obeyed. By late September, he arrived at the priory.

He soon visited Fieldhead—and stayed. After touring the neighborhood, he said no roof had offered him greater comfort than the gray manor of Briarfield. Modest compared to his own, yet he cherished it.

Soon, sitting beside Shirley in the panelled parlour wasn’t enough. He needed solitude—time to share his newest verses. So he invited her to his grounds, to forests, to valleys watered by the Aire. He led her through peaceful pastures, to still waters.

Her reputation grew. Her uncle foresaw greatness. He imagined himself casually mentioning “my nephew the baronet” with a nonchalant leg crossed. Now, he saw his niece not as a mad girl, but as a “most sensible woman.” He called her, in private, “a truly superior person—unusual, but brilliant.” He bowed before her, opened doors, stooped to pick up her gloves, suffering headaches from bending so often. He joked about women’s wit surpassing men’s wisdom, apologized for misjudging “a certain personage near Fieldhead.” In short, he strutted like a cock in polished shoes.

Yet Shirley remained calm. She seemed half-aware of his efforts. When accused of favoring Sir Philip, she replied simply: “I believe he likes me. And I like him. I never thought a man of rank—the only son of a loving mother, the only brother of devoted sisters—could possess so much goodness and sense.”

Time proved she was right. Sir Philip did like her. Perhaps he found in her the same “curious charm” Mr. Hall had noted. He sought her more and more—until her presence became essential to him. Around Fieldhead, strange emotions stirred. Hopes fluttered, anxieties haunted the halls. Some wandered restlessly through the fields. A silent expectation filled the air.

One thing was certain: Sir Philip was no man to scorn. He was kind. Though not deeply intellectual, he was thoughtful. Shirley could not say of him what she had of Sam Wynne—that his feelings were blunt, his tastes coarse, his manners vulgar. He had sensitivity. He cared for art, though not perfectly. He carried the grace of an English gentleman. His lineage and wealth far surpassed hers.

At first, her humor had mocked his appearance—boyish, slight, sandy-haired, small in stature. But she soon stopped. If anyone else made fun, she would defend him fiercely. “He has a pleasing face,” she insisted. “And there’s something in his heart worth more than three Roman noses, more than Absalom’s hair or Saul’s height.” She still teased gently about his poetry—but only allowed herself to do so.

In short, matters had reached a point where Mr. Yorke felt compelled to remark to Louis Moore:

“Your brother Robert seems either a fool or a madman. Two months ago, I’d have sworn he had the game in his hands. Now he’s running the country, spending weeks in London, and by the time he returns, he’ll find himself checkmated. Louis, ‘there is a tide in the affairs of men which, taken at the flood, leads on to fortune, but once missed, never returns.’ I’d write to Robert and remind him.”

“Robert had hopes for Miss Keeldar?” Louis asked, as if stunned.

“I suggested it to him. And he might have succeeded—she liked him.”

“As a neighbor?”

“As more. I’ve seen her flush and change color at the mere mention of his name. Write to him, I say. Tell him to come home. He’s a finer gentleman than this little baronet.”

“Doesn’t it seem presumptuous—for a penniless man to dream of a rich woman’s hand?”

“Oh, if you want lofty ideals, I’ve nothing to say. I’m plain and practical. If Robert chooses to surrender a royal prize to a pampered aristocrat, I’m content. But at _his_ age, in _his_ position—with _his_ reasons—I’d have fought. No baronet, no duke, no prince should steal my sweetheart without a battle. But tutors are solemn creatures—talking to you is like speaking to a parson.”

* * * * *

Though flattered and courted, Shirley was not spoiled. Beneath the attention, her true self remained. People no longer linked her name with Moore’s. Silence had fallen, seemingly approved by her own apparent forgetting. Yet she had not forgotten him—not entirely. She still cared, if not with love, then with interest. This was evident in the sudden tenderness she showed Louis Moore when illness struck him.

Moore may have caught the fever during visits to poor cottages with Mr. Hall and his pupil. Whatever the cause, he fell ill. After resisting for days, he was confined to bed.

One evening, Henry watched by his side, when a soft tap came at the door—too light for Mrs. Gill or the maid.

“How is Mr. Moore tonight?” asked a low voice from the dark gallery.

“Come in and see for yourself.”

“Is he asleep?”

“I wish he could sleep. Come talk to him, Shirley.”

“He wouldn’t like it.”

But she stepped in. Henry, seeing her hesitate, took her hand and led her to the bed.

The dim light barely revealed her—yet she was dressed elegantly. A party waited below, including Sir Philip. The ladies were in the drawing room. She had slipped away to visit the tutor.

Her white dress, fair arms, golden chain around her throat, glistened in the shadows. Her expression was quiet, thoughtful. She spoke gently.

“Mr. Moore, how are you?”

“I’ve not been very ill. I’m better now.”

“I heard you were thirsty. I brought grapes. Can you taste one?”

“No. But thank you for remembering.”

“Just one.”

She plucked a berry and offered it. He shook his head, turning his flushed face away.

“But what can I bring instead? You don’t want fruit—but your lips are dry. What would you prefer?”

“Mrs. Gill gives me toast-and-water. That’s best.”

Silence.

“Do you suffer? Any pain?”

“Very little.”

“What caused this?”

Silence.

“I wonder what sparked this fever? What do you think?”

“Miasma, perhaps. Autumn brings fevers.”

“I know you visit the sick often, with Mr. Hall. Be careful—recklessness isn’t wise.”

“That reminds me, Miss Keeldar—you should not enter here. I don’t believe my illness is contagious. I scarcely fear _you_ catching it. But why risk even a shadow?”

“Patience. I’ll go soon. But I’d like to do something for you—any small service.”

“They’ll miss you downstairs.”

“No. The gentlemen are still at table.”

“They won’t stay long. Sir Philip doesn’t drink wine. I heard him move from dining to drawing room.”

“It’s a servant.”

“It’s Sir Philip. I know his step.”

“Your hearing is sharp.”

“It’s never dull. And it seems sharper now. Sir Philip was here yesterday. I heard you sing him a song he brought. I heard him call you outside at eleven to look at the evening star.”

“You’re overly sensitive.”

“I heard him kiss your hand.”

“Impossible!”

“No. My room is above the hall. The window faces the front door. The sash was slightly open—I felt feverish. You stood ten minutes with him on the steps. I heard every word. And I heard the kiss.”

“Henry, bring me water.”

“Let me give it.”

But he rose slightly to take the glass from Henry, declining her help.

“And can I do nothing?”

“Nothing. You can’t guarantee me a night of peace. That’s all I need.”

“You don’t sleep well?”

“Sleep has abandoned me.”

“Yet you said you weren’t seriously ill?”

“I am often sleepless—even when healthy.”

“If I had the power, I’d wrap you in deep, quiet slumber—without dreams.”

“Blank oblivion? I don’t desire that.”

“With dreams of all you wish.”

“Monstrous fantasies! Then sleep would be delirium, waking—death.”

“Your wishes aren’t so unreal. You’re no dreamer.”

“Miss Keeldar, I suppose you think so. But my character isn’t as clear to you as a new novel.”

“That’s possible. But this sleep—I’d like to coax it to your pillow. Let me read aloud. I can spare half an hour.”

“Thank you. But I won’t keep you.”

“I’ll read softly.”

“It won’t help. I’m too feverish to bear a soft, soothing voice near me. Please leave.”

“All right. I’ll go.”

“And no good-night?”

“Yes, sir. Mr. Moore, good-night.” (Exit Shirley.)

“Henry, go to bed. It’s time.”

“Sir, I’d gladly watch all night.”

“Not necessary. I’m improving. Go.”

“Give me your blessing, sir.”

“God bless you, my best pupil.”

“You never call me your dearest.”

“No. And never shall.”

* * * * *

Perhaps Miss Keeldar resented the tutor’s refusal of her courtesy. She never repeated it. Though her footsteps often passed his door, she never paused. Her soft voice never disturbed the quiet again. Soon, the sickroom was no longer needed—Moore’s strong constitution overcame the illness. In a few days, he returned to duty.

Yet “auld lang syne” still held sway. Sometimes, he crossed the distance she kept between them, laying aside her reserve with quiet authority.

One afternoon, the Sympsons had gone out. Shirley, pleased to escape their company, stayed behind, saying she had business—letter-writing. Once finished, she walked to the garden.

It was a tranquil autumn day. The golden light of Indian summer bathed the fields. Woods stood ripe, leaves still clinging. Purple heather bloomed faintly on hills. The beck flowed silently to the Hollow. No wind stirred. The gardens bore signs of gentle decay—yellow leaves drifted onto swept paths. Flowers and fruit were gone, save a few apples. One blossom, pale and delicate, clung to a branch.

Shirley gathered these last blooms, threading them into a scentless bouquet. As she walked, Henry called from the house.

“Shirley, Mr. Moore wants to see you in the schoolroom. He’d like you to read a bit of French—if you’re free.”

He delivered the message plainly, as if it were ordinary.

“Did Mr. Moore tell you to say that?”

“Of course. Why not? Come now. Let’s be as we were at Sympson Grove. We used to enjoy school hours.”

Shirley may have thought things had changed. But she said nothing, only paused, then followed Henry quietly.

Shirley seemed to be in one of her most cheerful moods the morning after her evening with Sir Philip, and her spirits were evident as she descended for breakfast.

"Who would like to take a walk with me?" she asked brightly. "Isabella and Gertrude, will you join me?"

Such an invitation from Miss Keeldar to her cousins was so rare that they hesitated before accepting. But when their mother gave her approval, they quickly fetched their bonnets, and the three set off together.

Though they often walked separately, these young women rarely spent much time in each other’s company. Shirley preferred few companions among ladies—indeed, her truest affections were reserved for Mrs. Pryor and Caroline Helstone. She was polite, kind, even attentive toward her cousins, but conversation between them usually remained light and brief. Yet on this particular morning, she managed to captivate them with a warmth and liveliness that transformed even the most ordinary topics into something vivid and engaging. Her words sparkled with life, as if every sentence carried its own quiet fire.

What could have stirred such joy in her? There was no reason in the world around her. The day was dull—a fading autumn afternoon, overcast and grey. The paths through the woods were damp, the air heavy, the sky clouded. Yet inside Shirley’s heart seemed to glow with the sunlit brilliance of Italy, and in her eyes, clear and calm as English skies, danced a warmth that no season could dim.

As they neared Fieldhead on their return, Shirley paused to speak with her foreman, John. A delay of perhaps twenty minutes separated her from her cousins. During that time, she had spoken with him, then lingered by the gate, lost in thought. A call to luncheon brought her back into the house. She declined the meal and went upstairs.

“Isn’t Shirley coming to lunch?” Isabella asked. “She said she was hungry.”

An hour passed. Still, she did not appear. One of her cousins went to check on her. She found Shirley sitting at the foot of the bed, her head resting on her hand, her face pale, her expression thoughtful—almost sorrowful.

“You’re not unwell?” the cousin asked.

“A little sick,” Shirley replied quietly.

It was clear she was far changed from the woman who had walked beside them just hours earlier.

This shift—explained only by those three words—had no visible cause. It had come swiftly, in less than ten minutes, and it settled upon her like a shadow that refused to lift. At dinner, she spoke as usual, joined in conversation, stayed through the evening. When questioned again about her health, she insisted she was perfectly recovered. “Only a momentary faintness,” she said, brushing it aside. “Nothing worth thinking about.” Yet everyone sensed the difference—something deeper, quieter, more profound.

The next day, and the days after, that subtle change lingered. A stillness took hold of Shirley—her gaze, her movements, even her voice carried a new weight. The transformation wasn’t dramatic enough to warrant constant inquiry, but it was undeniable. It clung to her like a storm-cloud no wind could scatter. Soon, it became clear that drawing attention to it only troubled her. At first, she withdrew from conversation; if pressed, she met the concern with sharp pride. “Am I ill?” she’d ask, almost challenging. “I am not.”

“Is anything troubling you? Has something happened?”

She scoffed. “What do you mean by ‘spirits’? I have no spirits—black or white, blue or grey—to affect.”

“There must be something wrong. You’ve changed.”

“I suppose I have a right to change when I please. I know I’m plainer now. If I choose to grow less attractive, why should anyone fret?”

“There must be a reason. What is it?”

She turned away sharply. “Please leave me alone.”

Then she would try—desperately—to seem bright again. But when she failed, she would berate herself in private. Alone, she muttered harsh words: “Fool! Coward!” “Poltroon! If you must tremble, do it where no one sees!” She scolded herself: “How dare you show weakness? How dare you betray your fears? Shake them off. Rise above them. If you can’t, hide them.”

And hide them she did. She resumed her usual cheerfulness in public. But when exhaustion overwhelmed her, she fled—not to her room, where she refused to brood, but out into the wild open spaces, riding Zoë, her mare, across the moors. Long rides of half a day became her habit. Her uncle disapproved, but dared not protest. Even in her healthiest moments, Shirley’s temper was formidable; now, with her face thinner, her eyes hollowed, there was something in her silence and intensity that both moved and alarmed.

To strangers who commented on her altered appearance—unaware of the inner struggle—she repeated only one thing:

“I am perfectly well. Not a single ailment.”

And indeed, she must have been strong, to endure the weather she faced. Rain or shine, calm or gale, she rode daily over Stilbro' Moor, Tartar galloping beside her, relentless and tireless.

Twice, three times, curious eyes—those ever-watchful eyes of gossip—noticed that instead of turning back at Rushedge, the highest ridge of the moor, she continued all the way to the town. Observers soon learned she stopped at the home of Mr. Pearson Hall, a solicitor related to the vicar of Nunnely. For generations, his family had served the Keeldars as agents. Rumours began to spread. Some said Shirley was entangled in business matters involving Hollow's Mill—perhaps she had lost money and was forced to mortgage land. Others whispered she was to marry, and settlements were being arranged.

* * * * *

In the schoolroom, Mr. Moore waited for Henry Sympson to finish preparing his lesson.

"Henry, make haste. The afternoon is slipping away."

"Is it, sir?"

"Certainly. Are you nearly ready?"

"No."

"Not nearly ready?"

"I haven’t done a line."

Mr. Moore looked up. The boy’s tone held a strange note.

"The lesson isn’t difficult, Henry. If it is, bring it to me. We’ll work through it together."

"Mr. Moore, I can’t study."

"My boy, you’re unwell."

"Sir, I’m no worse physically than usual—but my heart is full."

"Close the book. Come here, Harry. Sit by the fire."

Harry limped forward. His tutor placed him in a chair. His lips trembled, his eyes filled with tears. He laid down his crutch, bowed his head, and wept.

"This pain isn’t physical, you say, Harry? You carry a grief. Tell me what it is."

"Sir, I’ve never known grief like this. I wish it could be lifted somehow—I can barely bear it."

"Perhaps talking will help. What caused it? Whom does it concern?"

"It’s Shirley, sir. It’s about Shirley."

"Does it? You think she’s changed?"

"Everyone who knows her thinks so—except perhaps you, Mr. Moore."

"Not seriously. I see only slight changes—ones that might fade in weeks. And she says she’s well. That counts for something."

"That’s exactly it. As long as she said she was well, I believed her. When I felt sad in her absence, I’d find joy again in her presence. Now… now…"

"Now, Harry? Has she said anything to you? You two were together in the garden this morning. I saw her speaking, and you listening. If Miss Keeldar has told you she’s ill—and asked you to keep it secret—don’t obey her. For her sake, tell everything. Speak, my boy."

"She say she’s ill? I believe, sir, if she were dying, she’d smile and say, 'Nothing ails me.'"

"What have you learned, then? What new truth?"

"I’ve learned she’s just made her will."

"Made her will?"

They sat in silence.

"She told me," Henry said after a pause, "quite calmly, as if it were nothing ominous—though I felt it was. She said I was the only person besides her solicitor, Pearson Hall, Mr. Helstone, and Mr. Yorke who knew about it. And to me, she wanted to explain its terms."

"Go on, Harry."

"'Because,' she said, looking down at me with her beautiful eyes—oh, they are beautiful, Mr. Moore! I love them! I love her! She is my star! Heaven must not claim her! She belongs here, in this world. Shirley is not an angel—she’s a woman, and she should live among men. Seraphs shall not have her! Mr. Moore, if one of the sons of God, with wings wide as the sky and bright as the sea, saw how fair she was and came to claim her, I would stand against him—even though I’m only a boy and crippled!'"

"Henry Sympson, go on when I tell you."

"'Because,' she said, 'if I died before you, Harry, and made no will, all my property would pass to you. But I don’t intend that—though your father would like it. But you,' she said, 'will inherit your father’s estate—larger than Fieldhead. Your sisters will get nothing, so I’ve left them some money, though I don’t love them both as much as I love one lock of your hair.' She called me her 'darling,' let me kiss her. Then she told me she’d left Caroline Helstone a sum too. This manor house—with its books and furniture—she’s given to me, because she doesn-’t want to take the old family seat from her own blood. And the rest—about twelve thousand pounds, beyond the legacies—she’s left not to me, since I’m already rich, but to a good man who would use it wisely. A man gentle and brave, strong and merciful—one who may not claim piety, but who has true faith. He visits the fatherless and widows in distress, keeps himself pure from the world. Then she asked, 'Do you approve, Harry?' I couldn’t answer. My tears choked me—as they do now.'"

Mr. Moore let the boy gather himself. Then he asked, "What else did she say?"

"After I agreed, she said I was generous, and proud of me. 'Now,' she added, 'if anything happens, you’ll know what to tell Malice when she whispers lies—suggesting I wronged you, that I didn’t love you. You’ll know I _did_ love you, Harry—more than any sister ever could. My treasure.' Mr. Moore, when I remember her voice, her look—my heart beats as if it would break. She may go to heaven before me—if God commands it, she must. But the rest of my life—short as it will be, and I’m glad of that now—will be a swift, purposeful journey along the path her footsteps have marked. I thought to lie in the Keeldar vault before her. If not, lay my coffin beside hers."

Moore answered with quiet gravity, a stark contrast to the boy’s passion.

"You’re both mistaken—you harm each other. Youth, once touched by fear, imagines darkness will last forever. Its first sorrow feels like a lifetime. What else did she say?"

"We discussed a few family matters."

"I’d like to hear them."

"But, Mr. Moore, you’re smiling. I couldn’t smile seeing Shirley like that."

"My boy, I’m neither poetic nor sentimental. I see things clearly. You don’t yet. Tell me these points."

"Just that she asked if I considered myself more Keeldar or Sympson. I said, 'Keeldar to the core of my heart and marrow of my bones.' She said she was glad—for besides her, I was the only Keeldar left in England. Then we agreed on a few things."

"Well?"

"If I inherit my father’s estate and her house, I’m to take the name Keeldar and make Fieldhead my home. Henry Shirley Keeldar—I said I’d be called that, and I will. Her name and her manor are ancient. Sympson and Sympson Grove are of yesterday."

"Come, neither of you are going to heaven yet. I have high hopes for you both—proud, ambitious souls. Now, what conclusion do you draw from all this?"

"That Shirley thinks she’s going to die."

"She mentioned her health?"

"Not once. But I swear she’s wasting. Her hands are thin, her cheeks hollow."

"Does she complain to your mother or sisters?"

"Never. She laughs at them when they ask. Mr. Moore, she’s extraordinary—so fair, so girlish—yet not masculine, not fierce—but towering above help and sympathy."

"Do you know where she is now, Henry? In the house—or riding?"

"Surely not out, sir. It’s raining hard."

"True. But that doesn’t mean she isn’t riding over Rushedge. Lately, weather has never stopped her."

"You remember, sir, how stormy it was last Wednesday? So wild she wouldn’t let Zoë be saddled. Yet she walked nearly to Nunnely, facing the gale on foot. I asked her if she feared cold. 'Not I,' she said. 'It would be too much luck. I don’t know, Harry, but the best thing that could happen to me would be to catch a fever and pass on like any Christian.' She’s reckless, sir."

"Reckless indeed. Go and find her. If you can, speak to her privately and ask her to come here for a moment."

"Yes, sir."

He grabbed his crutch and hurried away.

"Harry!"

He returned.

"Don’t deliver it formally. Say it as you once would have said a simple summons to the schoolroom."

"I understand, sir. She’ll be more likely to come."

"And, Harry—"

"Yes, sir?"

"I’ll call you when I need you. Until then, you’re excused from lessons."

He left. Moore stood alone, rising from his desk.

"I can be cool and aloof with Henry," he murmured. "I can mock his fears, look down on his passion from my height. To him, I can pretend they’re both children. But can I keep that role with her? I’ve known moments when I nearly lost it—when confusion and submission threatened to crush me. My tongue faltered. I almost let the mask fall. I stood before her—no longer master, but something else. I pray I never play such a fool. It’s good for Sir Philip Nunnely to blush when he meets her eye. He may submit. He may even tremble when he touches her hand—without shame. But if one of her farmers showed such feeling, he’d need a straitjacket. So far, I’ve held myself. She’s sat near me, and I’ve not shaken—only my desk. I’ve met her looks and smiles like a tutor, as I am. I’ve never touched her hand—never faced that test. I am not her servant, nor her serf. But I am poor, and I must guard my dignity. What did she mean by those cold people who turn flesh to marble? It pleased me—though I won’t admit why. I never examine her words or face. If I did, I’d forget reason and believe in romance. A secret ecstasy flares through me sometimes. I won’t encourage it. I won’t remember it. I resolve to keep my right to say, as Paul did, 'I am not mad, but speak truth and soberness.'"

He paused, listening.

"Will she come? How will she receive the message? Naively? With scorn? Like a child or a queen? Both exist in her nature."

"If she comes, what shall I say? How justify my request? Should I apologize? I could humbly. But would that place us rightly? I must remain the teacher—otherwise—"

A door opened.

Miss Keeldar entered. She had been sewing when Henry found her. She brought her work with her. She had not ridden today. She wore her indoor dress and silk apron. Not a warrior from the fields, but a quiet domestic figure from the hearth. Moore had her at an advantage. He should have addressed her solemnly, with rigid formality. Perhaps he would have—had she looked defiant. But her expression held none of that. A soft, youthful shyness shaded her eyes, tinged her cheeks. He stood silent.

She stopped halfway between the door and his desk.

"Did you want me, sir?" she asked.

"I ventured, Miss Keeldar, to send for you—to request a brief interview."

She waited, needle in hand.

"Well, sir," she said without lifting her eyes, "what about?"

"Sit down first. The matter I wish to discuss is serious. I may not have the right to raise it. I may need to apologize. But the liberty I’ve taken comes from a conversation with Henry. He is distressed about your health—your friends are too. I wish to speak of your health."

"I am quite well," she said simply.

"Yet you’ve changed."

"That concerns only myself. We all change."

"Will you sit?"

She did.

"Formerly, Miss Keeldar, I had some influence over you. Do I now? May I assume you do not consider my words presumptuous?"

"Let me read French, Mr. Moore—or even Latin. Let us end this talk about health."

"No. It’s time for discussion."

"Discuss away—but don’t choose me as your subject. I am healthy."

"Do you not think it wrong to repeat what is fundamentally untrue?"

"I say I am well. No cough, no pain, no fever."

"Is that the whole truth?"

"The direct truth."

Moore studied her closely.

"I see no sign of disease," he said. "But why are you changed?"

"_Am_ I changed?"

"We’ll test it."

"How?"

"Do you sleep as you used to?"

"No—but not because I’m ill."

"Do you eat as you once did?"

"No—but not because I’m ill."

"You remember this ring on my watch-chain? It belonged to my mother. Too small for my little finger. You’ve often taken it playfully. It fit your forefinger. Try it now."

She allowed the test. The ring slipped from her slender hand. Moore picked it up and fastened it back. A flush crept across his brow. Shirley said again, "It’s not because I’m ill."

"Not only have you lost sleep, appetite, and flesh," Moore continued, "but your spirit is low. There’s a nervous tension in your eyes, a restlessness in your manner. These were not yours before."

"Mr. Moore, let us stop here. You’re right—I am nervous. Now, talk of something else. Look at this rain—steady, pouring!"

"_You_ nervous? Yes. And if Miss Keeldar is nervous, it’s not without cause. Let me reach it. Let me look closer. The illness is not physical. I suspected that. It began suddenly. I know the day. I noticed the change. Your pain is mental."

"Not at all. Just nervous. Please—dismiss the topic."

"When it’s exhausted. Not before. Nervous fears should be shared, so they may vanish. I wish I could persuade you to speak freely. I believe confession, in your case, would be half-cure."

"No," she said abruptly. "I wish it were true—but I fear it’s not."

She paused, then leaned her head on her hand, elbow on the table. Moore felt he had finally gained ground. She was serious. Her willingness to acknowledge unease implied a surrender of sorts. She could no longer deny that something stirred within her.

He waited. Once, his lips moved to speak—but he held back. Shirley raised her eyes to his. Had he shown emotion, she might have retreated. But he remained calm, steady, trustworthy.

"I’d rather tell _you_ than my aunt, or my cousins, or my uncle," she said. "They’d make such a fuss. I dread that—the alarm, the commotion, the _éclat_. Honestly, I never liked being the center of a little domestic storm. You can handle a shock—can’t you?"

"A great one, if needed."

Not a muscle twitched. Yet his heart beat fiercely in his chest. What would she reveal? Was disaster already done?

"Had I thought it right to come to you," she continued, "I would never have kept it hidden. I’d have told you at once, and asked your advice."

"Why wasn’t it right to come to me?"

She looked at him—then smiled faintly.

"Because I didn’t know how."

Louis Moore was accustomed to quiet—quiet in thought, in habit, in the very rhythm of his days. He was not one to seek noise or company; rather, he found comfort in stillness, as though silence were a companion he could trust. And yet, tonight, solitude felt like a weight upon his chest. The house at Fieldhead lay hushed, all but him. Miss Keeldar, the Sympsons, even Henry—all had gone to Nunnely, summoned by Sir Philip’s kind invitation to meet his mother and sisters. The baronet had extended the same courtesy to the tutor, but Moore would have preferred meeting ghosts beneath the ancient oaks of Nunnely Forest than facing that gathering. A phantom abbess? A spectral nun wandering among the ruins? That would have been preferable. But not this—this polite, expectant world of manners and duty.

And so, he was left alone.

The evening was restless. Though the storm had passed, the sky still stirred with the breath of the equinox. The clouds broke apart slowly, revealing a moon that rode high and bold, unafraid of the wind that howled through the trees. It was a moon that loved the tempest, gliding through the gale as if it welcomed the touch of the wild air. No shepherd watched from the hills tonight, no Endymion waited for his goddess. There was no audience for such beauty—only Moore, standing in the schoolroom, listening to the wind lash the gables, feeling the loneliness press in.

“All the parlours are empty,” he murmured. “I am sick at heart of this cell.”

He rose and walked away from the sheltered end of the house, drawn toward the larger windows where the night stretched wide and clear. No candle was needed—the moon poured silver light across every surface, painting shadows and lifting corners into soft brilliance. He moved from room to room, as if following an unseen presence. Then he stopped—before the oak chamber.

It was warm here. The fire burned low but steady, casting flickering gold on the walls. Near the hearth stood a small worktable, a chair beside it, a desk open, keys still in the lock. On the table: a delicate glove, a silver pen, a seal, a few ripe berries resting on a green leaf, and a tiny satin bag hanging from the back of the chair.

Moore paused. His breath caught.

She had been here. Not long ago. In haste, perhaps. Forgotten things—trifles, yes—but each one spoke of her. Careless, yet refined. Absent-minded, yet full of grace. Her mark was everywhere. And in that moment, Moore did not feel alone.

“She has been here,” he whispered. “Careless, lovely thing. Left behind a little piece of herself. Why does she leave such traces? How can she be so heedless and never seem wrong? There is always something to chide in her—and yet, when I think of reprimanding her, the words turn into a sigh, and the reprimand melts into a wish to hold her close. Better spend half an hour scolding her than a day admiring any other woman alive.”

He hesitated. Was he speaking aloud? He looked around. Yes—he was alone. Yet, he knew better than to speak too freely. So he stopped.

Then he made himself comfortable. He drew the curtains shut, blocking out the moon and its court of stars. He stoked the fire, lit a candle, placed another chair opposite the one she had sat in. And then he sat down.

From his pocket, he took a small, thick book of blank paper and a pencil. He began to write—not for anyone else, but for himself, for the truth he could not say aloud.

“It is nine o'clock. The carriage will not return before eleven. I am free until then. Free to sit in her room, to rest my elbow on her table, to breathe in the air she breathed.

I once liked solitude. I thought of it as a quiet nymph, a mountain spirit descending to me in mist and chill. I used to find peace in her silence. But since that evening—since she came to me in the schoolroom, opened her heart, asked for help, leaned on me—I have come to hate solitude. It is cold. It is dead. It is the sister of death.

Now, I find joy in writing about what is near—what is real. This book is mine. No one can take it from me. Through these words, I can say what I dare not speak, what I cannot even think without trembling.

We have scarcely met since that night. Once, I was searching for a book in the drawing-room when she entered, dressed for a concert. She saw me, nodded slightly, and passed to the window. Her shyness—oh, her shyness!—it was not awkwardness, but a kind of sacred reserve. I have heard much about maiden modesty, but never truly understood it until now. When she turned away, her cheek flushed, I called her nothing but ‘stainless virgin’ in my mind. There was a glow about her—a quiet radiance. And I, plain and dull as I am, felt myself lifted by her presence.

I know I sound foolish. Dreaming. Romantic. But what can I do? She has awakened something in me—something I never knew existed. I see her now—looking up at me, pleading, almost desperate, asking me not to let them smother her, begging for a strong narcotic. I see the tear fall silently from her eyelash. I see her confessing that she is not as strong as people believe. And I remember thinking—yes, I thought it—she is childish. But I did not despise her. Oh, no. I felt something deeper—something tender. I felt the right to protect her, to stand beside her, to hold her when she trembles.

I worship her perfections. But it is her flaws—her little weaknesses—that draw me closer. They are the steps I climb to reach her. If she were perfect, polished, untouched by human frailty, I would not know how to love her. It is the uneven ground—the mossy slope, the hollows—that invites ascent. It is the natural hill, not the artificial mound.

Let me be honest. I delight in seeing her. I would recognize her anywhere. If I were a king and she a servant sweeping my stairs, I would still see her worth. If I were a gentleman and she served me at my table, I could not help liking her. Take away her dress, her education, her wealth—leave only her form, her smile, her warmth—and I would still want to stay. I would linger. I would regret leaving.

How careless she is—leaving her desk open, her keys hanging in the lock, a purse spilling from a satin bag. Robert would be furious. He would call her reckless. But I—my irritation is sweet. I enjoy finding fault with her. I would welcome her imperfections if she lived under my roof. She would give me reasons to lecture her—just as I sometimes do with Henry, though with him, a word suffices. With her… I would talk. I would argue. I would scold—yet never truly mean it.

When her eyes meet mine, when her mood turns teasing, mischievous—then I am most drawn to her. She is never more beautiful than when she returns from a ride, wind-tousled, fiery-eyed. I have waited in the courtyard, just to see her dismount. To catch her in my arms. And I have noticed—only to this page, never to another—that she allows no man but me to help her from the saddle. Even Sir Philip, whom she treats with gentle kindness, she politely declines. Sam Wynne, too—she refuses him with quiet dignity.

And I know—my heart knows—it is not reluctance. It is choice. She gives herself to me willingly. And I, I am not her slave. But my thoughts, my strength, my calm—they gather around her like genii drawn to a lamp. All my faculties wait for her command. And when she speaks, they leap to serve.

She is careless—but never vulgar. Her carelessness reveals refinement, not disorder. I have seen her belongings scattered—her gloves, her pens, her bags—and never found anything sordid. Everything bears her elegance. Look at this glove—pure kid, unsullied. The satin bag—fresh, clean. She is careless in manner, but meticulous in soul.

There is a difference between her and Caroline. Caroline is precise, exact, faultless. She would suit Robert—perfectly. But what could I do with someone so flawless? She is like a lily—pure, innocent, needing no change. But I—my heart desires the rose. Sweet, yes—but guarded by thorns. My future wife must stir me, challenge me, awaken my patience. I was not born to live with a lamb. I would rather tend a young lioness.

I like sweetness that is also sharp. Light that burns. Beauty that fights back when teased. Fascination that threatens to become fierce. I fear I would tire of innocence that never stirs. But I would rejoice in taming a wild spirit—in guiding a restless merlin.

O Shirley! O Peri! Too rebellious for heaven, too pure for hell. I shall never possess you. I can only watch, adore, and desire.

Yet I know—I know—I could make you happy. Will it be my fate to see you given to one who cannot?

Beware, Sir Philip Nunnely. I have seen you at her side. I have seen her lips tighten, her brow furrow, her body recoil slightly—as if enduring something she does9t admire, something she tolerates only because you are good in some other way. I have seen her face grow grave, her eyes flash with quiet resistance. And I think of Semele—but reversed.

Not the daughter of Cadmus longing to see Zeus in glory. But a priest of Juno, watching alone in the temple. For years, he has dreamed of her. He loves the idol. He prays for her favor. And she hears. She will be kind.

All Argos sleeps. The temple doors are closed. The priest waits at the altar.

Then—a shock. Not felt by the city, but by him. Silence. Then—light. From the heavens, through the shattered roof, pours a blaze. Stars fall. A god descends.

He has what he asked for. But he cannot look. He is blinded. He hears a sound—unbearable. He sees a glory—too terrible. Gods, have mercy!

In the morning, a pious man enters to offer prayer. Thunder struck last night. The shrine is broken. The marble split. Saturnia’s statue stands unharmed. At her feet—ashes. No priest remains. The watcher is gone.

* * *

There—there is the carriage!

I must lock the desk. Pocket the keys. She will come looking for them tomorrow. She will have to come to me.

I hear her voice—clear, hesitant, ashamed—‘Mr. Moore, have you seen my keys?’

Yes. That is how she will ask. Twenty times already. And I will keep her waiting. I will tease her. I will make her beg. And when I return them—only after a lecture. The bag. The purse. The glove. The pen. The seal. Each one, slowly, with confession, with entreaty.

I will make every touch a privilege. Every glance, every smile, every movement—mine to cherish. If I must be her slave, I will not lose my freedom. I will claim every moment, every detail, every breath she takes—for myself.”

He locked the desk. He gathered the items. He tucked them into his coat. Then he left the room, closing the door softly behind him, as if he had never been there at all.

Everyone agreed it was long past time for Mr. Moore to return home. Briarfield buzzed with curiosity over his prolonged absence, and even Whinbury and Nunnely added their own shares of astonishment.

Was there any explanation? Oh yes—there were at least forty plausible ones circulating through the village, each more convincing than the last. But one thing everyone did agree on: it wasn’t business that kept him away. That had been settled long before. He’d tracked down the four ringleaders, seen them tried, convicted, sentenced, and shipped off to transportation. The newspapers had reported every detail—the *Stilbro’ Courier* even embellished the story with dramatic flair.

Yet no one celebrated his success. Not openly, at least. The mill-owners were relieved, hoping the weight of justice would silence the unrest simmering beneath the surface. But the discontent still muttered in alehouses, swearing dark oaths over cheap beer and raising toast after toast in fiery gin.

Some said Moore dared not return to Yorkshire. They whispered he knew his life wouldn’t be safe if he stepped foot back into the county.

“I’ll tell him that,” Mr. Yorke declared when his foreman mentioned the rumor. “And if that doesn’t bring him galloping home, nothing will.”

Whatever it was—fear, duty, or something else—it finally brought him back. He told Joe Scott the exact day he’d arrive in Stilbro’, asking that a hackney be sent to the George for his lodging. Joe passed the word to Mr. Yorke, who made sure to be there to greet him.

It was market day. Moore arrived just in time for the customary dinner among the manufacturers. Though somewhat of an outsider, he was a man of consequence, and the gathering welcomed him with polite deference. Some men who would never have acknowledged him in public—afraid of being tainted by the hatred reserved for him—now spoke of him as a kind of champion behind closed doors. As wine flowed, their admiration warmed, but Moore’s calm indifference kept it from ever turning into fervor.

Mr. Yorke, the steady president of these dinners, watched his young friend with quiet satisfaction. Few things stirred his temper more than seeing a man flattered into pride; few things soothed him more than witnessing someone untouched by fame—someone who didn’t care what others thought.

Robert sat back in his chair, quiet, almost sullen, while clothiers and blanket-makers praised his courage, retold his exploits, and tossed in coarse insults against the working class. To Yorke, it was a delight to see Moore endure it all without a flicker of pleasure. Flattery could be laughed off. Abuse could be shrugged aside. But praise from those you despise? That cuts deep.

Yorke couldn’t help himself. “How do you like your supporters?” he asked. “Don’t you think they honor your cause?”

“But it’s a pity,” he added, “that you didn’t hang those four scoundrels. If you’d done that, the gentry here would’ve torn the horses from the coach, yoked twenty donkeys to it, and pulled you into Stilbro’ like a conquering hero.”

Moore soon left the table, abandoning the wine and the company. In less than five minutes, Mr. Yorke followed.

They rode out of Stilbro’ together.

The day was late, though not yet night. The last light of the sun had slipped from the clouds, and October’s chill was settling over the moors, cloaking the land in shadow.

Yorke, slightly warmed by his drink and pleased to have Moore back in Yorkshire, took the reins of conversation. He touched briefly, mockingly, on the trials and convictions, then drifted into local gossip—before turning directly to Moore.

“Bob,” he said, “I believe you’ve lost her—and you deserve it. Everything was smooth. Fortune herself seemed to fall for you. She’d handed you the grand prize—twenty thousand pounds. All you had to do was reach out and take it. And what did you do? You saddled up and rode off to Warwickshire, chasing foxes like a fool. Your sweetheart—Fortune, I mean—was patient. She said, ‘He’s young. I’ll forgive him.’ She waited, like Patience on a monument, until the hunt was done and the prey caught. She expected you’d come back, a good lad, and claim your reward. You might still have had her.”

“It stunned me—stunned me completely—to find that instead of riding home at breakneck speed, laying your laurels at her feet, you calmly took a coach to London. What you did there, only Satan knows. Probably just sat and sulked. Your face was never pale, but now it’s green with regret. You’re not half the man you used to be.”

“And who gets this prize you keep talking about?”

“Only a baronet—that’s all. I’m certain you’ve lost her. She’ll be Lady Nunnely by Christmas.”

“Hmm. Quite possible.”

“But she needn’t have been. Foolish boy! I swear you could’ve had her.”

“By what sign, Mr. Yorke?”

“Every sign—by the fire in her eyes, the flush on her cheeks. They turned red when your name came up, even though usually they’re pale.”

“My chance is gone, I suppose?”

“It should be. But try. It’s worth trying. I call Sir Philip weak tea. And he writes poetry, they say—trite rhymes. _You_ are above that, Robert, at least.”

“Would you advise me to propose, so late—this close to the end?”

“You can but try, Robert. If she cared for you—if, by my soul, I believe she did or once did—she’ll forgive much. But you’re laughing. At me? Better laugh at your own stubbornness. I see you’re grinning on the wrong side of your mouth. You look sourer than I’d wish to see anyone.”

“I’ve quarrelled with myself, Yorke. I’ve kicked against the pricks, struggled in a tight waistcoat, twisted my wrists in handcuffs, battered my head against a harder wall.”

“Ha! Good for you. Sharp exercise. I hope it’s helped—taken some of the arrogance out of you?”

“Arrogance? What is it? Self-respect? Even self-tolerance? Do you sell those? Know anyone who does? Point me to a shop. I’d buy them all—give my last guinea right now.”

“So it is with you, Robert? That’s spicy. I like honesty. What’s wrong?”

“The whole machine of my nature—every gear in this human mill. The boiler—my heart—is ready to burst.”

“That ought to be printed. It’s poetic. You’re almost writing verse already. If inspiration strikes, let it flow, Robert. Don’t mind me—I’ll bear it this time.”

“Hideous, terrible mistake! You can do something in a moment that you’ll regret for years—something life can’t erase.”

“Go on, lad. I call it pie, nuts, sugar-candy. I love the taste. Go on. Talking will help. The moor lies ahead, and we’re alone for miles.”

“I *will* talk. I’m not ashamed. There’s a wildcat in my chest, and I want you to hear its roar.”

“To me, it’s music. You and Louis both have powerful voices. When Louis sings—soft, deep tones like a bell—I’ve felt my bones tremble. The night is still. It listens. It leans toward you, like a black priest to a darker penitent. Confess, lad. Don’t hide anything. Be as honest as a Methody preacher at an experience meeting. Make yourself as wicked as Beelzebub. It’ll ease your mind.”

“As mean as Mammon, you’d say. Yorke, if I dismounted and laid myself across the road, would you ride over me—back and forth—about twenty times?”

“With the greatest pleasure—if there were no coroner’s inquest.”

“Hiram Yorke, I truly believed she loved me. I’ve seen her eyes spark with joy when she spotted me in a crowd. Her cheeks flushed crimson when she offered me her hand and said, ‘How do you do, Mr. Moore?’”

“My name had magic for her. When others said it, she changed color. I know she did. She said it herself, in the most musical tone of all her many voices. She was warm to me, interested, concerned, wished me well. She sought every chance to help me. I watched, studied, pondered. I could draw only one conclusion—this was love.”

“I looked at her, Yorke. Youthful, strikingly beautiful. Powerful. Her wealth meant redemption—for my honor, my standing. I owed her gratitude. She’d lent me five thousand pounds—substantial help. Could I forget that? Could I believe she loved me? Could I listen to reason urging me to marry her, and ignore every advantage, dismiss every flattering thought, reject every sound advice, and walk away? Young, graceful, generous—my benefactress, devoted to me, enamored of me. I used to say that to myself, repeat the words, swell with secret pride, admire myself entirely, unshaken even by respect for her. I smiled in private at her naïveté—her simplicity in loving first, in showing it.”

“That whip of yours looks heavy, Yorke. Swing it around your head. Knock me off my horse if you like. I’d welcome a good thrashing.”

“Hold on, Robert. Wait till the moon rises and I can see you. Speak plainly—did you love her or not? I want to know. I’m curious.”

“Sir—sir—I’ll say—she’s very pretty, in her way. Very captivating. At times, she seems made of fire and air—something I stand and marvel at, without wanting to hold her or kiss her. I felt her pull—strong on my interest, my vanity. But I never felt as if nature meant her to be my other half. When the question came, I pushed it aside. I told myself brutally: I’d be rich with her, ruined without her. I vowed to be practical—not romantic.”

“A sensible decision. What happened, Bob?”

“With that resolve, I went to Fieldhead one night last August. The very eve of my departure for Birmingham. I wanted to secure Fortune’s prize. I’d sent a note requesting a private meeting. I found her at home, alone.”

“She greeted me without hesitation—she thought I was there on business. I was nervous, but determined. I don’t remember how I managed it, but I pressed forward, cold and firm—frightening, I suppose. I offered myself—my person, my debts included—as a settlement.”

“It angered me—set my blood boiling—to see she didn’t blush, tremble, or lower her eyes. She replied, ‘I doubt whether I’ve understood you, Mr. Moore.’”

“I had to repeat the whole proposal twice, spell it out like ABC, before she grasped it. Then—what did she do? Instead of saying Yes with a soft voice, or keeping silent in confusion (which would’ve been sweet), she jumped up, walked twice quickly across the room—just the way *she* does, no one else—and gasped, ‘God bless me!’”

“Yorke, I stood by the fireplace, leaning against the mantel, bracing myself for anything. I knew my fate. I knew myself. Her expression, her voice—no misunderstanding. She stopped and looked at me.”

“‘God bless me!’ she repeated, in that shocked, indignant, yet sorrowful tone. ‘You’ve made a strange proposal—strange from *you*. And if you knew how strangely you said it, how you looked, you’d be horrified at yourself. You spoke like a brigand demanding my purse, not like a lover asking for my heart.’”

“A strange sentence, wasn’t it, Yorke? And as she said it, I knew it was true—strangely true. Her words were a mirror. I saw myself.”

“I stared at her, speechless, fierce. She enraged and shamed me.”

“‘Gérard Moore,’ she said, ‘you know you don’t love Shirley Keeldar.’”

“I could have lied—sworn I did—but I couldn’t lie in her clear face. I couldn’t perjure myself in her truth. Besides, such false oaths would’ve been meaningless. She wouldn’t have believed me any more than she’d believe Judas’s ghost if he rose from the night. Her heart sensed deeper than deception—she knew my admiration was half-coarse, half-cold, not true, throbbing love.”

“What happened next?” Yorke asked.

“She sat down in the window seat and wept. Passionately. Tears fell like rain, but also flashed—bright, dark, proud eyes fixed on me. They said, ‘You’ve hurt me. You’ve insulted me. You’ve deceived me.’”

“She spoke soon after.”

“‘I *did* respect you—I *did* admire you—I *did* like you,’ she said—‘yes, as much as if you were my brother. But *you—you* want to make a bargain of me. You’d sacrifice me to that mill, your Moloch!’”

“I stayed silent. I had the sense not to defend myself. I let her scorn me.”

“I was sold to the devil, for a time. I was infatuated. When I did speak, what do you think I said?”

“‘Whatever my feelings, I was convinced *you* loved *me*, Miss Keeldar.’”

“Beautiful, wasn’t it? She sat stunned. ‘Is it Robert Moore speaking?’ I heard her mutter. ‘Is it a man—or something lower?’”

“‘Do you mean,’ she asked aloud, ‘do you mean you thought I loved you as we love those we wish to marry?’”

“It *was* my meaning. I said so.”

“‘You held an idea offensive to a woman’s feelings,’ she answered. ‘You’ve expressed it in a way revolting to a woman’s soul. You suggest that every kind word I’ve given you was part of a bold, immodest scheme to trap a husband. You imply you come here now out of pity, offering your hand because I’ve pursued you. Let me say this: Your vision is jaundiced; you’ve seen wrongly. Your mind is twisted; you’ve judged wrongly. Your tongue betrays you; you now speak falsely. I never loved you. Be at peace on that. My heart is as pure of passion for you as yours is empty of affection for me.’”

“Did I get my answer, Yorke?”

“‘I seem to be a blind, foolish man,’ I said.”

“‘*Loved* you!’ she cried. ‘Why, I’ve been as open with you as a sister—never avoided you, never feared you. You cannot,’ she declared triumphantly, ‘make me tremble at your presence, nor quicken my pulse by your nearness.’”

“I argued that often, when she spoke to me, she blushed, and the sound of my name moved her.”

“‘Not for *your* sake!’ she said sharply. I pressed for explanation, but got none.”

“‘When I sat beside you at the school feast, did you think I loved you then? When I stopped you in Maythorn Lane, did you think I loved you then? When I visited you in the counting-house, walked with you on the pavement—did you think I loved you then?’”

“She questioned me. I said I did.”

“By God, Yorke, she rose—tall, radiant, almost glowing. A trembling ran through her, like live coal at its hottest.”

“‘That means you hold the worst opinion of me,’ she said. ‘You deny me everything I value. You accuse me of betraying all my sisters. You say I’ve acted as no woman can without dishonoring herself and her kind. You say I’ve sought where the pure of my sex naturally scorn and abhor to seek.’”

“We were silent for minutes. Then she continued: ‘Lucifer, Star of the Morning, thou art fallen! Once high in my esteem, you are cast down. Once close in friendship, you are cast out. Go!’”

“I didn’t go. I heard her voice shake, saw her lip quiver. I knew another storm of tears would come—and after that, calm. I waited.”

“The tears fell again—warm, fast, quieter than before. A softer sound mingled with them—regretful, sorrowful. I watched as her eyes lifted to me—more reproachful than proud, more mournful than angry.”

“‘O Moore!’ she said. Worse than ‘Et tu, Brute!’”

“I let out what should’ve been a sigh—but it became a groan. A feeling of Cain-like loneliness ached in my chest.”

“‘There has been error in what I’ve done,’ I said. ‘And it has earned me bitter wages. I’ll go and spend them far from her who gave them.’”

“I took my hat. I couldn’t bear to leave like that. I thought she wouldn’t let me go. And she wouldn’t—except for the wound I’d dealt to her pride, which silenced her compassion.”

“I had to turn back on my own, approach her, and say, ‘Forgive me.’”

“‘I could, if I weren’t also forgiving myself,’ she replied. ‘But to mislead a wise man so deeply, I must have erred.’”

“I burst out with some impassioned words I don’t recall. I know they were sincere—my aim was to absolve her in her own eyes. In truth, self-accusation was impossible in her case.”

“At last, she reached out her hand. For the first time, I wanted to hold her, to embrace her. I kissed her hand—many times.”

“‘One day,’ she said, ‘we may be friends again. When you’ve had time to understand my actions and motives rightly, not horribly misread them. Time may give you the key. Then, perhaps, you’ll understand me. Then we may be reconciled.’”

“Farewell tears rolled slowly down her cheeks. She wiped them away.”

“‘I am sorry,’ she sobbed. ‘Deeply sorry.’ So was I, God knows. And so we parted.”

“A strange tale,” said Mr. Yorke.

“I’ll never do it again,” Moore vowed. “Never will I mention marriage to a woman unless I feel love. From now on, credit and commerce can fend for themselves. Bankruptcy may come when it will. I’m done with fear of ruin. I’ll work hard, wait patiently, bear steadily. If the worst comes, I’ll take my axe and an emigrant’s passage, and go west with Louis—we’ve decided. No woman shall ever look at me as Miss Keeldar did, or feel toward me as she did. I’ll never again stand before a woman as both fool and knave, brute and pup.”

“Tut!” said Yorke, unmoved. “You’re making too much of it. Still—I’m impressed. First, that she didn’t love you. Second, that you didn’t love her. You’re both young, both handsome, both capable of wit and temper—if you’re seen properly. What went wrong that you couldn’t agree?”

“We never *have* been, never *could* be at ease with each other, Yorke. We admired each other from afar, but clashed up close. I’ve sat at one end of a room, watching her at the other—perhaps in a joyful moment, surrounded by her favorites—men like you and Helstone, with whom she’s playful, bright, eloquent. I’ve watched her when she was most natural, most lively, most lovely. My judgment said she was beautiful—beautiful when her mood and clothes matched her splendor. I’ve drawn closer, thinking our acquaintance gave me the right. I’ve joined her circle, caught her eye, held her attention. Others have withdrawn, leaving us alone. Were we happy then? For me, no. Always a tension. Always a tendency toward stiffness, distance. We talked politics, business. Never a soft intimacy, never warmth between us. Our confidences were of the counting-house, not the heart. She never made me better, gentler. She only sharpened my mind, heightened my wit. She never entered my heart, never touched its rhythm. And why? Because I never learned how to make her love me.”

“Well, lad, it’s a queer thing. I might laugh at you, mock your sensitivities. But since it’s dark and we’re alone, I’ll tell you—your words bring back a glimpse of my own past. Twenty-five years ago, I tried to win a beautiful woman’s love. She refused me. I couldn’t unlock her heart. She was a stone wall—doorless, windowless.”

“But you *loved* her, Yorke. You worshipped Mary Cave. Your behavior was that of a man—never a fortune-hunter.”

“Aye, I *did* love her. But she was as beautiful as the moon we don’t see tonight. Nothing like her exists now. Perhaps Miss Helstone has a hint of her—quiet, delicate—but no one else.”

“Who looks like her?”

“That black-coated tyrant’s niece—Miss Helstone. I’ve often put on my spectacles in church to watch her. Gentle blue eyes, long lashes. When she sits in shadow, pale, still, nearly asleep from the sermon’s length and the heat of the building—she looks like one of Canova’s marbles.”

“Was Mary Cave like that?”

“Far grander—less girl-like, less flesh-like. You wondered why she didn’t have wings and a crown. She was a stately, peaceful angel—my Mary.”

“And you couldn’t win her love?”

“Not with all I could do. I prayed Heaven, on my knees, many times, to help me.”

“Mary Cave wasn’t what you think, Yorke. I’ve seen her portrait at the rectory. She’s no angel. Just a fair, regular-faced, quiet woman—too white, too lifeless for my taste. But suppose she had been better than she was…”

The moment had come. Sir Philip Nunnely knew it. Shirley knew it. Mr. Sympson knew it. That evening, when the entire Fieldhead household dined at Nunnely Priory, the decision was sealed.

Several things had drawn the baronet to his resolve. He had noticed Miss Keeldar looking thoughtful and pale—more delicate than usual. This change stirred something tender in him, a poetic vulnerability. A sonnet formed itself in his mind before he could stop it. Then, one of his sisters persuaded her to sit at the piano and sing—one of Sir Philip’s own ballads. It was the simplest, most unadorned of all his verses, yet in its quiet honesty, it was perhaps his finest.

At that very moment, Shirley had been standing by a window, watching the storm-lit park. She saw the moonlight slashing through the trees—the broad oaks, the towering beeches—battling the wind. The forest roared below; clouds raced across the sky, and the moon seemed to dart past like a silver comet. She turned from the scene, not enchanted, but deeply moved—awakened, if not inspired.

She sang as asked. The ballad spoke of love that endured hardship, love that grew stronger in sorrow, love that clung tighter in poverty. The melody was old and noble; the words were plain, sweet, and true. On paper, they might lack fire—but sung with feeling, they needed nothing more. Shirley sang them with such depth, such passion, that she stirred every heart in the room—and captivated one in particular.

Afterward, she walked to the hearth and sat on a low stool-cushion. The ladies gathered around her, silent. The Misses Sympson and the Misses Nunnely stared at her as though she were an exotic bird—an egret, or an ibis—something foreign and unsettling. Why did she sing like that? _They_ never sang so. Was it proper? So expressive? So original? Certainly not. Strange things were wrong. Unusual things were improper. Shirley was judged.

Old Lady Nunnely watched her from her chair by the fire. Her eyes said plainly: “This woman is not of our kind. I disapprove of her as my son’s wife.”

Her son saw the look. He understood. He feared losing what he desired most. He must act quickly.

The room once served as a gallery. Sir Philip’s father, Sir Monckton, had transformed it into a salon, but it still held a sense of mystery—long shadows, deep recesses. One corner contained a small couch, a table, and a delicate cabinet. It was a space within a space—private enough for two people to speak without being overheard.

Sir Philip arranged for two of his sisters to perform a duet. He occupied the Sympsons. The older women chatted. While the others were distracted, Shirley rose to examine the paintings. He joined her and began telling a story about an ancestor—a dark-haired beauty, radiant as a southern flower.

There were relics of that lady in the cabinet. As Shirley bent to study the missal and rosary on the inlaid shelf, while the Misses Nunnely screeched their conventional duet, Sir Philip leaned down too—and whispered a few urgent words.

At first, Shirley froze—so still it seemed as though his voice had turned her to stone. But then she looked up and replied. They parted. She returned to the fire. He watched her go, then stepped behind his sisters. Only Mr. Sympson saw the exchange.

That man drew his own conclusions. Had he been half as sharp as he was intrusive, he might have read the truth in Sir Philip’s face. But he was shallow, hasty, and certain of himself. He went home triumphantly.

He was not one to keep secrets. When excited, he couldn’t help talking. The next morning, needing his son’s tutor to assist with paperwork, he announced, in grand, exaggerated tones, that the tutor should prepare to return south soon. The important business that had kept him in Yorkshire was nearing completion. His efforts, long and anxious, were finally rewarded. A most suitable alliance was about to be made.

“In Sir Philip Nunnely?” Louis Moore guessed.

Mr. Sympson responded with a pinch of snuff and a chuckle—cut short by dignity—and ordered the tutor back to work.

For days, he remained smooth and oily, yet restless as a hen on a hot stove. He paced, peered out windows, listened for carriage wheels. He waited—not just for news, but for formality, consultation, lawyers, settlements—every pompous detail of a grand union.

Then came a letter. He handed it to Miss Keeldar himself, recognizing the handwriting and the seal. He didn’t watch her open it—she took it to her room. Nor did he see her reply—she wrote it in private, and took hours. He asked if it was answered. She said yes.

He waited—silent now, unable to speak, stunned by something in her face—something terrible, unreadable, like the writing on the wall to Belshazzar. He almost called Daniel—Louis Moore—to interpret it. But pride forbade it. And Daniel himself seemed baffled—his face like a scholar staring at blank pages.

* * * * *

Mr. Sympson had gone out, seeking distraction among friends at De Walden Hall. He returned earlier than expected. His family and Miss Keeldar were gathered in the oak parlour. He addressed her directly.

“Miss Keeldar,” he said, “please step into another room with me. I wish to have a strictly private conversation.”

She rose without question, without surprise.

“Very well, sir,” she said, calm as a person told the dentist has arrived to pull the tooth that’s tormented her all month. She left her sewing and thimble in the window seat and followed him.

In the drawing-room, they sat opposite each other—armchairs, a few yards apart.

“I’ve been to De Walden Hall,” Mr. Sympson began. He paused. Shirley stared at the green-and-white carpet. No response was needed. She gave none.

“I’ve learned,” he continued slowly, “a circumstance that surprises me.”

She rested her cheek on her finger, waiting.
