“It seems Nunnely Priory is closed—the family has returned to their estate in ----shire. Sir Philip himself accompanied his mother and sisters.”

“Indeed,” said Shirley.

“May I ask if you share my astonishment?”

“No, sir.”

“Is it news to you?”

“Yes, sir.”

“I mean—I mean,” he stammered, fidgeting now, slipping back into his usual tangled speech, “I want a thorough explanation. I will not be put off. I shall insist on being heard. My questions must be answered. I demand clear, satisfactory replies. I am not to be trifled with.” (Silence.)

“It is strange—extraordinary—most odd! I thought everything was settled. And now—they’re gone!”

“I suppose they had a right to leave.”

“_Sir Philip is gone!_” (emphasizing it).

Shirley raised her brows. “Bon voyage.”

“This will not do. This must be changed, ma'am.”

He pushed his chair forward, then back. He looked furious and helpless.

“Come now, uncle,” Shirley said gently, “don’t fret. We’ll make no progress if you rage. Ask me what you want. I’m willing to explain. I promise truthful answers.”

“I demand to know,” he said, “whether Sir Philip made you an offer.”

“He did.”

“You admit it?”

“I admit it. Now proceed. Consider that point settled.”

“He proposed that night we dined at the priory?”

“It is enough to say he did. Go on.”

“In the recess—the old gallery converted into a saloon?”

No answer.

“You were both examining the cabinet. I saw it. My insight is never wrong. Later, you received a letter from him. What was it about?”

“None of your concern.”

“Ma'am, is this how you speak to me?”

Shirley tapped her foot sharply on the carpet.

“There you sit—silent, sullen—_you_ who promised truth.”

“Sir, I have answered thus far. Proceed.”

“I’d like to see that letter.”

“You cannot.”

“I must and shall. I am your guardian.”

“Having ceased to be a ward, I have no guardian.”

“Ungrateful creature! Raised as my own daughter—”

“Once more, uncle, please stay on topic. Let us remain calm. I do not wish to lose temper. But push me too far, and I care little what I say. Listen: You asked if Sir Philip offered. I’ve answered. What else do you wish to know?”

“I want to know whether you accepted or refused him. And know it I will.”

“Certainly, you ought to know. I refused him.”

“Refused him! _You_, Shirley Keeldar, refused Sir Philip Nunnely?”

“I did.”

The gentleman leapt up, pacing the room like a caged animal.

“There it is! There it is! There it is!”

“Sincerely, I regret your disappointment, uncle.”

Concession only hardens some men. Mr. Sympson was one such.

“_I_ disappointed? What does it matter to me? Do I have any interest?”

“You imply I have motives.”

“Most people do.”

“She accuses me! I, who raised her as my own child, accused of bad motives!”

“Bad motives I did not say.”

“And now you evade—no principles!”

“Uncle, you tire me. I want to go.”

“You shall not! I will have answers. What are your intentions, Miss Keeldar?”

“In what regard?”

“In marriage.”

“To be quiet—and do as I please.”

“As you please! Such words are indecorous.”

“Mr. Sympson, I advise you not to become insulting. You know I won’t endure it.”

“You read French. Your mind is poisoned by novels. You’ve adopted French ideas.”

“The ground beneath you echoes hollowly. Beware.”

“It will end in infamy. I’ve foreseen it.”

“Do you assert something involving me will end in infamy?”

“That it will. You said you’d do as you please. You acknowledge no rules.”

“Silly talk—vulgar nonsense!”

“Disregarding decorum, you plan to defy propriety.”

“You tire me, uncle.”

“What could be your reason for refusing Sir Philip?”

“At last—a sensible question. I shall answer. Sir Philip is too young. I see him as a boy. His family—especially his mother—would resent such a match. It would divide him from them. I am not his equal in society.”

“Is that all?”

“Our natures don’t align.”

“Why, he’s the most amiable man alive.”

“He is kind—excellent—truly estimable. But _not my master_. Not in one way. I could not trust myself with his happiness. I would not guard it for thousands. I will accept no hand that cannot hold me in check.”

“I thought you liked to do as you pleased. You’re inconsistent.”

“When I promise obedience, it must be with certainty I can keep it. I could not obey a youth like Sir Philip. Besides, he’d expect me to rule—to guide—and I have no taste for that role.”

“You—no taste for commanding?”

“Not my husband. Only my uncle.”

“What difference?”

“There is a slight difference—certainly. And I know any man who wishes to live comfortably with me must be able to control me.”

“I wish you had a real tyrant.”

“A tyrant wouldn’t last an hour. I’d rebel—break free—defy him.”

“Are you not confusing yourself?”

“I clearly bewilder you.”

“You mentioned Sir Philip’s youth. He’s twenty-two.”

“My husband must be thirty—with the wisdom of forty.”

“You’d choose an old man—white-haired, bald?”

“No, thank you.”

“You could dominate a fool—pin him to your apron.”

“I might do that with a boy. But it’s not my purpose. Did I not say I prefer a _master_—one whose presence makes me want to be good; one whose authority my temper must respect; a man whose approval rewards me, whose displeasure punishes; a man I cannot help but love—and may fear?”

“What prevents you from feeling this for Sir Philip? He’s a baronet—wealthy, connected, respected. If intellect matters, he writes poetry—while you, clever as you are, cannot.”

“His title, wealth, poetry—none give him the power I need. They’re lightweights. Solid sense would have served him better.”

“You and Henry both adore poetry! You used to ignite at the subject.”

“O uncle, nothing truly valuable—nothing glorious—is not poetry!”

“Marry a poet, then!”

“Show me one, and I will.”

“Sir Philip.”

“Not at all. You’re nearly as poetic as he.”

“Madam, you stray from the point.”

“Indeed, uncle, I wanted to. I’d be glad to lead you away. Let us not quarrel. It’s not worth it.”

“Out of temper, Miss Keeldar? I should like to know who is.”

“I am not—yet.”

“If you suggest I am, you’re impertinent.”

“You will be soon, if you continue.”

“There it is! With your tongue, you’d try Job’s patience.”

“I know I would.”

“No levity! This is serious. I’ll probe thoroughly. You described your ideal husband with shocking freedom. Did you paint from life?”

Shirley opened her mouth—then flushed crimson.

“I shall have an answer,” Mr. Sympson declared, bold now.

“It was a historical portrait, uncle—from several originals.”

“Several originals! Bless my soul!”

“I’ve loved many times.”

“Cynical.”

“Heroes of many nations.”

“What next—”

“And philosophers.”

“She’s mad—”

“Don’t ring the bell, uncle. You’ll alarm my aunt.”

“My poor dear aunt—what a niece!”

“Once I loved Socrates.”

“Pooh! No trifling.”

“I admired Themistocles, Leonidas, Epaminondas.”

“Miss Keeldar—”

“Skipping centuries, Washington was plain—but I liked him. But now—present realities—”

“Ah! The present.”

“To abandon schoolgirl dreams—face truth.”

“Truth! That’s your test, ma'am.”

“To reveal whom I now worship—my present idol—”

“You’ll hurry, if you please. Luncheon is near. Confess you shall.”

“Confess I must. My heart is full. I must speak. I only wish you were Mr. Helstone instead of Mr. Sympson—you’d understand better.”

“It’s common sense, not sentiment. Did you say Mr. Helstone?”

“Not exactly—but close. They resemble each other.”

“I’ll know the name. I’ll have details.”

“They are quite alike. Their faces—like human falcons—dry, direct, decisive. But my hero is greater. His mind has the clarity of deep water, the endurance of rocks, the force of waves.”

“Rant and nonsense!”

“I dare say he can be harsh as a saw, gruff as a raven.”

“Does he live in Briarfield? Answer me.”

“Uncle, I’m about to tell you. His name trembles on my lips.”

“Speak, girl!”

“Well said, uncle. ‘Speak, girl!’ It’s tragic. England has howled at this man—and one day she’ll roar in triumph. He’s unshaken by the howl, and unmoved by the shout.”

“I said she was mad. She is.”

“This country will shift again and again in her treatment of him. He will never change in his duty. Cease your agitation, uncle—I’ll tell you.”

“You shall—or—”

“Listen! Arthur Wellesley, Lord Wellington.”

Mr. Sympson rose, furious. He stormed out—then rushed back, slammed the door, and sat down.

“Ma'am, you _shall_ tell me this. Will your principles allow you to marry a man without money—below you?”

“Never a man below me.”

(In a high voice.) “Will you marry a poor man?”

“What right have you to ask?”

“I insist.”

“You don’t go the way to know.”

“My family’s reputation must not suffer.”

“A fine resolution—keep it.”

“Madam, _you_ shall keep it.”

“Impossible, sir. I am not part of your family.”

“Do you disown us?”

“I disdain your tyranny.”

“Whom _will_ you marry?”

“Not Mr. Sam Wynne—I scorn him. Not Sir Philip Nunnely—I only esteem him.”

“Who then?”

“Four rejected candidates.”

“Such stubbornness means you’re under influence.”

“What do you mean? Certain phrases make my blood boil. Improper influence? What old gossip is that?”

“Are you a young lady?”

“I am a thousand times better: an honest woman. Treat me as such.”

“Do you know”—he leaned forward, whispering with grim solemnity—“the whole neighbourhood buzzes with rumours about you and a bankrupt tenant—your foreigner Moore?”

“Does it?”

“It does. Your name is on every lip.”

“It honours them. May it purify them.”

“Is it _him_ who influences you?”

“More than any you’ve championed.”

“Is it he you’ll marry?”

“He is handsome, strong, commanding.”

“You declare it to my face! The Flemish rogue! The low trader!”

“He is talented, daring, resolute. Prince wears his brow, ruler stands in his bearing.”

“She glorifies him! She hides nothing! No shame, no fear!”

“When we speak Moore’s name, shame should vanish. Fear must be cast aside. The Moores know only honour and courage.”

“I say she’s mad.”

“You’ve provoked me until my blood boils. You’ve worn me thin.”

“That Moore is my son’s tutor’s brother. Would you let the usher call you sister?”

Shirley’s eyes flashed bright and clear.

“No. Not for a province. Not for a century.”

“You cannot separate husband from family.”

“What then?”

“Mr. Louis Moore’s sister—you will be.”

“Mr. Sympson, I am sick of this weak babble. I will bear no more. Your thoughts are not mine. Your aims, your gods—none of these are mine. We see the world differently. We speak different tongues. Let us part.”

“It is not,” she added, trembling now, “that I hate you. You are a decent man. Perhaps you mean well. But we do not suit. You irritate me with petty meddling, with small tyranny. You provoke my temper, and keep me angry. Your little rules, your prejudices, your dogmas—pack them away. Offer them to your god. I reject them all. I walk by another creed—another light, faith, hope. I wash my hands of yours.”

“Another creed!” he cried. “I believe she’s an infidel.”

“An infidel to _your_ religion—an atheist to _your_ god.”

“_An—atheist!!!_”

Briarmains, being closer than the Hollow, was where Mr. Yorke took his young companion after the attack. He had seen him laid in the finest bed of the house, with care as tender as if he were one of his own sons. The sight of blood welling from the wound—inflicted so treacherously—had stirred something deep within him. To see that tall, proud figure fallen across the road, that fine southern face lying low in the dust, that strong youth suddenly pale and lifeless—it was a moment that could not fail to win the heart of any man of feeling, especially one like Mr. Yorke.

There was no one else to help, no other voice to speak gently, no other mind to plan what must be done. It fell entirely upon him. And in that moment, with a wounded stranger wholly dependent on his kindness, Mr. Yorke found himself in a position he deeply relished: power over another’s life. It suited him perfectly.

His wife, too, found satisfaction in the turn of events. Some women might have been overcome by fear at seeing a bleeding man carried into their home at nightfall, laid out in their hall like a fallen warrior. But Mrs. Yorke was not one of them. She was more likely to faint over a maid who refused to leave the garden or over her son’s dream of escaping to Australia. Yet here—a man nearly murdered at her doorstep, half-dead in her best bed—she stood firm, composed, even energized. It gave her a sense of purpose. She wore her cap like a turban, her spirit lifted, her authority confirmed.

Mrs. Yorke was the kind of woman who would torment a servant with petty rules while nursing plague victims like a heroine. She almost came to care for Moore. When she saw him placed under her protection—left helpless in her arms, as vulnerable as a newborn child—something in her hard nature softened. She would have struck any servant who dared offer him water or adjust his pillow. She drove Jessie and Rose from the upper rooms, forbade the maids to step near the sick chamber.

Had the accident occurred at the rectory gates, and old Helstone taken charge of the injured man, neither Yorke nor his wife would have shown a shred of pity. They would have called it justice served upon a tyrant. But because it happened at Briarmains, Moore became, for now, the center of their attention.

It was strange, then, that Louis Moore was allowed to come—allowed to sit beside the bed, lean over the pillow, hold his brother’s hand, press his lips to his pale forehead. Mrs. Yorke endured it, even welcomed it. She let him stay half the day; once, she permitted him to sit through the entire night. At dawn, on a wet November morning, she rose herself, lit the kitchen fire, made breakfast, and served it to the brothers with her own hands. Dressed in a wide flannel wrapper, a shawl, and her nightcap, she sat and watched them eat, as contentedly as a hen watches her chicks feed.

Yet she fired the cook for preparing a bowl of sago-gruel for Mr. Moore without her permission. And the housemaid lost favor when, as Louis left, she brought him his coat fresh from the kitchen, helped him into it, and received a smile, a “Thank you, my girl,” and a shilling. Two ladies came calling, pale and anxious, begging just one glimpse of Moore. Mrs. Yorke turned them away—harshly, with no hesitation.

But when Hortense Moore arrived, things were different. Not as bad as they might have been. In fact, the whole Moore family seemed to fit Mrs. Yorke’s temperament better than any others ever had. She and Hortense shared an endless supply of topics—especially about servants. Their views were alike: suspicious, severe, watchful. Hortense never showed jealousy over her sister-in-law’s care of Robert. She let Mrs. Yorke take charge, while she busied herself inspecting the kitchen, reporting back, and generally proving useful. Together, they agreed on one thing: no visitors were to enter the sickroom. They held Moore captive, shielding him from the world.

Mr. MacTurk, the surgeon assigned to Moore’s case, declared the wound dangerous but not beyond hope. At first, he wanted to bring his own nurse. But neither Mrs. Yorke nor Hortense would allow it. They promised strict obedience. So Moore remained under their care—for a time.

They did their best, but something went wrong. The bandages were disturbed, perhaps tampered with. A great loss of blood followed. MacTurk, summoned urgently, arrived on horseback, breathless and furious. He was one of those surgeons whose temper could turn deadly—sharp even in calm moments, brutal when provoked. Seeing Moore’s condition, he vented his fury in colorful language, none of which need be repeated here. A few choice words landed on Mr. Graves, his stony assistant; another bouquet fell on his son, a miniature version of himself. But the full basket of insults was reserved for women—especially meddling women.

For most of that winter night, MacTurk and his men worked around Moore’s bedside, alone in the room. They wrestled with death, three men on one side of the bed, death on the other. The struggle was fierce, lasting until dawn, when the balance seemed so even that either side might claim victory.

At sunrise, Graves and young MacTurk stayed behind to watch over Moore, while the senior surgeon rode out to find reinforcements. He returned with Mrs. Horsfall—the finest nurse on his staff. To her, he handed Moore with solemn warning: she was responsible for his life. She accepted the duty without protest, and took her seat in the easy chair at the foot of the bed. From that moment, she ruled.

Mrs. Horsfall had one virtue: she obeyed MacTurk’s orders without question. His word was law to her. In every other way, she was formidable. Hortense retreated. Mrs. Yorke withdrew—overwhelmed. These two women, each of some standing in their own right, were reduced to silence before her presence. She moved freely between floors, smoked four times a day, drank gin three times, and claimed the room as her own.

No one dared ask after Moore now. Mrs. Horsfall had him under strict control. She was the one who cared for him—and the general belief was that she did so thoroughly.

MacTurk visited twice daily. Moore’s case, complicated by this new crisis, had become a challenge. He saw him as a broken clockwork mechanism—something that, if repaired, would reflect credit on his skill. Graves and young MacTurk, meanwhile, viewed Moore much as they viewed the body in the dissecting room at Stilbro’ Infirmary.

Robert Moore passed the long, cold days of November in pain, weak, unable to move or speak clearly. His keeper was a giantess, his only company the surgeons. He lay through the shrinking daylight and growing darkness, trapped in silence and stillness.

At first, Moore resisted Mrs. Horsfall. He loathed her size, feared her touch. But she taught him submission quickly. She ignored his height, his strength, and turned him in bed as easily as a mother turns a child. When he behaved well, she called him “my dear” or “honey.” When he resisted, she shook him. If he tried to speak during MacTurk’s visits, she raised a hand and said, “Hush!” like a nurse silencing a disobedient child. She smoked and drank—things Moore disapproved of—but MacTurk dismissed it: “Pooh! They all do. But Horsfall has one merit: drunk or sober, she always obeys me.”

* * * * *

Autumn faded. The fogs and rains lifted from England, carrying their sorrow elsewhere. Winter settled in—clear, still, and icy.

A quiet evening deepened into crystal clarity. The world wore colors of the North Pole: white, violet, pale green. Hills glowed lilac-blue; the sunset bled purple into red; the sky shimmered silver-azure. Stars rose like white crystals, not gold. The landscape was cool, pure, transparent—gray, cerulean, emerald—still and serene.

In a wood stripped bare of green, of russet, of warmth—what is that dark blue shape moving? A schoolboy. A boy from Briarfield grammar school, who has left his friends walking the highroad and wandered off to find a certain tree, its roots crowned with moss—a good place to sit. Why does he linger? The air is cold, the hour late. He sits down. What is he thinking?

Does he feel the beauty of the night? A pearl-white moon smiles through the gray branches. Does he notice?

Impossible to say. He is silent. His face shows nothing. Not yet a mirror of emotion, but a mask. He is fifteen—slender, tall for his age. His expression holds no softness, no deference. His eyes watch, ready to detect any attempt to dominate him. His features suggest alertness, readiness to resist. Wise teachers avoid him. Punishment would break him in vain; flattery would only insult. Best to leave him be. Time and experience will shape him.

Martin Yorke—yes, a young Yorke—does not believe in poetry. Speak of sentiment, and he answers with irony. Yet here he is, wandering alone, waiting patiently as nature unfolds a page of stark, silent poetry before him.

He pulls a book from his satchel—not Latin grammar, but a forbidden volume of fairy tales. There’s still light enough for an hour. Besides, the moon waits for him; her dim beam fills the glade.

He reads. He enters a wild mountain region—barren, colorless, desolate. Bells tinkle on the wind. Through the mist rides a vision: a lady in green robes, astride a snow-white palfrey. He sees her jewels, her dress, her steed. She stops him with a mysterious question. He is spellbound, drawn into fairyland.

Another tale carries him to the sea. Waves crash against dizzy cliffs. Rain falls, wind howls. Rocks stretch far into the sea, black and jagged. Spray flashes and leaps, swirling in foam. A lone wanderer walks the rocks, stepping carefully over seaweed, peering into hollows where the water is deep and emerald-clear—where stranger plants grow, stranger shells cluster—green, purple, pearly, coiled like snakes. Then he hears a cry. He looks up. At the edge of the reef stands a tall, pale figure—like a man, but made of spray—transparent, trembling, terrifying. Others rise from the waves—foam-women, Nereids, white and fleeting.

Hush! Close the book. Hide it. Martin hears footsteps. He listens. No—yes. Again, leaves rustle underfoot. He watches. The trees part, and a woman emerges.

She wears dark silk, her face veiled. Martin has never seen a lady in this wood before—only village girls gathering nuts. Tonight, the sight does not unsettle him. As she draws near, he sees she is not old or plain, but young, even beautiful—though he recognizes her immediately as Caroline Helstone, whom he has often mocked as ugly.

She passes him without speaking. He knew she would. All women are proud monkeys, he thinks—and none prouder than Caroline. But the thought barely forms when she steps back, lifts her veil, and looks at him.

“Are you one of Mr. Yorke’s sons?” she asks softly.

No human proof could ever convince Martin he blushed—yet he did, crimson to the ears.

“I am,” he replies bluntly, daring himself to wonder what comes next.

“You’re Martin, I think?” she says.

Perfect. Simple, artless, slightly hesitant—just right. It matched his nature. It silenced him, like a note of music.

Martin valued his identity. It pleased him that she distinguished him from his brothers. Like his father, he despised ceremony. To be addressed as “Martin”—not “Mr. Martin” or “Master Martin”—was respectful. Worse than formality was careless familiarity. Her slight hesitation, her bashfulness, felt natural.

“I am Martin,” he said.

“Are your father and mother well? And Rose and Jessie?”

“I suppose so.”

“My cousin Hortense is still at Briarmains?”

“Oh yes.”

Martin gives a half-smile, half-groan. The smile is returned. She knows exactly how Hortense is regarded in the Yorke household.

“Does your mother like her?”

“They suit so well about servants they can’t help liking each other.”

“It’s cold tonight.”

“Why are you out so late?”

“I lost my way in this wood.”

Martin lets out a laugh of scorn.

“Lost your way in the mighty forest of Briarmains? You deserve never to find it again.”

“I’ve never been here before. I may be trespassing. You could report me, Martin. Have me fined. It’s your father’s wood.”

“I should think I know that. But since you’re so simple as to lose your way, I’ll guide you out.”

“You don’t need to. I’m on the path now. I’ll be fine. Martin…” (a little quickly), “how is Mr. Moore?”

Martin had heard rumors. He decides to test her.

“Going to die. Nothing can save him. All hope gone!”

She removes her veil. She looks into his eyes.

“To die?”

“To die. All because of the women—my mother and the rest. They messed with his bandages. That finished everything. He’d have recovered if not for them. They should be arrested, jailed, sent to Botany Bay!”

She doesn’t respond. After two minutes, she moves forward—no goodnight, no question. Not amusing. Not what he expected. He hoped for drama, emotion. It wasn’t worth frightening her if she wouldn’t react.

“Miss Helstone!”

She doesn’t hear or turn. He hurries after her.

“Are you uneasy about what I said?”

“You know nothing of death, Martin. You’re too young for me to talk about such things.”

“Did you believe me? It’s all nonsense! Moore eats like three men. They make sago, tapioca—always something good. I’ve seen the saucepans boiling in the kitchen. I think I’ll play the old soldier and live off the fat of the land like him.”

“Martin! Martin!” Her voice trembles. She stops.

“It’s terribly wrong of you. You’ve nearly killed me.”

Again, she stops. Leans against a tree, trembling, pale as death.

Martin stares, fascinated. On one level, it was “nuts” to see—revealing so much. He was beginning to enjoy uncovering secrets. On another, it reminded him of hearing a blackbird mourn after Matthew crushed her nestlings. Not a pleasant memory.

He couldn’t think of anything to say to comfort her. Instead, he smiled. The smile transformed his face.

“Eureka!” he cries. “I’ll fix everything soon. You’re better now, Miss Caroline. Walk on.”

He guides her by a shortcut—without thinking how hard it might be for her to climb walls or cross hedges. He helps her over obstacles, grumbling at her weakness, yet enjoying the chance to be useful.

“Martin, before we part, tell me seriously—on your word of honor—that Mr. Moore is better.”

“How much you care about that Moore!”

“No—but—friends may ask. I want to give an honest answer.”

“You may tell them he’s fine—just idle. He eats mutton chops for dinner, arrowroot for supper. I stole half a basin myself once.”

“And who nurses him? Who tends to him?”

“Nurses him? The great baby! A woman as big as our largest water-butt—rough, hard-faced. I suspect she treats him badly. No one else is allowed near. He’s mostly in the dark. I sometimes listen through the wall. I think I hear her hitting him. You should see her fist. She could hold six of your hands in one palm. Still, despite the food, I wouldn’t trade places with him. Honestly, I think she eats most of what goes up to him. I hope she’s not starving him.”

Silence. Deep thought from Caroline. Watchful curiosity from Martin.

“You never see him, I suppose?”

“I? No. I don’t care to.”

Silence again.

“Didn’t you visit our house with Mrs. Pryor about five weeks ago, asking after him?”

“Yes.”

“I bet you wanted to see upstairs?”

“We did. We begged. But your mother refused.”

“Ay. She refused. I heard it all. She treated you like she treats guests sometimes—rude, harsh.”

“She wasn’t kind. You know, Martin, we’re related. It’s natural we’d care.”

“But here we part. We’re at your gate.”

“So? I’ll walk home with you.”

“They’ll miss you.”

“Let them. I can take care of myself.”

Martin knew he’d earned a lecture and dry bread for tea. But it didn’t matter. The evening had been an adventure. Better than muffins and toast.

He walked home with Caroline. On the way, he promised to see Moore—despite the dragon guarding him—and arranged to meet her the next day in the woods, at a certain tree. The plan led nowhere. But he liked it.

When he reached home, the punishment was delivered: dry bread, a lecture, early bed. He accepted it with grim stoicism.

Before going to his room, he slipped into the dining hall—a cold, quiet, seldom-used room. He stood before the mantelpiece, holding his candle to two portraits: two women’s faces. One, serene, innocent, beautiful. The other, more beautiful, but sorrowful, desperate.

“She looked like that,” he murmured, gazing at the second portrait, “when she wept, turned white, leaned against the tree.”

It was essential to Martin’s plan that he remain at home that day. So, when breakfast was served, he barely touched his plate, and just as the school bell would have summoned Mark away, he clutched his chest with a sudden, dramatic pain—so severe, he declared, that it made it unwise for him to venture out. Instead of walking to grammar school with his brother, he took over his father’s armchair by the fireside, along with the morning paper, which had previously been Matthew’s domain. The arrangement was settled quickly: Mark was gone to Mr. Summer’s class; Matthew and Mr. Yorke were absorbed in business at the counting-house. Now only three—no, four—tasks remained before him.

The first was to finally eat the breakfast he’d skipped, an act of hunger that no fifteen-year-old could afford to ignore. The second, third, and fourth: to ensure that his mother, Miss Moore, and Mrs. Horsfall were all safely occupied—and out of the way—by four o’clock that afternoon.

For now, though, food was paramount. His stomach was empty, and the energy required for what lay ahead demanded fuel. Martin knew exactly where the larder was, and without hesitation, he slipped into it. The servants were still eating their breakfast in the kitchen, doors tightly shut, while his mother and Miss Moore strolled on the lawn, whispering about the closed doors and suspecting mischief. But Martin was undisturbed. Inside the larder, he surveyed the provisions with the eye of a connoisseur.

His usual breakfast—bread and milk—was dull. Today, he would indulge in something more refined. He selected three rosy apples from a shelf lined with straw. From a dish, he chose an apricot puff and a damson tart. He passed over the plain bread, but paused at the currant tea-cakes—choosing one with quiet approval. With his clasp-knife, he cut a wing from a fowl and a slice of ham. A small portion of cold custard pudding seemed like the perfect finish. Satisfied, he gathered his feast and stepped out into the hall.

He was halfway across when the front door opened—and there stood Matthew.

If it had been the Old Gentleman, horned and hooved, Martin might have felt less exposed. But Matthew? A skeptic, a mocker—he had already questioned Martin’s heartache, muttering under his breath, “Shamming Abraham,” and casting disapproving glances at the armchair and newspaper. Seeing Martin now—arms full of stolen delicacies—Matthew’s suspicion flared into certainty.

Martin froze—just for a moment. Then, with the sharp clarity of youth, he saw not failure, but opportunity. This confrontation could serve his purpose perfectly. He knew that any clash between him and Matthew inevitably sent his mother into a fit of hysterics. And after such a storm, she always retreated to her room for the rest of the day—exactly where Martin wanted her.

So, the collision began. A dry laugh from Matthew. A sneering remark. A cutting retort from Martin—cool, calm, yet devastating. They lunged at each other. Martin, who usually kept his voice low during these spats, now raised it loudly. Servants rushed in. Mrs. Yorke and Miss Moore arrived too. No woman could separate them. Mr. Yorke was called.

“Sons,” he said sternly, “one of you must leave this house if this happens again. I will not have Cain and Abel quarreling under my roof.”

Martin allowed himself to be pulled away. He was hurt—but not angry. He was cool, even smiling. The hardest part was done.

Later, as he lounged on the sofa—still claiming illness—his thoughts drifted. “It’s not worth bothering myself for Caroline,” he murmured. But within minutes, he was back in the dining room, staring at the portrait of the man with wild hair and eyes clouded by despair. “Yes,” he whispered, “I made her cry. She shuddered. Almost fainted. I’ll see her smile before I’m done. And I want to outwit all these women.”

After dinner, Mrs. Yorke retired to her room—just as Martin had hoped.

Now came Hortense. She was comfortably seated in the back parlour, mending stockings, when Martin—pretending to read lazily on the sofa—began a casual conversation about Sarah, the maid at the Hollow. With careful drifts of talk, he hinted that Sarah had three suitors—Frederic Murgatroyd, Jeremiah Pighills, and John-of-Mally’s-of-Hannah’s-of-Deb’s—and that Miss Mann had claimed the girl often hosted them at meals, feeding them the best the cottage had to offer.

No more was needed. Hortense could not resist. She rose immediately, determined to investigate this scandal firsthand.

Only Mrs. Horsfall remained.

Martin, now in full command, rummaged through his mother’s workbasket and found a set of keys. He unlocked the sideboard, took out a black bottle and a small glass, placed them on the table, then hurried upstairs. He knocked gently on Mr. Moore’s door. The nurse answered.

“If you please, ma'am,” Martin said politely, “you’re invited to the back parlour for refreshment. You won’t be disturbed—the family are all out.”

He watched her descend, watched her enter. Then he shut the door behind her. She was safe. The hard work was complete.

Now, the pleasure.

He grabbed his cap and ran toward the wood. It was half-past three. The sky had darkened. Snow began to fall. The wind howled. The trees looked grim, the old oak spectral. Yet Martin welcomed the shadows. There was something thrilling in the gloom.

He waited. Back and forth he walked, watching the snow grow heavier. The wind moaned, then shrieked.

“She’s late,” he muttered, scanning the path. “I wonder why I want her so much? She’s not coming for me. But I hold power over her. And I need her to come… so I can use it.”

Time passed. Then, a sound—light, swift—barely audible beneath the rustle of leaves. A figure emerged, stepping bravely through the storm.

“Well, Martin,” she said, breathless, “how is he?”

He stared. “How strange,” he thought. “She thinks only of *him*. The snow, the cold—they mean nothing to her. And yet she’s just a ‘chitty-faced creature,’ as my mother would say. I’d give anything to wrap her in a cloak.”

But he said nothing.

“You’ve seen him?” she asked.

“No.”

“Oh! You promised you would.”

“I meant to do better than that. Didn’t I say I don’t care to see him?”

“But now it’s been so long. I can’t wait any longer. Martin, *please*—go see him. Tell him Caroline Helstone sends her regards. Ask if there’s anything that can help him.”

“I won’t.”

“You’ve changed. You were so kind last night.”

“We must go. It’s too cold to stand here.”

“But promise me—you’ll come back tomorrow with news.”

“No. I’m far too delicate for winter appointments. If you knew how much pain I had this morning, how I skipped breakfast, how I got knocked down—I’d feel you shouldn’t bring me out in this snow.”

“Are you really ill, Martin?”

“Don’t I look it?”

“You have rosy cheeks.”

“That’s hectic. Will you come—or won’t you?”

“Where?”

“With me. I was a fool not to bring a cloak. I’d have kept you warm.”

“You’re going home. My way is the opposite.”

“Put your arm through mine. I’ll take care of you.”

“But the wall—the hedge—it’s hard to climb. And you’re so young. You might hurt yourself.”

“You’ll go through the gate.”

“But—”

“But, but—will you trust me or not?”

She looked into his face.

“I think I will. Anything rather than return as anxious as I came.”

“I can’t guarantee that. But this I promise: follow me, and you’ll see Moore yourself.”

“See him—myself?”

“Yes.”

“But, dear Martin—does he know?”

“Ah! I’m dear now. No, he doesn’t.”

“And your mother? The others?”

“All is arranged.”

Caroline fell into silence, but walked on beside him.

They neared Briarmains.

“Have you decided?” he asked.

She didn’t answer.

“Decide. We’re almost there. I won’t see him—unless I announce your arrival.”

“Martin, you’re a strange boy. This is a strange step. But everything I’ve felt… has been strange. I will see him.”

“Then you won’t hesitate?”

“No.”

“There. We’re here. Don’t fear passing the parlour window—no one will see you. My father and Matthew are at the mill. Mark is at school. The servants are in the back kitchen. Miss Moore is at the cottage. My mother is in bed. Mrs. Horsfall is in paradise. Observe—no need to ring. I open the door. The hall is empty. The stairs are quiet. The gallery too. The whole house is under a spell. I won’t break it until you’re gone.”

“Martin, I trust you.”

“You never said a truer word. Let me take your shawl. I’ll shake off the snow and dry it. You’re cold. But there’s a fire upstairs. Are you ready?”

“Yes.”

“Follow me.”

He left his shoes on the mat and climbed the stairs barefoot. Caroline followed, silent and swift.

There was a gallery, then a passage. At the end, Martin paused before a door and tapped. Twice. Thrice.

A voice—familiar to one listener—finally replied, “Come in.”

Martin entered briskly.

“Mr. Moore,” he announced, “a lady called to inquire after you. None of the women were about. It’s washing-day. The maids are buried in soap-suds in the back kitchen. So I brought her up.”

“Up here, sir?” Moore asked.

“Up here, sir. But if you object, she can go down.”

“Is this a place—or am I a person—to bring a lady to, you absurd lad?”

“No. So I’ll take her off.”

“Martin, you stay. Who is she?”

“Your grandmother from that château on the Scheldt Miss Moore talks about.”

“Martin,” a soft voice whispered at the door, “don’t be foolish.”

“Is she there?” Moore asked urgently.

“She’s there—fit to faint. Standing on the mat, shocked by your lack of filial affection.”

“Martin, you’re an evil cross between an imp and a page. What does she look like?”

“More like me than you—for she’s young and beautiful.”

“You are to show her forward. Do you hear?”

“Come, Miss Caroline.”

“Miss Caroline!” Moore repeated.

And when Miss Caroline entered, she was met in the middle of the room by a tall, thin, wasted figure who took both her hands.

“I give you a quarter of an hour,” Martin said, stepping back. “No more. Say what you must in that time. I’ll wait in the gallery. Nothing shall approach. I’ll see you safely away. Should you stay longer, I leave you to your fate.”

He shut the door.

In the gallery, he felt like a king. No adventure had ever made him feel so powerful, so vital. This was the finest thing he’d ever done.

“You’ve come at last,” Moore said, gazing at her with hollow eyes.

“Did you expect me sooner?”

“For nearly two months we’ve been close—very close. I’ve suffered pain, danger, misery, Cary.”

“I couldn’t come.”

“Couldn’t you? The rectory and Briarmains are only two miles apart.”

Her face held both pain and relief. To defend herself was sweet and bitter.

“When I say I couldn’t come, I mean I couldn’t see you. I came with mamma the very day we heard the news. Mr. MacTurk told us no strangers could be admitted.”

“But afterward—every fine afternoon, I’ve waited. Something here”—he touched his chest—“told me you’d think of me. Not that I deserve it. But we are cousins. We are old friends.”

“I came again, Robert. Mamma and I came again.”

“Did you? That’s worth hearing. Sit. Let’s talk.”

They sat. Caroline drew her chair close. The snow thickened outside. An icy blast drove it wildly.

They heard nothing. Saw nothing. Only each other.

“So you came again?”

“And Mrs. Yorke treated us strangely. We asked to see you. ‘No,’ she said. ‘Not in my house. I’m responsible for his life. It shall not be risked for idle gossip.’ But I won’t tell you all she said—it was unpleasant. Still, we came again—mamma, Miss Keeldar, and I. We thought three against one, with Shirley on our side, would win. But Mrs. Yorke unleashed a storm.”

Moore smiled.

“What did she say?”

“Things that astonished us. Shirley laughed. I cried. Mamma was deeply annoyed. We were driven away. Since then, I’ve only walked past the house once a day—just to see your window, drawn with curtains. I dared not enter.”

“I’ve wished for you, Caroline.”

“I didn’t know that. I never dreamed you thought of me. If I’d imagined it…”

“Mrs. Yorke would still have stopped you.”

“She wouldn’t. I would have tried stratagem. I would have come to the kitchen door. The servants would have let me in. I would have walked straight upstairs. It was more fear of intrusion—of you—than of her that kept me away.”

“Only last night I despaired of seeing you again. Weakness has crushed me—terribly.”

“You sit alone?”

“Worse than alone.”

“But you’re getting better, since you can leave your bed?”

“I doubt I’ll live. After such exhaustion, decline seems certain.”

“You—must go home to the Hollow.”

“Dreariness would follow. Nothing cheerful would come near me.”

“I will change this. I will make it change—though there were ten Mrs. Yorkes to oppose me.”

“Cary, you make me smile.”

“Do smile. Smile again. Shall I tell you what I’d like?”

“Tell me anything—just keep talking. I am Saul. Without music, I perish.”

“I’d like you to be brought to the rectory. Given to me and mamma.”

“A precious gift! I haven’t laughed since they shot me—until now.”

“Do you suffer pain, Robert?”

“Not so much now. But I’m hopelessly weak. My mind—dark, barren, powerless. Can you see it in my face? I look like a ghost.”

“Altered. Yet I’d recognize you anywhere. But I understand. I’ve felt something similar. Since we met, I’ve been very ill.”

“Very ill?”

“I thought I’d die. My life seemed finished. Every night at midnight, I woke from terrible dreams. The book lay open—‘Finis.’ I had strange feelings.”

“You speak my experience.”

“I believed I’d never see you again. I grew so thin—as thin as you are now. I couldn’t rise or lie down. I couldn’t eat. Yet here I am—better.”

“Comforter—sad as sweet. I’m too weak to express what I feel. But while you speak, I *do* feel.”

“Here I am, at your side—where I thought I’d never be again. I speak to you. You listen. You look at me kindly. Did I count on that? I despaired.”

Moore sighed—a deep, almost groaning sigh. He covered his eyes.

“May I be spared to make some atonement,” he prayed.

“For what?”

“We’ll not speak of it now. Unarmed as I am, I cannot face it. Was Mrs. Pryor with you during your illness?”

“Yes.” Caroline smiled. “You know she’s mamma?”

“I’ve heard—Hortense told me. But I’ll hear it from you. Does she add to your happiness?”

“What! Mamma? She is _dear_ to me. How dear I cannot say. I was weary. She held me up.”

“I deserve to hear that—when I can scarcely lift my hand.”

“It is no reproach.”

“It is a coal of fire on my head. And every word you say—every glance you give—is a flame. Come closer, Lina. Give me your hand—if my thin fingers scare you.”

She took them—both hands cradling his. She bent her head and kissed them—*et les effleura de ses lèvres*. (I write it in French because *effleurer* is a perfect word.)

Moore trembled. Tears traced paths down his pale cheeks.

“I’ll keep these things in my heart, Cary. That kiss—I’ll treasure it. One day, you’ll hear of it again.”

“Come out!” Martin called, bursting the door. “Come away! You’ve had twenty minutes instead of fifteen.”

“She won’t move yet, you hempseed.”

“I dare not stay longer, Robert.”

“Can you promise to return?”

“No,” Martin said firmly. “This mustn’t become a habit. I can’t be bothered. Once is enough. I won’t allow it again.”

“You won’t allow it again.”

“Hush! Don’t upset him. We couldn’t meet today without him. But I *will* come again—if it’s your wish.”

“It *is* my wish—my *only* wish—almost the only thing I feel.”

“Come now. My mother coughed. She’s up. She’s put her feet on the floor. Let her catch you on the stairs, Miss Caroline. You’re not to bid him farewell. You’re to march.”

“My shawl, Martin.”

“I have it. I’ll put it on when you’re in the hall.”

He parted them. He would allow no farewell but one expressed in looks. He half-carried Caroline down the stairs.

In the hall, he wrapped her shawl around her. And if his mother hadn’t creaked on the gallery above, and if a quiet sense of modesty—true, natural, noble—hadn’t held him back, he might have said: “Now, Miss Caroline, for all this—give me one kiss.”

But before the words formed, she was across the snowy road—skimming through the drifts, not wading.

“She owes me,” Martin whispered. “And I *will* be paid.”

He convinced himself it was chance, not courage, that failed him. But he misjudged himself. He underestimated the depth of his own nature.

Martin Yorke had tasted the thrill of power and found it intoxicating. Once he’d known what it was to command attention, to be the center of a quiet storm of emotion, he could not bear the thought of returning to silence. Miss Helstone—once dismissed as plain, now haunting his thoughts like a shadow at dusk—had slipped into his world, and though she was gone, her presence lingered in every breath he drew. He sat at his desk in the grammar school, restless, his mind weaving new threads into the story he’d begun in secret: a tale not of kings or knights, but of himself and this girl whose eyes held more depth than any fairy legend ever promised.

On Saturday afternoons, he wandered the woods with a book of old tales in one hand and dreams in the other. But today, even the forest seemed dull compared to the possibility ahead—Sunday. For Martin, Sunday meant church, long sermons, and enforced stillness. Yet something stirred within him. Perhaps it was hope. Or pride. Or simply the need to see her again.

The morning broke under a sky heavy with snow. By breakfast, the drifts were deep enough that Mrs. Yorke declared no child would venture out. Instead, they were to spend two hours in the back parlour, reading from John Wesley’s sermons—her favorite, for his fire and reformist spirit. “Rose will do as she pleases,” Martin said, without looking up from his bread and milk, his voice low and calm as always.

“Rose will do as she is told,” replied his mother firmly. “And so will you.”

“I am going to church,” Martin said simply, the words falling like stones into silence.

His father frowned. “It’s not fit weather.”
No reply. Martin continued eating, slow and deliberate, as if chewing not only his meal but the very idea of disobedience.

“She hates church,” Mrs. Yorke muttered. “But she hates obedience more.”

“I suppose I’m just being stubborn?” Martin asked, half-serious.

“Yes,” she said. “You are.”

“Mother, I am not.”

“Then by what are you driven?”

“By a tangle of feelings so complicated, I couldn’t explain them any more than I could turn myself inside out and show you my bones.”

“Hear him!” Mr. Yorke laughed. “This boy is born for the bar. Hesther, our third son must become a lawyer—he has all the tools: courage, wit, and an endless supply of words.”

“Some bread, Rose, please,” Martin requested, as though nothing had been said. His tone was soft, almost sorrowful, yet unshakable. He rang the bell, then quietly asked for his walking shoes.

“Martin,” his father urged, “the snow is knee-deep. No man could walk through it.”

But Martin rose as the church bell began to toll. “If he wants to go,” said Mr. Yorke, “let him go. There’s a bitter wind and freezing sleet. Let him walk into it if he prefers it to a warm hearth.”

Martin donned his cloak and cap, stepped out without a word, and vanished into the storm.

As he walked, he whispered to himself: “If the weather keeps her away—or if Mrs. Pryor forbids her to come—I’ll be angry. But she *should* come. If she has any strength, any worth, she *will*. She’ll come to see me, just as I’ve come to see her. Not for duty, not for piety—but because we both know there’s something alive in this meeting. Adventure is life’s true flavor. Without it, everything tastes flat.”

He arrived early. The church stood cold and empty, save for one elderly woman. Slowly, others trickled in—old men, frail women, those who never missed a service, no matter how harsh the winter. The pews of the wealthy remained vacant. Only the humble wooden benches held the faithful.

Martin scanned the room. Where was she?

When the rector entered, when the service began, still she did not appear. He clenched his jaw. “She won’t come,” he muttered. “Worthless. Weak. Just another girl—plain, selfish, shallow. Her eyes aren’t bright, her nose isn’t fine, her mouth doesn’t hold the magic I imagined. She’s just a doll. A toy.”

So lost in his thoughts, he failed to rise during the litany. When the hymn began, he was still on his knees—caught in a moment of mock devotion. He jumped up, flushed with embarrassment. Worse, the church door opened again—the Sunday scholars poured in, their little feet patter-pattering across the stone floor.

They settled, then came the older girls—tall, quiet, serious. And among them, she stepped in. The French-gray cloak, the small beaver bonnet—his heart leapt. She had come.

Martin hid his face behind his hymn book for a moment, then straightened. She had not looked at him. Not once.

“If she ignores me,” he thought, “if she pretends I don’t exist… then I’ll despise her even more. To come here for those children, and not for me? Or for Moore? That would be the worst kind of failure.”

The sermon ended. The congregation dispersed. Still, she had not approached him.

Now, walking home, the wind bit through his coat. He chose a shortcut across the fields—a path untouched by foot, treacherous under snow. As he climbed toward the second stile, he saw movement beneath a tree—an umbrella struggling against the gale, and beside it, the familiar gray cloak.

He smiled. Then stopped. Sat on the stile, cool and composed.

“You should make a deal,” he said. “Trade me for Mrs. Pryor.”

“I wasn’t sure you’d come this way,” she answered. “But I took the chance. There’s no peace in church or churchyard—too many eyes.”

“Will you trade? Give Mrs. Pryor to my mother, and put me in her place?”

“What nonsense! Why bring her into this?”

“You call her ‘mamma,’ don’t you?”

“She *is* my mamma.”

“Impossible. Such a careless, indifferent mother. I’d be ten times better. You may laugh. I don’t mind. Your teeth—most people hate ugly teeth, but yours are lovely—like pearls, even, well-shaped and perfect.”

“Martin, what is this?” she said, startled. “I thought Yorkes didn’t give compliments.”

“They haven’t, until now. I feel called to change the family tradition. I’m tired of being like my ancestors—four generations of stern, solemn men. All named Hiram or Samuel or John. They were exactly like my father. Before them, there was Godfrey. We have his portrait—it hangs in Moore’s room. He looks like me. Long dark curls, fine clothes. Handsome. Different. I believe he was different. And I intend to be too.”

“You’re not handsome, Martin.”

“No,” he said, “but wait. I mean to become so. Starting today. I’ll cultivate myself. Polish myself. Watch.”

“You’re strange, Martin. And I doubt you’ll ever be handsome.”

“I’ll try. But let’s return to Mrs. Pryor. How can she allow her daughter to walk out in this storm?”

“She worried,” she admitted. “But I insisted. I *had* to come.”

“To see me?”

“Exactly. I feared the snow would keep you away. But when I saw you alone in your pew… I was relieved.”

“I came to fulfill my duty,” he said, “and set an example.”

“And you were obstinate?”

“I like obstinacy. I’d love to see you stubborn. I’d train you, if I could.”

“Don’t think you’ll ever be handsome,” she said, laughing.

“I’ll try.”

“Tell me about Mr. Moore,” she said quickly.

“That,” he replied, “is why you came.”

“Come on—just tell me.”

“Hang him—he’s no worse. But still imprisoned. Starved. They want to declare him mad. Horsfall treats him like a beast. You saw how thin he was.”

“You were good to him the other day.”

“What day? I’m always good. A model.”

“When will you be good again?”

“I see where you’re going. But you won’t wheedle me. I’m not a cat’s-paw.”

“It must be done. It’s right. Necessary.”

“You encroach. I helped before—not because I was asked, but because I wanted to.”

“And you’ll help again.”

“I won’t. It was too much trouble. I prefer ease.”

“Mr. Moore wishes to see me. I wish to see him.”

“I dare say,” he said coolly.

“It’s unfair of your mother to shut out his friends.”

“Tell her so.”

“His own family.”

“Go blow her up.”

“You know that would do nothing. But I’ll persist. I’ll see him. With or without help.”

“Good,” Martin said. “Nothing like self-reliance.”

“I have no time to argue. You’re provoking. Goodbye.”

She turned and left, shutting the umbrella against the wind.

Martin watched her go. Then smiled.

“She’s not vapid,” he murmured. “Not shallow. She’s strong. I’ll watch her—see how far she’ll go to get what she wants. If the storm were fire, she’d walk through it to speak to Moore for five minutes. I’ve had a good morning. The delays, the anger—they made the moment sweeter. She expected me to yield at once. She won’t win so easily. She’ll come again. And again. I’d like to see her angry. To see her cry. I want to know how far she’ll go. What she’ll risk. What she truly feels.”

He turned toward home, appetite stirring. “Won’t I enjoy that goose?” he said aloud. “And whoever gets the biggest slice of apple pie—Matthew or I—we’ll have to fight for it.”

Martin had planned well. He had woven a clever scheme for his own quiet amusement. Yet even the most cunning of schemers—older and wiser than he—often find their most carefully spun designs swept away by the sudden, sweeping hand of Fate, that relentless housewife whose red arm no one can resist. In this case, the broom that scattered his plans was forged from the unyielding fibers of Moore’s own stubborn will, bound tight with resolve. He was recovering his strength, and now pushing back against Mrs. Horsfall with astonishing force. Each morning brought a new surprise to that devoted matron. First, he dismissed her from her duties as valet—he would dress himself. Then he refused the coffee she offered—he would breakfast with the family. Finally, he barred her from his room. On the same day, amid the outcry of every woman in the village, he stepped outside into the open air. The next morning, he followed Mr. Yorke to the counting-house and sent word to fetch a chaise from the Red House Inn. He declared he meant to return home to Hollow Cottage that very afternoon. Mr. Yorke, instead of opposing him, quietly supported the decision. The chaise arrived, though Mrs. Yorke insisted it would be fatal. Moore, barely speaking, let his purse speak for him. He paid each servant and Mrs. Horsfall with generous coins. The latter understood perfectly; money made up for all past disobedience. They parted on the best of terms.

Having calmed the kitchen, Moore turned to the parlour. There was Mrs. Yorke, sunk in silent anger, lost in dark thoughts about human ingratitude. He approached, bending low. She could not help but look up—even if only to tell him to go away. Still, there was beauty in his pale, thin face. His hollow eyes held a quiet earnestness, a sweetness—especially when he smiled.

"Good-bye," he said, and as the words left his lips, the smile flickered and faded. He no longer had full control over his emotions; even the smallest feeling stirred visibly in his weakened state.

"And where are you going?" she asked. "We’ll keep you here, do anything in the world for you—if only you’d stay until you’re stronger."

"Good-bye," he repeated, then added softly, "You’ve been like a mother to me. Let your wilful son have one last embrace."

As a foreigner might, he offered first one cheek, then the other. She kissed him.

"What a burden I’ve been," he murmured.

"You’re the worst kind of trouble now, headstrong boy!" she replied. "Who will nurse you at Hollow’s Cottage? Your sister Hortense knows nothing about such things—she’s no more capable than a child."

"Thank God," he said, "for I’ve had enough nursing to last a lifetime."

The little girls came in—Jessie weeping, Rose quiet but solemn. Moore took them into the hall, soothing them, petting them, kissing them gently. He knew his sister-in-law could not bear to see anyone else cherished in her presence. She would have felt affronted if he had cuddled a kitten while she watched.

The boys stood near the chaise as Moore climbed in—but he gave them no farewell. To Mr. Yorke, he simply said, "You're rid of me. That was an unlucky shot, Yorke—it turned Briarmains into a hospital. Come visit me soon at the cottage."

He pulled down the windowpane. The chaise rolled off. Within half an hour, he stepped through the gate of his own garden. After paying the driver and dismissing the carriage, he leaned on the wicket for a moment—resting, reflecting.

"Six months ago," he said aloud, "I walked out of this gate—a proud, angry, disappointed man. Now I return sadder, wiser—weak, yes, but not broken. A cold, gray, yet peaceful world lies around me—one where I hope little, but fear nothing. All petty fears of shame or embarrassment have vanished. If the worst comes, I can work, like Joe Scott, for an honest living. Such a fate holds hardship, but no disgrace. Once, financial ruin meant personal dishonor to me. Not anymore. I know the difference. Ruin *is* an evil—but I am ready for it. I know the day it will come, because I've calculated it. I can delay it six months—no longer. If things change by then—which is unlikely—if the chains binding our trade were somehow loosened (the most improbable thing imaginable)—perhaps I could still win this long battle. Perhaps—I could do wonders! But that thought is madness. Let me see clearly. Ruin will come. It will strike at the roots of my fortune and cut them down. I’ll take a sapling, cross the sea, and plant it in American woods. Louis will go with me. Will anyone else join us? I don’t know. I cannot ask."

He entered the house.

It was late afternoon. Outside, twilight stretched starless and moonless—though the sky wore a mask of frozen clouds, locked fast. The mill-dam was ice-bound. The Hollow was hushed. Inside, darkness had already fallen. Sarah had lit a strong fire in the parlour; she was preparing tea in the kitchen.

"Hortense," Moore said as his sister hurried forward to help him with his cloak, "I’m glad to be home."

Hortense did not grasp the unusual weight of those words—coming from a brother who had never before called the cottage *home*, who had always seen its narrow walls as limiting rather than comforting. Still, anything that brought him joy pleased her, and she expressed her delight.

He sat, then rose again. He walked to the window, then returned to the fire.

"Hortense?"

"Mon frère?"

"This little parlour looks remarkably clean and cheerful—brighter than usual."

"It is true, brother," she said. "I’ve had the whole house cleaned thoroughly while you were away."

"Sister," he said, "on your first day back, you ought to have a guest or two for tea—just so they can see how fresh and neat you’ve made the place."

"True, brother. If it weren’t too late, I could send for Miss Mann."

"You might—but it’s too late to disturb her, and the evening is far too cold for her to venture out."

"How thoughtful of you, dear Gérard! We’ll wait till another day."

"I want someone tonight—someone quiet, who won’t tire either of us."

"Miss Ainley?"

"A good choice, they say—but she lives too far. Tell Harry Scott to run to the rectory and ask Caroline Helstone to come spend the evening with you."

"Wouldn’t tomorrow be better, dear brother?"

"I’d like her to see the house just as it is now—its brightness and order are proof of your care."

"It might set a good example."

"It must. She should come."

He went to the kitchen.

"Sarah, delay tea by half an hour." Then he instructed her to send Harry Scott to the rectory, giving her a hastily scribbled note in pencil, addressed to "Miss Helstone."

Scarcely had Sarah begun to worry about her toast when the messenger returned—with the guest in tow.

She entered through the kitchen, slipped quietly up the stairs to remove her bonnet and furs, and descended just as calmly—her curls neatly arranged, her graceful merino dress and delicate collar spotless, her bright work-bag in hand. She paused to exchange a few warm words with Sarah, admired the new tortoise-shell kitten basking by the hearth, spoke gently to the startled canary, and then made her way to the parlour.

A soft greeting passed between them—cousins meeting after time apart. A quiet pleasure, subtle as perfume, filled the room. The newly lit lamp burned brightly. The tea tray and singing urn were brought in.

"I’m glad to be home," Moore repeated.

They gathered round the table. Hortense did most of the talking. She praised Caroline for her evident improvement in health—her colour returning, her cheeks plump and rosy. It was true. Miss Helstone looked different. Everything about her seemed alive. The heaviness, fear, and sorrow had lifted. No longer drooping or dull, she appeared lightened—like one who had tasted healing and flown on wings of hope.

After tea, Hortense went upstairs. She hadn’t sorted through her drawers in weeks—and now the urge was irresistible. While she was gone, the conversation fell naturally into Caroline’s hands. She carried it effortlessly, slipping into her finest tone. Her speech flowed with grace and elegance, adding charm to familiar topics. Her voice, always soft, now held a new music—gentle, surprising, captivating. Unusual expressions lit her face with character and warmth.

"Caroline," Moore said after watching her intently, "you look as if you’ve heard good news."

"Do I?"

"I invited you tonight to cheer myself—but you cheer me more than I expected."

"I’m glad. And I truly cheer you?"

"You shine—your eyes sparkle, your movements are light, your voice sings."

"It’s pleasant to be here again."

"Indeed it is. And seeing health on your face and hope in your eyes is a joy, Cary. But what is this hope? What has brought this light I see around you?"

"For one thing," she said, "I’m happy with mamma. I love her so much—and she loves me. She nursed me long and tenderly. Now that her care has restored me, I can give back to her all day. I say it’s my turn to serve her—and I do. I’m her waiting-woman as well as her daughter. I enjoy—oh, you’d laugh if you knew how much pleasure I take in sewing dresses for her. She looks lovely now, Robert. I won’t let her be old-fashioned. And she’s wonderful to talk to—wise, thoughtful, rich in knowledge, endlessly observant. Every day I live with her, I love her more, respect her more, cherish her deeper."

"That’s one thing, then, Cary. You speak of 'mamma' in such a way it makes one jealous of the old lady."

"She’s not old, Robert."

"Of the young lady, then."

"She doesn’t pretend to be young."

"Well, of the matron. But you said 'mamma’s' affection was just one reason for your happiness. What’s the other?"

"I’m glad you’re better."

"What else?"

"I’m glad we’re friends."

"You and I?"

"Yes. I once thought we never would be."

"Cary, some day I mean to tell you something about myself—not to my credit, and so it won’t please you."

"Oh, don’t! I can’t bear to think ill of you."

"And I can’t bear that you think better of me than I deserve."

"But I already half know your secret—I believe I know all of it."

"You don’t."

"I believe I do."

"Whom does it concern besides me?"

She blushed, hesitated, stayed silent.

"Speak, Cary. Whom does it concern?"

She tried to whisper a name—but couldn’t.

"Tell me. Only we’re here. Be honest."

"But if I guess wrong?"

"I’ll forgive. Whisper, Cary."

He leaned close. Still, she wouldn’t—or couldn’t—speak plainly. Seeing his patience waning, she finally said, "Miss Keeldar stayed at the rectory about a week ago. The evening grew bitterly cold, and we persuaded her to stay overnight."

"And you curled your hair together?"

"How do you know?"

"And then you talked—she told you—"

"It wasn’t during curling. So you’re not as wise as you think. And she didn’t tell me."

"You slept together afterwards?"

"We shared the same room and bed. We didn’t sleep much—we talked all night."

"I’ll swear you did! And then it all came out—*tant pis*. I’d rather you’d heard it from me."

"You’re mistaken. She didn’t tell me what you suspect—she’s not the kind to reveal such things. But I guessed—partly from what she said, partly from rumors, mostly from instinct."

"But if she didn’t confess that I wanted to marry her for her money, and she refused me with scorn—(you needn’t start nor blush, nor prick your fingers with your needle. That’s the truth, whether you like it or not)—then what did she talk about? You said you talked all night. About what?"

"About things we’d never discussed before, even though we’ve been close friends. But you don’t expect me to tell you?"

"Yes, yes, Cary—you will tell me. You said we were friends. Friends confide in each other."

"But you promise not to repeat it?"

"Absolutely."

"Not even to Louis?"

"Not even to Louis. What does Louis care about young ladies’ secrets?"

"Robert," she said, "Shirley is a remarkable, noble creature."

"I suppose. I can imagine she has strange and grand qualities."

"I’ve found her guarded with her feelings. But when they burst forth—like a river rushing past you without warning—you stare, wonder, admire… and perhaps… love her."

"You saw that?"

"Yes. At midnight, when the house was silent, and starlight and snow-reflected glow shone in our room—that’s when I saw Shirley’s heart."

"Her heart’s core? Do you think she showed you that?"

"Her heart’s core."

"And how was it?"

"Like a shrine—for it was holy. Like snow—for it was pure. Like flame—for it was warm. Like death—for it was strong."

"Can she love? Tell me that."

"What do you think?"

"She has loved none who loved her."

"Who have loved her?"

He named several gentlemen, ending with Sir Philip Nunnely.

"She has loved none of them."

"Yet some were worthy of a woman’s affection."

"Of some women—yes. But not of Shirley’s."

"Is she better than others of her sex?"

"She is unique—and dangerous to take as a wife… rashly."

"I can imagine that."

"She spoke of you—"

"Oh, she did! I thought you denied it."

"She didn’t speak as you imagine. But I asked her—insisted she tell me what she thought of you, or rather, how she felt toward you. I needed to know. I’d long wanted to know."

"So had I. But let’s hear. She thinks poorly of you, feels contempt—doubtless?"

"She thinks of you almost as highly as a woman can think of a man. You know she can be eloquent. I still feel the warmth of her words in my mind."

"But how does she feel?"

"Till you shocked her—she said you shocked her, but wouldn’t explain how—she felt as a sister might toward a brother she both loves and admires."

"I’ll shock her no more, Cary—for the shock rebounded on me, and I staggered. But that comparison is nonsense. She’s too proud and wealthy to feel fraternal feelings toward me."

"You don’t know her, Robert. And somehow, I think now—though I once believed otherwise—that you never will. You and she are built differently—unable to truly understand each other."

"Perhaps. I admire her, esteem her. Yet my impressions are harsh—perhaps unjust. I believe, for instance, that she is incapable of love—"

"Shirley incapable of love!"

"That she will never marry. I think she fears compromising her pride, surrendering her power, sharing her wealth."

"Shirley has wounded your *amour propre*."

"She did. Though I had no tenderness, no passion for her."

"Then, Robert, it was wicked of you to want to marry her."

"And very base, my little pastor, my sweet priestess. I never wanted to kiss Miss Keeldar—though she has fine lips, red and round as cherries. Or if I did, it was only the eye’s desire."

"Now I doubt whether you’re telling the truth. The fruit is sour—'hung too high.'"

"She has a lovely figure, a lovely face, beautiful hair. I acknowledge her charms—but feel nothing for them, or only in a way she’d despise. I suppose I was tempted by the gold of the bait. Caroline, what a noble fellow your Robert is—great, good, selfless—and so pure!"

"But not perfect. He made a great mistake once—and we’ll hear no more about it."

"And shall we forget it, Cary? Shall we not despise him in our hearts—kind but just, compassionate but upright?"

"Never. We’ll remember: 'With what measure ye mete, it shall be measured to you.' So we’ll give no scorn—only love."

"Which won’t satisfy," he warned. "Something stronger, sweeter, warmer will be demanded one day. Is it there to give?"

Caroline was deeply moved.

"Be calm, Lina," Moore said gently. "I have no intention—nor right—to disturb you now, nor for months. Don’t look as if you’re leaving. Let’s drop this. Resume our chatter. Don’t tremble. Look at me. See how pale, how weak, how ghost-like I am—more pitiable than frightening."

She looked shyly. "There’s still something formidable," she whispered, her gaze falling under his.

"To return to Shirley," Moore continued. "Do you think she’ll ever marry?"

"She loves."

"Platonically—hypocritically—all humbug!"

"She loves—sincerely."

"Did she say so?"

"I can’t say she confessed it. No words like 'I love this man' passed her lips."

"I thought not."

"But the feeling broke through anyway. I saw it. She spoke of one man in a tone impossible to misunderstand. Her voice alone was testimony. After I forced her to judge your character, I asked for a second opinion—about someone I suspected, though my suspicions were tangled beyond belief. I *made* her speak. I shook her, scolded her, pinched her fingers when she tried to deflect me with jokes. And finally, it came. The voice—barely above a whisper—yet full of soft intensity. No confession, no confidence. These things she refuses. But I know that man’s happiness is as dear to her as her own life."

"Who is it?"

"I accused her. She didn’t deny it. Didn’t admit it. Just looked at me. I saw her eyes in the glimmer of snow-light. That was enough. I triumphed over her mercilessly."

"What right had you to triumph? Are you free yourself?"

"Whatever I am, Shirley is a prisoner. Lioness, she has found her captor. Mistress of all around her, yet not her own master."

"So you rejoice in recognizing a fellow slave—in one so fair and regal?"

"I do. Robert, you’re right—so fair and regal."

"You admit it—a *fellow*-slave?"

"I admit nothing. But I say haughty Shirley is no freer than Hagar."

"And who, pray, is the Abraham—the hero who conquered her?"

"You still speak with scorn. But I’ll make you change your tone before I’m done."

"We’ll see. Can she marry this Cupidon?"

"Cupidon? He’s just as much a Cupidon as you are a Cyclops."

"Can she marry him?"

"You’ll see."

"I want to know his name, Cary."

"Guess."

"Is it someone nearby?"

"Yes—within Briarfield parish."

"Then it’s someone unworthy of her. I know no one in Briarland equal to her."

"Guess."

"Impossible. I suppose she’s deluded—will plunge into absurdity."

Caroline smiled.

"Do you approve?" Moore asked.

"Completely. Completely."

"Then I’m puzzled. For the mind that owns this cascade of hazel curls is a brilliant little machine—accurate, steady, inherited from 'mamma,' I suppose."

"And I *fully* approve. Mamma was charmed."

"'Mamma' charmed—Mrs. Pryor! Then it can’t be romantic?"

"It *is* romantic. But it’s also right."

"Tell me, Cary—out of pity. I’m too weak to be teased."

"You’ll be teased—it won’t hurt you. You’re not as weak as you claim."

"I’ve twice tonight thought of falling at your feet."

"You’d better not. I won’t help you up."

"And worshipping you outright. My mother was a Roman Catholic. You look like one of her images of the Virgin. I’ll convert—kneel and adore."

"Robert, Robert, sit still. Don’t be absurd. I’ll go to Hortense if you carry on."

"You’ve stolen my senses. Now all I can think of is *les litanies de la sainte Vièrge. Rose céleste, reine des anges*!"

"*Tour d’ivoire, maison d’or*—isn’t that the jargon? Sit quietly and solve your riddle."

"But 'mamma' charmed—there’s the puzzle."

"I’ll tell you what mamma said when I told her. 'Depend upon it, my dear, such a choice will bring Miss Keeldar lasting happiness.'"

"I’ll guess once—and no more. It’s old Helstone. She’s going to be your aunt."

"I’ll tell my uncle! I’ll tell Shirley!" Caroline laughed delightedly. "Guess again, Robert—your mistakes are delightful."

"It’s the parson—Hall."

"No indeed. He’s mine, if you please."

"Yours! The whole female generation in Briarfield seems to worship that priest. I wonder why—he’s bald, sand-blind, gray-haired."

"Fanny will be here to fetch me before you solve it, if you don’t hurry."

"I’ll guess no more—I’m tired. And I don’t care. Miss Keeldar may marry *le grand Turc* for all I care."

"Must I whisper?"

"That you must—and quickly. Here comes Hortense. Come closer, my dear Lina. I care more for the whisper than the words."

She whispered. Robert gave a start, a flash in his eye, a brief laugh. Miss Moore entered, and Sarah followed with news that Fanny had arrived. The hour of conversation was over.

Robert managed a final whisper as Caroline descended the stairs, putting on her shawl.

"Must I call Shirley a noble creature now?" he asked.

"If you wish to speak the truth—certainly."

"Must I forgive her?"

"Forgive her? Naughty Robert! Was she wrong, or were you?"

"Must I, at last, love her—truly, deeply?"

Caroline looked up sharply, moving toward him—half loving, half teasing.

"Only give the word, and I’ll try to obey."

"Indeed, you must not love her. The very idea is perverse."

Louis Moore’s fears about Mr. Sympson’s sudden departure from Fieldhead proved well-founded. The very next day after the heated argument over Sir Philip Nunnely, a fragile peace was struck between uncle and niece. Shirley, who could never bring herself to appear unkind or unwelcoming—except in the case of Mr. Donne—earnestly begged the entire party to stay a little longer. Her plea carried such sincerity that it was clear she had a reason beyond mere hospitality. They accepted her invitation. Indeed, Mr. Sympson could not bear to leave her entirely unwatched, especially with Robert Moore possibly returning soon to claim what he believed was his right to her hand. So they remained.

In his initial fury against the Moore family, Mr. Sympson had treated Louis Moore with such harshness that the young man—used to hardship but unable to endure insult—had promptly resigned. Only Mrs. Sympson’s pleading, combined with his deep affection for his pupil, convinced him to return, at least until the family left Yorkshire. And perhaps there was another reason—something deeper, more personal—making it difficult for him to walk away from Fieldhead just yet.

For a time, things settled into a quiet rhythm. Miss Keeldar regained her health; her spirits returned, light and buoyant. Moore had eased every fear she once carried, and since opening her heart to him, all anxiety seemed to vanish. She became as carefree as a child who trusts its parents completely, leaving no thought for danger or sorrow. He and William Farren—through whom Moore checked on Phœbe—agreed the dog was not mad, only driven from home by cruel treatment. It was known her master frequently beat her. The groom and gamekeeper insisted otherwise, claiming hydrophobia was no real illness if it didn’t present clearly. But Louis Moore dismissed their claims. He reported only the hopeful news to Shirley, and she believed him. Right or wrong, the bite had no ill effect.

November slipped by; December arrived. The Sympsons were finally preparing to leave. Christmas loomed, and they must be home by then. Their trunks were packed; departure was near. One cold winter evening, during the last week of their stay, Louis Moore took out his small blank book and wrote:

* * * * *

She is more beautiful than ever. Since the shadow passed, all pallor and weariness have faded. It was astonishing how quickly youth restored her bloom—the vitality of life rising again like spring after frost.

This morning, after breakfast, I saw her, heard her, felt her—every nerve in my body stirred by her presence. Then I stepped into the chilly drawing-room. I picked up a small gilt book, filled with lyrics. I read a few poems. Whether the magic was in the verse or in me, I cannot say—but my heart warmed, my pulse quickened. I glowed, though the air outside was frozen. I am still young. Though she once said I wasn’t young, I’m barely thirty. There are moments when life itself shines brightly, simply because I am young.

It was time for school. I went. The room was pleasant in the morning light. Sun streamed through the low lattice windows. Books stood neatly arranged. No papers littered the floor. The fire burned clean, no ashes piled. I found Henry there, and he had brought Miss Keeldar with him. They were together.

I said she was more beautiful than ever. She is. A soft rose, pale but delicate, blooms on her cheek. Her eyes—always dark, clear, and expressive—now speak a language I cannot name. It is a silence full of meaning, like angels communing in heaven’s hush. Her hair has always been dark as night, fine as silk; her neck fair and graceful. But now, there is something new. The tresses fall like shadows, and the shoulders beneath them carry a grace that belongs to goddesses. Once, I only *saw* her beauty. Now, I *feel* it.

Henry was reciting his lesson before bringing it to me. One hand held the book; the other, hers. That boy enjoys privileges beyond his years. He dares to touch her—and is touched in return. How tenderly she treats him! Too tenderly. If this continues, in a few years, when his soul is formed, he may offer it at her altar—as I have offered mine.

I saw her eyelid flutter when I entered, but she did not look up. Now, she rarely looks at me. She seems quieter too—speaks little to me, and even less to others when I’m near. In darker moments, I wonder if this change means indifference, or worse. But in brighter ones, I interpret it differently. Were I her equal, I might see in this shyness a kind of coyness—and in that coyness, love. As it is, can I hope? What would I do if I found it?

This morning, I dared to arrange an hour alone with her. Not merely to wish it—but to *will* it. I summoned solitude to guard us. I called Henry to the door without hesitation.

“Go wherever you like, my boy,” I said. “But don’t return here until I call.”

Henry clearly disliked being sent away. He is young, but thoughtful. His gaze sometimes holds a strange depth. He senses something between us—he half guesses that the reserve with which I am treated brings a deeper joy than any affection he receives. The young, lame lion growls at me occasionally, resentful that I’ve tamed his lioness and now stand as her keeper. But discipline and affection keep him subdued. Go, Henry. You must learn to accept your share of life’s bitterness, like every other son of Adam. Your fate will not be different. Be thankful your love is denied so early—before it can become passion. A moment of frustration, a pang of envy—these are enough to express what you feel. Jealousy fierce as the equatorial sun, rage destructive as tropical storms—your emotions still ignore such extremes.

I sat at my desk, as usual. I am fortunate in my ability to mask inner turmoil with outward calm. No one looking at my steady face would guess the storm swirling within—engulfing thought, destroying caution. It is a comfort to move forward peacefully, without alarming anyone with sudden motion. I had no intention of declaring my love today. I meant only to observe her closely—to read one line in the book of her heart. Before I left, I wanted to know what I was leaving behind.

I had quills to shape into pens. Most men’s hands would tremble under such emotion. Mine worked steadily. My voice, when I spoke, remained firm.

“This time next week, you’ll be alone at Fieldhead, Miss Keeldar.”

“Yes. I think my uncle’s decision to go is final.”

“He leaves you dissatisfied.”

“He is not pleased with me.”

“He departs as he came—no better for his journey. That is painful.”

“I trust his failed plans will take away his desire to make new ones.”

“In his way, Mr. Sympson meant well. Everything he did, he believed was best.”

“You’re kind to defend someone who treated you so rudely.”

“I never hold grudges for actions true to character. And his violent outburst against me—when he’d already lost the argument—was perfectly in character.”

“You’re no longer Henry’s tutor?”
